The table of contents lay open, a map of a journey that refused to begin. It listed eighty-nine chapters, a procession of titles promising blood, love, and the grand movements of history. *Harry to the Rescue.* *Fortunatus Nimium.* *Yankee Doodle comes to Town.* These were the promises of the epic, the scaffolding built to support a hero who would never quite stand.

Instead, the narrative started in the silence between the lines.

One of the Virginians visited Home. Not a triumphant return, but a visitation where the house itself seemed to judge the visitor. Harry had to pay for his supper, a transaction that cost him more than silver shillings; it cost him dignity, a small coin of self-respect exchanged for the privilege of eating. The Esmonds in Virginia were not a dynasty rising like a storm cloud, but a family jar, cracked and leaking, holding together only through the sheer friction of daily survival.

Harry found a new relative, a stranger in blood who offered no comfort, only a new set of expectations. The Virginians began to see the world, but their vision was narrow, focused on the immediate horizon of debt and duty rather than the distant glory of empire. Preparations for war were made not with fanfare, but with the quiet, grinding anxiety of men who knew the cost of a bullet. George suffered from a common disease, a vulnerability that stripped away any pretense of invincibility.

Hospitalities were extended, yet the warmth was thin. A hot afternoon stretched endlessly, a pause in the machinery of fate where nothing happened except the slow decay of patience. The two Georges prepared for blood, sharpening edges that would soon turn inward. News from the camp arrived late, filtered through layers of rumor until it sounded like fiction. The quest was profitless, a hunt for something that could not be caught.

Harry flew to England, seeking a stage larger than the one he left behind, only to find himself trapped in the same cycles of vanity. Sundays at Castlewood were marked by the ticking of clocks and the settling of dust. Gumbo showed skill with the old English weapon, a proficiency that highlighted the absurdity of training for battles that might never come. An old story was told, a ghost haunting the present moment.

Love and luck arrived together, indistinguishable from one another, leading to a descent that was too easy, too smooth. The company fell silent, eyes fixed forward, waiting for a signal that never came. Harry smoked his pipe at home, watching the smoke curl upward, a solitary figure in a crowded room. Letters to Virginia carried news that changed nothing. The bear and the leader faced each other across a divide of misunderstanding.

Chains and slavery were discussed as abstract concepts, far removed from the hands that forged them. Visitors in trouble sought shelter, adding weight to the household scales. An apparition flickered in the corner, dismissed as fatigue. Friends in need were counted, then lost. Morality was found in the finest moments, fleeting sparks in a dark room. All eyes were held fast, listening to a story that ended before it began.

Harry lived to fight another day, a promise that became a curse. Soldiers returned empty-handed. Courting was a performance of roles played badly. A tragedy was acted, and two more were begun, overlapping in a chaotic mess of grief. Harry went westward, chasing a horizon that receded. Cupid played a considerable part, pulling strings that snapped under tension. Favors were granted and withheld. My Lady climbed the ladder, reaching for a rung that did not exist.

Christmas at Castlewood in 1759 was celebrated with food that tasted of ash. News from Canada drifted across the ocean, irrelevant to the local concerns. The course of true love ran counter to reason. Mr. Warrington jumped into a landau, and everyone else followed suit, moving in a synchronized dance of folly. Pyramus and Thisbe reenacted their deaths in a garden that refused to bloom. Comedy and tragedy merged into a single, confusing noise.

Pocahontas was remembered as a legend, not a person. Distress at home was the only constant. Miles moidores were spent on things that vanished instantly. Harry submitted to the common lot, accepting his place in the queue. A port was found, but it was not the harbor he sought. He was finally at home, surrounded by people who were strangers. The last notes of God Save the King faded into Yankee Doodle, a march for a nation that hadn't yet decided what it wanted to be.

They fought and ran away simultaneously. Under the vine and fig tree, the sun beat down on a world that cared nothing for their struggles. The Virginians closed the book, not because the story was finished, but because the next page promised only more of the same.

The library wall in Boston holds two crossed swords, relics of a single family fractured by history. One blade gleams with the polish of royal service; the other bears the scars of republican defiance. They belong to a man whose genius commands respect across the Atlantic, a neutral ground where the ghosts of opposing armies finally rest. These weapons frame the story of the Warringtons, descendants of the Old Dominion who found themselves enemies in the Revolutionary War, only to reunite as brothers in peace. Their portraits hang side by side in the English parlor of the Warringtons, alongside letters and papers that whisper of a life lived between worlds. To the family, these figures are simply "The Virginians," a name that christens their memoirs and defines their dual existence.

Both men spent their youth drifting on the edge of the Old World, navigating a landscape of fortunes and faces that seemed frozen in amber. Their correspondence reveals encounters with historical giants, voices that feel startlingly immediate despite the decades separating us. Some pages are stained with the ink of youthful despair, sent from grand balls or prison cells; one letter bears a hole where a bullet tore through the paper, the surrounding text blurred by the blood of its carrier. These documents survived only because of a mother's obsessive thrift, preserving everything from Henry's early notes about a sprained ankle at Castlewood to her final farewell, penned hours before her death. Though she rarely traveled, taking refuge in Richmond when fire consumed her ancestral home, she clung to the name Madam Esmond, dismissing the Warrington lineage as secondary to her own legacy.

The narrative begins in the summer of 1756, under the reign of George II. The *Young Rachel*, commanded by Edward Franks, ascended the Avon River, returning from the Potomac. Mooring near Trail's Wharf, the vessel became the stage for a collision of commerce and destiny. Mr. Trail, a merchant with a grave demeanor, surveyed the ship from his counting-house before boarding to inspect the cargo. Franks, a jovial man prone to humor, introduced the sole passenger: a young man of nineteen draped in deep mourning, carrying a cloak and a sword.

"Gumbo, you idiot, why don't you fetch the baggage?" the stranger barked at a servant, then addressed the captain with weary resignation. "Our journey is ended. Give my love to Polly, Betty, and Little Tommy... I thought the voyage would never end, and now I am almost sorry it is over."

Trail's face darkened at the sight of a free passenger occupying the entire cabin. "Confound you, Franks, and your luck!" he muttered, comparing the current arrival unfavorably to the fourteen enslaved Africans brought by the previous ship. When Franks pointed out the unpaid fare, the young man stepped forward. Trail bowed profusely, recognizing the name. "Mr. Warrington! Son of my benefactress, Madam Esmond Warrington!" he declared, offering a boat and a handshake.

Franks interjected, pulling a brass tobacco box from his pocket. "There's no sweeter tobacco from Virginia than the Three Castles," he boasted, stuffing a quid into his mouth. He praised the late Mrs. Warrington's generosity, contrasting her with Trail's stinginess. Trail, eager to curry favor, offered to supply the plantation with healthy young negroes from the Guinea trade.

"We are averse to the purchase of negroes from Africa," the young man replied, his voice cold and detached. "My grandfather and my mother have always objected to it."

"It is for their good, my dear young sir!" Trail cried, seizing the opportunity. "For their temporal and spiritual good! Let me introduce you to a Christian family."

"Can't say," grumbled Franks, dismissing the invitation. "Never asked me to take a bite or sup at your table."

Ignoring the captain, Trail pressed his advantage, assuming the visitor was the heir to the vast Esmond estate. "As an honest factor, I cannot refrain from urging the propriety of increasing your ample means..."

"My name is not George," the young man interrupted, turning away with tears filling his eyes. "My name is Henry."

"Gracious powers! What do you mean?" Trail sputtered, confused by the correction.

"Hold your tongue, you fool!" Franks roared, striking the merchant sharply on the flank. "Don't you see the young gentleman swabbing his eyes? Note his black clothes."

"Mr. George is the heir," Trail insisted, pointing toward the deck. "I know the Colonel's will."

"Mr. George is there," Franks corrected, gesturing upward toward the rigging. "Dead a year, sir. Come next 9th of July. He went out with General Braddock to Belle Rivière. He and a thousand more never came back. Murdered as they fell."

Franks painted a vivid, somber picture of the lost brother, describing how the deceased had been a shy, silent figure who loved books, unlike the lively, song-filled Henry standing before them. The captain recounted how Henry had taken to his bed with a fever contracted in the swampy Potomac, only to recover during the voyage. Yet, the grief lingered; Henry would suddenly turn melancholy, wishing his dead brother could share the view, unable to bear the mention of his name. Even the honest captain's eyes grew wet as he spoke of the tragedy.

Trail adopted a mournful expression, preparing a tragic greeting, but Henry responded with curt indifference. He declined hospitality, accepting only a glass of wine and a sum of cash needed for his journey. Parting ways warmly with Franks, Henry left the *Young Rachel* to the cheers of the crew, stepping onto solid ground to begin his pilgrimage home.

Before him lay the map of England, a route charted countless times by American dreamers. His path wound past London's cathedrals and the grim Tower of London, where loyalists had bled; past Whitehall, the site of Charles I's execution; and through the parks and playhouses of a nation steeped in wit and splendor. He would pass Shakespeare's resting place in Warwickshire and the battlefields of Derby, Falkirk, and Culloden. Yet, above all these monuments of history and glory, one destination held supreme sanctity: the old Castlewood in Hampshire, the home spoken of so fondly by their parents, the anchor of a family torn apart by war and reunited by memory.

The iron of the bell handle bit into Harry's palm, a sharp, metallic promise ignored by the silence within Castlewood. For a long moment, the summons hung in the air, unanswered by ghosts or living breath. Finally, a servant emerged, his face a mask of indifference. The family was gone, retreating into the vacuum of their absence, leaving John to his cards and Thomas to the window-seat. To this household, bloodline was irrelevant currency; the housekeeper, however, was a merchant of attention, busy polishing the future arrival of the Lords and Ladies. Only through sheer persistence did Harry pry open a crack in her schedule, granted access to the sitting-room and the gallery. There, pinned to the wall, lay the ancestors: his grandfather in the stiff rigidity of a periwig and breastplate, a mirror image of the statue in Virginia; his grandmother, Lady Castlewood, captured in the loose, golden chaos of Charles II's era, her neck exposed, her hair a cascade of ringlets once snow-white, now faded to memory. These images held him captive until the housekeeper's grip tightened, driving him out as the family's shadow approached.

Outside, the air tasted of anticipation and neglect. Harry scribbled his lineage onto a scrap of paper from his pocket-book—a declaration of identity in a world that seemed determined to erase it. *Henry Esmond Warrington, of Castlewood, in Virginia.* He placed the note on the hall table. The lackeys, rising from their games to collect their wagers, opened the door not with reverence, but with the mechanical efficiency of servants paid to serve strangers. Gumbo, the gatekeeper, accepted a guinea with the casual ignorance of a man who does not understand value, taking the coin without grasping the weight of the gift. Harry walked away from the ancestral hall, the disappointment settling in his chest like a stone. If they had known him—if they had ridden up to his Virginia home—the protocol of hospitality would have been automatic. Instead, standing before the monument to his heritage, he was forced to beg for bacon and eggs at a roadside alehouse.

Later, under the fading light, Harry sat on the bridge. The sun dipped below the horizon, painting the old house in shades of dying gold, while rooks cawed their chaotic symphony above the elms. His mind drifted, constructing knights and cavaliers across the ford, imagining his grandmother's first husband hunting with hawk and hound. Then, the ghost of his lost brother surfaced, striking him with a tenderness so acute it brought tears to his eyes. The sorrow of a shared life cut off too soon washed over him. Around him, the blacksmith's hammer fell in a rhythmic, mechanical pulse, mixing with the bird calls and the distant hum of the evening. Suddenly, the rhythm broke. A pair of riders dashed over the bridge. One swore, kicking up dust, telling Harry to clear the path; the other, believing he had nearly thrown the pedestrian into the water, spurred his horse forward, shouting for his companion to follow. Before Harry could process the insult, the advance party was joined by livery servants on horseback, their faces grimacing with a distinctly British hostility. Behind them came the coach-and-six, a ponderous beast requiring a team of horses to drag it along. Three pale faces peered out at Harry as the carriage crossed the bridge, offering no return to the salute he rendered, recognizing the family crest. The gentleman seated behind the carriage glared, a look of pure hauteur that made Harry feel utterly invisible.

Desolation settled deep in his bones. He considered turning back to the *Rachel*, the ship with its tossing cabin feeling suddenly warmer than the stone bridge. The innkeepers knew nothing of the Warrington name. They informed him that the lady in the coach was the Countess, accompanied by her stepdaughter, Lady Maria, and her daughter, Lady Fanny. The young man in the grey frock was Mr. William; the powdered youth beside him was the Lord. It was the latter who had screamed the loudest, and the grey frock that had nearly galloped Harry into the ditch.

The landlord of the Three Castles had provided a bedchamber, but Harry refused to unpack his trunks, clinging to the delusion that the great house would eventually summon him. Hours bled into darkness. No invitation came. Reluctantly, he opened his case, pulling on his slippers and gown. Just as twilight deepened, a second chariot rolled over the bridge, drawn by four horses. A stout, high-colored lady with dark eyes scanned the room, fixing her gaze on Harry. This was the Baroness Bernstein, the landlady confirmed, the aunt of the Lord. Harry recalled the first Lady Castlewood's German origins, the lineage of Earls and Counts. Then, the procession vanished behind the castle gates, leaving Harry alone in the gloom, overwhelmed by a crushing sense of abandonment. Sleep eluded him, broken instead by the raucous noises spilling from the barroom downstairs—cursing, giggling, screaming.

Then, Gumbo's voice cut through the night, protesting outside. "You cannot go in, sar—my master asleep, sar!" But a shrill, drunken voice answered, drowning out the plea. "Stupid, negro woolly-pate," the voice cursed, pushing past the barrier. A flood of oaths poured into the room, followed by a young gentleman stumbling inside.

"Beg your pardon, Cousin Warrington," the intruder blurted, reeling. "Are you asleep? Beg your pardon for riding you over on the bridge. Didn't know you—course shouldn't have done it—thought it was a lawyer with a writ. Dressed in black, you know. Gad! Thought it was Nathan come to nab me." Mr. William laughed, a sound devoid of coherence, clearly intoxicated.

"You did me great honour to mistake me for a sheriff's officer, cousin," Harry replied, sitting upright in his tall nightcap, maintaining a dignity that contrasted sharply with the scene around him.

"Gad! I thought it was Nathan, and was going to send you souse into the river. But I ask your pardon. You see I had been drinking at the Bell at Hexton, and the punch is good at the Bell at Hexton. Hullo! you, Davis! a bowl of punch; d'you hear?"

"I have had my share for to-night, cousin, and I should think you have," Harry continued, his tone grave.

"You want me to go, Cousin What's-your-name, I see," Mr. William declared, his voice dropping to a mock seriousness. "You want me to go, and they want me to come, and I didn't want to come. I said, I'd see him hanged first—that's what I said. Why should I trouble myself to come down all alone of an evening, and look after a fellow I don't care a pin for? Zackly what I said. Zackly what Castlewood said. Why the devil should he go down? Castlewood says, and so said my lady, but the Baroness would have you. It's all the Baroness's doing, and if she says a thing, it must be done; so you must just get up and come." Mr. Esmond delivered this rambling manifesto with amiable rapidity, darting about the room as he spoke. But Harry's wrath flared. "I tell you what, cousin," he cried, "I won't move for the Countess, or for the Baroness, or for all the cousins in Castlewood."

Just then, the landlord entered with the bowl of punch Mr. William had ordered. The young gentleman in bed erupted. "Turn that sot out of the room!" he screamed.

"Sot, you little tobacconist! Sot, you Cherokee!" Mr. William shrieked back, leaping toward the bed. "Jump out of bed, and I'll drive my sword through your body. Why didn't I do it to-day when I took you for a bailiff—a confounded pettifogging bum-bailiff!" He continued his tirade, a cacophony of oaths and incoherence, until the landlord, the drawer, the hostler, and the entire kitchen staff converged to haul him away. Left alone, Harry pulled his covers tight around him, sulking in a storm of anger, before finally succumbing to sleep.

By the next morning, the landlord's demeanor had shifted completely. Obsequious and eager, he greeted his young guest, now fully aware of his name and station. Messengers had arrived from the castle the previous night to retrieve the young gentlemen. Poor Mr. William had returned in a wheelbarrow, a mode of transport he was apparently accustomed to. "He never remembers nothin' about it the next day," the landlord vowed. "He is of a real kind nature, Mr. William... He's the devil when he's tipsy, Mr. William, but when he is sober he is the very kindest of young gentlemen."

While Harry waited outside, a domestic council convened within the walls of Castlewood House. The family had found the note Harry had left, detailing his arrival. My Lord Castlewood reasoned that since they hadn't drowned the man on the bridge, they must invite him. A servant should carry a note. Lady Fanny suggested it would be more civil if one of the brothers visited their kinsman, especially given the rude greeting they had exchanged. Lord Castlewood agreed, nodding toward his brother William. But William's response was immediate and vehement: he would be hanged if he went. Lady Maria, however, saw something else. "Castlewood is dreadfully dull, I

The year ended with the dust of war settling over the realm. Henry Esmond, Esq., a colonel who had worn the uniform through the campaigns of Queen Anne, found himself stranded in the aftermath. The nation had chosen a new dynasty, preferring the House of Hanover to the restored Stuarts. Those who opposed the change, including Esmond, were advised to leave the three kingdoms. Though Esmond regretted his role in the conflict and the new prince proved a pacific ruler, the path forward was paved with exile. Friends managed to smooth the edges of his situation, securing a pardon, but the decision to depart remained.

Esmond belonged to the line of Castlewood in Hampshire. It was a known fact that King James II and his son had once offered him the title of Marquis, alongside the Irish peerage inherited by his family. He had refused to correct the informality required to claim it. Weary of European politics and personal turmoil, he chose Virginia instead. There, he took possession of a vast estate granted by King Charles I to his ancestor. It was in this American soil that his daughter and grandsons were born, and where his wife eventually died. She had been the widow of his kinsman, the Viscount Castlewood, slain in a duel by Lord Mohun near the end of William's reign.

He named his American home Castlewood, mirroring the ancestral seat left behind. The colony was modeled meticulously on English customs, a loyal outpost where the Virginians claimed Charles II reigned before he did in England. The English church and king were honored with rigid fidelity. The local gentry, allied with old English families, held their heads high above the Dutch traders of New York and the mercantile Roundheads of Pennsylvania. They were far from republican in spirit.

Life on these great estates followed a patriarchal rhythm. Each property commanded a multitude of hands—purchased and assigned servants—who obeyed the master's will. The land provided food, livestock, and game; the great rivers teemed with fish. Ships loaded tobacco from private wharves on the Potomac or James River, carrying it to London or Bristol in exchange for manufactured goods. Hospitality was boundless; no stranger was turned away. The gentry traveled between homes in a state nearly feudal. The institution of slavery existed, though the specific question of its morality was not yet born. To own black servants did not shock a Virginian gentleman, nor was the exercise of power over them generally savage. Food was plentiful, and the enslaved lived without want, though the whip and the corn-bag remained tools of control.

Madam Esmond accepted this order as naturally as letting horses roam loose. Her father, however, possessed a skeptical turn of mind. His doubts did not manifest as active rebellion, but rather as a quiet disaffection. Once, he had sought glory in active life, but later, a certain bankruptcy of the heart settled over him. He was not unhappy; he was kind, affectionate, and obsequious to the women of his family, rarely contradicting them. Yet, he submitted to life rather than enjoying it. He found his greatest spirits not in living, but in the prospect of laying it down.

With his wife gone, his daughter assumed management of the estate and her father's affairs. He acquiesced completely, caring only for his books and quiet. When visitors came, he entertained them handsomely with a pleasant, sarcastic wit, feeling no sorrow when they departed.

"My love," he told his daughter, "I shall not be sorry to go myself. And you, though the most affectionate of daughters, will console yourself after a while. Why should I, who am so old, be romantic? You may, who are still a young creature." He spoke this knowing she was a matter-of-fact person, lacking the capacity for such sentiment.

Fifteen years passed. Affairs prospered, and the worthy proprietor agreed to his daughter's plan for a mansion grander and more durable than the plain wooden edifice he had inhabited. She desired a habitation worthy of their noble name, perhaps fueled by ambition to take rank among neighbors who had already built handsome houses. Colonel Esmond cared little for quarters or quarterings, but his daughter held a high opinion of her lineage's merit and antiquity. In his serene decline, he humored her peculiarities, aiding her with his antiquarian learning and his proficiency in painting.

Heraldry had been part of the education of noble ladies and gentlemen a century prior. During her visit to Europe, Miss Esmond had studied family histories and pedigrees with grave diligence, returning to Virginia with documents she treated with implicit trust, alongside edifying French and English volumes on the noble science. These works convinced her that the Esmonds descended from Norman warriors who arrived with their victorious chief, and from native English of royal dignity. Two magnificent heraldic trees, painted by the Colonel's hand, depicted the family springing from Emperor Charlemagne, drawn in plate armor with imperial mantle and diadem, and from Queen Boadicea, portrayed in the light costume of an ancient British queen, wearing a prodigious gilded crown, a fur mantle, and a symmetrical figure tastefully tattooed with brilliant blue designs. From these illustrious stocks, the family tree rose until it united in the thirteenth century in the person of a fortunate Esmond claiming descent from both.

She wrote herself Esmond Warrington but was universally called Madam Esmond of Castlewood after her father's death. It is feared that quarrels over precedence occasionally disturbed her temper. Though her father had burned a marquis's patent from King James, she acted as if it existed and held full force. She considered the English Esmonds of inferior dignity to her own branch and asserted her superiority over the colonial aristocracy. Consequently, quarrels, angry words, and occasional scuffles arose at the Governor's assemblies in Jamestown. What purpose does recalling these squabbles serve? The participants are beyond reach now, and the republic has ended such social inequalities. Before independence, no country was more aristocratic than Virginia. Thus, the Virginians, whose history we narrate, were bred to respect home institutions fully. The rightful king had no more faithful subjects than the young twins of Castlewood.

When the boys' grandfather died, their mother, in great state, proclaimed her eldest son, George, the successor and heir. Harry, George's younger brother by half an hour, was always enjoined to respect his senior. The entire household, including the large and happy family of negroes and the assigned servants from Europe whose lot was made bearable under the Lady of Castlewood's government, was instructed to pay him honor.

There was scarcely a rebel in the family except Madam Mountain, the faithful friend and companion of Mrs. Esmond, and Harry's foster-mother, a faithful negro woman. She could never understand why her child should not be first, insisting he was handsomer, stronger, and cleverer than his brother, though in truth, there was little difference in their beauty, strength, or stature. In disposition, they were exceedingly unlike, yet in feature they resembled each other so closely that, aside from the color of their hair, it was difficult to distinguish them. In their beds, covered with vast ribboned nightcaps worn by great and small ancestors alike, it was impossible for anyone but a nurse or mother to tell one from the other.

Though alike in form, they differed in temper. The elder was peaceful, studious, and silent; the younger was warlike and noisy. Harry was quick to learn once begun but slow to start. No threat of the ferule would provoke him to study in an idle fit, nor prevent George from helping his brother. Harry had a strong military turn; he drilled the little negroes on the estate, caned them like a corporal, and engaged in many good boxing matches with them, never bearing malice if worsted. George, conversely, was sparing of blows and gentle with all around him.

As custom dictated, each boy had a special little servant. It was a known fact that George, finding his little wretch of a blackamoor asleep on his master's bed, sat beside it and brushed flies off the child with a feather fan. This act horrified old Gumbo, the child's father, who found his young master so engaged, and enraged Madam Esmond, who ordered the young negro off to the proper officer for a whipping. In vain George implored, entreated, burst into passionate tears, and besought a remission of the sentence. His mother was inflexible regarding the young rebel's punishment. The little negro went off beseeching his young master not to cry.

A fierce quarrel ensued between mother and son. Her son would not be pacified. He declared the punishment a shame—a shame—that he was the master of the boy and no one, not even his mother, had a right to touch him. He acknowledged she might order correction and that he would suffer the punishment as he and Harry often had, but no one should lay a hand on his boy. Trembling with passionate rebellion against what he conceived as injustice, he actually shrieked out an oath that shocked his fond mother and governor, who had never heard such language from the usually gentle child. He vowed that on the day he came of age, he would set young Gumbo free. He visited the child in the slaves' quarters and gave him one of his own toys.

The young black martyr was an impudent, lazy, saucy little personage who would be none the worse for a whipping, as the Colonel no doubt thought. He acquiesced in the child's punishment when Madam Esmond insisted, laughing in his good-natured way when his indignant grandson called out, "You let mamma rule you in everything, grandpapa

Castlewood was a fortress of comfort, a principality defined by the sheer weight of its possessions. It possessed an ancient name, a sprawling estate, and a fortune sufficient to buy silence. Within its walls, black and white servants scrambled over one another's heels to execute commands; the air smelled of tobacco, expensive upholstery, and the certainty of survival. Yet, amidst this abundance, the atmosphere was thin, stripped of warmth by a singular, rigid presence.

Madam Esmond Warrington sat enthroned in the center of this domestic universe. She was a creature of absolutes, a little queen whose reign was maintained not by love, but by the relentless application of will. She prayed daily, scrupulously, listing her blessings—the good cook, the cellar, the library—but these recitations served only to reinforce her own centrality. To her, obedience was the metric of virtue; resistance was a crime requiring punishment. If a subject yielded, she was benevolent; if they hesitated, she became a storm.

Her husband, the Colonel, was absent, having died in the prime of his youth, a fortunate accident that spared the household further friction. Without him to check her, Madam Esmond expanded her dominion. She dismissed the English factor, hired replacements, and micromanaged the planting of tobacco fields. She imported tutors and oversaw the construction of fine buildings, though the project halted abruptly when she decided to stop buying books. Books were dangerous; they harbored Jesuits and bad principles.

This shift marked the beginning of a slow erosion of her connections. The letters to the Warrington family in England ceased after a judge's wife recounted Madam Esmond's insults regarding her relatives. The correspondence to the Hampshire Esmonds followed suit. She had married an English baronet's younger son merely to please her parents, a transaction she viewed as a permanent stain. Now, she demanded precedence over colonial dignitaries, fighting duels of etiquette with the wives of judges and governors. She believed herself the elder branch of the family, superior to her half-brothers, the Lords Castlewood, and utterly disdainful of the Warringtons.

The fracture within the house crystallized around the legacy of six thousand pounds. Upon the deaths of her father and uncle, this sum belonged to her youngest son, Harry. Madam Esmond, however, viewed the money as hers to command. She argued with the London trustee, Mr. Draper, calling him an insolent pettifogger who doubted the honor of an Esmond. She refused to believe that the funds were held in trust for Harry alone.

George, the elder son, sided with reason. He told his mother he did not want the money and wished to give it to Harry if he could. But feelings, not logic, governed the household. Madam Esmond saw this refusal as a betrayal. She screamed of injustice, of a child left destitute while the London lawyers withheld aid. Tears flowed freely, accusations flew, and the bond between mother and sons snapped under the strain.

The final break occurred in the family coach on a Sunday. Madam Esmond, dressed in silks, lamented the lost inheritance. "Three hundred and eighty guineas," she cried, looking at Harry. "If only I had saved it for thee!"

"You will give me plenty while you live," Harry replied, his tone light despite the gravity of the situation. "And George will give me plenty when you die."

"Not unless he changes in spirit," Madam Esmond retorted, casting a grim glance at her eldest son. "Heaven must soften his heart."

Mrs. Mountain, the widow companion seated behind them, offered a dry observation. "There seems no need to disturb yourself so, madam."

"Of course there is!" the widow shrieked, rustling her skirts. "My eldest is a disobedient son, and my poor Harry is a mess of pottage!"

George, overwhelmed, buried his face in his hands and wept. Harry moved instantly, wrapping his arms around his brother's neck, kissing him repeatedly. "Don't mind her, George. She doesn't mean it."

"She does mean it," the mother insisted, her voice rising.

"HOLD YOUR TONGUE!" Harry roared, the sound cutting through the carriage. "It is a shame to speak so to him."

Mrs. Mountain shook Harry's hand. "You never said a truer word in your life."

"Mrs. Mountain, do you dare set my children against me?" the widow demanded.

"From this very day, madam—"

"Turn me and my child into the street?" Mrs. Mountain interrupted calmly. "Do. That will be a fine revenge because the English lawyer won't give you the boy's money. Find another companion who will tell you black is white. I am packing."

The widow looked at her children with eyes of extreme affection, perhaps seeing both of them clearly for the first time. During the sermon, George kept his head bowed, while Harry sat close, his arm draped protectively around his brother's neck.

Later, in the wainscoted parlor, the scene shifted. Madam Bernstein, a visitor from outside the immediate circle, listened to Harry's account with infinite patience. She found amusement not in the grandeur of the castle, but in the boy's voice. When the family members lingered nearby, she insisted they leave, claiming she needed to hear her cousin speak undisturbed.

"Do you consider me your cousin, too?" Harry asked, noting her keen attention.

"That picture above you is by Sir Godfrey," she said, gesturing to a portrait of a young woman with auburn hair and a fair forehead. "Sir Godfrey thought himself the greatest painter in the world. But he was not so good as Lely, who painted your grandmother, my Lady Castlewood. Nor as Van Dyck, who painted your great-grandfather. Some of us are painted blacker than we are."

"I fancied I knew the portrait from instinct," Harry admitted. "Perhaps because of a likeness to my mother."

"Your mother is my half-sister," the lady revealed softly. "Before she married your grandfather, my mother had another daughter in England. She was called Beatrix Esmond. Your mother has never even mentioned my name."

Harry stared at the painting, the realization settling over him like a physical weight. The lineage he had known, the stories of the Esmonds, suddenly contained a missing piece, a shadow cast by a sister who existed only in the gaps of memory. The rigid hierarchy of the house, the feuds with neighbors, the hoarded wealth—it all seemed to pivot on this single, silent revelation. The little princess had ruled her dominion, but she had ruled over a kingdom built on secrets.

The air inside Castlewood House carried the weight of a thousand small grievances, settled into the woodwork and the silence between the ticking clocks. Madam Warrington moved through this domestic architecture with the brisk efficiency of a woman who believed that order was the only antidote to chaos. To her, the sprawling estate was not a collection of rooms but a machine of duties, and she was its sole operator. A pie was not baked until her little finger had approved the dough; a spinster maid was not left alone until her loom was humming; the overseers in the tobacco fields were watched with eyes that missed nothing. Even sickness was managed with a clinical resolve, her medicine chest stocked with distillations and elixirs that terrified the neighbors into health. She was a queen of the mundane, ruling a kingdom where the currency was labor and the law was routine.

Outside, the world of Virginia was still a patchwork of scattered settlements, little villages clinging to the land like barnacles. Around Castlewood, other gentry ruled their own fiefdoms, often with a desperate grandeur. They were men of noble blood but ragged pockets, hosting endless feasts for strangers while their liveries frayed at the cuffs. Among them, Madam Warrington stood apart—not by wealth, for she was generous, but by a rigid economy of spirit. Rumors circulated that the Duke of Cumberland might be appointed viceroy, a joke that made her gossips titter. With grave dignity, she dismissed the notion, reminding them that royalty itself had once sought alliance with the Esmonds. Her authority was absolute, rooted in a belief that her station demanded a certain hauteur, however misplaced.

Her household included Mrs. Mountain, a widow who had arrived from England aboard the *Young Rachel*, carrying with her the ghost of a marriage that had cost her everything. Once a companion to Madam Esmond at the Kensington Academy, Mrs. Mountain had defended the American girl against the teasing of English peers, forging a bond that survived the deaths of husbands and fortunes. She was a woman of cheerful resilience, playing cards, singing, and managing the linen press with a jolly competence that charmed the unmarried gentlemen of the colony. Yet, she refused to remarry, choosing the solitude of Castlewood over the uncertainty of new vows. The two women existed in a state of perpetual friction, a dance of quarrels and reconciliations that defined the rhythm of the house. When illness struck, they fought side by side, their rivalry dissolving into shared maternal vigilance. Mrs. Mountain's loyalty was absolute; she stayed year after year, declining Captain Franks' invitations to leave, content to serve the memory of the Colonel, whom Madam Warrington revered above all else.

Suitors inevitably arrived, drawn by the reputation of the Esmond heiress. Madam Warrington received them with practiced grace, accepting their flattery as tribute due to her rank. She played the role of the imperious princess, growing bored and cruel or kind depending on her whims, aware that such behavior was expected of her. She tolerated the verses stolen from pocketbooks by Rattling Jack Firebrace and the bets placed by Tom Humbold regarding knighthoods, treating them as the inevitable taxes of her position. But beneath this performance lay a deep-seated insecurity. The younger son, Harry, reveled in the chaos, hunting and fighting alongside the jesters, while the elder, George, viewed their mother's vanity with a burning, private rage. He despised the sycophants, mocking their presence with juvenile satire that drove his mother to tears. "The Esmonds were always of a jealous disposition," she would lament, unaware that her own pride was the very poison her son identified.

Seeking salvation from this domestic discord, Madam Warrington took the boys to Williamsburg, hoping the formal education of the capital would civilize them. There, they encountered the thunderous voice of Mr. Whitfield, a preacher whose eloquence shattered the dull complacency of the local clergy. The boys, initially swept up in the fervor, sang hymns with genuine zeal, imagining lives of piety instead of military service. But Whitfield's mission was too vast for a single congregation; he departed to preach to the benighted masses, promising to send a successor who could teach them not just letters, but the "precious" sciences of the soul.

That successor arrived in the person of Mr. Ward, a handsome young man with a voice as resonant as his predecessor's. He preached night and day, drawing crowds of servants and slaves alike to the porch, his sermons striking a chord particularly deep within the hearts of the enslaved population. Madam Warrington was captivated, her credulity feeding on his confident delivery. She insisted that her neighbors attend, ordering conversions with the same authority she wielded over her household staff. Her favorite, the young George Washington, was urged to abandon his travels and settle at Castlewood, but he remained elusive, always riding out just as the sermon began.

The dynamic shifted when the boys turned their attention to their new instructor. They found him lacking—a dull scholar, a man of coarse manners who stumbled over Latin and Greek. George, already sharp-tongued, exposed his master's errors with surgical precision, reducing the divine to choking confusion. Harry, bolder still, mimicked Ward's table manners and speech, forcing laughter from Mrs. Mountain and Madam Esmond alike. The mother, furious at the defiance of her authority, doubled down, insisting that spiritual worth outweighed worldly refinement. "Heaven does not choose its elect from among the great and wealthy," she declared, commanding her sons to kneel and pray away their pride. Ward seized upon this moment, interpreting the boys' resistance as the biblical pride of Naaman the Syrian, convinced that only submission to his guardianship could save them from damnation.

The tension reached a breaking point as the boys, now fourteen, rebelled openly. George, inheriting a cynical streak from his grandfather, weaponized sarcasm, making the entire household uncomfortable with his wit. He mocked Ward's pomposity until the young divine choked on his food, and he provoked his mother into anger by sending Harry into fits of uncontrollable laughter. Her command was defied; her officers were scorned; her youngest child was corrupted. Desperate to restore order, Madam Warrington consulted Mr. Ward, seeking a solution to the insurrection. The athletic pedagogue, having secured his comfortable berth at Castlewood, readily provided the theological justification for force. In those days, the cane was a common instrument of discipline, wielded by sergeants, masters, and overseers alike. Having grown accustomed to it under their former tutor, Harry had long since desensitized himself to pain, but the atmosphere had changed. Since the Colonel's death, the rod had been laid aside, allowing the youths to roam unchecked. Now, with their natural authority spurned, the mother and her lieutenant resolved to reclaim control through coercion, preparing to bring the rebellion to heel with the familiar crack of the switch.

The silence in the house was heavier than the snow outside. George Esmond carried the weight of a quarrel he refused to speak of, a tension that settled in his chest until it manifested as a fever. For nights, the boy tossed on his bed, his mind fracturing under the strain, shrieking words that echoed in the dark: "Broken! Broken! It never, never can be mended!" His mother watched, helpless, as the illness defied her remedies, draining her strength and her patience.

Desperation drove her to the door of Mr. Dempster. She rode through the falling snow on a pony, seeking aid for her son. The old physician accepted her plea, burying his own resentment beneath the necessity of healing. With a lancet and a small bag of medicines, he retreated to his hut, leaving the sick boy behind. Under his care, the fever receded, the attacks softened, and George was restored to a fragile health. Doctors suggested a voyage to England, but the widow remembered her estranged relations there. Instead, they turned north and east.

George, Harry, and Mr. Dempster boarded a ship bound for New York, traveling up the Hudson to Albany, then moving into the French provinces. It was a circuitous education. Harry camped with the Indians, trading for furs and shooting bears. George, however, found no joy in the hunt. His delicacy made him a favorite among the French ladies, who admired his fluency in their tongue. He perfected his accent until he could pass for one of them, dancing the minuet with elegance and playing the violin with a technical precision that silenced the room. He learned the latest songs, though his voice had broken, shifting from treble to bass. In his absence, Harry earned an affair of honor with a young ensign, a duel that ended in a scarred shoulder and a sworn friendship. Madame de Mouchy wrote to the widow, praising George's behavior. The praise felt hollow to Mr. Whitfield, who would have seen the vanity in such a display.

Ten months later, the boys returned. Time had blurred their distinctions; George had grown tall enough to stand beside his younger brother. Their hair, usually powdered, was now natural—George's raven black, Harry's light locks tied with ribbons. Yet, the internal landscape remained unchanged. George returned with no goodwill toward the honorable gentleman who would become a legend, nor did his view of his mother's friend soften.

Washington looked older than his years, carrying a gravity that belied his age. He managed the family affairs with a simplicity and trustworthiness that commanded respect from peers twice his senior. But Mrs. Mountain, the widow's companion, saw only patterns of obsession. She was a matchmaker by nature, convinced everyone sought to marry everyone else. She believed Mr. Ward coveted the widow, that Washington desired a wealthy bride despite his youth compared to her. She calculated the odds: major generals marrying young mistresses was a tradition of the family. Her logic was circular and absolute.

She poured these theories onto George, begging him to listen. He pleaded for silence, invoking heaven itself. She promised to close her lips but claimed she could not close her eyes. She listed circumstantial evidence, the invisible threads connecting Washington and the widow. "Poor Harry," she sighed, "he cannot keep a secret. If I speak to him, he will tell your mother, and there will be a quarrel."

"Keep this quiet, Mountain," George said, his voice tight with dignity. "Neither to me nor anyone else must you mention such an absurd suspicion."

Why absurd? Because Washington was everywhere. His name hung in the air whenever the widow spoke. She consulted him on every decision regarding the estate, from buying horses to selling barrels of tobacco. There was a room at Castlewood designated solely for him, filled with his clothes and portmanteau even when he was gone. "One day he won't go away," Mountain groaned, returning to the forbidden topic.

George responded with a frigid courtesy that chafed the honest gentleman, or a sharp sarcasm that cut through the air like a rifle shot. While Harry rode with sporting friends, Washington visited Castlewood. He treated the mistress with a tenderness that nearly sparked a rupture in George's jealousy. But this visit was a farewell.

Washington was departing for the western frontier, a dangerous journey into the unknown. The French had begun to press inward, their aggression alarming the governments of Virginia and Pennsylvania. The British held a theoretical claim to the land stretching to the Pacific, a charter that defined boundaries north and south but ignored the vast expanse to the west. The French, conversely, sought to connect their northern and southern colonies via the Mississippi and the great lakes, ignoring the indigenous owners of the soil entirely. The logicians of St. James and Versailles viewed the conflict as a European dispute, using the tomahawk merely as a tool for their litigation.

The Ohio Company, granted lands by Virginia, found their settlements invaded by French detachments. They appealed to Governor Dinwiddie, who decided to send an ambassador to demand the cessation of hostilities. Young Washington seized the opportunity for distinction. In the autumn of 1753, accompanied by a guide and interpreters, he followed Indian tracks from Williamsburg to Lake Erie, finding the French commander at Fort Le Boeuf. The reply was brief and final: hold the place, drive out the English. The French intended to possess the Ohio.

Washington returned through forests and frozen rivers, navigating by compass and camping by firelight. Harry, absent on a cockfight, cursed his bad luck, missing the noble sport of diplomacy. Upon his return, Washington was hailed as a hero. The widow pointed to him as a model, contrasting his wilderness vigilance with Harry's trivial amusements. "Learning is good," she told George, "but I wish my eldest son were serving his country."

"I desire no better," George replied, eager to join the ranks. "Will you not have me serve under Mr. Washington?"

"An Esmond serves only with the King's commission," the widow stated firmly. "As for asking favors from Lieutenant-Governor Dinwiddie, I would rather beg my bread."

Washington was raising a regiment, a small force of provincials attempting to halt the French advance. Another officer, Colonel Trent, had been sent ahead to fortify positions near what would become Pittsburgh. The French, vastly outnumbering the English, attacked the outpost. A Virginian officer with forty men faced twenty times that number of Canadians. He withdrew without resistance; the French fortified his position, renaming it Fort Duquesne.

No shots had been fired yet. The armies stood in presence, guns loaded, but the word "fire" had not been spoken. It was strange that in a savage forest, a young Virginian officer should eventually strike the first blow, igniting a war that would last sixty years, reshaping continents, and costing empires. It would create a great Western republic and tear apart the Old World. Of all the myriads involved, the greatest fame would belong to the man who pulled the trigger.

He knew none of this. A simple gentleman, anxious to do his duty, he volunteered for the first service. In the following year, he took command of the provincial troops marching to repel the French. He encountered their vanguard and fired, killing their leader. But the momentum was irreversible. Compelled to retreat before superior forces, he was forced to capitulate. On July 4, 1754, Colonel Washington marched out from the hastily built Fort Necessity, surrendered the place to the conqueror, and walked home, the first shot of a global conflict already fired.

The air inside Mr. Washington's room had thickened, becoming a solid weight that pressed against the ribs. It was no longer simply a room; it was a vessel containing the entire trajectory of a family's future, suspended in a state of high-tension equilibrium. Outside, the world was shifting gears. The quarrel between the French and the English, once a distant rumble in the colonial periphery, had erupted into a national roar. Reinforcements poured from France into Canada; the expectation of English troops in Virginia hung over the landscape like a storm front.

Madame Esmond moved through the house with a frantic energy, her praise for her son Mr. Washington rising in volumes that filled the corridors. She contrasted his sterling qualities with the vacillating nature of Harry, the wild boy, and the listless musings of George. Yet, George, standing amidst this manufactured admiration, felt no warmth. Instead, he watched the performance with a detached, saturnine humor. He offered jokes so dense with irony that his mother, bewildered by their depth, sat silent, unable to parse the meaning of her own son's moody silence. He was a pest to himself, a creature of internal friction, watching the external machinery grind forward.

Public events dictated the rhythm of private lives. The conflict had expanded, demanding resources and blood. Letters arrived from Quebec, written by Madame la Presidente de Mouchy, describing a destiny that seemed cruelly inverted. She spoke of George quitting the embrace of an adored mother to throw himself into the arms of Bellona. She hesitated to wish victory upon the French lilies when she saw her friend ranged under the banners of the Leopard. Her tone was one of affectionate sorrow, mingled with the political necessity of the moment. She mentioned the Chevalier de la Jabotiere, burning to meet his rival on the field of Mars, and the cold Hippolyte, preferring the chase to the soft conversation of ladies. These were the threads of a tapestry being woven in blood and ink.

The banner of the Leopard was unfurled. Regiments were raised, fleets dispatched. In February 1755, Commodore Keppel anchored in Hampton Roads with the Centurion, carrying General Braddock and his staff. The Duke of Cumberland, a figure of complex estimation, had appointed the General. A fleet of transports followed, laden with stores, men, and money. The great man landed at Alexandria and proceeded to Annapolis, summoning the governors of the colonies to a council of war.

The arrival of the General ignited a frenzy throughout the provinces, particularly at Castlewood. Harry was the first to move, rushing to see the troops encamped at Alexandria. The sight of the lines, the inspiring music of fifes and drums, captivated him. He sought out the officers, eager to join the expedition, becoming a welcome guest at their mess. Madame Esmond viewed this association with satisfaction, believing the English gentlemen of good fashion were refining her sons, distancing them from the horse-racing Virginian squires and the petty lawyers of Williamsburg. She possessed a sharp eye for flattery and a low tolerance for the squabbles of officialdom.

Harry and George smiled politely at their mother's compliments regarding the elegance of the camp. They did not know that the surface of propriety masked a rotting core. The officers swaggered, frightening the peaceful villagers with their riot. The General raged against the disorder of his troops and the niggardliness of the provincials. The soldiers treated the land as conquered territory, insulting the wives of their Indian allies. The General was forced to forbid Indian women from his camp; their husbands, amazed and outraged, withdrew their support, depriving the army of crucial aid just months later.

Yet, Madame Esmond refused to hear these stories. Those who visited Castlewood were polite gentlemen. Soon, the General himself sent messages. His father had served under Marlborough alongside Colonel Esmond. With permission, he wished to pay his respects to the daughter of such a meritorious officer. If Madame Esmond had known the source of this politeness, the charm might have faded. The Commander-in-Chief held levees at Alexandria. Among the gentry who paid their respects were the twins of Castlewood, riding in state on their best nags, accompanied by their negro-boys in smart liveries.

Braddock was sulky, angry with the provincial gentry, and barely noticed the young gentlemen, asking casually of his aide-de-camp who the young Squire Gawkeys were in their blue and gold waistcoats. Mr. Dinwiddie, the Lieutenant-Governor, and a Philadelphia gentleman were dining with him. Dinwiddie identified the boys as the sons of the Princess Pocahontas. Enraged, Braddock demanded to know who she was. Dinwiddie, harboring resentment against the imperious little lady, launched into a disrespectful account of Madam Esmond, mocking her pomposity and pretensions until the General fell asleep.

When he awoke, Dinwiddie was gone, but the Philadelphia gentleman remained. The General resumed the conversation, speaking of Madam Esmond in curt, soldierly terms, asking again for the name of the old fool. He vented his contempt for the gentry and the country. The Philadelphia gentleman, Mr. Franklin, repeated the widow's name but offered a radically different assessment. He knew her estates, her cattle, her horses, her stores—all assets valuable in the current juncture. He advised the General to conciliate her. Having already decided Franklin was shrewd, the General graciously invited the two young men to dine the following day.

They arrived, behaving with the modesty and good-breeding expected of their station. They returned home delighted, and so did their mother. In reply to Braddock's message, Madam Esmond penned a billet in her finest style, fixing the time for his reception at Castlewood.

The campaign became the central obsession of the household. For Harry, it was the dearest wish of his life. He dreamed only of war, scouring and polishing every gun and sword in the house, organizing the negroes under arms. His mother, possessing a gallant spirit herself, knew the time had come for one of her boys to leave. She dreaded deciding which. She admired the elder, but she loved the younger with a passion that terrified her.

Harry, eager as he was, dared not broach the subject directly. When he finally did, George's countenance wore an ominous look. Harry worshipped his elder brother, giving way to him in all things. This obedience terrified him further as he realized George was also consumed by military matters. George had the wars of Eugene and Marlborough memorized, the military books of his grandfather, and the warlike lives of Plutarch. He practiced fencing with Dempster, the old Scotchman, an adept in the martial arts though shy of revealing his origins.

Madam Esmond entrusted her boys with the reply to the General's message, attaching substantial presents for the staff and officers. The General thanked Mr. Franklin repeatedly for securing this alliance. "Would not one of the young gentlemen like to see the campaign?" he asked. A friend, Mr. Washington, had already promised to join as aide-de-camp; the General would gladly take another Virginian. Harry's eyes brightened, his face flushing. "He would like with all his heart to go!" he cried. George, looking intently at his younger brother, stated that one would be proud to attend, while the other's duty was to remain and care for their mother. Harry yielded. His will was still obedient to George's. Despite his longing, the desire itself made him timid. He dared not speak openly as they rode home, silencing themselves for miles, each aware of the other's turmoil, afraid to force the issue.

At home, they told their mother of the offer. "I knew it must happen," she said. "At such a crisis, our family must come forward. Have you settled which of you is to leave me?" She looked anxiously from one to the other.

"The youngest ought to go, mother; of course I ought to go!" cried Harry, turning red.

"Of course he ought," said Mrs. Mountain, present at the talk.

"There! Mountain says so! I told you so!" Harry exclaimed, shooting a sidelong glance at George.

"The head of the family ought to go, mother," said George, sadly.

"No! No! You are ill, and have never recovered your fever. Ought he to go, Mountain?"

"You would make the best soldier, I know that, dearest Hal. You and George Washington are great friends, and could travel well together. He does not care for me, nor I for him, however much he is admired in the family. But, you see, 'tis the law of Honour, my Harry." Mrs. Mountain spoke to her brother with extraordinary kindness. "The grief I have had is that I must refuse thee. I must go. Had Fate granted you that extra half-hour of life I possess, it would have been your lot. You would have claimed your right to go first, you know you would."

"Yes, George," said poor Harry, "I own I should."

"You will stay at home, and take care of Castlewood and our mother. If anything happens to me, you are here to fill my place. I would like to give way, my dear, as you, I know, would lay down your life to serve me. But each of us must do his duty. What would our grandfather say if he were here?"

Madam Esmond looked proudly at her sons. "My papa would say that his boys were gentlemen," she faltered, leaving them, unwilling

The house of Castlewood was dressed in splendor, a performance of silk and polish designed to mask the sadness of the occasion. Madame Esmond wore a gown of magnificence beyond her custom, striving to make the entertainment pleasant for her guest despite the weight pressing on her own spirit.

The General's new aide-de-camp arrived first. Mr. Washington. He dismounted at the steps, his servants leading the horses away. No young gentleman in the colony possessed finer mounts or greater skill on horseback than he. In the covered gallery, before the Major could retreat to remove his riding-boots, he and the hostess paced together. She spoke of her son's departure; he confirmed his appointment to General Braddock. Between them, the negro servants carried dishes past, descending the steps onto the rough lawn, moving through the shadows like silent attendants to a play they did not understand.

Washington announced the arrival of Mr. Franklin of Pennsylvania, riding in the Commander-in-Chief's coach. Madame Esmond recalled that Franklin had once been a printer's boy. Things had come to a strange pass when such men rode in coaches. Washington countered that Franklin was shrewd and sensible. Madame Esmond believed the Major was too liberal in his praise. Yet Washington insisted the printer was ingenious and meritorious.

"I am glad," Madame Esmond said in her stately manner, "that as my boy goes to make the campaign, he will not be with tradesmen, but with gentlemen, with gentlemen of honour and fashion."

Washington had seen the gentlemen of honour and fashion over their cups. Perhaps he knew their sayings did not tend to edify a young man entering life. He chose silence, stating only that Harry and George must take their share of good and bad, hearing what both sorts had to say.

"To be with a veteran officer of the finest army in the world," the widow faltered, listing the virtues: gentlemen bred in the court, friends of the Duke. "With you, dear Colonel Washington, by whom my father always set such store. You don't know how much he trusted in you. You will take care of my boy, sir?"

She leaned heavily on his arm as they entered the mansion. Here they were joined by George. He came accurately powdered and richly attired, saluting his parent and friend with low, respectful bows. Unlike the modern youth who walk into rooms with hobnailed shoes and wideawakes, puffing cigars in faces, George bowed low. Yet he held no good humour. A polite smile played around the lower part of his countenance, while watchfulness and wrath glared from the eyes above.

"What had been said or done?" nothing that could not be spoken before decent company. So why did Madame Esmond blush, and the brave Colonel look red as he shook the young friend's hand?

"Have you had good sport?" Washington asked.

"No," George said curtly.

They looked at the picture of his father hanging in the parlour. The Colonel, usually taciturn, launched into a long description of his sport. He described the woods, the king's officers, the birds shot, and the game brought down. Not a jocular man, he became animated describing General Braddock's heavy person and great boots floundering through the Virginian woods. He recounted how the General hunted with a pack of dogs gathered from various houses, accompanied by negroes barking as loudly as the dogs, actually shooting the deer when they came in sight.

"Great God, sir!" Braddock had exclaimed, puffing and blowing, "what would Sir Robert have said in Norfolk, to see a man hunting with a fowling-piece in his hand, and a pack of dogs actually laid on to a turkey!"

"Indeed, Colonel, you are vastly comical this afternoon!" Madame Esmond laughed neatly, while her son listened, looking more glum than ever.

"The General meant Norfolk at home, madam, not Norfolk in Virginia," Washington corrected gently. "Braddock was talking of a visit to Sir Robert Walpole, who lives in that county, and of the great hunts the old Minister keeps there... I should like to see a good field and a good fox-chase at home better than any sight in the world," the honest sportsman added with a sigh.

"Nevertheless, there is good sport here," young Esmond sneered.

"What sport?" the other asked, looking sharply.

"Why, sure you know," George continued, stamping his foot as if charging with foils. "Are you not the best sportsman of the countryside? Are there not all the fish of the field, and the beasts of the trees, and the fowls of the sea—and the fish of the trees, and the beasts of the sea—and the—bah! You know what I mean. I mean shad, and salmon, and rock-fish, and roe-deer, and hogs, and buffaloes, and bisons, and elephants, for what I know. I'm no sportsman."

"No, indeed," Washington replied with a look of scarcely repressed scorn.

"Yes, I understand you. I am a milksop. I have been bred at my mamma's knee. Look at these pretty apron-strings, Colonel! Who would not like to be tied to them? See of what a charming colour they are! I remember when they were black—that was for my grandfather."

"And who would not mourn for such a gentleman?" the Colonel said, as the widow, surprised, looked at her son.

"And, indeed, I wish my grandfather were here, and would resurge, as he promises to do on his tombstone; and would bring my father, the Ensign, with him."

"Ahh, Harry!" Madame Esmond burst into tears as her second son entered the room—in just such another suit, gold-corded frock, braided waistcoat, silver-hilted sword, and solitaire, as his elder brother wore.

"Oh, Harry, Harry!" she cried, flying to her younger son.

"What is it, mother?" Harry asked, taking her in his arms.

"What is the matter, Colonel?"

"Upon my life, it would puzzle me to say," the Colonel answered, biting his lips. "A mere question, Hal, about pink ribbons, which I think vastly becoming to our mother; as, no doubt, the Colonel does."

"Sir, will you please to speak for yourself?" the Colonel bustled up, then sank his voice again.

"He speaks too much for himself," the widow wept.

"I protest I don't any more know the source of these tears, than the source of the Nile," George declared. "And if the picture of my father were to begin to cry, I should almost as much wonder at the paternal tears. What have I uttered? An allusion to ribbons! Is there some poisoned pin in them, which has been struck into my mother's heart by a guilty fiend of a London mantua-maker? I professed to wish to be led in these lovely reins all my life long," and he turned a pirouette on his scarlet heels.

"George Warrington! what devil's dance are you dancing now?" Harry asked, loving his mother, loving Washington, but above all loving and admiring his brother George.

"My dear child, you do not understand dancing—you care not for the politer arts—you can get no more music out of a spinet than by pulling a dead hog by the ear. By nature you were made for a man—a man of war—I do not mean a seventy-four, Colonel George, like that hulk which brought the hulking Mr. Braddock into our river. His Excellency, too, is a man of warlike turn, a follower of the sports of the field. I am a milksop, as I have had the honour to say."

"You never showed it yet. You beat that great Maryland man was twice your size," Harry broke out.

"Under compulsion, Harry. 'Tis tuptu, my lad, or else 'tis tuptomai, as thy breech well knew when we followed school. But I am of a quiet turn, and would never lift my hand to pull a trigger, no, nor a nose, nor anything but a rose," George said, handling one of Madame Esmond's bright pink apron ribbons. "I hate sporting, which you and the Colonel love, and I want to shoot nothing alive, not a turkey, nor a titmouse, nor an ox, nor an ass, nor anything that has ears. Those curls of Mr. Washington's are prettily powdered."

The militia colonel, offended by the first part of the talk and puzzled by the last, had taken a modest draught from the great china bowl of apple-toddy standing to welcome guests in all Virginian houses. He cooled himself by pacing the balcony in a very stately manner. Almost reconciled with the elder, the appeased mother stood giving a hand to each of her sons. George put his disengaged hand on Harry's shoulder.

"I say one thing, George," Harry said with a flushing face.

"Say twenty things, Don Enrico," the other cried.

"If you are not fond of sporting and that, and don't care for killing game and hunting, being cleverer than me, why shouldst thou not stop at home and be quiet, and let me go out with Colonel George and Mr. Braddock?" Harry delivered his speech.

The widow looked eagerly from the dark-haired to the fair-haired boy. She knew not from which she would like to part.

"One of our family must go because honneur oblige, and my name being number one, number one must go

The carriage wheels bit into the dust of the road, a rhythmic, grinding percussion that announced the arrival of His Excellency. He rode in splendor, the first personage of the North American colonies, flanked by a guard of dragoons and a loyal, shouting crowd of negroes whose voices rose and fell like a single, chaotic instrument. Inside the ponderous vehicle, the air was thick with the scent of leather and unwashed ambition. Captain Talmadge paced beside the coach, while Major Danvers sat opposite Benjamin Franklin, the former printer's boy.

Franklin was a small, sharp thing, his eyes moving rapidly over the landscape of the carriage interior. He possessed a knowledge of the colony and England that baffled the gentlemen around him—a variety of learning acquired not through the rigid channels of the university, but through the casual friction of life.

"Extraordinary," the General observed, tapping his hat brim against the ceiling. "How a person of such humble origin should acquire such politeness."

Franklin bowed, his movements economical. "Occasional good fortune, sir. I studied their honors' manners to adapt myself. My education... well, my father was in straitened circumstances. I gathered what I could. Nothing like what you have in England."

Braddock erupted in laughter, a sound that shook the carriage frame. "Education! By George, there are gentlemen of the army who cannot spell 'bull' with two b's. I hear the Duke of Marlborough was no special good penman. He thrashed the French soundly, though. No scholar, but effective."

"I am aware of both facts," Franklin replied, his tone flat, devoid of irony.

"Nor is my Duke a scholar," Braddock continued, leaning forward, his face a map of red velvet and arrogance. "You winked at that, Postmaster."

Franklin instantly withdrew the gesture, his eyes clearing to the innocence of a child. "He is no scholar, but he is a match for any French general that ever swallowed the English for fricassee de crapaud. He saved the crown for the best of kings."

From his powdered wig, a halo of starch escaped, framing a face that seemed to absorb the light rather than reflect it. "He is the soldier's best friend," Franklin said, his voice steady. "Enemy of the beggarly red-shanked Scotch rebels and the intriguing Romish Jesuits. Your Royal Highness is not a scholar, but one of the finest gentlemen in the world."

"He is indeed," Franklin added, referencing a review in Hyde Park. "Very fine on horseback."

"You shall drink his health today," Braddock commanded, the weight of command settling heavily on the small man. "Best of masters, best of sons."

"Epaulets are out of my way, sir," Franklin laughed softly. "I live in a Quaker City."

"Every man to his business," Braddock dismissed, swinging his legs. "We encourage you to read. We fight the enemy and govern the country. Look at these roads! How this confounded coach plunges!"

He gestured to the rear. Two negro boys in livery rode alongside, one on a good gelding.

"It is Mr. Washington," Danvers noted.

"I would like him for a corporal of the Horse Grenadiers," Braddock mused. "Good figure on a horse. Knows the country."

"Yes, indeed," Franklin agreed. "Monstrously genteel, considering his opportunities. I should have thought he had the polish of Europe."

"He does his best," Franklin said, watching the stout chief sway in his seat. Braddock was an exemplar of English elegance, swearing at every other word, ignorant of parade save for the merits of a bottle and the looks of a woman. Proud of his no-ancestry, brave as a bulldog, savage and lustful, yet gentle in soft moods. Utterly unread. Believing his country the first in the world and himself as good a gentleman as any in it.

"He was beat at Fort What-d'ye-call-um last year," Franklin remarked, testing the waters. "Down by the Thingamy river. What's the name on it, Talmadge?"

"The Lord knows, sir," Talmadge admitted. "And I dare say the Postmaster knows too, who is laughing at us both."

"Oh, Captain!"

"Caught in a regular trap. Only militia and Indians."

"Good day, Mr. Washington," Braddock called out as the young man approached, followed by natty negro grooms in velvet hunting-caps. "Pretty nag, sir. That was your first affair?"

"That at Fort Necessity? Yes, sir," Washington answered, saluting gravely. "I began ill, sir. Never having been in action until that unlucky day."

"You were all raw levies," Braddock said, dismissing the sentiment. "You should have seen our militia run from the Scotch. You should have had some troops with you."

"Your Excellency knows 'tis my passionate desire to see and serve with them," Washington replied.

"By George, we shall try and gratify you," Braddock promised, rolling the heavy carriage toward Castlewood. Washington asked leave to gallop ahead to announce the arrival.

The progress was glacial. Humble persons invited to meet the Commander-in-Chief found themselves unable to pass the great man, forming a procession in the dusty wake of his wheels. First came Mr. Dinwiddie, Lieutenant-Governor, attended by negro servants and Parson Broadbent, the jolly Williamsburg chaplain. They were joined by little Mr. Dempster, the schoolmaster, in his great Ramillies wig, and Mr. Laws, the judge, with Madam Laws on a pillion behind him, her negro man carrying a box containing her cap, bestriding a mule.

The procession looked ludicrous. Major Danvers and Mr. Franklin saw it and laughed outright, though quietly enough not to disturb the sleeping General inside the lead carriage. There was room for all at Castlewood. Meat, drink, and the best tobacco awaited.

Inside, the honest General required assistance with every dish. He held out his glass constantly, pronouncing Nathan's sangaree excellent. There was cider, ale, brandy, and Bordeaux wine, some brought by Colonel Esmond himself, fit for pontificis coenis, according to little Mr. Dempster.

Parson Broadbent returned the wink, drinking without caring for the Latin. He was a gambling, guzzling divine who had passed time in the Fleet Prison and Newmarket. Sent out to Virginia, he had fought matches of cocks with young George Esmond, hunted roe, and hauled in shad and salmon. It was said he was the midnight poacher on whom Mr. Washington set his dogs, caned by the river-side at Mount Vernon. Broadbent had scrambled to his boat in the dark, emerging two Sundays later with a black eye and a new collar.

All the birds of the Virginia air and fish of the sea lay on Madam Esmond's board. Gumbo was declared perfection—the young Mr. George's black servant had been discovered behind a door with his head in a bowl of the hotch-potch, grimly christened on the spot. Stewed terrapins were worthy of London aldermen, or any duke, or even emperor.

Negro-women possessed a genius for cookery. In Castlewood kitchens, adepts worked under the keen eye of the late and present Madam Esmond. Certain dishes, especially sweets and flan, she prepared herself with neatness and dexterity. Carving several principal pieces, she showed the prettiest round arms and small hands, performing the ancient rite of hospitality not so languid as theirs.

The old law of the table dictated that the mistress press her guests with decent eagerness, knowing culinary secrets and executing carving operations upon fowls, fish, and joints of meat. She cheered her guests to fresh efforts, whispering to Mr. Braddock, "I have kept for your Excellency the jowl of this salmon—I will take no denial!" To Mr. Franklin, she offered water despite the wholesome wine in the cellar. To Mr. Justice, she asked if he loved woodcock pie.

"Because I know who makes the pastry," said Mr. Laws, the judge, with a profound bow. "I wish, madam, we had such a happy knack of pastry at home as you have at Castlewood. I often say to my wife, 'My dear, I wish you had Madam Esmond's hand.'"

"It is a very pretty hand; I am sure others would like it too," said the Postmaster of Boston. Mr. Esmond looked but half-pleased.

"Such a hand for a light pie-crust," continued the Judge. "And my service to you, madam." He thought the widow could not but be propitiated. She said simply that she had lessons when at home in England, that certain dishes her mother taught her were liked by her father and sons. She was very glad if they pleased her company.

More remarks followed. More dishes. Ten times as much meat as was needful. Mr. Washington did not embark in the general conversation much, but he and Mr. Talmadge, Major Danvers, and the Postmaster were deep in talk about roads, rivers, conveyances, sumpter-horses, and artillery trains. The provincial militia Colonel had bits of bread laid at intervals on the table before him, stations marked out on which he placed his finger, talking to his brother aides-de-camp until a negro servant changed the courses, brushing off the Potomac

The heat pressed down on Castlewood like a physical weight, distorting the air above the verandah where General Braddock and his entourage lingered. Once the formalities of the evening were concluded, the boys retreated to their room, the silence of the corridor giving way to the frantic, circular logic of their youth. They could not comprehend the transaction unfolding downstairs. How could the representative of the Marquises of Esmond—their lineage, their history—marry the younger son of a colonial surveyor? To hand over their mother, their home, their entire world to a man of thirty-two, a stranger to their blood, seemed a grotesque error. Harry's mind raced toward immediate action: he imagined bursting into his mother's chamber, where her maids were stripping her of the jewels she had worn for the feast, and demanding they flee to their small estate, living in obscurity forever rather than submit to this unnatural union.

George, however, possessed a colder clarity. He outlined a strategy that felt inevitable, a mathematical solution to a moral problem. His mother could not marry a man connected to the battlefield in such a specific way—a man they had fought, or whom they had killed. Therefore, the obstacle had to be removed physically. "We must have him out, Harry," George stated, his voice carrying the flat certainty of someone solving an equation.
