Harry recognized the brilliance of the deduction instantly. "Mother cannot marry our murderer," he agreed, the realization settling over him with the gravity of a verdict. "She won't be that cruel. And if we strike him down, the question is settled. *Cadit quaestio.*" He looked at his brother, the envy and admiration warring in his chest, and asked if he should dispatch his servant with a challenge to Colonel George immediately.

"My dear Harry," the elder replied, a hint of complacency coloring his tone regarding his own affair of honor at Quebec, "you are not accustomed to matters of this magnitude."

"No," Harry admitted, sighing, his gaze fixed on his senior with a mixture of longing and inferiority.

"We cannot insult a gentleman within our own walls," George continued, his posture stiffening into something resembling majesty. "The laws of honor forbid such inhospitality. But we can ride out with him. Once the park gates are shut, we can speak our minds plainly."

"That we can!" Harry cried, seizing his brother's hand. "That we will! Say it, Georgy..." His face flushed violently, and his brother paused, asking what he intended to say.

"This is my turn, brother," Harry pleaded, his voice rising. "If you undertake the campaign, I deserve the other affair. Truly, I do." He prayed for this elevation, desperate to claim his due.

"The head of the house must lead," George said, his demeanor shifting to a superior calm. "If I fall, my Harry will avenge me. But I must fight George Washington, Hal. And it is best I do so; for indeed, I hate him the most. Was it not he who counseled my mother to order that wretch, Ward, to seize me?"

"Ah, George," the younger brother interjected, attempting a note of peace, "you ought to forget and forgive."

"Forget? Never, sir, as long as memory serves. You cannot command remembrance to vanish from a man's mind. A wrong committed yesterday remains a wrong tomorrow. I have never harmed anyone knowingly, and I will not suffer harm if I can prevent it. I think poorly of Mr. Ward, but not so poorly as to believe he will ever forgive the blow with the ruler. Colonel Washington is our enemy, mine specifically. He advised one injustice against me and plots a greater. We must punish him."

The old Bordeaux had ignited George's pale complexion into a flame. Harry, his devoted follower, could only admire his brother's haughty bearing and rapid declamation, preparing himself with his customary obedience to follow his leader. That night, they retired to their beds, the elder issuing special instructions to his junior to maintain civility toward all guests while they remained under their mother's roof.

Decorum and a reluctance to recount scandals forbade naming which of Madame Esmond's guests first succumbed to the weight of her hospitality. The respectable descendants of the Talmadges and Danvers, aides-de-camp to the General, might prefer ignorance of their ancestors' intoxications a century prior; yet the gentlemen themselves bore no shame, and there is little doubt they or their companions were tipsy twice or thrice weekly. Imagine them staggering to bed, supported by sympathetic negroes; imagine their vinous General, too stout a drinker to surrender to only six bottles of Bordeaux, escorted to his chamber by the young gentlemen of the house, quickly succumbing to the sleep that friendly Bacchus grants. The good lady of Castlewood observed the condition of her guests without surprise or horror. She rose early, providing cooling drinks for their parched throats, which the servants distributed to their respective rooms.

At breakfast, one of the English officers rallied Mr. Franklin, who consumed no alcohol whatsoever, refusing the morning draught of toddy. The officer pointed out how the Philadelphia gentleman forfeited two pleasures simultaneously. The young man replied that the disease was pleasant and the remedy delicious, laughing as he proposed continuing the cycle. Colonel Washington, the General's new American aide-de-camp, remained sober and serene. The British officers vowed to intervene, to teach him the ways of the English army, but the Virginian gentleman gravely declined, stating he cared not to learn that particular branch of English military education.

The widow, exhausted by the cares of a grand dinner followed by a substantial breakfast, had scant leisure to scrutinize her sons' behavior closely. Yet she noted that George treated her favorite, Colonel Washington, with scrupulous politeness, extending the same courtesy to all other guests.

Before Mr. Braddock departed, he held a private audience with Madame Esmond, formally offering to adopt her son. When the arrangements for George's departure were finalized between his mother and his future chief, Madame Esmond, though she felt the dread, showed no squeamish terror regarding the dangers of the bottle, recognizing them as among the severest evils her son would face. She knew her boy must take his part in the world, encountering his portion of good and evil. "Mr. Braddock is a perfect fine gentleman in the morning," she declared stoutly to her aide-de-camp, Mrs. Mountain. "Though my father did not drink, it is certain that many of the finest companies in England do." The jovial General shook hands with George, who presented himself to his Excellency after the maternal interview concluded. The General welcomed him and instructed him to attend at Frederick in three days, shortly thereafter setting forth on the expedition.

Now the great coach was requisitioned again. The General's escort pranced around it; the other guests and their servants mounted their horses. The lady of Castlewood accompanied his Excellency to the steps of the verandah. The young gentlemen followed, standing on either side of the coach door. The guard trumpeter blew a shrill blast; the negroes shouted, "Huzzay, and God save de King," as Mr. Braddock graciously took leave of his hospitable entertainers and rolled away on his road to headquarters.

As the boys ascended the steps, the Colonel was once more taking leave of their mother. No doubt she had been urging George into his namesake's care again. Colonel Washington said, "With my life. You may depend on me," as the lads returned to their mother and the few remaining guests in the porch. The Colonel was booted and ready to depart. "Farewell, my dear Harry," he said. "With you, George, 'tis no adieu. We shall meet in three days at the camp."

Both young men were heading toward danger, perhaps toward death. Colonel Washington was taking leave of her, and she would not see him again before the campaign. No wonder the widow was deeply moved.

George Warrington watched his mother's emotion, interpreting it with a pang of malignant scorn. "Stay yet a moment, and console our mamma," he said, his countenance steady. "Only enough time to get ourselves booted, and my brother and I will ride with you a little way, George." George Warrington had already ordered his horses. The three young men were swiftly underway, their negro grooms trailing behind. Mrs. Mountain, knowing she had sown discord between them and trembling for the result, felt a vast relief that Mr. Washington had departed without a quarrel with the brothers, without, at any rate, an open declaration of love to their mother.

No man could be more courteous in demeanor than George Warrington toward his neighbor and namesake, the Colonel. The latter was pleased and surprised by his young friend's altered behavior. The shared prospect of danger, the necessity of future fellowship, the softening influence of the long friendship binding him to the Esmond family, and the tender farewells exchanged with the mistress of Castlewood inclined the Colonel to forget the unpleasantness of recent days, making him unusually friendly with his young companion. George was quite gay and easy; it was Harry who was now melancholy. He rode silently and wistfully beside his brother, keeping his distance from Colonel Washington, to whose side he had previously pressed eagerly. If the honest Colonel remarked on his young friend's conduct, he undoubtedly attributed it to Harry's known affection for his brother and his natural anxiety to remain close to George as the day of their parting approached.

They discussed the war further and the probable conclusion of the campaign; none of the three doubted its successful termination. Two thousand veteran British troops with their commander must overcome any

The air inside the tavern was thick with the scent of unwashed bodies and impending violence. In one corner, Colonel Washington paced, his movements a slow, grinding rhythm against the silence. His hands were clasped behind his back, his head bowed low enough to obscure the gravity settling on his handsome face. He was preparing for blood, not with the manic energy of a duelist, but with the grim resignation of a man accepting a sentence he had no power to alter. The law of honor demanded payment for the insult, and the country practiced cruelty as a routine.

Across the room, the atmosphere was different, charged with a frantic, childish desperation. Young George Warrington sat rigid, his face pale beneath the powder. Beside him, his brother Harry wept openly, his small hands clutching a sheet of paper until the ink blurred. The document was a testament to a death that might happen tomorrow—a list of bequests for a freed slave, a tutor, a silver knife, and a lock of hair for a sister who would never know her brother's end. George dictated the words with a majestic indifference, treating the finality of his potential demise as a bureaucratic formality. He spoke of debts paid and grievances settled, sealing the letter with a family crest that seemed suddenly heavy and obsolete.

"Two hours and a half," George noted, glancing at a gold watch inherited from a grandfather who was now a distant memory. "Time is running out."

Harry refused to let go of his brother, his voice cracking with a mixture of terror and loyalty. "If he kills you, I'll kill him. I swear it." The promise hung in the air, absurd and terrible, a vow made by a boy who understood the mechanics of a pistol but not the weight of a soul.

Outside, the world was already waking up to the noise. The tavern grounds, usually a chaotic arena for cockfights and brawls, fell silent except for the distant, rhythmic pounding of hooves. Sady, the enslaved boy tasked with delivering the pistols, burst into the courtyard like a storm front. He fired his weapon twice, the report echoing sharply against the wooden siding, silencing the cackling pigs and startled chickens. He grinned, a wide, unsettling expression, and pointed the barrel down the road, signaling the arrival of the inevitable.

Then came the clatter of hooves, breaking the tension. First, Dr. Dempster, spurring his pony with clumsy urgency. Behind him, Mrs. Mountain, astride a sturdy mare, galloped into the yard. She did not stop. She did not dismount gracefully. She crashed through the crowd of negro servants, elbowing past them, and ran straight for the garden where the two brothers stood frozen.

She threw herself around George's neck, sobbing. "It's a mistake! Oh, George, my darling, it's all my fault!"

George pulled away, his face hardening into a mask of aristocratic disdain. "What is a mistake?" he asked, his voice flat.

"It's the paper," Mrs. Mountain gasped, wiping tears from her cheeks. "The one you found in his portfolio. The one saying he intends to marry a widow with two children. I thought... I thought it was you, George. But it isn't. It's Miss Custis. The Colonel is marrying her after the campaign. Your mother is furious, boys. Furious."

The revelation hit the room like a physical blow. The justification for the duel evaporated, leaving only the raw, ugly residue of misunderstanding. George's anger, which had been building like pressure in a boiler, found a sudden vent. "I acted under a wrong impression," he admitted, the words tasting bitter. "But the behavior remains."

Harry, trembling, scrambled forward. He ignored the protocol of distance, ignoring the formalities that governed adult men. He ran toward the figure standing alone on the bowling green—the tall, stooped silhouette of Colonel Washington. With a hundred stumbling steps and a voice thrumming with genuine affection, Harry launched his story. He spoke of the error, of the misread letter, of the relief that washed over him.

Washington listened. When Harry finished, the Colonel did not offer a speech. He simply stepped forward and embraced the boy, holding him tight as if anchoring himself to something solid. "Thank God," Washington whispered, his voice rough. "Thank God for this."

He released Harry, turning to George. Their hands touched, a formal clasp between enemies who had just discovered they were allies in confusion. But the warmth did not spread. George's apology was stiff, a recitation of duty rather than forgiveness. "I find I have done you a wrong, Colonel Washington," George said. "I must apologize not for the error, but for the behavior that followed."

"The error was mine," Washington replied, his tone cool and detached. "I found the paper. I showed it to George. Jealousy is a disease, madam." He glanced at Mrs. Mountain, whose face was flushed with shame. "Mischief comes from secrets kept too long. Pain could have been spared if only private things remained private."

He offered no further comfort. No platitudes about friendship or future reconciliation. Instead, he issued a command: "Take a true friend's advice and try to be less ready to think evil of your friends. We shall meet again at the camp. Until then, keep our weapons for the enemy."

With a bow that was precise and empty, Washington turned his horse. He rode away, leaving the tavern, the weeping brothers, and the confused adults behind. The sun broke through the clouds, illuminating the dust motes dancing in the air, indifferent to the drama that had just unfolded. The threat of blood remained, suspended in the silence, waiting for the next order.

The silence in Castlewood was not empty; it was a heavy, suspended thing, filled with the ghost of a brother who had walked away. Harry sat at the head of the table, his fingers tracing the rim of a silver tankard, the metal cool against his skin. He drank, but the liquid did not settle. Across the room, Madame Warrington moved with a rigid cheerfulness that seemed to strain against the gravity of the situation. She spoke of George as if he were merely stepping out for a moment, her voice bright, her posture upright, arranging the household around a vacancy that refused to be acknowledged. She insisted on the toast of "The King," her solemnity on Sundays a performance of faith that masked the terror of absence. She walked into the empty chambers, touching the neatly folded linens and the stacked papers, treating the arrangement of objects as a substitute for the living man. Mrs. Mountain, meanwhile, hovered on the edge of hysteria, her whispers rising whenever the name was spoken, while Harry wore a mask of ghastly alarm, terrified of the grief he dared not name.

Yet, amidst this domestic suspension, the war pressed in through the letters. George wrote frequently, compiling a rough journal that became the central artifact of their evenings. On long summer nights, the house settled into a ritualistic stillness. Madame Esmond sat upright, refusing the comfort of a chair's back; little Fanny Mountain stitched her sampler with mechanical precision; Mr. Dempster and Mrs. Mountain played cards in the gloom; the old servants moved like shadows. In this circle, Harry read aloud the words of his brother, the ink on the yellow paper seeming to vibrate with a life of its own.

"It must be owned that the provinces are acting scurvily by his Majesty King George II.," Harry read, his voice flat, reciting the grievances of a distant front. "Virginia is bad enough... but Pennsylvania is worst of all. We pray them to send us troops... and we promise to maintain the troops when they come. We not only don't keep our promise... but our people insist upon the most exorbitant prices for their cattle and stores, and actually cheat the soldiers who are come to fight their battles."

He paused, letting the bitterness hang in the air. "No wonder the General swears, and the troops are sulky. The delays have been endless. Owing to the failure of the several provinces to provide their promised stores... weeks and months have elapsed, during which time, no doubt, the French have been strengthening themselves..."

Then came the mention of Washington. "Though there never will be any love lost between me and Colonel Washington, it must be owned that your favourite... is a brave man and a good officer. The family respect him very much... Indeed, he is almost the only man who has seen the Indians in their war-paint, and I own I think he was right in firing upon Mons. Jumonville last year."

Madame Esmond set her cup down with a sharp clink. "I wonder at the taste of some men, and the effrontery of some women," she said, her voice cutting through the domestic haze. "I wonder at any woman who has been married once, so forgetting herself as to marry again!"

"Monstrous!" Mr. Mountain muttered, his eyes fixed on his glass of punch.

Harry choked, a laugh bubbling up from somewhere deep and suppressed, but his mother silenced him gently. "Go on, Harry! Continue with your brother's journal. He writes well: but, ah, will he ever be able to write like my papa?"

Harry resumed, his voice steadying. "We keep the strictest order here in camp... The roll of each company is called at morning, noon, and night... The men are punished, and the drummers are always at work. Oh, Harry, but it made one sick to see the first blood drawn from a great strong white back, and to hear the piteous yell of the poor fellow."

"Oh, horrid!" Madame Esmond whispered.

"I think I should have murdered Ward if he had flogged me. Thank Heaven he got off with only a crack of the ruler!" Harry read, the horror of the scene translating directly into the room. "The men... are looked after carefully enough. I wish the officers were. The Indians have just broken up their camp... because the young officers were for ever drinking with the squaws—and—and—hum—ha."

Mr. Harry hesitated, sensing the weight of the unsaid, the crude reality of the frontier that little Fanny's prim presence could not absorb.

"Pass over that about the odious tipsy creatures," Madame commanded.

Harry shifted gears, adopting a louder, more satisfying tone. "Each regiment has Divine Service performed at the head of its colours every Sunday. The General does everything in the power of mortal man to prevent plundering... He has declared soldiers shall be shot who dare to interrupt or molest the market-people. He has ordered the price of provisions to be raised a penny a pound, and has lent money out of his own pocket to provide the camp. Altogether, he is a strange compound, this General. He flogs his men without mercy, but he gives without stint. He swears most tremendous oaths in conversation, and tells stories which Mountain would be shocked to hear—"

"Why me?" Mr. Mountain asked, defensive. "And what have I to do with the General's silly stories?"

"Never mind the stories; and go on, Harry," cried the mistress of the house.

"—would be shocked to hear after dinner; but he never misses service. He adores his Great Duke, and has his name constantly on his lips. Our two regiments both served in Scotland, where I dare say Mr. Dempster knew the colour of their facings."

"We saw the tails of their coats, as well as their facings," Mr. Mountain growled, a Jacobite tutor finding purchase in the past.

"Colonel Washington has had the fever very smartly... Had he not better go home and be nursed by his widow? When either of us is ill, we are almost as good friends again as ever. But I feel somehow as if I can't forgive him for having wronged him. Good Powers! How I have been hating him for these months past! Oh, Harry! I was in a fury at the tavern the other day, because Mountain came up so soon, and put an end to our difference. We ought to have burned a little gunpowder between us, and cleared the air. But though I don't love him, as you do, I know he is a good soldier, a good officer, and a brave, honest man; and, at any rate, shall love him none the worse for not wanting to be our stepfather."

"A stepfather, indeed!" Madame Esmond exclaimed, her composure cracking. "Why, jealousy and prejudice have perfectly maddened the poor child! Do you suppose the Marquis of Esmond's daughter and heiress could not have found other stepfathers for her sons than a mere provincial surveyor? If there are any more such allusions in George's journal, I beg you skip 'em, Harry, my dear. About this piece of folly and blundering, there hath been quite talk enough already."

"'Tis a pretty sight," Harry continued, ignoring the interruption, "to see a long line of redcoats, threading through the woods or taking their ground after the march. The care against surprise is so great and constant, that we defy prowling Indians to come unawares upon us... They are such cruel villains, these French and their painted allies, that we do not think of showing them mercy. Only think, we found but yesterday a little boy scalped but yet alive in a lone house, where his parents had been attacked and murdered by the savage enemy... our General has offered a reward of five pounds for all the Indian scalps brought in."

"When our march is over, you should see our camp... Our baggage and our General's tents and guard are placed quite in the centre... We have outlying sentries by twos, by threes, by tens, by whole companies. At the least surprise, they are instructed to run in on the main body and rally round the tents and baggage, which are so arranged themselves as to be a strong fortification. Sady and I, you must know, are marching on foot now, and my horses are carrying baggage. The Pennsylvanians sent such rascally animals into camp that they speedily gave in. What good horses were left, 'twas our duty to give up: and Roxana has a couple of packs upon her back instead of her young master. She knows me right well, and whinnies when she sees me, and I walk by her side, and we have many a talk together on the march."

"July 4. To guard against surprises, we are all warned to pay especial attention to the beat of the drum; always halting when they hear the long roll beat, and marching at the beat of the long march. We are more on the alert regarding the enemy now. We have our advanced pickets doubled, and two sentries at every post. The men on the advanced pickets are constantly under arms, with fixed bayonets, all through the night, and relieved every two hours. The half that are relieved lie down by their arms, but are not suffered to leave their pickets. 'Tis evident that we are drawing very near to the enemy now. This packet goes out with the General's to Colonel Dunbar's camp, who is thirty miles behind us; and will be carried thence to Frederick, and thence to my honoured mother's house at Castlewood

The reports of the British disaster grew heavier with every mile Harry Warrington traveled toward Pennsylvania. The two famous regiments, forged in Scottish and Continental fires, had vanished before an enemy that was barely visible. Their discipline, their valor, had dissolved into silence against a handful of savages and scattered French infantry. The commander had died bravely, his horse shot beneath him four times, twice wounded, finally struck down by a mortal blow three days after the battle. Harry's informants painted the scene with grim detail: the river crossing, the long, futile advance through the wilderness, the firing lines, the artillery struggling to clear a path, and then the ambush. Fire erupted from behind every bush and tree—a murderous fusillade that had cut the expeditionary force in half. Yet, not everyone was lost. One aide-de-camp, a Virginian gentleman, lay ill with fever and exhaustion at Dunbar's camp.

Which one? Harry hurried to the camp, arriving eventually to find George Washington stretched in a tent. It was not his brother. A sharper pain than the fever gripped Washington when he saw Harry, offering no news of George. Washington dared not speak fully. For three days after the fight, his duty had bound him to the General's side. On the fatal ninth of July, he had seen George move forward with orders from the chief, never to return. After Braddock died, the aide had retraced his steps to the field. The remaining corpses were stripped and horribly mutilated. One body he had buried, convinced it was George Warrington's. His own illness deepened, perhaps caused by the anguish of that desperate search.

"Ah, George! If you had loved him, you would have found him dead or alive," Harry cried. Nothing satisfied him but to go to the ground himself, to examine the earth. With money, he procured guides. He forded the river where the army had crossed, walking from one end to the other of the dreadful field. The Indians were gone, but birds of prey fed on mangled, festering carcasses. Save for his grandfather, lying calm with a sweet smile, Harry had never seen the face of Death. The horror of the mutilation made him turn away, shuddering. What news could the vacant woods or those rotting bodies offer the lad of his lost brother? He considered going unarmed, with a white flag, to the French fort where the victors had retired, but his guides refused. "Keep your hair for your lady mother, my young gentleman," the guide said. "'Tis enough that she loses one son in this campaign."

When Harry returned to the English encampment at Dunbar's, it was his turn to succumb to the fever. Delirium seized him; he lay in tents and beds while his friend recovered. For days, he did not know who watched him. Poor Dempster, who had tended him through previous illnesses, feared the widow would lose both her children. But the fever subsided enough for the boy to mount a horse. Mr. Washington and Dempster escorted him home. With heavy hearts, the three beheld the gates of Castlewood once more.

A servant announced their arrival. First came Mrs. Mountain and her little daughter, welcoming Harry with tears and embraces, though she offered no nod to Mr. Washington. The little girl approached the young officer, hands clasped behind her back, asking why he had not brought George back too. Harry did not hear; the sobs and caresses of his nurse kept him from listening to Fanny.

Dempster was received graciously. "Whatever could be done, we know you would do, Mr. Dempster," Mrs. Mountain said, taking his hand. "Make a curtsey to Mr. Dempster, Fanny, and remember to be grateful to all who have been friendly to our benefactors. Will it please you to take any refreshment before you ride, Colonel Washington?"

Washington had ridden enough. He counted on Castlewood's hospitality as surely as he counted on the shelter of his own roof. "The time to feed my horse, and a glass of water for myself, and I will trouble Castlewood hospitality no further," he said.

"Sure, George, you have your room here, and my mother is above-stairs getting it ready!" Harry cried. "That poor horse of yours stumbled with you, and can't go farther this evening."

"Hush! Your mother won't see him, child," Mrs. Mountain whispered.

"Not see George? Why, he is like a son of the house," Harry insisted.

"She had best not see him. I don't meddle any more in family matters, child: but when the Colonel's servant rode in and said you were coming, Madam Esmond left this room, my dear, where she was sitting reading Drelincourt, and said she felt she could not see Mr. Washington. Will you go to her?" Harry took his friend's arm, excused himself to the Colonel, promising to return shortly, and left the parlor for the upper rooms where Madam Esmond waited.

He hurried across the corridor, averting his head from one special door he disliked looking at—the room of his brother—but as he reached it, Madam Esmond emerged, folding him to her heart and leading him inside. A settee stood by the bed; a book of psalms lay on the coverlet. Everything else in the room remained exactly as George had left it.

"My poor child! How thin thou art grown—how haggard you look! Never mind. A mother's care will make thee well again. 'Twas nobly done to go and brave sickness and danger in search of your brother. Had others been as faithful, he might be here now. Never mind, my Harry; our hero will come back to us—I know he is not dead. One so good, and so brave, and so gentle, and so clever as he was, I know is not lost to us altogether." Perhaps Harry thought within himself that his mother had not always spoken of her eldest son thus. "Dry up thy tears, my dear! He will come back to us, I know he will." When Harry pressed her for a reason, she said she had seen her father two nights running in a dream, and he had told her her boy was a prisoner among the Indians.

Madam Esmond's grief had not prostrated her as Harry's had; instead, it had stirred and animated her. Her eyes were eager, her countenance angry and revengeful. The lad wondered almost at the condition in which he found his mother.

But when he besought her to go downstairs and welcome George Washington, who had accompanied him, the lady's excitement painfully increased. "I should shudder at touching his hand," she said. "He declared Mr. Washington had taken her son from her; she could not sleep under the same roof with him."

"He gave me his bed when I was ill, mother; and if our George is alive, how has George Washington a hand in his death? Ah! please God it be only as you say," Harry cried, bewildered.

"If your brother returns, as return he will, it will not be through Mr. Washington's help," Madam Esmond said. "He neither defended George on the field, nor would he bring him out of it."

"But he tended me most kindly in my fever," Harry interposed. "He was yet ill when he gave up his bed to me, and was thinking only of his friend, when any other man would have thought only of himself."

"A friend! A pretty friend!" the lady sneered. "Of all his Excellency's aides-de-camp, my gentleman is the only one who comes back unwounded. The brave and noble fall, but he, to be sure, is unhurt. I confide my boy to him, the pride of my life, whom he will defend with his, forsooth! And he leaves my George in the forest, and brings me back himself! Oh, a pretty welcome I must give him!"

"No gentleman," Harry cried warmly, "was ever refused shelter under my grandfather's roof."

"Oh no—no gentleman!" the little widow exclaimed. "Let us go down, if you like, son, and pay our respects to this one. Will you please to give me your arm?" Taking an arm that was little able to give her support, she walked down the broad stairs into the apartment where the Colonel sat.

She made a ceremonious curtsey, extending one of the little hands, allowing it to rest in his for a moment. "I wish that our meeting had been happier, Colonel Washington," she said.

"You do not grieve more than I do that it is otherwise, madam," the Colonel replied.

"I might have wished that the meeting had been spared, that I might not have kept you from friends whom you are naturally anxious to see—that my boy's indisposition had not detained you. Home and his good nurse Mountain, and his mother and our good Doctor Dempster, will soon restore him. 'Twas scarce necessary, Colonel, that you, who have so many affairs on your hands, military and domestic, should turn doctor too."

"Harry was ill and weak, and I thought it was my duty to ride by him," the Colonel faltered.

"You yourself, sir, have gone through the fatigues and dangers of the campaign in the most wonderful manner," the widow said, curtseying again, looking at him with her impenetrable black eyes.

"I wish to Heaven, madam, some one else had come back in my place!"

"Nay, sir, you have ties which must render your life more than ever valuable and dear to you, and duties to which, I know

The library was a room of dust and silence, where the air hung still and heavy, smelling of old paper and the slow decay of time. I sat at a desk carved from wood that had absorbed generations of secrets, tasked with arranging the dead into a coherent lie called history. It was a mechanical labor, the quiet shuffling of falsehoods to create a semblance of order. In this sterile atmosphere, a volume arrived, printed in Amsterdam by a man named Pierre Rouge. Its title, *Memoirs of M. d'Artagnan*, suggested a flight from the Bastille, a refuge for those whose honesty carried the price of imprisonment. Now, both the writer and his jailers were equally forgotten ghosts.

I opened the book. Within its pages lay the usual statues of kings and queens, cardinals and courtiers—interchangeable faces painted in the varnish of officialdom. They held no interest for me. But in the margins of this grand narrative, the author described a mundane waiting room, an antechamber belonging to M. de Tréville. Here, amidst the bureaucratic hum of the Musketeers, d'Artagnan encountered three young men in uniform.

Athos. Porthos. Aramis.

The names were artificial constructs, mythological remnants ending in *-os* and *-is*. They were clearly false, pseudonyms adopted by men who had likely traded their given identities for uniforms born of financial ruin or social disgrace. They had erased themselves to become functions of the state. I felt a sudden, inexplicable kinship with this erasure.

I began to search for them. I spent weeks requesting catalogs, folios, and registers, compiling lists of nothing. The effort was immense and entirely pointless. I was on the verge of abandoning the inquiry, ready to accept that these men were merely the inventions of a mediocre soldier, when a prompt from an acquaintance named Paulin Paris led me to request another manuscript.

***

Harry Warrington possessed none of the rhetorical power of Aeneas, yet his narrative struck a chord in the solitary heart of Madam Bernstein. His voice was artless, cordial, and devoid of the dangerous eloquence that had doomed Dido, but it was undeniably touching. More than once, the Baroness found herself yielding to a softness she rarely permitted. Life had been a desert for her, a long stretch of loneliness punctuated by few sweet resting places. Until this friendly voice entered her ears, causing her heart to beat with strange pangs of love and sympathy. She doted on the lad, intoxicated by this new sensation of compassion. Save for a fleeting moment in her earliest youth, she had felt no tender sentiment for any human being. Such a woman watches her own sensations keenly, and surely she must have smiled inwardly at the sight of this boy, noting how her pulses rose above their ordinary rhythm. She longed for him. Her cheeks flushed with happiness when he approached; her eyes welcomed him with fond pleasure.

*"Wait,"* whispered Conscience, the dark scoffer mocking within her, *"wait, Beatrix Esmond. You know you will weary of this inclination, as you have of all. You know that when the passing fancy subsides, the boy may perish, and you will have no tear for him; or he may talk, and you will tire of his stories. Your lot in life is to be lonely—lonely."*

Well? Suppose life is a desert? There are halting-places and shades, and refreshing waters; let us profit by them for today. We know we must march when tomorrow comes, and tramp on our destiny onward. She smiled inwardly, recognizing in his simple tales about his mother traits of family resemblance. Madam Esmond was very jealous? Yes, Harry admitted. Was he fond of Colonel Washington? He liked him, but only as a friend, Harry declared. A hundred times he had heard his mother vow she felt no other emotion toward him. He was ashamed to admit that he himself had once been absurdly jealous of the Colonel.

"Well, you will see that my half-sister will never forgive him," said Madam Beatrix. "And you need not be surprised, sir, at women taking a fancy to men younger than themselves; for don't I dote upon you? And don't all these Castlewood people fret with jealousy?"

However great their jealousy of Madame de Bernstein's new favorite, the family of Castlewood allowed no feeling of ill-will to appear in their language or behavior toward their young guest and kinsman. After a couple of days in the ancestral house, Mr. Harry Warrington had become Cousin Harry to the young and middle-aged. Especially in Madame Bernstein's presence, the Countess of Castlewood was most gracious to her kinsman. She seized many amiable private opportunities to inform the Baroness how charming the young Huron was, vaunting the elegance of his manners and appearance, and wondering how, in his distant province, the child should ever have learned to be so polite.

These notes of admiration or interrogation, the Baroness took with equal complacency. Speaking parenthetically, the present chronicler cannot help putting in a little respectful remark regarding the conduct of ladies toward one another, and the things they say, which they forbear to say, and which they whisper behind each other's backs. With what smiles and curtseys they stab each other! With what compliments they hate each other! With what determination of long-suffering they refuse to be offended! With what innocent dexterity they can drop the drop of poison into the cup of conversation, handing round the goblet, smiling, to the whole family to drink, and make the dear, domestic circle miserable! I burst out of my parenthesis. I fancy my Baroness and Countess smiling at each other a hundred years ago, giving each other the hand or the cheek, calling each other *My dear, My dear creature, My dear Countess, My dear Baroness, My dear sister*—even when they were most ready to fight.

"You wonder, my dear Maria, that the boy should be so polite?" cried Madame de Bernstein. "His mother was bred up by two very perfect gentlefolk. Colonel Esmond had a certain grave courteousness, and a grand manner, which I do not see among the gentlemen nowadays."

"Eh, my dear, we all of us praise our own time! My grandmamma used to declare there was nothing like Whitehall and Charles the Second."

"My mother saw King James the Second's court for a short while, and though not a court-educated person, as you know—her father was a country clergyman—yet was exquisitely well-bred. The Colonel, her second husband, was a person of great travel and experience, as well as of learning, and had frequented the finest company of Europe. They could not go into their retreat and leave their good manners behind them, and our boy has had them as his natural inheritance."

"Nay, excuse me, my dear, for thinking you too partial about your mother. She could not have been that perfection which your filial fondness imagines. She left off liking her daughter—you have owned that she did—and I cannot fancy a complete woman who has a cold heart. No, no, my dear sister-in-law! Manners are very requisite, no doubt, and, for a country parson's daughter, your mamma was very well—I have seen many of the cloth who are very well. Mr. Sampson, our chaplain, is very well. Dr. Young is very well. Mr. Dodd is very well; but they have not the true air—as how should they? I protest, I beg pardon! I forgot my lord bishop, your ladyship's first choice. But, as I said before, to be a complete woman, one must have, what you have, what I may say and bless Heaven for, I think I have—a good heart. Without the affections, all the world is vanity, my love! I protest I only live, exist, eat, drink, rest, for my sweet, sweet children—for my wicked Willy, for my self-willed Fanny, dear naughty loves!"

(She rapturously kissed a bracelet on each arm which contained the miniature representations of those two young persons.)

"Yes, Mimi! Yes, Fanchon! You know I do, you dear, dear little things! And if they were to die, or you were to die, your poor mistress would die too!"

Mimi and Fanchon, two quivering Italian greyhounds, jumped into their lady's arms and kissed her hands, but respected her cheeks, which were covered with rouge.

"No, my dear! For nothing do I bless Heaven so much (though it puts me to excruciating torture very often) as for having endowed me with sensibility and a feeling heart!"

"You are full of feeling, dear Anna," said the Baroness. "You are celebrated for your sensibility. You must give a little of it to our American nephew—cousin—I scarce know his relationship."

"Nay, I am here but as a guest in Castlewood now. The house is my Lord Castlewood's, not mine, or his lordship's whenever he shall choose to claim it. What can I do for the young Virginian that has not been done? He is charming. Are we even jealous of him for being so, my dear? And though we see what a fancy the Baroness de Bernstein has taken for him, do your ladyship's nephews and nieces—your real nephews and nieces—cry out? My poor children might be mortified, for indeed, in a few hours, the charming young

The second day after Harry's arrival at Castlewood was a Sunday. The chapel belonging to the castle served as the village church, a single edifice where the house opened directly onto a great state pew reserved for the family. After the appointed hour, they filed into their assigned seats in strict order, while a considerable congregation from the village filled the pews below. Ancient, dusty banners hung from the rafters; Harry found pleasure in imagining them carried by retainers during the Commonwealth wars, conflicts in which his ancestors had fought with loyalty and distinction. Behind the altar rails knelt the effigy of the Esmond of King James the First, the common progenitor of the assembly.

Madame de Bernstein, in her capacity as Bishop's widow, attended without fail. Her devotion possessed a gravity nearly equal to that of the stone ancestor, who knelt perpetually on his marble hassock before his great desk and book, beneath his emblazoned shield. The clergyman, a tall, high-complexioned young man, read the service in a lively, agreeable voice, imparting a theatrical emphasis to the scriptural chapters. The music was competent, provided by one of the young ladies at the organ, though it lacked polish due to an interruption—a burst of laughter emanating from the servants' pew. This was caused by Mr. Warrington's lacquey, Gumbo. Knowing the air intended for the psalm, Gumbo launched into it with a voice so excessively loud and sweet that the entire congregation turned toward the African warbler. The parson covered his mouth with a handkerchief, and the liveried gentlemen from London were thrown into disarray. Perhaps pleased with the sensation he generated, Gumbo continued his performance until it resembled a solo, silencing the clerk entirely. Though Gumbo held the book alongside Pretty Molly, the porter's daughter who had welcomed the strangers to Castlewood, he sang and recited by ear, unable to read a single syllable of the verses before him.

Once this choral disruption concluded, a brief sermon followed, which Harry deemed far too short. In a lively, familiar, and striking discourse, the clergyman recounted a scene witnessed the previous week: the execution of a horse-thief following the Assizes. He described the man's former good character, his family, and the agony of separation. He depicted the execution with startling, terrible, and picturesque vividness, omitting the scriptural phrasing Harry was accustomed to from the somewhat Calvinistic preachers his mother favored. Instead, he spoke as one man of the world addressing other sinners, offering advice that might prove profitable. The unfortunate man had once been a farmer of promise; he had succumbed to drinking, card-playing, horse-racing, and cock-fighting—the vices of the age—which the young clergyman condemned with generous indignation. Then came poaching and horse-stealing, for which he suffered. The divine rapidly sketched striking, fearful images of these rustic crimes. He startled his hearers by revealing that the Eye of the Law watched the poacher at midnight, set traps to ensnare the criminal, galloped the stolen horse over highways and commons from county to county, yet showed Retribution ever pursuing, seizing the malefactor at a country fair, dragging him before the justice, and refusing to unlock his manacles until they dropped at the foot of the gallows. Heaven be pitiful to the sinner! The clergyman enacted the scene. He whispered in the criminal's ear at the cart; he dropped his handkerchief onto the clerk's head. Harry recoiled as the cloth fell. The clergyman had spoken for over twenty minutes. Harry could have listened for an hour more and would not have realized he had been in the pulpit for five. The gentlefolk in the great pew were thoroughly enlivened. Occasionally, Harry, observing the pew where the household servants sat, noted their intense attention, particularly Gumbo, his own man, seated in an attitude of profound consternation. Yet the smock frocks seemed unconcerned, exiting the church with total indifference. Gaffer Brown and Gammer Jones accepted the matter as it unfolded, while the rosy-cheeked, red-cloaked village lasses sat under their broad hats entirely unmoved.

"My lord," the clergyman observed from his pew, nodding slightly as the divine's head and wig surged up from the cushion, "Sampson has been strong today. He has assaulted the Philistines in great force."

"Beautiful, beautiful!" exclaimed Harry.

"Bet five to four it was his Assize sermon. He has been over to Winton to preach and to see those dogs," cried William.

The organist led the small congregation out into the sunshine. Only Sir Francis Esmond, temp. Jac. I., remained kneeling on his marble hassock before his prayer-book of stone. Mr. Sampson emerged from his vestry in his cassock and nodded to the gentlemen still lingering in the great pew.

"Come up, and tell us about those dogs," said Mr. William, and the divine nodded with a laughing assent.

The gentlemen passed from the church into the gallery connecting it to the house. Mr. Sampson traversed the court and soon joined them. Introduced by my lord to the Virginian cousin of the family, Mr. Warrington, the chaplain bowed profoundly, hoping Mr. Warrington would benefit from the virtuous example of his European kinsmen. Was he related to Sir Miles Warrington of Norfolk? Sir Miles was Mr. Warrington's father's elder brother. What a pity he had a son! 'Twas a pretty estate, and Mr. Warrington looked as if he would secure a baronetcy and a fine holding in Norfolk.

"Tell me about my uncle," cried Virginian Harry.

"Tell us about those dogs!" said English Will, breathlessly.

"Two more jolly dogs, two more drunken dogs, saving your presence, Mr. Warrington, than Sir Miles and his son, I never saw. Sir Miles was a staunch friend and neighbor of Sir Robert's. He can drink down any man in the county, except his son and a few others. As for the other dogs regarding which Mr. William is anxious—for Heaven hath made him a prey to dogs and all kinds of birds, like the Greeks in the Iliad—"

"I know that line in the Iliad," said Harry, blushing. "I only know five more, but I know that one." His head fell. He was thinking, "Ah, my dear brother George knew all the Iliad and all the Odyssey, and almost every book that was ever written besides!"

"What on earth" (mentioning only a place under the earth) "are you talking about now?" asked Will of his reverence.

The chaplain reverted to the dogs and their performance. He believed Mr. William's dogs were superior matches. From dogs, the conversation drifted to horses. Mr. William was eager regarding the Six Year Old Plate at Huntingdon. "Have you brought any news of it, Parson?"

"The odds are five to four on Brilliant against the field," said the parson, gravely, "but mind you, Jason is a good horse."

"Whose horse?" asked my lord.

"Duke of Ancaster's. By Cartouche out of Miss Langley," said the divine. "Have you horse-races in Virginia, Mr. Warrington?"

"Haven't we!" cried Harry; "but oh! I long to see a good English race!"

"Do you—do you—bet a little?" continued his reverence.

"I have done such a thing," replied Harry with a smile.

"I'll take Brilliant even against the field, for ponies with you, cousin!" shouted Mr. William.

"I'll give or take three to one against Jason!" said the clergyman.

"I don't bet on horses I don't know," said Harry, wondering to hear the chaplain now, remembering his sermon half an hour prior.

"Wouldn't you better write home and ask your mother?" said Mr. William, with a sneer.

"Will, Will!" called out my lord, "our cousin Warrington is free to bet, or not, as he likes. Have a care how you venture on either of them, Harry Warrington. Will is an old file, in spite of his smooth face, and as for Parson Sampson, I defy our ghostly enemy to get the better of him."

"Him and all his works, my lord!" said Mr. Sampson, bowing.

Harry was highly indignant at this allusion to his mother. "I'll tell you what, cousin Will," he said, "I am in the habit of managing my own affairs in my own way, without asking any lady to arrange them for me. And I'm used to make my own bets upon my own judgment, and don't need any relations to select them for me, thank you. But as I am your guest, and, no doubt, you want to show me hospitality, I'll take your bet—there. And so Done and Done."

"Done," said Will, looking askance.

"Of course it is the regular odds that's in the paper which you give me, cousin?"

"Well, no, it isn't," growled Will. "The odds are five to four, that's the fact, and you may have 'em, if you like."

"Nay, cousin, a bet is a bet; and I take you, too, Mr. Sampson."

"Three to one against Jason. I lay it. Very good," said Mr. Sampson.

The air in the drawing room was thick with the scent of brandy and the low hum of calculation. Money changed hands not as currency, but as a fluid exchange between men who viewed the world through a lens of probability rather than morality. My cousin, Harry, had won enough to buy a small estate, yet he felt compelled to return it, a gesture that would surely disappoint his mother and the chaplain. They preferred tea and order; Harry preferred the chaos of the card table. From the moment dinner ended at three, the house shifted gears. Whist and quadrille were polite diversions, but the real business—the high-stakes games involving fortunes—began then. Even Lord Castlewood, usually reserved, had softened toward his kinsman since their last conversation. They gambled openly at backgammon and piquet, a breach of the strict silence maintained by the ladies, who demanded Harry promise never to lead his cousin astray. He gave them his word, a solemn vow broken only when the family retired and the three men slipped into Harry's quarters with dice and rum to play until dawn.

Harry's descriptions of his American home were not lies, nor were they boasts. He spoke honestly of a land where the aristocracy amused itself with hunting, cards, and punch, surrounded by a workforce of enslaved men who cultivated the tobacco and corn. In Virginia, hospitality was a law of physics; every house was a neighbor's, and the idle gentry roamed from mansion to mansion seeking sport and rough plenty. Harry had known a life of straw mattresses and cracked bowls, chasing hounds in the dark, a stark contrast to the sober, bookish nature of his younger brother. Nature divided them cleanly: one loved tea and discipline, the other Burgundy and the gallop. This difference was quickly noted by his English hosts, who found the looseness of colonial manners refreshing compared to the rigid Puritanism they feared. Names were spoken freely, actions taken boldly, things done that would scandalize a modern audience. Yet, amidst this freedom, Harry kept his own counsel. He was a formidable opponent at the gaming table, matching the three gentlemen in skill and holding his own at billiards, a game his grandfather had imported from Europe. While Mr. Will initially dominated, Harry soon became his equal, proving himself superior in many respects. Yet, despite his prowess, Harry frequently lost to the ladies, particularly one. He treated every woman with profound courtesy, a trait inherited from his lineage, and thus he was beloved by all in the Castlewood establishment—from the stern housekeeper to the giggling maids. Madame de Bernstein observed him closely, noting his behavior without speaking.

Then there was Lady Maria Esmond. Her age was a mystery wrapped in the fog of the era; peerage books were scarce, and memory was unreliable. When Will claimed she was thirty-five, he exaggerated, for she was his half-sister, the daughter of a German lady married during Queen Anne's wars. Born perhaps under George I, she claimed to be seventy-two, yet to Harry, she seemed ancient and wise. "I am old, child," she would say, calling him "child" when alone, claiming she might be his mother. Harry, however, saw only vitality. "You don't look twenty," he insisted. Lady Maria possessed a dazzling complexion, abundant fair hair, and arms that gleamed under the candlelight. When she stretched across the billiard table to strike a ball, Harry witnessed a vision: a glimpse of ankle, a clocked stocking, a black satin slipper with a red heel. It was a sight that filled him with rapture, a fixation that made him forget his own mother's feet were far more slender. No doubt she was kind to him in private, mocking him publicly as a simpleton and mimicking his provincial accent until he blushed and corrected himself under the tutelage of his English governesses.

As the seasons turned, Lord Castlewood grew increasingly familiar with his guest. With the harvest complete, the focus shifted to sport. Cock-matches at Winchester and bull-baiting at Hexton Fair provided entertainment, but the true joy lay in the travel. Harry rode with Will to various fairs and races, introduced to the local gentry—the Henleys, the Crawleys, the Redmaynes. Neighbors arrived in heavy coaches, staying for days in country fashion. Fewer guests came than expected due to the presence of Madame de Bernstein, whose rustic company she disliked. "We shall be merrier when my aunt leaves," the young people admitted, acknowledging her power. She had secured the earldom through her influence at court, ruling the household with an iron rod that even the Earl's father had feared. Without her, the house was louder, fuller of life. "Come to us in our gay days, Harry," the Earl urged, extending a soft hand with friendly blue eyes. Harry grasped it with ardour, eager to claim the privileges of cousinship, unaware of his own timidity. He realized his earlier letters, describing a cold reception, had been a mistake. The Earl was kind, promising introductions to London and White's. Castlewood was his English home. In his second dispatch to Virginia, Harry wrote with contrition, adding a clumsy spelling error: "My cousin, the Lady Maria, is a perfect Angle."

"Ille praeter omnes angulus ridet," muttered little Mr. Dempster back in Virginia.
"The child can't be falling in love with his angle!" cried Mountain.
"My niece Maria is forty," said Madam Esmond dismissively, recalling her as a gawky creature with a foot like bellows. Who holds the truth? Is beauty objective, or does it exist only in the squinting eyes of the lover? Perhaps Venus herself has a splay-foot and a crooked back. Let the fairy anoint the eyes, let the mistress's peepers remain clear, so that the beloved object appears as a paragon and the lover's ears receive only fresh roses.

While Harry captivated the drawing room and the servant's hall, his valet, Gumbo, commanded a different kind of admiration. Gumbo possessed a hundred talents: he was a fisherman, huntsman, blacksmith, and hairdresser trained by a Swiss gentleman. He cooked exquisite Virginian dishes, learned French culinary secrets, and sang melodies that made the girls dance. He played the fiddle so well that he earned free ale at the Three Castles whenever he visited. Good-natured and playful with the village children, he was a universal favorite in the Castlewood domain.

Yet, beneath this veneer of competence, Gumbo was a liar. It was undeniable. During church services, he pretended to read from Molly's psalm-book, singing words that bore no relation to the text, which he could not decipher. When the Swiss valet tested his musical knowledge, Gumbo turned the sheet music upside down. These acts of deception were daily occurrences, obvious to everyone in the household. They knew he lied, yet they forgave him, perhaps preferring the comfort of belief to the harshness of reality.

Gumbo kept his true feelings of wonder and envy hidden, observing the splendor of Castlewood with a cool detachment. He noted the horses, saying they were fine, though in Virginia there were six times as many, tended by fourteen or eighteen grooms. He marveled at Madam Esmond's carriages, laden with gold, and her gardens, which produced pineapples and peaches—fruits so common in his homeland they were fed to pigs. He counted twenty or forty gardeners, all black gentlemen like himself. Inside the house, there were twenty or forty gentlemen in livery, alongside a multitude of women-servants, perhaps fifty, all property of Madam Esmond, each worth hundreds of pieces of eight. He calculated her wealth: twenty or thirty thousand guineas a year, rooms full of gold and plate, arriving in ships he could not count, owning estates stretching a week's journey, covered in tobacco and negroes. He asked if Master Harry was the heir, noting that Master George had been killed and scalped by Indians—a tragedy Gumbo had attempted to prevent, having slain many Indians himself. Though dressed in black for his deceased master, Harry was rich beyond measure, with chests of gold, lace, and jewels in Bristol. Harry was the richest man in Virginia, entitled to twenty or sixty servants, yet he traveled with only one, the one who needed him most: Gumbo.

This elaborate tale was not invented spontaneously. It emerged gradually from Gumbo, refined over repetitions in the servants' hall or the butler's private apartment. By the third telling, the narrative was consistent, accurate in its details regarding the number of slaves and the magnitude of Madam Esmond's income. The truth was that in American establishments, four or five blacks performed the work of one white man, meaning the domestic staff swarmed with numbers unseen in England. Like most Virginian mansions, Madam Esmond's home and stables teemed with negroes, a silent army supporting the visible grandeur.

The silence in the room was not empty; it was heavy with the residue of a sudden explosion. Young Harry Warrington's rebellion had struck Madame de Bernstein with such velocity that her only defense was the immediate, violent eruption of anger we witnessed moments prior. As she retreated, her exit was marked by two sharp, piercing glances directed at Lady Fanny and her mother—a silent accusation that hung in the air longer than her footsteps. Lady Maria, however, remained a statue of indifference behind her tambour-frame. Her hands moved mechanically over the fabric, her head barely lifting from the embroidery to register the aunt's departure or the electric exchange of looks between the matriarch and her daughter.

"So," the maternal gaze seemed to articulate, "despite everything, you have secured him?"

"Secure what?" Lady Fanny's eyes queried, wide and feignedly perplexed. Yet innocence was a currency she could not afford to spend. She possessed none of the calculated purity of Maria. Had guilt truly weighed upon her, she would have mastered the art of appearing blameless, composing a face tailored to deflect scrutiny. Instead, whatever turmoil swirled behind her irises, her mother-in-law stared with a ferocity that suggested a desire to excise them entirely.

Lady Castlewood, however, lacked the theatrical cruelty of the monsters in *King Lear*. She did not attempt to pluck her daughter's eyes in the drawing room. Such acts required privacy; she would retreat, wrap her arm possessively around her child's waist, and administer the punishment in solitude.

"So you do not intend to accompany the old lady to Tunbridge Wells?" she asked, masking her intent with a smile of impeccable breeding.

"And small blame to our cousin!" My Lord Castlewood interjected, his voice cutting through the tension. The figure behind the tambour-frame paused, her needle hovering. "A young man cannot dedicate his entire existence to idleness and holidays. He must blend utility with pleasure. Let him attend the concerts and pump-rooms of Tunbridge or Bath later. Mr. Warrington must learn to manage a great estate in America; let him observe how ours are conducted here. Will has already shown him the kennels and stables, and the fashionable pastimes. In autumn, we shall introduce him to English falconry and shooting; in winter, the hunt. Although relations with our aunt-kinswoman in Virginia have cooled, our blood remains the same. Before sending our cousin back to his mother, let us demonstrate the essence of an English gentleman's domestic life. I wish to read with him as much as to sport alongside him, which is precisely why I have pressed him to remain."

His lordship spoke with such transparent sincerity that his mother-in-law, half-brother, and sister were forced to pause in their conspiracy. These three often plotted in the shadows, grumbling against the head of the house. Yet, when he adopted this tone of open benevolence, his meaning became opaque. He did not assert facts; he constructed realities. It was a convention among them that Lord Castlewood harbored a noble desire to cultivate affection and educate his young relative, that he had developed a genuine fondness for the lad, and that Harry should linger near him.

"What is Castlewood's game now?" William whispered to his mother and sister as they slipped away down the corridor. "Wait! By George, I have it!"

"What, William?"

"He intends to manipulate him into gambling, to win the Virginia estate back from him. That is the plot."

"But the lad holds no claim to the Virginia estate if he loses," his mother noted.

"If my brother lacks a scheme, may I—"

"Hush! Of course he has a scheme. What is it?"

"He cannot mean Maria—she is as old as Harry's mother," Mr. William mused.

"Nonsense! With her aged visage, sandy hair, and freckled complexion! Impossible!" Lady Fanny exclaimed, letting out a sigh that betrayed her own suppressed thoughts.

"Of course, your ladyship fancied the Iroquois, too!" his mother retorted sharply.

"I trust I know my station and duty better, madam! Even if I had desired him, that is no justification for marriage. Your ladyship has taught me that lesson well."

"My Lady Fanny!"

"I am certain you married our father without loving him. You have confessed this a thousand times!"

"And if you did not love our father before the vows, you certainly did not fall in love with him afterward," Mr. William interrupted with a laugh. "Fan and I recall how our honored parents fought. Do we not, Fan? And our brother Esmond maintained the peace."

"Do not summon those dreadful, low scenes, William!" his mother cried. "When your father drank excessively, he became a madman; his behavior serves as a warning to you, sir, who share this abhorrent habit."

"I am sure, madam, you were no happier marrying the man you despised, and your ladyship's title brought scant comfort," Lady Fanny whimpered. "What utility is a coronet with the jointure of a tradesman's wife? How many are richer than we? Recently, a grocer's widow from London Bridge moved into our square at Kensington. Her daughters wear three gowns where I possess one. Though served by only a man and two maids, I know they consume and drink a thousand times better than we do, subsisting on scraps of cold meat while our great, flaunting, impudent, lazy lackeys loiter!"

"He! He! Glad I dine at the palace rather than at home!" Mr. Will declared. Through his aunt's influence with Count Puffendorff, Groom of the Royal Powder-Closet, he held a minor post as Deputy Powder.

"Why should I not be content without any title save my own?" Lady Frances continued. "Many are. I dare say they find happiness even in America."

"Yes—with a mother-in-law who is a perfect Turk and Tartar, judging by reports, with Indian war-whoops echoing around you, and with the constant peril of losing your scalp or being devoured by a wild beast whenever you attend church."

"I would not go to church," Lady Fanny stated flatly.

"You would go with anyone who asked, Fan!" Mr. Will roared. "And so would old Maria, and so would any woman, that is the fact." He laughed at his own wit.

"Pray, good folks, what is this merriment?" Madame Bernstein peered in from the tapestried door leading to the gallery where they conversed.

Will explained that his mother and sister were quarreling—a rarity for Madame Bernstein, who knew them well—because Fanny desired to marry their cousin, the wild Indian, and my lady Countess forbade it. Fanny vehemently denied this. Since her mother first commanded her to avoid the young gentleman, she had exchanged fewer than two words with him. She understood her station. She feared neither scalping by wild Indians nor consumption by bears.

Madame de Bernstein looked bewildered. "If he is not staying for you, for whom is he staying?" she asked. "At the houses to which he has been escorted, you have ensured no woman appears before him unless she is either a fright or confined to a nursery. I believe the boy is too proud to fall in love with a dairymaid, Will."

"Hmph! That is a matter of taste, ma'am," Mr. William shrugged.

"The taste of Mr. William Esmond, as you say; but not of that boy. The Esmonds of his grandfather's upbringing would not seek courtship in the kitchen."

"Well, ma'am, every man to his taste. A fellow might fare worse than my brother's servants' hall. Besides Fan, there is only the maids or old Maria to choose from."

"Maria! Impossible!" Yet, as the word left her lips, a sudden possibility seized Madame Bernstein. This elderly Calypso might have captivated her young Telemachus. She recalled half a dozen instances in her own experience of youths infatuated with older women. She remembered the frequency of Harry Warrington's recent absences, which she had attributed to field sports. She also recalled how often, during those absences, Maria Esmond was likewise missing. Walks in cool avenues, whispers in garden temples, concealment behind clipped hedges, casual hand-squeezes in twilight corridors, sweet glances and lingering stares on the stairs—all these possibilities, suggesting a lively fancy and intimate worldly knowledge, surged through her mind just as she dismissed them as impossible.

"Impossible, ma'am! I don't know," Will persisted. "My mother warned Fan off him."

"Oh, your mother did warn Fanny?"

"Certainly, my dear Baroness!"

"Did she not? Did she not pinch Fanny's arm black and blue? Did they not fight over it?"

"Nonsense, William! For shame, William!" both implicated ladies cried in unison.

"Now, since we have heard how rich he is, perhaps it is sour grapes. And now, since he is barred from the young bird, perhaps he is hunting the old one. That is all. Why impossible? You know old Lady Suffolk, ma'am?"

"William, how can you speak of Lady Suffolk to your aunt?"

A grin spread across the young gentleman's face. "Because Lady Suffolk was a special favorite at Court? Well, others have succeeded her."

"Sir!" Madame de Bernstein cried, sensing offense.

"So they have, I say. Or who, pray, is my Lady Yarmouth now? And did not old Lady Suffolk fall in love with George Berkeley

The air in the saloon was thick with the scent of stale lavender and the damp wool of coats left too long by the fire. Through one door, Lady Castlewood, her son, and daughter withdrew, leaving the silence to settle over the remaining figures. By another exit, my Lord Castlewood departed, his footsteps measured and final.

From the tambour-frame, where they had been pinned to the woodwork like moths to a lantern, the demure eyes lifted. They rested on Harry Warrington. He stood beneath the great fireplace, an ancestral portrait looming above him, a silent judge of his posture. On his face, a heap of blushes had accumulated—a rare bloom, gathering on cheeks, forehead, and even the youthful ears, as if the season of gentleness had finally decided to arrive late in the year.

"Why did you refuse to go with our aunt, cousin?" the lady of the tambour frame asked, her voice small against the vast room.

"Because your ladyship bade me stay," the lad replied, the words falling flat and obedient.

"I bid you stay!" She laughed, a sharp, brittle sound. "Child! What one says in fun, you take in earnest! Are all you Virginian gentlemen so obsequious that you mistake every idle word for a command? Virginia must be a pleasant country for our sex if it be so!"

"You said—" Harry stopped, searching for the memory, his voice trembling with a sudden, violent intensity. "When—we walked in the terrace two nights since—O heaven!"

"Ah, that sweet night, cousin!" the Tambour-frame echoed, her tone shifting instantly from mockery to performance.

"When you gave me this rose from your own neck," Harry roared, tearing a crumpled, decaying vegetable from the deep fold of his waistcoat. He held it out, a shriveled thing, brown and dead. "Which I will never part with—no, by heavens, whilst this heart continues to beat! You said, 'Harry, if your aunt asks you to go away, you will go, and if you go, you will forget me.' Didn't you say so?"

"All men forget!" the Virgin sighed, a sound of weary resignation.

"In this cold, selfish country they may, cousin, not in ours," Harry continued, his voice rising in a feverish exaltation that seemed to ignore the physics of the room. "I had rather have lost an arm almost than refused the old lady. It went to my heart to say no to her, and she so kind to me, and who had been the means of introducing me to—to—O heaven!"

(A kick struck an intervening spaniel, sending the animal yelping from before the fire. Harry advanced rapidly on the tambour-frame.)

"Look here, cousin! If you were to bid me jump out of yonder window, I should do it; or murder, I should do it."

"La! but you need not squeeze one's hand so, you silly child!" Maria remarked, withdrawing her hand as if burned.

"I can't help it—we are so in the south. Where my heart is, I can't help speaking my mind out, cousin—and you know where that heart is!" He paused, breathless. "Ever since that evening—that—O heaven! I tell you I have hardly slept since—I want to do something—to distinguish myself—to be ever so great. I wish there was giants, Maria, as I have read of in—in books, that I could go and fight 'em. I wish you was in distress, that I might help you, somehow. I wish you wanted my blood, that I might spend every drop of it for you. And when you told me not to go with Madame Bernstein..."

"I tell thee, child? never."

"I thought you told me. You said you knew I preferred my aunt to my cousin, and I said then what I say now, 'Incomparable Maria! I prefer thee to all the women in the world and all the angels in Paradise—and I would go anywhere, were it to dungeons, if you ordered me!' And do you think I would not stay anywhere, when you only desired that I should be near you?" he added, after a moment's pause.

"Men always talk in that way—that is, that is, I have heard so," the spinster corrected herself, smoothing her skirts. "For what should a country-bred woman know about you creatures? When you are near us, they say you are all raptures and flames and promises and I don't know what; when you are away, you forget all about us."

"But I think I never want to go away as long as I live," groaned the young man, collapsing into a chair. "I have tired of many things; not books and that, I never cared for study much, but games and sports which I used to be fond of when I was a boy. Before I saw you, it was to be a soldier I most desired; I tore my hair with rage when my poor dear brother went away instead of me on that expedition in which we lost him. But now, I only care for one thing in the world, and you know what that is."

"You silly child! don't you know I am almost old enough to be...?"

"I know—I know! but what is that to me? Hasn't your br...—well, never mind who, some of 'em—told me stories against you, and didn't they show me the Family Bible, where all your names are down, and the dates of your birth?"

"The cowards! Who did that?" cried out Lady Maria, her composure fracturing. "Dear Harry, tell me who did that? Was it my mother-in-law, the grasping, odious, abandoned, brazen harpy? Do you know all about her? How she married my father in his cups—the horrid hussey!--and..."

"Indeed it wasn't Lady Castlewood," interposed the wondering Harry.

"Then it was my aunt," continued the infuriate lady, her voice climbing. "A pretty moralist, indeed! A bishop's widow, forsooth, and I should like to know whose widow before and afterwards. Why, Harry, she intrigues with the Pretender, and with the Court of Hanover, and, I dare say, would with the Court of Rome and the Sultan of Turkey if she had had the means. Do you know who her second husband was? A creature who..."

"But our aunt never spoke a word against you," broke in Harry, more and more amazed at the nymph's vehemence.

She checked her anger. In the inquisitive countenance opposite to her, she thought she read some alarm as to the temper which she was exhibiting.

"Well, well! I am a fool," she said softly. "I want thee to think well of me, Harry!"

A hand was somehow put out and seized, and no doubt, kissed by the rapturous youth. "Angel!" he cries, looking into her face with his eager, honest eyes.

Two fish-pools irradiated by a pair of stars would not kindle to greater warmth than did those elderly orbs into which Harry poured his gaze. Nevertheless, he plunged into their blue depths, and fancied he saw heaven in their calm brightness. So that silly dog (of whom Aesop or the Spelling-book used to tell us in youth) beheld a beef-bone in the pond, and snapped at it, and lost the beef-bone he was carrying. O absurd cur! He saw the beefbone in his own mouth reflected in the treacherous pool, which dimpled, I dare say, with ever so many smiles, coolly sucked up the meat, and returned to its usual placidity. Ah! what a heap of wreck lie beneath some of those quiet surfaces! What treasures we have dropped into them! What chased golden dishes, what precious jewels of love, what bones after bones, and sweetest heart's flesh! Do not some very faithful and unlucky dogs jump in bodily, when they are swallowed up heads and tails entirely? When some women come to be dragged, it is a marvel what will be found in the depths of them. Cavete, canes! Have a care how ye lap that water. What do they want with us, the mischievous siren sluts? A green-eyed Naiad never rests until she has inveigled a fellow under the water; she sings after him, she dances after him; she winds round him, glittering tortuously; she warbles and whispers dainty secrets at his cheek, she kisses his feet, she leers at him from out of her rushes: all her beds sigh out, "Come, sweet youth! Hither, hither, rosy Hylas!" Pop goes Hylas. (Surely the fable is renewed for ever and ever?) Has his captivator any pleasure? Doth she take any account of him? No more than a fisherman landing at Brighton does of one out of a hundred thousand herrings.... The last time. Ulysses rowed by the Sirens' bank, he and his men did not care though a whole shoal of them were singing and combing their longest locks. Young Telemachus was for jumping overboard: but the tough old crew held the silly, bawling lad. They were deaf, and could not hear his bawling nor the sea-nymphs' singing. They were dim of sight, and did not see how lovely the witches were. The stale, old, leering witches! Away with ye! I dare say you have painted your cheeks by this time; your wretched old songs are as out of fashion as Mozart,

The dinner concluded without a trace of the earlier storm. Madame de Bernstein's face was smooth, her manner a practiced cordiality that extended even to Harry. She praised the cook, declared the fricassee superior to anything the Castlewood moats could produce, and dismissed the idea of opening a new bottle for an elderly woman. When the vintage was poured, she raised her glass to Madam Esmond Warrington, hoping Harry had secured his mother's blessing to bring an English bride home. Harry did not recall his grandmother; he recalled only the Baroness's mother, a figure of legendary beauty whose marriage to the older Colonel Esmond served as proof that disparity was a vanity.

My Lady Castlewood and her children remained silent observers while the Baroness spun tales of her lineage, her happiness, and the universal admiration of her union. The atmosphere shifted, the tension dissolving into a collective performance of reconciliation. Lord Castlewood watched with a puzzled stillness, masking any suspicion of his aunt's sudden transformation. The Baroness's good humor was infectious; no guest escaped a gracious word.

When the chaplain noted the change, the Baroness confessed her sins with theatrical humility. She recounted how, as a child in this very room, she had once been wayward, scolding her sister and boxing Harry Warrington's ears simply because he refused to accompany her to Tunbridge Wells. "I was just as wayward then," she declared, "at seven years old, as I am today at seventy." She begged forgiveness for her weariness, for telling Maria that her company was burdensome.

"I absolve your ladyship!" the chaplain cried.

"But you do not know how cross I was," she insisted, detailing her cruelty. "Maria, I ask your pardon."

"Sure, madam, you have done me no wrong," Maria replied softly.

"Indeed I have," the Baroness countered, her voice rising. "Because I was weary of myself, I told you your presence would be wearisome. Yet you offered to come. I beg, I pray, I command that you come."

Lord Castlewood filled his glass, his expression unreadable. The comedy was complete.

"You must come and stay with me, my dear," Maria said, forcing a smile. "Will you spare your sister to me?"

"Lady Maria Esmond is of age to decide," Lord Castlewood bowed. "If any of us can be of use..."

The sentence hung in the air, interpreted by the household as a dismissal of the old woman's interference. "Oh, Tunbridge will be delightful!" Maria sighed.

Harry drew patterns on the tablecloth with his finger. His mind raced through a catalogue of anticipated pleasures: walks, rides, endless conversation, secluded summer houses, moonlight, and the soft sounds of affection. The day was approaching. Everyone else was departing—the ladies to the waters, the lords to their friends—and he was to remain alone with his divine charmer. The thought was maddening. These people were leaving him to enjoy a heaven that felt too real to be true.

"I knew it couldn't be," Harry thought. "Something would happen to take her from me."

"But you will ride with us to Tunbridge, nephew Warrington?" Madame de Bernstein asked. "To keep us from the highwaymen?"

Harry felt the heat rise in his cheeks. He forced his voice steady. Yes, he would ride. He would ensure their safety. Danger appealed to him; he would slay a thousand highwaymen if they threatened his mistress's coach. Even if he could not speak to her, he would be near.
