He would press her hand at the inn, feel it rest in his as he guided her to the carriage. Two days to Tunbridge, perhaps a night or two at the Wells. Was the condemned man at Newgate not grateful for a brief reprieve?

They had not chosen to describe the depth of Harry's imbecility, only to indicate it. Some suffer from love gently; others are consumed by the fever until death or old age carries the scars. It was unfair to record his ravings, especially since he loved a woman twice his age. Had not a young gentleman committed suicide for Mademoiselle Ninon de l'Enclos, only to discover she was his grandmother? Perhaps Harry was making an ass of himself. Let the honest fellows kick and abuse him; they recognized a brother-donkey.

"You will stay with us for a day or two at the Wells," the Baroness continued. "Then you can return to Castlewood and the partridge-shooting."

"A whole week of heaven!" Harry agreed.

"And as there is sure to be plenty of company," the lady added graciously, "I shall be able to present you."

"Company! Ah! I shan't need company," Harry sighed.

"I mean that I shall be quite contented in the company of you two ladies," he corrected eagerly.

That evening, as the ladies retired and Lord Castlewood withdrew to his chambers, the three gentlemen assembled in Harry's small room before the punch-bowl. Will produced fresh decks of cards, proposing to settle Harry's debts by doubling or acquitting them. The chaplain possessed no ready cash, nor did Lord Castlewood's younger brother. Harry desired neither victory nor defeat; he feared giving pain to the brother of his adored Maria or appearing ungenerous to her kin. He was willing to grant them their revenge.

Until midnight, he would play whatever stakes they named.

The dice rattled, the cards shuffled. Likely, Harry paid no attention to the game itself. While the cards flew, his mind was elsewhere. He pictured his beloved sitting with her golden hair spread beneath her maid's fingers. He saw her kneeling in prayer, then sinking to rest behind her damask curtains. "Bless, bless her!"

"You double us all round?" Will shouted. "I'll take a card on each of mine. Ten... Queen... Two natural vingt-et-un! Since you doubled us, you owe me—"

Volleys of oaths erupted from Will and the chaplain at the Virginian's fortune. He won because he did not wish to win. Fortune, that coquettish jade, favored him because his thoughts dwelt on another nymph, perhaps as fickle as herself. Will and the chaplain played against him, assuming the wealthy Virginian wished to let them recover their losses. But this was not Harry's intent.

He had played hundreds of such games at home, keeping the details from his mother, learning to play fair and expect fairness in return.

"The luck seems to be with me, cousin," Harry said calmly, ignoring Will's curses. "I do not want to press it, but I am not a fool to fling it away. I have quite a heap of your promises on paper. If we continue, let us have the dollars on the table, or their worth."

"Always the way with you rich men," Will grumbled. "Never lend except on security—always win because you are rich."

"Faith, cousin, you have been flinging my riches in my face lately," the chaplain groaned. "What have we to play against our conqueror? My new gown? Set me five pieces against it? Or my Chrysostom, Foxe's Martyrs, Baker's Chronicle, and a cow with her calf?"

"I will bet one of cousin Will's notes for twenty pounds," Harry cried, producing a document. "Or my brown mare, backed red against your notes of hand."

"I have my horse. Fifty guineas," Will bellowed.

Harry accepted both offers. Within ten minutes, the horse and the mare had changed hands. Will swore more fiercely than before; the parson threw his wig to the ground, matching his pupil in volume. Harry remained calm, not elated by his triumph. They had asked him to play, and he had played. He knew he would win.

O beloved slumbering angel, he thought, am I not sure of victory when you are kind to me?

He looked out his window toward the casement on the opposite side of the court, the one he knew belonged to her. He had forgotten his victims and their groans, their bad luck crossing the courtyard. Under the brilliant flickering star, behind that lit window, lay his joy, his heart, his treasure.

The departure of the Baroness was a mechanical event, a shifting of weights in the household balance. While the Bishop of Cambray, in his romances, lamented the sorrow of Calypso and her maid walking the same plank of grief, the reality at Castlewood was simpler: a distribution of gold and a redistribution of luggage. The menials moved through the corridors with the silent efficiency of gears turning. Some sought a final glance at the departing figures; others waited by the doors for gratuities, the customary grease for the wheels of social exit.

Harry Warrington stood amidst this flow. He was a lover, and therefore, by the laws of that age, a distributor of largesse. He scattered his coins to the right and left, from the major-domo to the shoeblack, mimicking the wealth of the man he claimed to be—a descendant of Ashantee kings. It was a performance of generosity, a necessary act to ingratiate oneself with the entire ecosystem of a family where one holds affection for a single member. He paid the grim housekeeper, the feeble porter, smoothing the transactional friction of their separation. When a man loves one woman in a house, he inevitably becomes fond of every shadow cast by her presence. He smiled at old stories, drank port he would otherwise despise, and beat time to imaginary music, securing the loyalty of the household through the currency of attention.

He had won the brown horse, Prince William, a sound and swift animal, from his cousin Will. This victory completed his inventory of assets: money, autographs, and now, a steed. He retired to his dormitory, the stars shining over his mistress's window, calculating the pleasure of a future tour across the island with Maria on his pillion. His mind was a closed loop of romantic satisfaction, oblivious to the chaotic variables awaiting him in the morning.

Chaplain Sampson, however, introduced a variable of genuine emotion. He sent his black mare via the groom, footman, and gardener, who wept openly as they kissed the beast's white nose. Gumbo, the Virginian lad, absorbed this display of sentimentality. He produced two guineas, a small sum that instantly purchased the silence of the grieving boy and secured the transfer of the mare. Courtesy, in this instance, was a transaction settled in cash.

The morning meal became a formal theater of farewells. My Lord Castlewood descended, a rare occurrence, to bid his guests adieu. Will Esmond was absent, having left a note in handwriting of questionable orthography announcing his flight to Salisbury Races. He had already transferred ownership of the brown horse to Harry's servant, Tom. The remaining party drank tea amicably, the tension of departure dissolving into the routine of travel preparations.

The carriages rolled into the courtyard, laden with the vast baggage train of the present age. The Baroness and her niece occupied a landau guarded by men with blunderbusses; the maids and trunks filled a second vehicle. Harry's modest valises were tucked beneath the maid's supervision, while Gumbo proposed to ride by the window, claiming the role of the chief companion.

My Lord, with his stepmother and Lady Fanny, escorted the Baroness to the carriage steps. A crowd of villagers gathered under the trees, shouting huzzays as the ponderous vehicles departed. My Lord lingered in the courtyard with Harry, discussing the horses.

"I hear you carry away some of our horses," My Lord said.

"A gentleman cannot refuse a fair game at the cards," Harry replied, blushing slightly. "I never wanted to play, nor would have played for money had not my cousin William forced me. As for the chaplain, it went to my heart to win from him, but he was as eager as my cousin."

"There is no blame to you, my boy," My Lord conceded. "At Rome you can't help doing as Rome does. I am glad you gave Will a lesson. He is mad about play—he would gamble his coat off his back—and I and the family have had to pay his debts ever so many times. How much did you win?"

"Eighteen pieces the first day or two, a note for a hundred and twenty more, and the brown horse, sixty—that makes nigh upon two hundred. All was fair, and it was even against my will that we played at all. Will ain't a match for me, my lord—that is the fact."

"He is a match for most people, though," My Lord noted. "You won the brown horse, Prince William?"

"Yes. Out of Constitution. You don't suppose I would set him sixty against his bay?"

"Oh, I didn't know. I saw Will riding out this morning; most likely I did not remark what horse he was on. And you won the black mare from the parson?"

"For fourteen. He will mount Gumbo very well. Why does not the rascal come round with the horses?" Harry's mind drifted to Maria, longing to be trotting by her side.

"When you get to Tunbridge, cousin Harry, you must be on the look-out against sharper players than the chaplain and Will. There is all sorts of queer company at the Wells."

"A Virginian learns pretty well to take care of himself," Harry nodded knowingly.

"My sister is as innocent as one," My Lord added, recommending her protection. "Thou wilt see that she comes to no mischief?"

"I will guard her with my life!" Harry cried.

"And I will write thee word when you shall bring my sister back to me. Here come the horses. Have you bid adieu to the Countess and Lady Fanny? They are kissing their hands to you from the music-room balcony."

Harry ran upstairs for a brief ceremony, anxious to reach the charmer of his heart, and descended to mount his newly acquired steed. Gumbo held the reins, astride the parson's black mare, while another horse stood nearby.

"What is this?" Harry cried.

"Your honour's new horse," the groom said, touching his cap.

"This brute?" Harry exclaimed, using the expressive vocabulary of the era. "Go and bring me round Prince William, Mr. William's horse, the brown horse."

"Mr. William have rode Prince William this morning away to Salisbury Races," the groom explained calmly. "His last words was, 'Sam, saddle my bay horse, Cato, for Mr. Warrington this morning. He is Mr. Warrington's horse now. I sold him to him last night.' And I know your honour is bountiful: you will consider the groom."

My Lord burst into laughter at the groom's words. Harry, for his part, indulged in remarks of indignation that politeness forbade recording.

"Mr. William said he never could think of parting with the Prince under a hundred and twenty," the groom added, looking at the young man.

My Lord laughed harder. "Will has been too much for thee, Harry Warrington."

"Too much for me, my lord! So may a fellow with loaded dice throw sixes, and be too much for me. I do not call this betting, I call it ch—"

"Mr. Warrington! Spare me bad words about my brother, if you please. Depend on it, I will take care that you are righted. Farewell. Ride quickly, or your coaches will be at Farnham before you;" My Lord waved him off and retreated into the house, leaving Harry and Gumbo to ride out of the courtyard.

As the youth departed, eager to rejoin the carriages and his beloved, he failed to notice the unutterable affection Gumbo shot from his fine eyes toward a young creature in the porter's lodge.

Inside, the chaplain and My Lord finished their breakfast in peace. The two ladies had not returned.

"That was one of Will's confounded rascally tricks," My Lord said. "If our cousin breaks Will's head I should not wonder."

"He is used to the operation, my lord," the chaplain grinned. "And yet, when we were playing last night, the colour of the horse was not mentioned. I could not escape, having but one: and the black boy has ridden off on him. The young Virginian plays like a man, to do him justice."

"He wins because he does not care about losing. I think there can be little doubt but that he is very well to do. His mother's law-agents are my lawyers, and they write that the property is quite a principality, and grows richer every year."

"If it were a kingdom I know whom Mr. Warrington would make queen of it," the chaplain remarked obsequiously.

"Who can account for taste, parson?" My Lord asked with a sneer. "All men are so. The first woman I was in love with myself was forty; and as jealous as if she had been fifteen. It runs in the family. Colonel Esmond married my grandmother, who was almost old enough to be his. If this lad chooses to take out an elderly princess to Virginia, we must not balk him."

The air in the bedroom was thick with the scent of lavender and the metallic tang of dried blood. Harry Warrington lay in the bed, a pale, beautiful object suspended between life and death, his shoulder recently reset by Colonel Lambert's steady hands. The Colonel had bled him, a crude medical necessity that left the boy's arm stark white against the sheets, a visual proof of the violence inflicted upon the flesh. To Mrs. Lambert, watching him breathe in a sweet, inarticulate slumber, he was simply a pretty lad, a stranger who had fallen through the cracks of the world into her sanctuary. She compared him silently to her own sons, finding none half so handsome, unaware that the comparison was a trap she had willingly stepped into.

Outside the door, the Colonel held his wife's arm, guiding her down the broad oak staircase lined with the painted ghosts of past Lamberts. They moved with a rhythm that suggested a shared conspiracy, stepping carefully around the sleeping boy's fate. In the dressing closet, smelling of fresh lavender and old wood, the Colonel spoke with a waggish humor that masked the gravity of the situation. He teased his wife about her burning desire to sell her daughters, framing marriage as a transactional exchange of goods, yet his tone betrayed a deep-seated reluctance to part with them. He questioned whether good husbands were as common as sacks of coal found by the roadside, a cynical observation that clashed with the providential coincidence of the Virginian's arrival at their very gate.

Mrs. Lambert sighed, admitting defeat against her husband's penetrating gaze. She confessed that she had seen him in the candlelight three nights prior, a vision that had haunted her dreams alongside memories of his wounds in Minorca and Scotland. The Colonel dismissed her visions as the natural superstition of a woman who projected her fears onto empty space, yet he allowed her to hold his arm, walking together into the adjacent room where the French lessons awaited.

There, the atmosphere shifted from the somber reality of injury to the artificial brightness of theater. The Colonel treated his daughter Theo with mock reverence, addressing her as Madam, elevating her status through the pretense of ceremony. They read scenes from Molière's *Tartuffe*, the text unfolding in a language of polite deception that mirrored the real-world charades they navigated daily. Orgon spoke of recognizing Mariane's gentle spirit, praising her worthiness as a spouse, while Mariane responded with the submissive laughter of a girl trapped in a script written by a father. The Colonel laughed aloud at the end of the passage, turning to his wife with a grin that illuminated the room, oblivious to the fact that the play they were performing was a rehearsal for the very tragedies awaiting them all. The performance ended not with applause, but with the quiet settling of the house, leaving the strangers—the Virginian in the bed, the parents in the closet—to exist in the same space, separated by silence and the unspoken weight of the future.

The hospital room was a vessel of suspended animation, filled with the sterile rhythm of healing. Sunlight, sharp and uninvited, sliced through the curtains, illuminating dust motes dancing in the stillness. Harry Warrington lay beneath the sheets, his body a map of recent violence, ravaged by blood loss and fever. Hunger gnawed at him, a primal demand that superseded pain.

"Chicken," the doctor's wife announced, her voice carrying the weight of domestic authority. "Prepared by my own daughter."

A flash of memory, jagged and fleeting, pierced the haze. Two figures. Red cheeks, dark hair, a smile that vanished as quickly as it appeared. Then, an older face, stern and distant. The contrast struck him with the force of a physical blow. Pearls were glass. Gold was base metal. Time had stretched into a canyon, separating the boy he once was from the man he feared he had become. He writhed, a silent scream trapped in his throat, the realization settling over him like a shroud.

"What troubles you, my dear?" Mrs. Lambert asked, her tone softening the edges of his distress.

"A cramp," Harry lied, his voice raspy. "Just a spasm in the shoulder."

"You speak to your hosts," she corrected gently. "We are old friends, Mr. Warrington. Your father and my husband served together. We were schoolgirls at Kensington. You were never a stranger to us."

"They are here?" Harry asked, his eyes searching the room.

"At Tunbridge Wells," she replied. "They sent a rider from Reigate yesterday."

He looked down at his bandaged arm, the white cloth stark against his pale skin.

"I have done well by you," the Colonel stated, his presence filling the corner of the room. "Now, Mrs. Lambert and the cook will attend to you."

"Nonsense," the lady interjected. "Theo prepared the chicken and rice herself. Shall Mr. Warrington rise after breakfast? We shall send your valet."

"If whining is proof of loyalty," the Colonel mused, "your servant is a devoted creature indeed."

"He allowed your luggage to depart in your aunt's carriage," Mrs. Lambert noted. "You must wear my husband's linens. They are coarse, I admit."

"Pish!" the Colonel scoffed. "These shirts suit any Christian."

"They are Theo's and Hester's work," the lady added, glancing at her husband with a knowing tilt of her head. "And Theo stitched your sleeve to accommodate your injury."

Harry's gaze drifted to a porcelain vase on the table, overflowing with crimson roses. "Beautiful blooms."

"My daughter cut them this morning," the Colonel laughed. "She did cut them!"

Perhaps the Colonel felt his wife had overstayed her welcome in the narrative of the sickroom, perhaps he simply nudged her toward propriety with a subtle shift of weight.

"And I thought I heard someone singing the Evening Hymn last night," Harry murmured. "Or was it a dream?"

"Theo again," the Colonel chuckled. "Our servants claim your negro began humming it in the kitchen as if he were a church organ."

"Our household sings it," Harry said, his voice dropping. "My grandfather loved it. His wife's father was a friend of Bishop Ken, who composed it. And my brother..." He trailed off, the name hanging in the air.

Mrs. Lambert leaned forward, compelled by the sudden vulnerability in the boy's posture. Her heart, hardened by the cynicism of the last six weeks, softened under the warmth of gratitude and the comfort of a warm meal. The air around him cleared, shedding the taint of selfishness and corruption that had followed him from England. Fate, or perhaps a simple change of circumstance, had placed him in the company of the wise, whose lamps were trimmed and whose hearts kept a modest watch.

The matron withdrew, leaving Harry to consume the rice and chicken, while the Colonel settled beside the bed. Gratitude and satiety aligned his thoughts. He spoke more freely now, breaking his customary silence. He was not a scholar of books, nor did he offer profound commentary on literature, but he possessed a keen eye for dogs, horses, and the mechanics of daily life.

Lambert, a man of shrewd observation, quickly gauged his guest. For the first time, Harry learned that Mrs. Lambert had been a childhood friend of his mother, and that the Colonel's father had fought alongside Harry's grandfather, Colonel Esmond, during Queen Anne's wars. Harry felt the ground shift beneath him; he was no longer an outsider.

"My sons are away," the Colonel explained. "So you have the company of my wife and her daughters. Mrs. Lambert mentioned Theo—the broth, the roses, the mending of your coat. She is not the miracle her mother claims, but she is a competent housekeeper and a cheerful soul."

"It is very kind of Miss Lambert," Harry said.

"She does no more than her duty," the Colonel replied, smiling. "I mock my wife for praising our children, yet I am equally foolish. God has blessed us with dutiful offspring, and I feel no need to hide my thanks. Do you have a sister?"

"No, sir. I am alone."

"Indeed. I apologize for my intrusion. Your valet informed us of your misfortune last year. I served with Braddock in Scotland; I trust he recovered before passing. He was a wild man, yet truthful beneath his rough exterior. Your black servant speaks freely of you, assuming you allow such liberties because he saved your life."

"Saved me?" Harry exclaimed.

"From numerous Indians on that very expedition," the Colonel continued. "Molly and I were unaware we were hosting a man of such wealth. He claims half of Virginia belongs to you. Even if all of North America were yours, we could offer only our best."

"Negroes lie like the father of all lies," Harry countered sharply. "They exaggerate our riches to flatter us. My mother owns a vast estate in England, sufficient for our needs. Our splendor exists only in Mr. Gumbo's delusion. He never rescued me from an Indian; he would flee at the sight of one, just as my brother's boy did on that fatal day."

"The bravest men falter at unlucky moments," the Colonel observed calmly. "I witnessed elite troops rout before a ragged Highland mob at Preston."

"That was because the Highlanders fought for a righteous cause," Harry insisted.

"Do you believe the French Indians fought for a just cause in last year's conflict?" the Colonel asked.

"The scoundrels!" Harry shouted, his fist tightening. "They invaded British territory. But the Highlanders fought for their king."

"We fought for our king, too," the Colonel said, laughing. "And we won."

"Ah!" Harry cried. "If His Royal Highness the Prince had not retreated at Derby, your king and mine would now be King James the Third!"

"Who instilled such Tory sentiments in you, Mr. Warrington?" Lambert asked.

"The Esmonds have always been loyal," Harry answered. "Had we stayed home, twenty years earlier, my brother and I would have wagered our lives for the crown."

"Your neck remains safer on your shoulders than on a pike at Temple Bar," the Colonel remarked grimly. "I have seen them there; they do not look pleasant."

"I shall remove my hat and salute them whenever I pass the gate," Harry declared. "Provided the king and the entire court stand by!"

"I suspect your relative, Lord Castlewood, is less steadfast across the water," the Colonel said, his expression sobering. "Nor is your aunt, the Baroness of Bernstein, who entrusted you to our care. Though her old loyalties may have faded, she has rallied to our side, placing her nephews in the Household and seeking matches for her nieces. If you hold Tory views, Mr. Warrington, heed an old soldier's counsel: keep them to yourself."

"Why, sir, I do not believe you would betray me!" Harry protested.

"Not I, but others might. You did not speak thus at Castlewood, did you? The old Castlewood you came from?"

"I would be safe among my own kin, surely!" Harry cried.

"Doubtless. Yet even kin can play slippery games. Lacquies possess ears as well as masters, spreading tales wherever they go. Consider your black servant; he is eager to recount everything he knows, and much more besides."

"Did he mention the broken-kneed horse?" Harry blurted out, flushing red.

"To be frank, my groom seemed aware of the tale, remarking it was shameful for a gentleman to sell such a beast, especially a cousin. I am not here to act as a mentor or judge servants' gossip. Once you have seen more of your cousins, you will form your own judgment. Until then, follow an old soldier's advice: be cautious with whom you deal and what you say."

Shortly thereafter, Harry rose with the help of Gumbo, his valet. For the hundredth time, Harry promised a severe beating if Gumbo ever repeated his master's affairs in the servants' hall—a vow Gumbo swore to uphold eternally, even as he likely chattered every detail of the Castlewood secrets to the Colonel's kitchen staff. The hostess had undoubtedly heard fragments of these stories, though one cannot blame a lady of her station for curiosity regarding neighbors. After all, how does one stop servants from talking, or from listening when the faithful ones speak directly to you?

The illusion shattered quietly, not with a crash, but with the slow deflation of a balloon left in the wind. Mrs. Lambert's reverie ended. Miss Theo and her mother were forced, in the silent theater of their own minds, to admit the truth: the hero was merely a man. They spoke little of it, yet the understanding between them was complete, a shared language of affection that required no translation. To soothe the girl's quiet disappointment, the mother offered a tenderness that went beyond words, a physical warmth pressed against her daughter's cheek.

"Never mind, my dear," the whisper of the kiss suggested. "Our hero has turned out to be but an ordinary mortal. And none such is good enough for my Theo. Thou shalt have a real husband ere long, if there be one in England. Why, I was scarce fifteen when your father saw me at the Bury Assembly, and while I was yet at school, I used to vow that I never would have any other man. If Heaven gave me such a husband—the best man in the whole kingdom—sure it will bless my child equally, who deserves a king if she fancies him!"

Perhaps Mrs. Lambert, knowing the age of the Prince of Wales and the extent of his handsomeness, harbored a private fantasy that he too would ride past her gate, stumble from his horse, and be taken in to heal, thereby earning a regiment for Martin Lambert and winning the heart of Theo.

Colonel Lambert and his second daughter, Miss Hetty, stood on the opposite shore of this emotional river, laughing with a scornful disbelief. Match-making was the Colonel's trade; Mrs. Lambert was addicted to novels, weeping over them with such assiduity that her eyes seemed permanently swollen. No coach passed the gate without her expecting a husband to alight and ring the bell. Miss Hetty, however, wielded her tongue with a saucy frequency that bordered on the grotesque. She introduced Prussia and Persia into their conversation with an abominable pertness, asking if the current King of Prussia was called the Shaw or the Sophy, and calculating the distance from Ispahan to Saxony—a war her father was currently mapping. She produced the Persian Tales from her mother's closet and laid them on the parlor table where the family sat.

"I would not marry a Persian prince," Hetty declared, preferring a gentleman who possessed only one wife at a time. She dubbed the Virginian guest "Theo's prince" and asked her mother if she wished Hetty to claim the other visitor, the black prince, for herself. She rallied her sister and mother unceasingly on their sentimentalities, refusing to stop until she had provoked them into anger, at which point she would burst into tears, kiss them violently one after another, and coax them back into good humor.

Simple Harry Warrington knew nothing of these jokes, tears, quarrels, or hymeneal plans. He was the innocent occasion around which they spun, unaware that a hundred Parthian arrows were being shot at him. Did he hear the mention of Shaws or Sophys? Likely he thought a Sophy was a lady, and the notion that a man with a great black beard bore such a name would have seemed to him the height of absurdity.

We often drop like stones into a still pool, perfectly compact and cool, little realizing the flutter and excitement we create beneath the surface, disturbing the fish and frightening the ducks. How should Harry know the effect his sudden appearance produced in this quiet, sentimental family? He thought well enough of himself on many points but remained diffident regarding women, belonging to that age when young gentlemen require encouragement and introduction, raised in modest, primitive relations with the other sex. Thus, Hetty's jokes circled the lad like summer gnats; he minded them no more than insects buzzing near a wall. It was not stupidity, as Hetty believed, but simplicity—he was too occupied with his own honest affairs to consider others. Who among us does not remain ignorant, self-satisfied, and blind to the tragedies, comedies, and intrigues playing out in friends' drawing-rooms? While the sisters sat combing their flowing ringlets or talking in the great bed, according to the fashion of the day, how could Harry know he held such a large share in their thoughts?

Three days after his arrival, his new hosts walked with him through the fine park of my Lord Wrotham, wandering freely. On a sheet of water, they encountered swans, which the young ladies fed with bread. As the birds approached, Hetty cast a queer look at her mother and sister, then glanced at her father, standing nearby in a red waistcoat, honest and happy.

"Mamma's swans are something like these, papa," Hetty said.

"What swans, my dear?" the mother asked.

"Something like, but not quite. They have shorter necks than these, and are scores of them on our common," Hetty continued. "I saw Betty plucking one in the kitchen this morning. We shall have it for dinner, with apple-sauce and—"

"Don't be a little goose!" Miss Theo interrupted.

"And sage and onions. Do you love swan, Mr. Warrington?"

"I shot three last winter on our river," the Virginian replied. "Ours are not such white birds as these—they eat very well, though."

The simple youth had no idea that he himself was an allegory at that moment, and that Miss Hetty was narrating a fable concerning him. In some exceedingly obscure Latin work, I have read that long before Virginia was discovered, other peoples were equally dull of comprehension.

This was a premature sentiment on Miss Theo's part—that little tender flutter of the bosom she felt upon first beholding the Virginian, so handsome, pale, and bleeding. It was not the great passion she knew her heart could feel. Like the birds, it had awakened and begun to sing at a false dawn. *Hop back to thy perch, and cover thy head with thy wing, thou tremulous little fluttering creature! It is not yet light, and roosting is as yet better than singing.* Soon, anon, will come morning, and the whole sky will redden, and you shall soar up into it and salute the sun with your music.

One phrase, some thirty-three lines back, perhaps caught the eye of the fair and suspicious reader: "Three days after his arrival, Harry was walking with..." If he could walk—which he did perfectly well—what business had he to linger at Oakhurst instead of accompanying Lady Maria Esmond on the Pantiles at Tunbridge Wells? His shoulder was set; his health was entirely restored. He lacked even a change of coats, owing his raiment to the Colonel. Surely a young man in such a condition had no right to delay, bound by every tie of duty, convenience, love, and relationship, including a gentle heart waiting for him and, finally, a washerwoman, to depart for Tunbridge. Why did he stay, unless he loved one of the young ladies—and we assert he did not?

Could it be that he did not wish to go? Had the gracious reader understood the meaning of the mystic 'S' with which the last chapter commenced, depicting Sinbad the Sailor surmounted by that odious old man of the sea? What if Harry Warrington were that sailor, and his fate that choking, deadening, inevitable old man? What if, for two days past, he had felt those knees throttling him round the neck? If his fell aunt's purpose was answered, and if his late love was killed as dead by her poisonous communications as fair Rosamond was by her royal rival, then perhaps Hero was lighting the lamp and preparing supper while Leander sat comfortably with another party, never thinking of taking to the water. Since that coward's blow struck in Lady Maria's back by her own relative, surely kind hearts must pity her ladyship. I know she has faults—yes, and wears false hair—but a woman in distress commands pity. Are we to laugh at her because of her years? Between her old aunt and her unhappy delusion, Lady Maria Esmond was having no pleasant time at Tunbridge Wells. No one protected her; Madam Beatrix had her all to herself. Lady Maria was poor, hoping for money from her aunt, holding secrets the old woman knew and brandished over her. I am quite melted, growing soft-hearted as I think of her. Imagine her alone, a victim to that old woman! Paint to yourself that antique Andromeda, with her rich flowing head of hair falling over her shoulders, chained to a rock on Mount Ephraim and given up to that dragon of a Baroness! Succour, Perseus! Come quickly with thy winged feet and flashing falchion! Perseus is not in the least hurry. The dragon has her will of Andromeda for day after day.

Harry Warrington, who would not have allowed his dislocated and mended shoulder to keep him from hunting, remained day after day contentedly at Oakhurst, finding the kindly folks who welcomed him increasingly to his liking. Perhaps he had never, since his grandfather's death, been in such good company. His lot had lain amongst fox-hunting Virginian squires, with whose society he had put up contentedly, riding their horses, living their lives, and sharing their punch-bowls. The ladies of his own and mother's acquaintance were very well bred, decorous, and pious, no doubt, but somewhat narrow-minded. It was but a little place, his home, with its pompous ways, small etiquettes and punctilios, small flatteries, small conversations and scandals. Until he left, some time later, he did not know

The road from Oakhurst to Tunbridge was measured not in miles, but in the slow erosion of presence. Mrs. Lambert and her daughters stood beneath the limes, watching the figures shrink until the green canopy swallowed them whole. This was the familiar geometry of departure: husband to war, sons to school, the rhythmic subtraction of bodies from the domestic equation. The empty room retained the phantom weight of the sleeping boy; the hallway held the ghost of the whip; the dinner table remembered the cherry tart eaten in silence. Evening prayer fell flat without the scholar's voice; midnight brought a silence that felt heavier than noise. The limes served as a barrier between the world and the women left behind, a screen where Mrs. Lambert carved invisible names into the bark, seeking permanence in wood that offered none.

Colonel Lambert tipped his hat as the gentlemen passed the trees. "We are out of sight," he said, the gesture final. "My wife watches until we round this corner. Perhaps, Mr. Warrington, seeing the house again will bring better sport."

"I leave old friends," Warrington replied, the sentiment feeling heavy in the cool air.

"The host at Westerham has a son, James Wolfe," Lambert added, his tone shifting to the casual delivery of gossip. "An old friend of ours. My wife, a notorious matchmaker, would have paired him with one of my girls, but fate intervened."

"Ah!" Warrington sighed, the sound involuntary.

"Brave fellows existed before Agamemnon," Lambert mused.

"Were his name Aga...?" Warrington asked, meekly searching for the syllable.

"No, James Wolfe," Lambert corrected, smiling. "Scarce thirty. The youngest lieutenant-colonel in the army, barring the nobility who hold rank by birthright rather than merit."

His companion nodded with the automatic deference of a colonial aristocrat.

Lambert continued, describing a man who commanded captains older and more experienced than himself, yet possessed neither their seniority nor their luck. Wolfe was beloved, a scholar fluent in multiple tongues, a consummate soldier. Warrington listened, feeling the sharp edge of his own inadequacy. "I feel I have neglected my youth," he admitted. "George, my brother, would have represented our name better. He was a scholar, a musician. I am glad to be home, if only to learn how ignorant I am."

"If you know that well, 'tis a gain," Lambert said.

Warrington confessed that recently, bereft of his brother, he had believed himself a fine fellow, flattered by the locals who cared only for dogs, horses, and betting. Now, he wished to know more about books and less about them.

"Dogs and horses are books in their own right," Lambert countered. "Some men are not made for scholarship. Jack Hunt, riding behind, was a private soldier and is now a worthy groom. Does it matter if one holds a baton or a currycomb? When I began, I dreamed of success and honor. Now, I think of duty and the folks we left. Let us trot on; Westerham awaits."

At Westerham, they were greeted by a stately matron and her son, the young Lieutenant-Colonel of Kingsley's regiment. Wolfe was lean, pale, with high cheekbones and red hair, lacking conventional beauty but possessing a courtesy that disarmed observers. Supper revealed the mechanics of the matchmaking machine. The hostess whispered to Lambert about a connection to the Lowther family of the North.

"If she were the daughter of Charlemagne," Lambert laughed, "she is not too good for James Wolfe."

"She is the priceless pearl," the mother insisted, dismissing Wolfe's protestations of unworthiness. "We tend our children through fever and smallpox, break our hearts at parting, and then a girl arrives with bright eyes, and our boy leaves, caring nothing for us."

"How did you marry James's father?" Wolfe asked gently.

"Because he was gouty and needed care," the lady answered simply. "Not because I liked him."

The evening dissolved into easy talk, the friction of separation smoothed by the warmth of shared hospitality.

Next morning, Lambert handed Warrington over to Wolfe's charge. As Lambert rode home, Warrington's thoughts lingered on the parlour at Oakhurst, the three days of calm that now seemed distant. Wolfe, however, spoke of the Lambert household with reverence. "To have such a name, to live such a life," he said, "seems the height of human ambition."

"And glory and honour?" Warrington pressed. "Are those nothing?"

"They were my dreams once," Wolfe answered, his voice quieter now. "Now, my desires are tranquil. I have followed arms since fourteen, seen every duty, served in every garrison. I have done nearly everything a soldier does, except command an army. Now, I seek quiet, books, a wife to love me, children to dandle. True love is better than glory. A tranquil fireside, with the woman of your heart beside it—that is the greatest good the gods can send."

Warrington assented, but his face betrayed a lack of cheer. "You say 'yes' as if a fireside were not to your taste."

"There are other things a young fellow enjoys," Warrington replied. "After sixteen years of the world, I am still tied to my mother's apron-strings. After campaigns, after distinction, after making the world talk of me, then I may retire."

Wolfe responded with a rhapsody on marriage and the beauties of his mistress, a theme that resonated deeply with him but struck Warrington as melancholy. Their ride ended without incident, save for a moment of confusion near Tunbridge Wells where Warrington drew his pistol at innkeepers' agents, mistaking them for highwaymen. Wolfe laughed, calming him, as Gumbo's runaway horse was eventually tamed.

They parted ways at the inn, each heading to the ladies they sought.

Warrington found his aunt in handsome lodgings, attended by a retinue of lacqueys. Her cousin, Lady Maria, was absent. Instead, he encountered a beau in a rich suit and two dowagers with voluminous hoops. The Baroness introduced him as the young Croesus from Virginia, an estate larger than Kent, infinitely fruitful, populated by armies of negroes and gold mines. She spoke of his mother as a sovereign princess, a tale spun with such confidence that the modest youth blushed under the weight of invented grandeur.

"You could not have come at a better moment," the Baroness said as the visitors departed. "They will trumpet your virtues in every company. I have lied about the geography of Virginia to fill out your estate with diamonds and spices. Your income is a mystery, but undoubtedly vast. Prepare to be flattered as you never have been."

"To what end?" Warrington asked, skeptical.

"In the first place, do not contradict your aunt," she replied firmly. "As for your reputation, a London newspaper has already claimed you are immensely wealthy. You cannot help yourself. Tomorrow, the congregation will turn from their prayers to worship the golden calf in your person. Would you have me undeceive them and speak ill of my own flesh and blood?"

The air inside Madame de Bernstein's house was thick with the scent of melting tallow and the visual noise of a hundred years of accumulated clutter. Cousin Maria arrived, flanked by gardener's boys carrying baskets of flowers meant to mask the decay of the drawing-room. Three footmen, gorgeously laced with worsted, arranged card tables that seemed to multiply under their hands. A major-domo, dressed in black with fine ruffles and a bag slung over his shoulder, followed the lacquers bearing fasces of wax candles. He placed pairs of candles on the tables and in the silver sconces along the wainscoting, fighting a losing battle against the slanting rays of the sun that gilded the walls and the view of the green common beyond. Outside, groups of figures in hoops and powder sauntered, dappling the plain with shadows. Across the way lay the Pantiles, a perpetual fair where the clatter and buzz of society drowned out everything else. A band played for the visitors to the Wells.

This was a place designed not for a recluse, but for those who preferred bustle and a bright, chaotic light. Yet even here, the darkness of the era pressed in. There was not one wax candle for ten as there would be today; gas and electric illumination were fantasies. Horrible guttering tallow smoked in the passages. The candle-snuffer was a notorious officer in the theatre. In Hogarth's pictures, feasts were begrimed with grease. Even in Lord Viscount Squanderfield's grand saloons, where parties ended with yawning couples staring at horrified stewards, there were but eight candles—one per table, half a dozen in a brass chandelier. If Jack Briefless had convoked friends to oysters and beer in Pump Court, he would have needed twice as many lights. It was a relief to think that Louis Quatorze held his revels in the dark, and to bless Mr. Price and other Luciferous benefactors for eventually banishing the abominable mutton of youth.

Maria, the fairest flower among them, planted her roses and sweet-williams in various vases, adorning the apartment to the best of her art. She lingered fondly over bowls and dragon jars, casting sly, timid glances at young cousin Harry. Her blush would have become any young woman, suggesting she might intend to outstay her aunt. But the Baroness, seated in her armchair with a crooked tortoiseshell stick, pointed imperiously to the servants. She rated Tom for a darn in his stocking and John for greasing his locks too profusely from the candle-box, maintaining a stern domination over the household. This was the life of Jeames a hundred years prior: sleeping two to a bed, four to a room, likely in a cellar, washing in a trough seen nowhere in London outside the barracks of Her Majesty's Foot Guards.

If Maria hoped for a present interview, her heart was disappointed. "Where are you going to dine, Harry?" Madame de Bernstein asked. "My niece Maria and I shall have a chicken in the little parlour—I think you should go to the best ordinary. There is one at the White Horse at three; we shall hear his bell in a minute or two. Understand, sir, you ought not to spare expense. Behave like Princess Pocahontas's son. Your trunks have been taken to the lodging I engaged for you. It is not good for a lad to always hang about the aprons of two old women. Is it, Maria?"

"No," said her ladyship, dropping her meek eyes, while the other lady glared in triumph. Andromeda had been exposed to the Dragon for five or six days; if Perseus had cut the latter's head off, it would have been monstrous murder. But he bore no sword or shield; he only looked mechanically at the lacquers in tawny and blue as they creaked about the room.

"There are good mercers and tailors from London always here to wait on the company," the lady continued. "You had better see them, my dear, for your suit is not of the very last fashion—a little lace—"

"I can't go out of mourning, ma'am," said the young man, looking down at his sables.

"Ho, sir!" cried the lady, rustling up from her chair and rising on her cane. "Wear black for your brother till you are as old as Methuselah, if you like. I am sure I don't want to prevent you. I only want you to dress, and to do like other people, and make a figure worthy of your name."

"Madam," said Mr. Warrington with great state, "I have not done anything to disgrace it that I know."

Why did the old woman stop and give a little start as if struck? Let bygones be bygones. She and the boy had a score of little passages where swords were crossed and thrusts rapidly dealt or parried. She liked Harry none the worse for his courage in facing her. "Sure a little finer linen than that shirt you wear will not be a disgrace to you, sir," she said, with a forced laugh.

Harry bowed and blushed. It was one of the homely gifts of his Oakhurst friends. He felt pleased to think he wore it; thought of the new friends, so good, so pure, so simple, so kindly, with immense tenderness. While invested in this garment, he felt as if evil could not touch him. He said he would go to his lodging and return arrayed in the best linen he had.

"Come back here, sir," said Madame Bernstein. "And if our company has not arrived, Maria and I will find some ruffles for you!" Under a footman's guidance, the young fellow walked off to his new lodgings.

Harry found not only handsome and spacious apartments but a groom waiting to be engaged and a second valet, should he wish to hire one to wait upon Mr. Gumbo. Ere many minutes had passed, emissaries from a London tailor and bootmaker waited with cards and compliments from Messrs. Regnier and Tull. The best articles in his modest wardrobe were laid out by Gumbo, alongside the finest linen his thrifty Virginian mother had provided. Visions of the snow-surrounded home in his own country rose before him—the crackling logs, the trim quiet ladies working by the fire. For the first time, a thought crossed his mind that the homely clothes were not quite smart enough, the home-worked linen not so fine as it might be. That he should be ashamed of anything belonging to him or to Castlewood! That was strange. The simple folks there were only too well satisfied with all things done, said, or produced at Castlewood. Madam Esmond, when sending her son forth on his travels, thought no young nobleman need be better provided. The clothes might have fitted better and been of a later fashion, to be sure—but still, the young fellow presented a comely figure when he issued from his apartments, his toilet over. Gumbo called a chair, marched beside it, and they proceeded to the ordinary where the young gentleman was to dine.

Here he expected to find the beau he had met a few hours earlier at his aunt's lodging, the man who had indicated the White Horse was the most modish place. He mentioned the friend's name to the host. But the landlord and waiters, leading him into the room with many smiles and bows, assured his honour that he needed no other introduction than his own. They helped him hang up his coat and sword, asked if he would drink Burgundy, Pontac, or champagne, and led him to a table.

Though the most fashionable ordinary in the village, the White Horse was not crowded. Monsieur Barbeau, the landlord, informed Harry that a great entertainment at Summer Hill had taken away most of the company. Indeed, when Harry entered, there were but four other gentlemen. Two were drinking wine, having finished their dinner; the other two were young men in the midst of their meal. As the landlord passed, he must have whispered the name of the newcomer to them, for they looked at him with some appearance of interest and made a slight bow across the table as the smiling host bustled away for Harry's dinner.

Mr. Warrington returned the salute of the two gentlemen, who bade him welcome to Tunbridge and hoped he would like the place upon better acquaintance. Then they smiled and exchanged waggish looks, of which Harry did not understand the meaning, nor why they cast knowing glances at the two other guests over their wine.

One person was in a somewhat tarnished velvet coat with a huge queue and bag, voluminous ruffles, and embroidery. The other was a little beetle-browed, hook-nosed, high-shouldered gentleman, whom his opposite companion addressed as *milord*, or *my lord*, in a very high voice. My lord, sipping his wine, barely glanced at the newcomer before addressing his companion.

"And so you know the nephew of the old woman—the Croesus who comes to arrive?"

"You're thrown out there, Jack!" said one young gentleman to the other.

"Never could manage the lingo," said Jack. The two elders had begun to speak in French.

"But assuredly, my dear lord!" said the gentleman with the long queue.

"You have shown energy, my dear Baron! He has been here but two hours. My people told me of him only as I came to dinner."

"I knew him before! I have met him often in London with the Baroness and my lord, his cousin," said the Baron.

A smoking soup arrived for Harry, borne by the smiling host.

The air inside the White Horse Tavern tasted of stale punch and unwashed linen. Beyond the eating-room windows lay a bowling-green, a patch of manicured earth where guests might sip tea or watch the world pass. Dinner concluded simultaneously for the three gentlemen, and Mr. Morris proposed they move to the Green for a bottle.

"My Lord Chesterfield," said my Lord March, "would adjourn to the Dust Hole under the pretense of thirst."

"Each gentleman has his own method of descending to the deuce," Jack Morris replied, accepting the logic. "My weakness is simply a bottle."

"My Lord Chesterfield's deuce is deuce-ace," corrected my Lord March. "He cannot keep away from cards or dice."

"My Lord March possesses not one devil, but several," Jack countered, listing them with rhythmic precision: gambling, racing, betting, drinking, eating, money, women. "You have fallen into bad company, Mr. Warrington, when you found him. He will play you for every acre in Virginia."

"With the greatest pleasure in life!" my Lord March interjected.

"And for all your tobacco, your slaves, your oxen, and your asses."

"Shall we begin?" my Lord March asked, turning to Jack. "You are never without a dice-box or a bottle-screw. I will set Mr. Warrington for whatever he desires."

"Unfortunately," Mr. Warrington noted, "the tobacco, slaves, and beasts are not yet mine. I am just of age, and my mother, scarcely twenty years older, has as good a chance of longevity as I do."

"I will bet you that you survive her," my Lord March offered. "I will pay a sum now against four times that sum at her death. Set a fair price over this table against the reversion of your estate in Virginia upon her departure. What is your place called?"

"Castlewood."

"A principality, I hear," my Lord March mused. "Its value has surely been exaggerated tenfold among the gossip-mongers here. How did you acquire the name? Are you related to my lord? Ah, yes—I recall. Your mother descends from the head of the house. He took the losing side in '15. I have heard this story a dozen times from my old Duchess. She knew your grandfather. He was a friend of Addison, Steele, Pope, and Milton, I dare say. It is a pity he did not remain at home and transplant the other branch of the family to the plantations."

"I have just stayed at Castlewood with my cousin," Mr. Warrington admitted.

"Hm! Did you play with him? He is fond of pasteboard and bones."

"Never, except for sixpences and a pool of commerce with the ladies."

"So much the better for both of you. But did you play with Will Esmond if he was present? I will lay ten to one you did."

Harry blushed, admitting that evenings with his cousin involved a few games of cards.

"And Tom Sampson, the chaplain?" Jack Morris cried. "Was he of the party? I wager Tom made a third, and the Lord deliver you from Tom and Will Esmond together!"

"Nay," Mr. Warrington said. "The truth is, I won of both of them."

"And they paid you? Miracles will never cease!"

"I did not speak of miracles," Harry smiled over his wine.

"You do not tell tales out of school—the *volto sciolto*—hey, Mr. Warrington?" my Lord March teased.

"I beg your pardon," Harry replied. "French is the only language besides my own of which I possess a slight knowledge."

"My Lord March has learned Italian at the Opera, and a pretty penny his lessons have cost," Jack Morris observed. "We must show him the Opera. Mustn't we, March?"

"Must we, Morris?" my Lord March asked, his tone suggesting he barely tolerated the familiarity.

Both gentlemen wore identical attire: small scratch-wigs without powder, blue frocks with plate buttons, buckskins, riding-boots, and little hats adorned with a narrow cord of lace. No outward marks of fashion distinguished them.

"I care little for the Opera," Harry said, warming to the Burgundy. "But I wish to go to Newmarket and see a good English hunting-field."

"We will show you Newmarket and the hunt," my Lord March promised. "Can you ride well?"

"I think so," Harry answered. "And I can shoot, and jump."

"What is your weight? I bet we weigh even, or I weigh more. I bet Jack Morris beats you at birds or a mark at fifty paces. I bet I jump farther than you on flat ground, right here on this green."

"I do not know Mr. Morris's shooting. I have never seen either gentleman before. But I take your bets, my lord, at whatever you please," Harry cried, flushed with wine. By now, he was more than warm.

"Ponies on each!" my Lord March shouted.

"Done and done!" my Lord March and Harry exclaimed in unison. The young man felt it was an honor for his country not to be ashamed of any wager made to him.

"We can try the final bet now, if your feet are steady," my Lord March said, springing up. He stretched his arms and limbs, examining the crisp, dry grass. He removed his boots, then his coat and waistcoat, buckling his belt tight around his waist before casting his clothes onto the ground.

Harry treated his garments with greater reverence. It was his finest suit. He removed the velvet coat and waistcoat, folding them daintily. Seeing the nearby tables slopped with drink, he carried them to a table in the eating-room, where the windows stood open.

Here, a new guest had entered. This was no other than Mr. Wolfe, eating a chicken and salad with a modest pint of wine. Harry's spirits soared. He told the Colonel he had a wager with my Lord March and asked if the Colonel would stand him halves. The Colonel claimed poverty prevented betting, but offered to witness fair play. With all his heart, he agreed. Colonel Wolfe set down his glass and stalked through the open window after his young friend.

"Who is that tallow-faced Put with carroty hair?" Jack Morris asked, the Burgundy having taken its toll.

Mr. Warrington explained this was Lieutenant-Colonel Wolfe of the 20th Regiment.

"Your humble servant, gentlemen!" the Colonel bowed, a rigid military motion.

"Never saw such a figure in my life!" Jack Morris cried. "Did you, March?"

"I beg your pardon, I think you said March?" the Colonel asked, looking genuinely surprised.

"I am the Earl of March, sir, at Colonel Wolfe's service," the nobleman bowed. "My friend, Mr. Morris, is so intimate with me that, after dinner, we are quite like brothers."

Why is not all of Tunbridge Wells here to hear this? Morris thought. Delighted, he shouted, "Two to one on my lord!"

"Done!" called Mr. Warrington. The enthusiastic Jack was obliged to cry "Done!" as well.

"Take him, Colonel," Harry whispered to his friend.

But the Colonel said he could not afford to lose, and therefore could not hope to win.

"I see you have won one of our wagers already, Mr. Warrington," my Lord March remarked. "I am taller than you by an inch or two, but you are broader round the shoulders."

"Pooh, my dear Will! I bet you you weigh twice as much as he does!" Jack Morris cried.

"Done, Jack!" my Lord March laughed. "The bets are all ponies. Will you take him, Mr. Warrington?"

"No, my dear fellow—one's enough," Jack said.

"Very good, my dear fellow," my Lord March replied. "Now we will settle the other wager."

Already arrayed in his best silk stockings, black satin-net breeches, and neatest pumps, Harry had no desire to remove his shoes, unlike his antagonist, whose heavy riding-boots and spurs were ill-suited for leaping. Before them lay a fine, even green turf of some thirty yards, sufficient for a run and a jump. A gravel walk circled this green, beyond which stood a wall and gate-sign: a field azure bearing the Hanoverian White Horse rampant between two skittles proper, with the motto naming the landlord and the animal.

My Lord March's friend placed a handkerchief on the ground as the mark from which the leapers would start, while Mr. Wolfe stood at the far end of the grass-plat to note where each landed.

"My lord went first," Mr. Warrington wrote later in a letter to Mrs. Mountain at Castlewood, Virginia, a document still extant. "He urged I take the lead; but, remembering the story of the Battle of Fontenoy my dearest George used to tell, I said, 'Monseigneur le Comte, tirez le premier, s'il vous plaît.' So he took his run in his stocking feet, and for the honor of Old Virginia, I had the gratification of beating his lordship by more than two feet—viz., two feet nine inches. I jumped twenty-one feet three inches, by the drawer's measured tape, and his lordship only eighteen-six. I had won from him about my weight before (which I knew the moment I set my eye upon him). So he and Mr. Jack paid me these two w

The pavement of the common was hard, worn smooth by the tread of countless generations, yet beneath the soles of Harry Warrington's boots, it offered no cushion against the weight of his own momentum. Maria walked beside him, her pace measured, her eyes scanning the horizon for the inevitable decay of the evening.

"Let me hear about those children," she said, her voice carrying the flat, practiced tone of someone reciting a script written by necessity. "Those poor souls running about the house where you were taken in. Oh, Harry, that fall! I thought I should have died watching it. You needn't squeeze my arm so tightly. Do you not care for me?"

Harry pulled his hand free, the motion sharp and dismissive. "The people are the very best, kindest, dearest people I have ever met in the world," he declared, his voice rising to match the cadence of a victory speech. "Mrs. Lambert was a friend of my mother's. Colonel Lambert served in Scotland, in Flanders, at Minorca. No natural parents could have been kinder. How can I repay them? I must give them a present. A token of gratitude." He reached into his pocket, fingers closing around the crisp, metallic weight of the spoils from Morris and March.

"We can go to the toy-shop," Maria suggested, turning her head slightly. "Buy dolls for the children. You would insult them by offering payment for their kindness."

"Dolls for Hester and Theo!" Harry exclaimed, a note of genuine surprise coloring his tone. "Why, do you think a woman ceases to be a woman until she is forty, Maria?" Under his arm, Maria stiffened, a microscopic contraction of muscle betraying the friction between his exuberance and her reality. "Miss Hester does not consider herself a child. Miss Theo is older than her sister. They speak multiple languages. They have consumed mountains of books. They play the harpsichord and sing admirably; Theo composes her own songs."

"I scarcely saw them," Maria replied, her voice dropping. "I thought they were children. They looked quite childish. I had no idea they possessed such perfection, such wonders of the world."

"That is precisely the error of your sex," Harry countered, striding forward with a confidence that bordered on arrogance. "At home, if George or I praise a woman, Mrs. Esmond and Mountain will inevitably find fault with her."

"I would find fault with no one who is kind to you, Mr. Warrington," Maria sighed, falling into step behind him. "Though you are not angry with me for envying them? For having to nurse you when you were wounded and ill, whilst I... whilst I had to leave you?"

"You dear, good Maria!"

"No, Harry! I am not dear and good. There, sir, cease your pressuring attentions." She pointed across the street. "Look! Your black man walks with a dozen others in livery. The horrid creatures are heading to the tea-garden to fuddle, to get tipsy like their masters. That dreadful Mr. Morris was perfectly drunk when I found you, frightening you half to death."

"I had just won great bets from both of them," Harry announced, his spirit buoyant, fueled by the adrenaline of the gamble. "What shall I buy for you, my dear cousin?" He recounted the triumphs of the evening, laughing, braggadocio spilling from his lips. "For the honor of Virginia, I was determined to show them what jumping was. With practice, I believe I could leap two feet farther."

Maria watched him, a faint smile touching her lips despite her weariness. "But you must beware of play, child. It has been the ruin of our family. My brother Castlewood, Will, our poor father, our aunt Lady Castlewood—they were all victims. And my Lord March? He is the most dreadful gambler and the most successful of all the nobility."

"I do not intend to fear him, nor his friend Mr. Jack Morris," Harry said, fingering the notes in his pocket again. "What do you play at Aunt Bernstein's? Cribbage, all-fours, brag, whist, commerce, piquet, quadrille? I am ready for any of them. What clock is that striking? Seven!"

"And you wish to begin now," Maria said, her voice plaintive. "You do not care to walk with your poor cousin. Not long ago, you did."

"Hey! Youth is youth, cousin!" Harry cried, tossing his head back. "And a young fellow must have his fling!" He strutted alongside her, confident, happy, eager for the next sensation. Yesterday he had wanted to be near Theo and Hester, near Mrs. Lambert. But pleasure, life, gaiety, the desire to shine and conquer, held their own seductive pull. The lad seized the cup like any other, refusing to calculate the headache awaiting him in the morning.

While they talked, the fiddles from the open orchestra on the Parade emitted a great tuning and squeaking, preparing for the evening concert. Maria knew her aunt was already awake, already trapped in her cycle. She must return to her slavery. Harry never asked about that slavery, though he must have known it, had he taken the trouble to think. He never pitied his cousin. He was not thinking of her at all. Yet when his misfortune eventually struck, she would be wounded far more cruelly than he. She had scarce ever been out of his thoughts, a fact she buried under smiling hypocrisies, as was the custom of her sex. Perhaps Mrs. Grundy thought her an old fool? Did fools' caps not cover grey hair as well as jet or auburn? Bear gently with our elderly *fredaines*, O Minerva of a woman! Or perhaps you are so wise you do not read novels. I know there are late crops of wild oats, as well as early harvests; and from observation of self and neighbor, I suspect the *avena fatua* grows right to the last day of the year.

Madame Bernstein and her attendants, like worldly parents eager to discard a troublesome child, had put the sun to bed while it was still light. They drew the curtains shut and busied themselves with cards, candles, tea, and negus. One chair after another deposited ladies at the Baroness's door—some painted, some patched, some brocaded. Gentlemen in gala raiment followed. Mr. Poellnitz's star was the largest, his coat the most embroidered.

My Lord March and Ruglen appeared transformed from the ragged figure Harry had met at the White Horse. The tight brown scratch was gone, replaced by a neatly curled feather top, powdered grey, and a rich French suit. Mr. Jack Morris wore a similar wig and a suit of stuff mimicking his lordship's elegance. Mr. Wolfe attended his beautiful mistress, Miss Lowther, and her aunt, who loved cards as the world did.

When Lady Maria Esmond entered, her appearance contradicted Madame Bernstein's earlier accounts. Her shape was fine, her dress revealing much of it. Her complexion was naturally exceeding fair, enhanced by a dark frilled ribbon clasped with a jewel around her neck, accentuating its snowy whiteness. Her cheeks were not redder than other ladies', the roses openly purchased from perfumery shops. An artful patch or two, it was rumored, added to the lustre of her charms. Her hoops were not larger than the iron contrivances of the present day; the costume, absurd in points, was pleasing altogether. Suppose our ladies adopted bangles and nose-rings? We might laugh at the ornaments, yet not dislike them, and lovers would make no difficulty lifting the ring to approach the rosy lips underneath.

As for the Baroness de Bernstein, when she took the pains of a grand toilette, she appeared handsome still, magnificent, yet melancholy, even terrifying. One reads the past in some old faces; others lapse into mere meekness. The fires go out of some eyes, as crow's-feet pucker round them; they flash no longer with scorn, anger, or love. They gaze, and no one is melted by their sapphire glances; they look, and no one is dazzled.

If you are not as perfect a beauty as the peerless Lindamira, Queen of the Ball, and if, at the end of the night, you retire to bed owning that you had but two or three partners while Lindamira had a crowd—console yourself. Think that at fifty, you will look as kind and pleasant as you appear now at eighteen. You will not have to lay down your coach-and-six of beauty and see another step into it, walking yourself through the rest of life. You will forgo no long-accustomed homage; you will not witness the depreciation of your smiles. Fashion will not forsake your quarter. You will not remain all dust, gloom, and cobwebs within your once splendid saloons, with placards in your sad windows, gaunt, lonely, and to let! You may not have known grandeur, but you will not feel desertion. You will not have enjoyed millions, but you will have escaped bankruptcy.

"Our hostess," whispered My Lord Chesterfield to his friend, unaware of the loudness of his voice, "reminds me of Covent Garden in my youth. Then it was the court end of the town, inhabited by the highest fashion. Now, a nobleman's house is a gaming-house, or you may go in with a friend and call for a bottle."

"Hey! A bottle and a tavern are good things in their way," said My Lord March,

The water at Tunbridge Wells tasted of iron and gossip. Within two days, Harry Warrington ceased to be a visitor and became a fixture, a local deity carved from colonial timber and imported currency. The Bishop of Salisbury commanded deference, yet Harry drew stares that lingered longer, eyes tracking his movement through the market square as if he were a specimen in a glass case. Matrons at the drawing-room tables maneuvered their daughters into his orbit, offering smiles that were less affectionate than strategic. Every soul in the place possessed a ledger of his affairs: the acreage of his estate, the yield of his income, the precise sum he had wagered and lost—or won—at every tea table. Even his servant, Gumbo, was elevated to a position of influence, fed ale and cold meat by strangers eager to purchase news of their young master. Gumbo fattened on the attention, becoming a pillar of the valetocracy, spinning tales so grandiose they threatened to swallow the truth whole.

No gathering was considered complete without Mr. Warrington. The lad watched this ascent with a mixture of amusement and astonishment, accepting his honors with a stoicism bred from a lifetime of self-regard. He did not boast; he simply existed as a winner. His code was peculiar, a rigid adherence to a personal honor that demanded attendance whenever summoned, whether for a race, a drinking bout, or a card game played in the name of Old Virginia. And the locals, hungry for entertainment, were eager to test him. They found him formidable: a strong head, a skilled hand, a steady seat, nerves that did not flinch. The representative of the colonies emerged victorious in his friendly rivalry with the mother country.

Madame de Bernstein, who had exhausted her deck of cards nightly to repair previous losses, found fresh delight in her nephew's triumphs. He had bested Jack Morris at shooting, outpaced Mr. Scamper on horseback, and held his own against Captain Batts at tennis despite having never touched a racket before. He faced the celebrated gamblers, the Lords of Chesterfield and March, and earned their testimony to his coolness and breeding. At the books, he lacked brilliance, yet his handwriting was competent enough to pass among men of fashion, and the naive simplicity of his ignorance provided the old lady with endless amusement. She preferred the world to the library, trading novels for games, and she supervised his transformation with maternal fervor, selecting fine laces for his ruffles and procuring a diamond brooch for his collar. He became the favorite of all her kin. Lady Maria, too, seemed pleased by his success, though her brothers must have trembled with envy as they witnessed their uncle's rapid climb in the affections of the wealthy aunt.

Two weeks in Tunbridge had transformed Harry into a personage. He knew the good company, and inevitably, the bad. Was it his fault that he accepted the world as it presented itself, drinking from the cup of sparkling pleasure filled to the brim? The aunt enjoyed his triumphs, urging him to pursue his enjoyments without moral reservation. She was not a rigorous preceptress. Had the Cattarina sent billets-doux, the aunt might have encouraged acceptance, but Harry carried a stock of modesty from his colonial home, worn alongside his honest homespun linen. Libertinism was unknown in the rough towns of America where he came from; the vices of great cities were foreign concepts. Harry blushed at the daring talk of his new European associates. Even the worldly jokes of Aunt Bernstein astounded him, reducing the old woman to calling him "Joseph" or "simpleton."

Yet innocence offered no shield against the world's judgment. The world credited him with the sins of others. How could he know that associating with the saucy Cattarina was forbidden? He had seen Lord March drive her in his phaeton. To Harry, giving her his arm on the public walks seemed harmless. When she desired a trinket from the toy shop, he bought it—a locket she coveted. The next day, lace. The next day, something else. Her fancies were endless, until Harry stopped, deflecting her requests with laughter. He was shrewd, generous, but not reckless; he had no intention of buying diamond drops for her ears.

Who, then, was to credit his modesty? Old Bernstein insisted her nephew was playing the role of Don Juan, supplanting Lord March. She pressed the point harder when Maria was present, deriving a dark pleasure from stabbing the spinster's tender heart, reveling in her niece's piteous silence.

"Why, my dear," the Baroness declared, "boys will be boys, and I don't want Harry to be the first milksop in his family!" The bread Maria ate at her aunt's expense often choked her. Some women possess a cruel alchemy, turning sustenance into poison.

Colonel Wolfe arrived frequently from Westerham to court the lady of his love. Knowing the Colonel was entirely occupied, Harry expected little interaction, though he valued the officer's conversation. It differed sharply from the ribald chatter surrounding Harry. Wolfe spoke of no cards, no horse pedigrees, no boasts of hunting feats or favors won from women, no retailing of the era's innumerable scandals. It was not a good time. The old world was more dissolute than theirs, populated by a king with mistresses openly in his train, honored by the great. There was a nobility mad with reckless pleasure, a looseness of word and act that honest historians must note without detailing, lest they shock the virtuous. Harry had lighted upon some of the wildest of these people and found an old relative living in the very midst of the rout.

Harry failed to notice how Colonel Wolfe avoided him, or how the Colonel's demeanor grew cold and altered during their casual meetings. He did not know the stories circulating about him. Who tells them? Who are the fathers of those wondrous lies? Poor Harry remained unaware of the reputation he was cultivating. While he rode, played, and frolicked, he was passing among respectable persons as the most abandoned, profligate, and godless of young men.

Alas, to think that the gentle, quiet lad they liked so well—the simple soul easily pleased—should be a hardened profligate, a spendthrift, a confirmed gamester, a frequenter of abandoned women! These stories reached honest Colonel Lambert at Oakhurst. First one tale, then another, then a flood of them, filling the good man's heart with grief and care until his family sensed the disturbance. Initially, Lambert refused to speak, setting aside his wife's fond inquiries. Mrs. Lambert imagined a great misfortune: ruin, danger, illness, death. Who can resist an anxious woman, or escape the cross-examination of the conjugal pillow? Lambert was forced to reveal a portion of what he knew. The wife was grieved and amazed. From the bedroom, the grief, stifled momentarily under pillows, descended the stairs. Theo and Hester inherited the complaint, suffering terribly. O, kind, little, wounded hearts! Hester initially turned red, flew into a passion, clenched her fists, and vowed disbelief, but eventually succumbed. Scandal masters people, especially the good and innocent. Oh, the serpent they had nursed by their fire! Oh, the wretched boy! To imagine him walking with that horrible painted Frenchwoman, giving her diamond necklaces, parading his shame before the society at the Wells! After the three ladies wept and the father was deeply moved, they confided in the parson. In vain he preached his favorite sermon on scandal next Sunday, inveighing against humanity's propensity to think evil. We repent, we promise to do so no more; yet when the next bad story concerns our neighbor, we believe it. So did the kind, wretched folk of Oakhurst believe the rumors about poor Harry Warrington.

Meanwhile, Harry was too pleased with himself to perceive how ill his friends thought of him. He pursued an idle, pleasant, if unprofitable life, oblivious to the hubbub he created and the dreadful repute he held. Emerging from a tennis match with Captain Batts, satisfied with his play and the world's approval, Harry overtook Colonel Wolfe, who had just visited the lady of his heart. Harry extended his hand; the Colonel took it, but the salute was so cold that the young man could not help noticing it, particularly how Wolfe, returning a fine bow from Captain Batts's hat, barely touched his own with his forefinger. The tennis captain walked away disconcerted, Harry remaining to speak with his friend from Westerham. Wolfe walked beside him for a moment, erect, silent, and icy.

"I have not seen you these many days," Harry said.

"You have had other companions," Wolfe replied curtly.

"But I had rather be with you than any of them," Harry cried.

"Indeed, I might be better company for you than some of them," the other said.

"Is it Captain Batts you mean?" Harry asked.

"He is no favorite of mine," Wolfe admitted grimly. "He bore a rascally reputation in the army, and I doubt he has mended it since being turned out. You certainly might find a better friend than Captain Batts. Pardon the freedom."

"Friend! He is no friend; he only teaches me to play tennis. He is hand-in-glove with my lord and all the fashionable people here who play."

"I am not a man of fashion," Wolfe stated.

"My dear Colonel, what is the matter? Have I angered you? You speak as if I had, and I am not conscious of having done anything to forfeit your regard."

"I will be free with you, Mr. Warr

The air in the spa town was thick with the scent of mineral water and stagnation. Madame de Bernstein remained, content enough to scold her niece and play her rubber, while Harry drifted through the assembly rooms and card tables. At twenty, nearly every place offers a semblance of life: lively company, fresh air, the promise of sport. Youth approaches pleasure with alacrity, counting the days until arrival; later life regards it with a gentle, cooling indifference. Do you not recall your first ball? Does your memory not wander to that happy past as you sit ornamenting the wall while your daughters dance? I remember thinking it delightful to walk three miles to dine with old Captain Jones. To imagine walking three miles now, to drink his half-pay port bought from a country merchant for a small sum—it lacks the flavor youth once bestowed. *Viximus nuper.*

I judge Harry's idleness less severely than his friend, the stern Colonel of the Twentieth Regiment. Blessed idleness! Divine lazy nymph! Reach me a novel as I lie in my dressing gown at three in the afternoon; compound a sherry-cobbler; bring me a cigar. Dear slatternly, smiling enchantress! They may assail thee with bad names, swear thy character away, calling thee the Mother of Evil; yet thou art the best company in the world.

Lord March departed for the North; Lord Chesterfield, finding the Tunbridge waters useless for his deafness, returned to his solitude at Blackheath. Other gentlemen remained to sport. Mr. Warrington had quite enough of companions at his ordinary at the White Horse. He soon learned to order a French dinner as well as the best man of fashion from St. James's; he spoke to Monsieur Barbeau in his native tongue more fluently than most; he discovered an elegant taste in wines, distinguishing Clos Vougeot from Romande with remarkable skill. He became the young King of the Wells, surrounded by easygoing men of the world who were not shocked by his reputation for gallantry and extravagance—a reputation that had frightened Mr. Wolfe.

Though our Virginian lived amongst the revellers, swimming and sporting in the same waters as the loose fish, the boy possessed a natural shrewdness and honesty that kept him clear of the snares set for the unwary. He made few foolish bets with the jolly idle fellows around him; the oldest hands found it difficult to take him in. He engaged in games indoors and out because he possessed a natural skill and aptitude, holding matches with almost any fair competitor. He was scrupulous to play only with gentlemen he knew and always settled his own debts on the spot. He would have made a poor figure at a college examination, yet he possessed prudence, fidelity, keen perception, great generosity, and dauntless personal courage. Occasions arose to show what stuff he was made of.
