When the unhappy little Cattarina, who had brought him so much trouble, carried her importunities beyond the mark where Harry thought his generosity should stop, he withdrew from the advances of the Opera-House Siren with perfect coolness and skill, leaving her to exercise her blandishments upon some more easy victim. In vain did the mermaid's hysterical mother wait upon Harry, vowing that a cruel bailiff had seized all her daughter's goods for debt and that her venerable father languished in a London gaol. Harry declared that between himself and the bailiff there could be no dealings. Because he had the good fortune to become known to Mademoiselle Cattarina and to gratify her caprices by presenting her with various trinkets, he was not bound to pay the past debts of her family. He must decline being bail for her papa in London or settling her outstanding accounts at Tunbridge.

The Cattarina's mother first called him a monster and an ingrate, then asked, with a veteran smirk, why he did not take pay for the services he had rendered to the young person. At first, Mr. Warrington could not understand the nature of the payment. When the old woman explained, the honest lad rose in horror, thinking that a woman should traffic in her child's dishonor. He told her he came from a country where even the savages would recoil from such a bargain. Having bowed the old lady ceremoniously to the door, he ordered Gumbo to mark her well and never admit her to his lodgings again. No doubt she retired breathing vengeance against the Iroquois; no Turk or Persian, she declared, would treat a lady so. She and her daughter retreated to London as soon as their anxious landlord would let them.

Then Harry faced his perils of gaming, as well as his perils of gallantry. A man who plays at bowls must expect to meet with rubbers. After dinner at the ordinary, having declined to play piquet any further with Captain Batts, and being roughly asked his reason for refusing, Harry fairly told the Captain that he only played with gentlemen who paid, like himself. Yet he expressed himself so ready to satisfy Mr. Batts as soon as their outstanding little account was settled that the Captain declared himself satisfied *d'avance*, straightway left the Wells without paying Harry or any other creditor.

He also had an occasion to show his spirit by beating a chairman who was rude to old Miss Whiffler one evening as she went to the assembly. Finding that the calumny regarding himself and that unlucky opera-dancer was repeated by Mr. Hector Buckler, one of the fiercest frequenters of the Wells, Mr. Warrington stepped up to Mr. Buckler in the pump-room, where the latter was regaling a number of water-drinkers with the very calumny. He publicly informed Mr. Buckler that the story was a falsehood and that he should hold any person accountable to himself who henceforth uttered it. So, though our friend, being at Rome, certainly did as Rome did, he showed himself to be a valorous and worthy Roman. Hurling with the wolves, he was acknowledged by Mr. Wolfe himself to be as brave as the best of them.

If that officer had told Colonel Lambert the stories which had given the latter so much pain, we may be sure that when Mr. Wolfe found his young friend was innocent, he took the first opportunity to withdraw the odious charges against him. Joy reigned among the Lamberts, in consequence of the lad's acquittal—something, doubtless, of that pleasure felt by higher natures than ours at the recovery of sinners. Never had the little family been so happy—not even when they got the news of Brother Tom winning his scholarship—as when Colonel Wolfe rode over with the account of the conversation he had with Harry Warrington.

"Hadst thou brought me a regiment, James, I think I should not have been better pleased," said Mr. Lambert. Mrs. Lambert called to her daughters who were in the garden, kissed them both when they came in, and cried out the good news to them. Hetty jumped for joy, and Theo performed some uncommonly brilliant operations upon the harpsichord that night. When Dr. Boyle came in for his backgammon, he could not, at first, account for the illumination in all their faces until the three ladies, in a happy chorus, told him how right he had been in his sermon and how dreadfully they had wronged that poor dear, good young Mr. Warrington.

"What shall we do, my dear?" says the Colonel to his wife. "The hay is in, the corn won't be cut for a fortnight—the horses have nothing to do. Suppose we..." And here he leans over the table and whispers in her ear. "My dearest Martin! The very thing!" cries Mrs. Lambert, taking her husband's hand and pressing it. "What's the very thing, mother?" cries young Charley, who is home for his Bartlemytide holidays. "The very thing is to go to supper. Come, Doctor! We will have a bottle of wine tonight, and drink repentance to all who think evil." "Amen," says the Doctor; "with all my heart!" And with this the worthy family went to their supper.

The air inside the White Horse Ordinary tasted of stale tobacco and cheap claret, a thick suspension of gossip and humidity. Mr. Warrington entered, finding the room already saturated with the noise of Parson Sampson. The clergyman sat amidst a circle of country gentlemen, his face flushed with a rosy, artificial vitality that seemed to defy the damp London evening outside. He spoke in rapid bursts, weaving tales of Kensington and Pall Mall, of prisons and racecourses, constructing a narrative where names shifted like sand but the essence remained: a world of wicked fun and dangerous intrigue. To these listeners, accuracy was irrelevant; the texture of the lie mattered far more than the grain of the truth. If Sampson called Mrs. Polly by the name of Lucy, or attributed a duel to the wrong regiment, the audience swallowed the error with a smile, eager for the next bout of laughter.

When Warrington stepped into the light, the room's rhythm fractured. Sampson stopped mid-sentence, his eyes widening until they lost focus. With a whisper meant only for the squire beside him—a muttered invocation of debt—he scrambled from his chair. He did not walk; he stumbled forward, propelled by a frantic need to bridge the distance between the parson and the Virginian. "My dear sir! My conqueror of spades!" Sampson cried, his voice cracking with exaggerated delight. "You look fresh! Well-preserved!"

Warrington returned the greeting with a polite warmth, observing the chaplain's rosy gills with genuine appreciation. The squire, recognizing the newcomer, gestured for Sampson to resume his seat, attempting to steer the conversation back to Lord Ogle and a grocer's wife, but his interruption was drowned out by a shout and an oath regarding a gouty toe. Sampson apologized, turning his attention fully to Warrington. He delivered the news of Castlewood with the precision of a courier: messages from the Viscountess, updates on the waters at Harrogate, the fortunes of Mr. Will, and the distress of Molly over the missing valet, Gumbo. He wove these fragments into a tapestry of local significance, ensuring that even the highest titles sounded intimate.

As the other guests drifted away, leaving the room quieter, the dynamic shifted. Sampson, emboldened by the solitude, poured toast after toast, his energy seemingly infinite. Warrington matched him, his demeanor warming, becoming generous and open. Why should the chaplain endure the expense of an inn when Warrington offered a room? The order went forth: Gumbo was summoned to prepare the quarters, and Sampson was to inspect the stables. There, amidst the smell of hay and leather, the connection deepened. Sampson recognized his own horse, a creature that whinnied and nuzzled its old master with a familiarity that transcended language. Warrington, struck by the bond, declared the animal a gift. Sampson seized the offer, collapsing into tears of gratitude that mingled with the scent of Bordeaux wine, and the two friends walked arm-in-arm toward Madame Bernstein's lodgings, carrying the lingering odors of the stable into the drawing room.

Madame Bernstein received the chaplain with a mixture of politeness and amusement. She tolerated his broad jokes and rapid speech much as she tolerated a novel dish on its first few servings, but her patience held firm only for cards. "The cards do not cheat," she stated, her tone final. "They speak the truth." Yet, when her mood lifted, she invited Sampson to lead grace before meals. Initially hesitant, citing the high stakes of her games and his depleted funds, Sampson eventually secured a small cache of coins from Warrington's overflowing purse, allowing him to join the table.

Warrington embraced this arrangement with the ease of someone adapting to a new ecosystem. His attire remained dark and severe, yet cut with a sharp elegance that drew comparisons to Hamlet, complete with ribbons and stockings pulled down. He acquired the accoutrements of his station: fine shaving plates, brocade bedgowns, French watches with painted backs, and snuff boxes crafted by artisans. A retinue of grooms, jockeys, and tradesmen waited in his anteroom, filtered through Gumbo before reaching the Virginian. In parallel, Warrington pursued the arts of gentility, mastering the foil and the minuet under the tutelage of foreign instructors. He learned to dance with a grace that silenced Lady Maria's critics and to ride with a confidence that rivaled Colonel Washington, dismissing the need for further instruction in the native Virginian style.

Sampson flourished in this environment, his knowledge of the world expanding alongside his patron's wealth. He became a confidant, dispensing stories that were half-truth, half-fiction, seasoned with the wine of a hundred dinner tables. Warrington listened with admiration, captivated by the way the older man's wit aged like a vintage, gaining depth rather than losing it. This symbiotic relationship culminated in a formal exchange of letters, preserving the moment of transition.

To Mrs. Esmond Warrington of Castlewood, Richmond, Virginia:

"HONOURED MADAM—Your letter of 20 June, conveyed by Mr. Trail of Bristol, has reached me safely. I thank you for your counsel and for the remembrances of our ancestral home, which I cherish despite the distance.

"I wrote previously of the journey's minor accidents and the kind reception I received. Since then, I have profited from the fine weather and the excellent company, forging acquaintances among the nobility. Among them are the Earl of Chesterfield, the Earl of March and Ruglen, soon to be Duke of Queensberry, and the Duchess, a celebrated beauty of the Queen's time. These figures frequent my aunt's assemblies, the most crowded gatherings in this bustling place. On my journey, I also stayed at Westerham with Lieutenant-General Wolfe, a veteran of Marlborough's campaigns, whose son, Lieutenant-Colonel James Wolfe, is engaged to Miss Lowther and holds the esteem of the entire army.

"I acknowledge receipt of the quarterly allowance of 52l. 10s. via Mr. Trail. While I currently possess sufficient means, I intend to practice rigorous economy to maintain my credit for the upkeep of horses, Gumbo, and the necessaries of a gentleman of my standing. The linens and clothes I brought will suffice for some years, though I may require finer shirts for occasions of consequence.

"I am relieved to report that I have not needed your family pills. Gumbo, however, has benefited greatly from English beef, ale, and air, growing fat and saucy. He sends his duty to his mistress and requests that Mrs. Mountain remember him to his fellow servants, particularly Dinah and Lily, for whom he purchased posy rings at Tunbridge Fair.

"Regarding my education, I assure you I remain diligent. I have masters in fencing and dancing, and Reverend Mr. Sampson, having joined me to drink the waters, occupies a room at my lodging. We break together and read in the mornings; he assures me I am not quite the dunce I once appeared. Our studies include Mr. Rapin's History, Dr. Barrow's Sermons, Shakespeare, Pope's Homer, and a French translation of an Arabian tale collection. Several scholars reside here, including Mr. Richardson, author of the works you and my brother cherished. He expressed pleasure upon learning his writings occupy a shelf in Virginia and extended his respects to my mother. Mr. Richardson is a short, stout man, lacking the fiery brilliance often associated with genius.

"My aunt and cousin, Lady Maria, send their affectionate greetings. Enclosed is a note for Mountain. I remain, Honoured madam, your dutiful son, H. ESMOND WARRINGTON."

The air in the drawing room at Tunbridge Wells was thick with the scent of stale lavender water and suppressed gossip. To the old ladies gathered there, particularly Madame de Bernstein, Harry Warrington was not a nobleman of promise but a cautionary tale, a young Englishman already rotting from the inside out. Madame de Bernstein, a creature of ancient Roman stoicism who believed her nephew should emulate the vices of Rome itself, found comfort in his infamy. Her vigilance over Lady Maria had slackened, replaced by a weary indifference. Dragons, she knew, grew soft with age; their scales dulled, their claws became clumsy props, and their venom lost its lethality, reducing to a mere annoyance. She watched them trail their languid tails back to their caves, unaware that the danger had simply shifted forms.

While the Baroness dismissed the threat, the atmosphere at Oakhurst remained tense. Rumors of Harry's dissipation traveled faster than the post coaches. When the little dancer returned to London, the narrative was fixed: she had been abandoned by a heartless lover. The whispers at the Wells were a cacophony of speculation, mixing women of fashion with those of dubious repute, all united in their pursuit of health or distraction. This collective delusion soothed the Baroness's conscience. She could now speak freely of finding Harry a suitable wife, a transactional arrangement to secure his future, dismissing his attachment to Maria as a childish phase.

Mr. Sampson, the chaplain, observed the scene with a mixture of amusement and calculation. He possessed the natural wit and animal spirits of a man who had navigated the treacherous waters of the world since his days as a servitor in college. He was a professional "toad-eater," a term that carried a weightier connotation in those days than today. Back then, a gentleman's toad-eater did not pretend to equality; he bowed low, called his patron "Sir" aloud, and swallowed his own dignity to advance the interests of the powerful. Sampson wore this role with pride, acting as Harry's aide-de-camp, managing his tradesmen, and collecting percentages for his introductions. Yet, beneath the obsequious exterior lay a genuine, if cynical, affection for the young Virginian. He admired Harry's blushing cheeks, his bright eyes, and his shyness—a stark contrast to the cynicism Sampson himself cultivated.

Their intimacy deepened quickly. Sampson, who claimed to follow the maxim *Video meliora, deteriora sequor* ("I see better things, but choose worse"), offered Harry a shield against the harsh realities of adult life. He spoke of his own rough past, of keeping a "decent tongue" for his boarding school sister, and positioned himself as a mentor who would guide Harry away from the "savages" of his homeland and the Methodists of his mother. He was eager to marry Harry off, perhaps to the widow Patcham or Mrs. Hooper, viewing these unions as strategic victories in his own career.

One afternoon, returning from the coffee-house, Sampson burst into the room with news that nearly ignited Harry's temper. "What a reputation, sir, they have made for you!" Sampson exclaimed, his voice rising. "Monsieur de Richelieu was nothing to you!"

Harry, confused by the reference to a French duke he had never met, asked what he meant. Sampson explained the local gossip: the widow Patcham's desperation, the Alderman fetching his wife away from the scandal, the entire town buzzing with accusations.

"Who says so?" Harry cried, his face flushing with indignation. "I should like to meet the man who dares say so, and confound the villain!"

"I should not like to show him to you," Sampson laughed, a dry, nervous sound. "It might be the worse for him."

"It's a shame," Harry continued, pacing the room, his hands clenched. "To speak with such levity about the character of ladies or gentlemen."

Sampson nodded gravely, though his eyes sparkled with satisfaction. "So I told them."

"It's a shame," Harry insisted, his voice trembling with righteous fury. "Do you know, in our country, a fellow's ears would not be safe. Before I left home, three brothers shot down a man for having spoken ill of their sister."

"Serve the villain right!" Sampson cheered.

"They have started this calumny here, Sampson," Harry said, his composure cracking. "About me and the poor little French dancing-girl."

"Wicked, wasn't it?"

"Abominable."

"They said the very same thing about my Lord March. Isn't it shameful?"

"Indeed it is," Sampson replied, maintaining a mask of solemn concern.

"If these stories reach my mother's ears..." Harry trailed off, imagining the devastation. "It would break her heart. Why, only a few days ago, Mr. Wolfe told me how horrible lies were circulating. What do they think a gentleman of my name and country can be? A seducer of women? They might as well say I was a horse-stealer or a housebreaker. I vow, if I hear any man say so, I'll have his ears!"

Sampson chuckled, shaking powder from his wig. "I have read, sir, that the Grand Seignior of Turkey receives bushels of ears sometimes. If you collected all those who had heard scandal against you, what basketsful you would fill!"

"And so I would," Harry declared, his voice ringing with a dangerous intensity. "Any fellow who speaks against a lady or a gentleman of honour!"

"If you go down to the Well, you'll find a harvest of them," Sampson mused, gesturing vaguely toward the window. "It was the high tide of Scandal. Detraction was at its peak. You may see the *nymphas discentes* and the *aures satyrorum acutas*."

"That may be as you say, Sampson," Harry replied, stopping his pace. He looked at the chaplain, his expression hardening into something final. "But if ever I hear any man speak against my character, I'll punish him. Mark that."

The air inside the Oakhurst parlor shifted, a subtle pressure change preceding the storm of anticipation. The secret Mr. Lambert had whispered to his wife—the destination of Tunbridge Wells—hung suspended, dissolving the tension of the previous days. With the hay secured and the corn untouched, the farm horses were free. The logic was simple, almost arithmetic: if the beasts were idle, the family might move.

"Put them in the coach," Colonel Lambert proposed, the words landing with the weight of a decree. "We shall all go."

The reaction was immediate and visceral. Mamma embraced the idea, her posture softening into relief. The children leaped, a chaotic burst of kinetic energy. The girls vanished instantly, a whirlwind of fabric and motion, scouring the presses for their finest garments. Calamancoes, paduasoys, falbalas, furbelows—they emerged from the dark recesses of the wardrobe, items reserved for solemn occasions, now repurposed for a journey. Mamma's robes, usually retired to lavender and silence, were laid out. The Colonel produced his laced hat, his waistcoat, and the silver-hilted hanger, the metal gleaming under the lamplight. Charley donned a rasee holiday suit, a garment worn by his father during his own marriage, which Mrs. Lambert cut up later with a pang of loss. Even the animals participated in the ritual; Ball and Dumpling had their tails and manes bound with ribbon. Chump, the old white cart-horse, was designated the unicorn leader, tasked with guiding the carriage-horses up the initial incline from Oakhurst to Westerham.

The coach itself was an artifact of history, an ancient vessel rumored to have transported George I from Greenwich to London upon his accession. It had belonged to Mr. Lambert's father, and for generations, the family had regarded it as one of the most splendid conveyances in the three kingdoms. Brian, the coachman—and ploughman—occupied the box, flanked by Mr. Charley. The precious clothes were stacked in imperials atop the roof. The Colonel's pistols found their place in the carriage pockets, while the blunderbuss hung behind the box, within reach of Brian, a veteran of the old wars.

No highwayman harassed the convoy. No innkeeper levied contributions. Colonel Lambert, with his slender purse and large family, was beyond the reach of such depredators. A modest lodging had been arranged by young Colonel Wolfe at his customary house, a location he accompanied them to on horseback.

These lodgings lay opposite Madame Bernstein's establishment. As the Oakhurst family settled into their quarters on a Saturday evening, the scene outside their window unfolded with a rhythmic, almost hypnotic regularity. Chair after chair discharged powdered beaux and patched beauties onto the pavement before the Baroness's door. She held one of her many card-parties. The sun had not yet fully surrendered the sky; the ancestors of this era began their dissipations early, indulging in meat, drink, or cards anytime after three in the afternoon until the break of dawn. From their vantage point, the young country ladies and their mother watched the stream of personages entering the Bernstein rout.

Colonel Wolfe identified the arrivals for the girls. It was nearly as delightful as attending the party itself. Hetty and Theo observed not only the guests arriving but peered through the Baroness's open casements, watching the figures settle within. Among the passing faces was the Duchess of Queensberry. When Mr. Wolfe explained her identity, Martin Lambert was quick to recite a score of lines from his favorite Mat Prior regarding "Kitty, beautiful and young."

"Think that that old lady was once like you, girls!" the Colonel cried.

"Like us, papa?" Miss Hetty tossed her head, defiant. "Well, certainly we never set up for being beauties!"

"Yes, like you, you little baggage; like you at this moment, who want to go to that drum yonder:"

*"Inflamed with rage at sad restraint Which wise mamma ordained, And sorely vexed to play the saint Whilst wit and beauty reigned."*

"We were never invited, papa," the satirist of the family interjected. "And I am sure if there's no beauty more worth seeing than that, the wit can't be much worth the hearing."

"Oh, but he's a rare poet, Mat Prior!" the Colonel continued, ignoring the interruption. "Though, mind you, girls, you'll skip over all the poems I have marked with a cross. A rare poet! And to think you should see one of his heroines! 'Fondness prevailed, mamma gave way' (she always will, Mrs. Lambert!)"

"I am sure it must have been very inflammable," Mamma remarked.

"So it was, my dear, twenty years ago, much more inflammable than it is now," the Colonel noted.

"Nonsense, Mr. Lambert," Mamma answered sharply.

"Look, look!" Hetty cried, running forward to point at the little square and the covered gallery where the door to Madame Bernstein's apartments stood. A crowd of street urchins, idlers, and yokels surrounded it, watching the company.

"It's Harry Warrington!" Theo exclaimed, waving a handkerchief. But Warrington did not see Miss Lambert. The Virginian walked arm-in-arm with a portly clergyman in a crisp, rustling silk gown, disappearing into Madame de Bernstein's door.

"I heard him preach a most admirable sermon here last Sunday," Mr. Wolfe said. "A little theatrical, but most striking and eloquent."

"You seem to be here most Sundays, James," Mrs. Lambert observed.

"And Monday, and soon till Saturday," the Colonel added. "See, Harry has beautified himself already; hath his hair in buckle, and I have no doubt is going to the drum too."

"I had rather sit quiet generally of a Saturday evening," said sober Mr. Wolfe. "At any rate, away from card-playing and scandal; but I own, dear Mrs. Lambert, I am under orders. Shall I go across the way and send Mr. Warrington to you?"

"No, let him have his sport. We shall see him tomorrow. He won't care to be disturbed amidst his fine folks by us country-people," said meek Mrs. Lambert.

"I am glad he is with a clergyman who preaches so well," Theo murmured, her eyes suggesting, *You see, good people, he is not so bad as you thought him.* "The clergyman has a very kind, handsome face."

"Here comes a greater clergyman," Mr. Wolfe announced. "It is my Lord of Salisbury, with his blue ribbon, and a chaplain behind him."

"And whom a mercy's name have we here?" Mrs. Lambert broke in as a sedan-chair, covered with gilding and topped with no less than five earl's coronets, approached the door. Carried by bearers in richly laced clothes and preceded by three footmen in matching splendor, it halted before the Baroness's entrance. The Bishop, about to enter, stopped, ran back with respectful bows and curtseys to the sedan-chair, offering his hand to the lady who stepped forth.

"Who on earth is this?" Mrs. Lambert asked.

"Sprechen sie Deutsch? Ja, meinherr. Nichts verstand," the waggish Colonel replied.

"Pooh, Martin."

"Well, if you can't understand High Dutch, my love, how can I help it? Your education was neglected at school. Can you understand heraldry? I know you can."

"I make," Charley cried, reciting the shield, "three merions on a field or, with an earl's coronet."

"A countess's coronet, my son. The Countess of Yarmouth, my son."

"And pray who is she?"

"It hath ever been the custom of our sovereigns to advance persons of distinction to honour," the Colonel continued gravely. "And this eminent lady hath been so promoted by our gracious monarch, to the rank of Countess of this kingdom."

"But why, papa?" the daughters asked together.

"Never mind, girls!" Mamma said.

But the incorrigible Colonel persisted.

"Y, my children, is one of the last and the most awkward letters of the whole alphabet. When I tell you stories, you are always saying Why. Why should my Lord Bishop be cringing to that lady? Look at him rubbing his fat hands together, and smiling into her face! It's not a handsome face any longer. It is all painted red and white like Scaramouch's in the pantomime. See, there comes another blue-ribbon, as I live. My Lord Bamborough. The descendant of the Hotspurs. The proudest man in England. He stops, he bows, he smiles; he is hat in hand, too. See, she taps him with her fan. Get away, you crowd of little blackguard boys, and don't tread on the robe of the lady whom the King delights to honour."

"But why does the King honour her?" the girls asked once more.

"There goes that odious last letter but one! Did you ever hear of her Grace the Duchess of Kendal? No. Of the Duchess of Portsmouth? Non plus. Of the Duchess of La Valliore? Of Fair Rosamond, then?"

"Hush, papa! There is no need to bring blushes

The Colonel's first reaction was anger. He wished the boy who stole his daughter's heart had broken his nose against a doorpost instead of theirs. "A plague upon all scapegraces," he cried. His wife, Mrs. Lambert, offered a mild interposition, suggesting the fall at their door held something providential. Who else would have sent the young gentleman to them? Perhaps it was for the blessing of them all.

"It's hard, Molly!" the Colonel groaned. "We cherish and fondle them. We toil and scheme. We hoard money in stockings and patch our own old coats. If they have a headache, we cannot sleep; if they have a wish, we work day and night to compass it. On Tuesday morning I am king of my house. On Tuesday evening, Prince Whippersnapper appears, and my reign is over. A whole life is forgotten for a pair of blue eyes and a head of yellow hair."

"'Tis written that we women should leave all to follow our husband," the matron said, laying her hand on his arm. "'Tis human nature, and what can you expect?" sighed the Colonel.

"And I think I did my duty to my husband, though I own I left my papa for him," Mrs. Lambert added softly.

"Excellent wench! Perdition catch my soul! but I do love thee, Molly!" the Colonel declared. "But, then, mind you, your father never did me; and if ever I am to have sons-in-law—"

"Ever, indeed! Of course my girls are to have husbands, Mr. Lambert!" cried mamma.

"Well, when they come, I'll hate them, madam, as your father did me; and quite right too, for taking his treasure away from him."

"Don't be irreligious and unnatural, Martin Lambert! I say you are unnatural, sir!"

"Nay, my dear, I have an old tooth in my left jaw; and 'tis natural that the tooth should come out. But when the toothdrawer pulls it, 'tis natural that I should feel pain. Do you suppose, madam, that I don't love Hetty better than any tooth in my head?"

No woman was ever averse to the idea of her daughter getting a husband, however fathers revolted against the invasion of the son-in-law. Mothers and grandmothers are married over again in the marriage of their young ones; their souls attire themselves in the laces and muslins of forty years ago; the postillion's white ribbons bloom again, and they flutter into the postchaise, and drive away. What woman, however old, has not the bridal favours and raiment stowed away, and packed in lavender, in the inmost cupboards of her heart?

"It will be a sad thing, parting with her," Mrs. Lambert sighed.

"You have settled that point already, Molly," the Colonel laughed. "Had I not best go out and order raisins and corinths for the wedding-cake?"

"And then I shall have to leave the house in their charge when I go to her, you know, in Virginia. How many miles is it to Virginia, Martin? I should think it must be thousands of miles."

"A hundred and seventy-three thousand three hundred and ninety-one and three-quarters, my dear, by the near way," answered Lambert, gravely. "That through Prester John's country. By the other route, through Persia—"

"Oh, give me the one where there is the least of the sea, and your horrid ships, which I can't bear!" cried the Colonel's spouse. "I hope Rachel Esmond and I shall be better friends. She had a very high spirit when we were girls at school."

"Had we not best go about the baby-linen, Mrs. Martin Lambert?" her husband interposed.

Mrs. Lambert thought there was no matter for wonderment at all, having remarked some very pretty lace caps and bibs in Mrs. Bobbinit's toy-shop. On that Sunday afternoon, when the discovery was made, and while little Hetty lay upon her pillow with feverish cheeks, closed eyes, and a piteous face, her mother looked at the child with the most perfect ease of mind, and seemed to be rather pleased than otherwise at Hetty's woe.

The girl was not only unhappy, but enraged with herself for having published her secret. Perhaps she had not known it until the sudden emotion acquainted her with her own state of mind; and now the little maid chose to be as much ashamed as if she had done a wrong, and been discovered in it. She was indignant with her own weakness, and broke into transports of wrath against herself. She vowed she never would forgive herself for submitting to such a humiliation. So the young pard, wounded by the hunter's dart, chafes with rage in the forest, is angry with the surprise of the rankling steel in her side, and snarls and bites at her sister-cubs, and the leopardess, her spotted mother.

Little Hetty tore and gnawed, and growled, so that I should not like to have been her fraternal cub, or her spotted dam or sire. "What business has any young woman," she cried out, "to indulge in any such nonsense? Mamma, I ought to be whipped, and sent to bed. I know perfectly well that Mr. Warrington does not care a fig about me. I dare say he likes French actresses and the commonest little milliner-girl in the toy-shop better than me. And so he ought, and so they are better than me. Why, what a fool I am to burst out crying like a ninny about nothing, and because Mr. Wolfe said Harry played cards of a Sunday! I know he is not clever, like papa. I believe he is stupid—I am certain he is stupid: but he is not so stupid as I am. Why, of course, I can't marry him. How am I to go to America, and leave you and Theo? Of course, he likes somebody else, at America, or at Tunbridge, or at Jericho, or somewhere. He is a prince in his own country, and can't think of marrying a poor half-pay officer's daughter, with twopence to her fortune. Used not you to tell me how, when I was a baby, I cried and wanted the moon? I am a baby now, a most absurd, silly, little baby—don't talk to me, Mrs. Lambert, I am. Only there is this to be said, he don't know anything about it, and I would rather cut my tongue out than tell him."

Dire were the threats with which Hetty menaced Theo, in case her sister should betray her. As for the infantile Charley, his mind being altogether set on cheese-cakes, he had not remarked or been moved by Miss Hester's emotion; and the parents and the kind sister of course all promised not to reveal the little maid's secret.

"I begin to think it had been best for us to stay at home," sighed Mrs. Lambert to her husband.

"Nay, my dear," replied the other. "Human nature will be human nature; surely Hetty's mother told me herself that she had the beginning of a liking for a certain young curate before she fell over head and ears in love with a certain young officer of Kingsley's. And as for me, my heart was wounded in a dozen places ere Miss Molly Benson took entire possession of it. Our sons and daughters must follow in the way of their parents before them, I suppose. Why, but yesterday, you were scolding me for grumbling at Miss Het's precocious fancies. To do the child justice, she disguises her feelings entirely, and I defy Mr. Warrington to know from her behaviour how she is disposed towards him."

"A daughter of mine and yours, Martin," cries the mother, with great dignity, "is not going to fling herself at a gentleman's head!"

"Neither herself nor the teacup, my dear," answers the Colonel. "Little Miss Het treats Mr. Warrington like a vixen. He never comes to us, but she boxes his ears in one fashion or t'other. I protest she is barely civil to him; but, knowing what is going on in the young hypocrite's mind, I am not going to be angry at her rudeness."

"She hath no need to be rude at all, Martin; and our girl is good enough for any gentleman in England or America. Why, if their ages suit, shouldn't they marry after all, sir?"

"Why, if he wants her, shouldn't he ask her, my dear? I am sorry we came. I am for putting the horses into the carriage, and turning their heads towards home again."

But mamma fondly said, "Depend on it, my dear, that these matters are wisely ordained for us. Depend upon it, Martin, it was not for nothing that Harry Warrington was brought to our gate in that way; and that he and our children are thus brought together again. If that marriage has been decreed in Heaven, a marriage it will be."

"At what age, Molly, I wonder, do women begin and leave off match-making? If our little chit falls in love and falls out again, she will not be the first of her sex, Mrs. Lambert. I wish we were on our way home again, and, if I had my will, would trot off this very night."

"He has promised to drink his tea here to-night. You would not take away our child's pleasure, Martin?" asked the mother, softly.

In his fashion, the father was not less

The air in the Assembly Rooms was thick with the scent of damp wool and stale champagne, a heavy atmosphere where movement felt less like celebration and more like a series of necessary adjustments. Harry Warrington, a young Virginian whose wealth was as fluid as the currency he spent, had arranged the evening with the mechanical precision of a man fulfilling a duty. He had secured the space, divided the rooms, and populated them with the remaining company of the Wells, treating them to an entertainment that felt less like a party and more like a scheduled inspection of social hierarchy.

In one corner, card tables stood rigid, occupied by those who required the distraction of gambling to endure the evening. In another, a supper of profuse champagne and bowls of negus awaited consumption. The largest hall was reserved for the dance, though the activity within lacked the vitality of genuine joy. Minuets were called, executed by couples moving with the stiff grace of puppets on strings. Among them, Lady Maria Esmond, an earl's daughter and the highest-ranking presence save for the crippled Lady Augusta Crutchley, danced the first minuet with Harry. He acquitted himself with an elegance that satisfied the room, surpassing even Mr. Wolfe, who stood awkwardly beside Miss Lowther. Following this, Harry requested Miss Theo for the next minuet. She accepted, her cheeks flushing with a happiness that seemed artificial under the scrutiny of her parents and the seething resentment of Miss Humpleby, who had expected to follow Lady Maria.

After the minuets came the country dances, accompanied by a harp, a fiddle, and a flageolet perched precariously in a small balcony. These instruments emitted a thin, melancholy thread of sound that permeated the room. If one were to take up an old book of music today and attempt to replicate these tunes, the result would be a revelation of sorrow; the melodies possess an inherent bitterness, a *amari aliquid*, that suggests they were once joyful but have since decayed into the limbo of the past. Yet, despite this sad accompaniment, the guests laughed, courted, and frisked, oblivious to the fact that their ancestors would later mock the very music that sustained them.

Harry Warrington held the honor of dancing with the Duchess of Queensberry, a woman of considerable stature and influence. Though the Duchess deliberately turned her back on a present countess, laughing loudly and pointing at her with a fan, the entire company ignored her Grace. Instead, they bowed, cringed, and smiled toward the Countess of Yarmouth-Walmoden, a figure favored by King George II. Earlier that morning, this countess had greeted Harry with graciousness, inviting him to cards. Purblind Colonel Blinkinsop, mistakenly believing the invitation was for him, had bowed profoundly. The Countess corrected him with a dismissive "Pooh!" clarifying she meant the young Virginian. Consequently, the room thronged around Lady Yarmouth, eager for her approval. My Lady Blanche Pendragon, Sir Lancelot Quintain, and the Dean of Ealing all vied for a single smile from her. Even Lady Maria and Madame de Bernstein offered their services with meek respect and grave courtesy.

Yet, amidst this display of deference, Miss Hester chose indignation. During her dance with Harry, she spoke scarcely a word. As they moved to the supper room, passing Madame Walmoden's card table, the Countess called out cheerfully, asking if her "briddy liddle bardner" enjoyed dancing.

"I thank your ladyship," Miss Hester replied, tossing her head, "I don't like dancing, and I don't like cards." With a curtsey that resembled a falling cheese, she strutted away from the Countess's table.

Harry was visibly offended. Sarcasm from the young toward the aged pained him; flippant behavior toward himself wounded him further. He had extended courtesy to everyone he met, expecting a reciprocal politeness. Hetty, however, wore an ingenuous smile that masked her intent. Approaching the sideboard, she asked artlessly, "What a horrid, vulgar old woman that is; don't you think so?"

"What woman?" Harry asked.

"That German woman—my Lady Yarmouth—to whom all the men are bowing and cringing."

"Her ladyship has been very kind to me," Harry said grimly. "Won't you have some of this custard?"

"And you have been bowing to her, too! You look as if your negus was not nice," Hetty continued harmlessly.

"It is not very good negus," Harry admitted with a gulp.

"And the custard is bad too! I declare 'tis made with bad eggs!" cried Miss Lambert.

"Wish, Hester, that the entertainment and the company had been better to your liking," Harry said, his voice low.

"'Tis very unfortunate; but I dare say you could not help it," Hetty retorted, tossing her curly head.

Harry groaned inwardly, clenching his fists and teeth. The little torturer pressed on, "You seem disturbed: shall we go to my mamma?"

"Yes, let us go to your mamma," Harry agreed, his eyes glazing with anger. A curse escaped his lips at an unlucky waiter blocking his path.

"La! Is that the way you speak in Virginia?" asked Miss Pertness.

"We are rough there sometimes, madam, and can't help being disturbed," Harry said slowly, his whole frame trembling as fire flashed in his eyes. Hetty saw nothing clearly afterward until she reached her mother. Never before had she seen Harry appear so handsome, so noble.

"You look pale, child!" cried her mother, anxious. "'Tis the cold—or the heat. Thank you, Mr. Warrington." She offered a faint curtsey as Harry bowed tremendously and walked away among his guests, still furious.

His agitation was interrupted by another altercation involving his aunt and the Duchess of Queensberry. As the royal favorite passed the Duchess, her Grace bestowed an awful stare, her eyes lacking the brightness of her youth when she claimed to set the world on fire. Turning to her neighbor with an affected laugh, she unleashed a ceaseless barrage of giggles and sneers aimed at the Hanoverian lady. The Countess pursued her cards, seemingly unaware or unwilling to acknowledge the gibes directed at her. A feud of many years existed between the Duchesses of Queensberry and the royal family.

"How you all bow down to the idol! Don't tell me! You are as bad as the rest, my good Madame Bernstein!" the Duchess declared. "Ah, what a true Christian country this is! And how your dear first husband, the Bishop, would have liked to see such a sight!"

"Forgive me, if I fail quite to understand your Grace," Madame de Bernstein replied.

"We are both of us growing old, my good Bernstein, or perhaps we simply won't understand when we don't choose to. That is the way with us women, my good young Iroquois."

"Your Grace remarked that it was a Christian country," Madame de Bernstein said, "and I failed to perceive the point."

"Indeed, my good creature, there is very little point in it! I meant we were such good Christians because we were so forgiving. Don't you remember reading, when you were young, or your husband the Bishop reading in the pulpit, how when a woman amongst the Jews was caught doing wrong, the Pharisees were for stoning her out of hand? Far from stoning such a woman now, look how fond we are of her! Any man in this room would go round it on his knees if yonder woman bade him. Yes, Madame Walmoden, you may look up from your cards with your great painted face and frown with your great painted eyebrows at me. You know I am talking about you; and I intend to go on talking about you. I say any man here would go round the room on his knees, if you bade him!"

"I think, madam, I know two or three who wouldn't!" Harry interjected with spirit.

"Quick, let me hug them to my heart of hearts!" cried the old Duchess. "Which are they? Bring 'em to me, my dear Iroquois! Let us have a game of four—of honest men and women; that is to say, if we can find a couple more partners, Mr. Warrington!"

"Here are we three," said the Baroness Bernstein with a forced laugh, "let us play a dummy."

"Pray, madam, where is the third?" the Duchess asked, looking around.

"Madam!" cried the other elderly lady, "I leave your Grace to boast of your honesty, which I have no doubt is spotless: but I will thank you not to doubt mine before my own relatives and children!"

"See how she fires up at a word! I am sure, my dear creature, you are quite as honest as most of the company," the Duchess said.

"Which may not be good enough for her Grace the Duchess of Queensberry and Dover, who, to be sure, might have stayed away in such a case, but it is the best my nephew could get, madam, and his best he has given you. You look astonished, Harry, my dear—and well you may. He is not used to our ways, madam."

"Madam, he has found an aunt who can teach him our ways, and a great deal more!" cried the Duchess, rapping her fan.

"She will teach him to try and make all his guests welcome, old or young, rich or poor.

The Colonel and his wife had decided to move Hetty away, believing distance would cure the ailment. Yet the disease persisted, clinging to her despite the miles and the changing climate. Men might be whipped into obedience by absence, but women require a different regimen. To secure their fidelity, one needs a judicious mixture of neglect and occasional violence—a brisk dose of the latter acting as an alterative, followed by sparse intervals of affection. Too much sweetness cloyes the palate; a young woman with every wish granted will yawn in her lover's face, preferring the petty attentions of a fool. Conversely, the woman who receives a bootjack thrown at her or curses before the servants returns at tea-time, nuzzling at the knee of the man who has just finished his wine and nap. She looks around proudly, signaling to the butler that her Henry is good. This is the dynamic of power: the henpecked versus the master who commands with a whistle.

Hetty's removal was futile. She intended to remain as devoted to Harry in his absence as she had been in his presence. Before the ball and Dobbin's departure, she should have bid him farewell, admitting her wilfulness and thanking him for his kindness. She would have shown no special emotion, too ashamed of her secret to betray it. Harry, preoccupied, failed to read the grief behind her glances or the artifice of her innocent smiles. He parted calmly, assuming she was happy to return to her music, poultry, and garden.

He did not ride partway home beside his friend's carriage. Other engagements awaited him. By the time he returned, the Lamberts were gone from Tunbridge Wells. Their windows stood open, a card in one announcing the rooms were once more to let. A fleeting sorrow at the blankness of spaces recently filled by frank, friendly faces might have crossed the young gentleman's mind, but he dined promptly at the White Horse, ate, and demanded his bottle fiercely. Meanwhile, poor little Hester choked over her tea as the Lamberts arrived at their friends' house in Westerham. Her cheeks were wan, dark circles framing her eyes. The thunder had raged; the night was hot; she could not sleep. Tomorrow, she would recover. Home they came.

There was the gate where he had fallen. There was the bed he had occupied, the chair where he used to sit. Ages seemed to have passed. What a gulf separated today from yesterday! Who was that little child calling chickens or watering roses yonder? Was she and that girl the same Hester Lambert? She seemed older than Theo now—Theo, who had always been composed, clever, and prematurely aged. But in a night or two, Hester had lived through long, long years. Poppy and mandragora would never medicine them to the sweet sleep they had tasted yesterday.

Maria Esmond watched the Lambert cavalcade depart, feeling a grim relief. When Harry entered his aunt's card-room, flushed with Barbeau's wine, she looked at him with hot eyes. He laughed, rattling in reply to his aunt's question about which girl was his sweetheart. He claimed to love them both like sisters, praising the Lamberts as the finest people he knew. Why wasn't Lambert a general? He had been a distinguished officer, favored by the Duke. Madame Bernstein suggested Harry make interest with Lady Yarmouth for his protégé.

"Elle ravvole de fous, cher bedid anche!" Madame Bernstein mimicked the Countess's German accent, delighted by her son's success. "You carry off the hearts of all the old women, don't you, Maria?" she sneered at her niece, who quivered under the stab.

"You were quite right," the Baroness continued, "not to perceive that she cheated at cards. You play like a grand seigneur."

"Did she cheat?" cried Harry, astonished. "I am sure, ma'am, I saw no unfair play."

"No more did I, my dear, but I am sure she cheated. Bah! Every woman cheats, I and Maria included, when we can get a chance. But when you play with the Walmoden, you don't do wrong to lose in moderation; and many men cheat in that way. Cultivate her. She has taken a fancy to your beaux yeux. Why should your Excellency not be Governor of Virginia, sir? You must go and pay your respects to the Duke and his Majesty at Kensington. The Countess of Yarmouth will be your best friend at court."

"Why should you not introduce me, aunt?" Harry asked.

The old lady's rouged cheek grew redder. "I am not in favour at Kensington," she said. "I may have been once; and there are no faces so unwelcome to kings as those they wish to forget. All of us want to forget something or somebody. I dare say our ingenu here would like to wipe a sum or two off the slate. Wouldst thou not, Harry?"

Harry turned red, as did Maria, and his aunt laughed one of those wicked laughs unpleasant to hear. What did those guilty signals on their cheeks mean? What account was scored upon their memories that they desired to efface? Madame Bernstein was likely right; most folks possess ugly reckonings written on their consciences, which they are glad to be quit of.

If Maria had known one of the causes of Harry's disquiet, the middle-aged spinster would have been more unquiet still. For several days, Harry had missed a pocket-book. He remembered having it the day he drank claret at the White Horse and Gumbo carried him to bed. He searched for it in the morning, but none of his servants had seen it. He inquired at the White Horse, but found no trace. He dared not cry the book, making only cautious inquiries. If Lady Maria Esmond knew that the outpourings of her heart were in the hands of the public, her condition would be dire. The letters contained disclosures: a hundred family secrets narrated by an artless correspondent, laden with satire and abuse of persons connected to Mr. Warrington. There were expostulations about his attentions to other ladies, scorn, scandal, jokes, appeals, and protests of eternal fidelity—the usual farrago of love-letters. Would she like those letters read by anyone else? Did she recall her unfavorable opinions of the Miss Browns or Mrs. Thompson? Did she remember her words regarding Jones himself, whom she subsequently married after breaking off her engagement to Edward due to settlement disputes? Would Mr. J. like to see those remarks? She knew she wouldn't. Therefore, she should withdraw the imputation she had already cast in her mind upon Lady Maria Esmond.

No doubt her letters were foolish, as most love-letters are, but it did not follow that there was anything wrong in them. They are foolish when written by young folks to one another, and how much more foolish when written by an old man to a young lass, or vice versa. No wonder Lady Maria disliked them being read. Why, the very spelling—but that mattered less in her ladyship's days, and people are just as foolish now, though they spell better. No, it is not the spelling that matters; it is the writing at all. I, for one, am determined never to speak or write my mind out regarding anything or anybody. I intend to say of every woman that she is chaste and handsome; of every man that he is handsome, clever, and rich; of every book that it is delightfully interesting; of Snobmore's manners that they are gentlemanlike; of Screwby's dinners that they are luxurious; of Jawkins's conversation that it is lively and amusing; of Xantippe, that she has a sweet temper; of Jezebel, that her colour is natural; of Bluebeard, that he really was most indulgent to his wives, and that very likely they died of bronchitis. What? A word against the spotless Messalina? What an unfavourable view of human nature! What? King Cheops was not a perfect monarch? Oh, you railer at royalty and slanderer of all that is noble and good! When this book is concluded, I shall change the jaundiced livery which my books have worn since I began to lisp in numbers, give them rose-coloured coats with cherubs on the cover, and ensure all characters within are perfect angels.

Meanwhile, we exist in a society of men and women from whose shoulders no wings have yet sprouted, who undoubtedly possess little failings. There is Madame Bernstein, who has fallen asleep after dinner, having eaten and drunk too much. Mr. Harry Warrington has gone to play billiards with Count Caramboli; I suspect idleness is his failing. This is what Mr. Chaplain Sampson remarked to Lady Maria as they spoke in a low tone, avoiding interruption of Aunt Bernstein's doze in the neighboring room.

"A gentleman of Mr. Warrington's means can afford to be idle," Lady Maria said. "Surely you love cards and billiards yourself, my good Mr. Sampson?"

"I don't say, madam, my practice is good, only my doctrine is sound," the Chaplain replied with a sigh. "This young gentleman should have some employment. He should appear at court, enter the service of his country, as befits a man of his station. He should settle down and choose a woman of suitable rank as his wife." Sampson looked in her ladyship's face as he spoke.

"Indeed, my cousin is wasting his time," Lady Maria said, blushing slightly.

"Mr. Warrington might see his relatives of his father's family," the Chaplain suggested.

The air in the drawing room was thick with the scent of stale powder and suppressed rage, a mixture that clung to the linen curtains like a second skin. The Baroness Bernstein sat with a posture of rigid composure, her face a mask of porcelain indifference, though her eyes betrayed a flicker of calculation. Across from her, Lady Maria, the niece, held herself with the brittle dignity of someone who knows she is being watched.

"Of course, my Lady," the Baroness said, her voice smooth as polished glass. "You cannot expect me, as Harry Warrington's relative, to rejoice at the prospect of his marrying a woman older than his mother, penniless and... well, unsuitable. Yet, if he insists on such folly, it is not my concern. I doubt I ever intended to take that offer of five thousand pounds seriously; it was said in the heat of the moment. So, it was already settled at Castlewood? If I had known the depth of your resolve, I might have spared my objections. Once a pitcher is shattered, no amount of railing can restore its shape."

"Madam!" Lady Maria interrupted, her tone sharp.

"Pardon me," the Baroness replied, unbothered. "I speak only of facts. Your honor is secure, as Harry attests, and as you claim. What further proof is needed?"

"You have spoken to Mr. Warrington?"

"And he confirmed the promise made at Castlewood. You possess his writing."

"Certainly," Lady Maria admitted, her chin lifting.

"Ah," the elder lady noted, showing no sign of distress. "I admit I misread your letters initially. They implicated other family members—"

"Who slandered me and sought to poison Mr. Warrington's view of me. Yes, I wrote against them to clear my name."

"But surely, you are pained that a wretch should seize tales damaging our lineage and turn them into public scandal. That explains your recent agitation."

"Exactly," Lady Maria conceded. "With Mr. Warrington, I need conceal nothing. But that differs vastly from exposing the private disputes of a noble house."

"My dear Maria, I admire you now, and I owe you an apology. For twenty years, perhaps."

"I am glad you finally grant me justice," the niece said, her relief palpable.

"When I saw you last night, opening the ball with my nephew, what did you imagine, my dear?"

"I have no idea what the Baroness de Bernstein imagined," Lady Maria retorted, her pride stiffening.

"I recalled you performing to that same tune with the dancing-master at Kensington."

"It was a lie," Lady Maria declared.

"For which the poor dancing-master received a beating for nothing."

"It is cruel to revive such falsehoods," Maria said, her spirit flaring. "I shall decline residing with anyone who speaks thus."

"You wish to depart? I suspect Tunbridge will feel the heat if those letters surface."

"There was not a word against you in them," Maria assured her.

"So Harry said, and you deserve credit. Well, we are weary of one another. Separation suits us."

"That is my sentiment," Lady Maria curtseyed.

"Mr. Sampson will escort you to Castlewood. You and your maid may take a postchaise."

"We shall take the chaise, and Mr. Sampson will guide me," the younger lady echoed, playing the dutiful niece perfectly. "You see, I act with propriety."

"Do you know," the Baroness mused, "I suspect Mr. Sampson holds the letters?"

"I confess the thought crossed my mind."

"And you intend to flee home, coaxing the documents from him? Delilah. Well, they serve no purpose for me. I trust you recover them. When do you leave? The sooner, the better. We are women of the world, Maria. We trade insults only in passion. We require no company. Let us part amicably. Shall we attend Lady Yarmouth's gathering? It is her night. A change of scenery suits you after your nervous episodes; cards dispel gloomy thoughts better than physicians."

Lady Maria agreed to the cards, dressing quickly to await her aunt in the drawing room. As Madame Bernstein descended, she noticed Maria's door ajar. *She carries the letters,* the old lady thought. Together, they departed in separate carriages, exchanging a display of cordiality and respect that masked the bitterness of their conflict.

That night, returning from the Countess's assembly, Mrs. Brett, the Baroness's maid, approached Lady Maria's chamber bell. But Betty, Mrs. Brett's subordinate, was unfit to appear. She had been reveling with Mr. Warrington's black servant, Lord Bamborough's valet, and various others of similar station. The liquor, Mrs. Brett confessed with shock, had overwhelmed Betty. Should Mrs. Brett assist her mistress? Lady Maria declined, preferring solitude, dismissing Mrs. Brett. *She has the letters in her stays,* Madame Bernstein surmised. They parted on the stairs with feigned warmth.

Betty faced a reprimand the following morning, emerging from the closet adjacent to Lady Maria's apartment. She admitted, with contrite humility, her fondness for rum-punch, brewed delicately by Mr. Gumbo. She accepted her chastisement meekly, attended to her duties, and withdrew.

Betty was one of the Castlewood maids, captivated by Gumbo, and possessed a striking blue-eyed beauty that caught the eye of Mr. Case, the Baroness's confidential man. Jealousy had festered between Gumbo and Case, leading to quarrels. Gumbo, naturally peaceable, feared the Baroness's gentlemen, who threatened to break his bones or end his life if he persisted in courting Betty.

Yet, on the night of the rum-punch, despite finding Gumbo and Betty whispering in the doorway, Mr. Case displayed remarkable grace. He proposed the bowl of punch, brewed and consumed in Betty's room, concocted by Gumbo with skill. He praised Gumbo's music. Though usually sober, he insisted on more drinking until Betty succumbed to the state that warranted Lady Maria's censure.

Mr. Case, however, suffered greatly. Lying outside the house, he remained bedridden the entire next day, lacking the strength to attend his ladies until supper. His mistress, good-naturedly, rebuked him for his indiscretion.

"Why, Case," Mr. Warrington observed later, dining with his relatives, "I could swear it was you riding the London road this morning."

"Me! Law bless you, sir! I was in bed, my head splitting with pain. I ate at six, drank small beer, and am nearly restored. But that Gumbo... I shall never taste his punch again." The honest major-domo resumed his duties among the bottles.

After the meal, Madame Bernstein offered fortifying drinks to Maria against her fainting spells, urging consultation with her London physician via Harry. Harry was departing for two days on an errand for his aunt. "Business beneficial to him," she explained. "I wish Mr. Draper to include him in my will. Since I travel soon, I request Mr. Warrington carry my trinket-box to London in his postchaise; robberies are frequent, and I dislike being halted by highwaymen."

Maria looked blank at the prospect of Harry's departure, hoping for his escort back to Castlewood, where her elder brother had returned. "Nay," his aunt countered. "The lad has been tied to our apron-strings long enough. A day in London will do him no harm. He can complete my errand and return by Saturday."

"I would accompany Mr. Warrington," Mr. Sampson interjected, noting he preached on Friday before Lady Yarmouth, a performance Madame Bernstein had influenced the King's favorite to witness.

Harry embraced the idea of a rattling journey to London, eager for sport. He promised his pistols were reliable and would deliver the diamonds safely to the banker's vault. Would he stay at his aunt's London house? No, it was dreary, staffed only by a maid and groom. He preferred the Star and Garter in Pall Mall or an inn in Covent Garden. "Ah! I have often discussed this journey," Harry said, his expression darkening.

"And with whom, sir?" Lady Maria asked.

"With one who promised to join me," the young man murmured, his mind drifting tenderly to his lost brother. Sudden gusts of grief often swept over him—the separation from his sibling, the memory of George's fall, plans abandoned. "I doubt," the chaplain whispered to Madame Bernstein after one such episode, "that some in England mourn their elder brother so profoundly."

Nevertheless, sorrow was not eternal. Mr. Warrington set forth for London with eagerness, cheerful and happy, perhaps relieved to escape his elderly attachment. At Castlewood, on an unfortunate evening, he had offered his heart to his mature cousin, who had accepted. Marriage was now impossible; he must consult his mother, the mistress of the Virginian estate. Her refusal was certain. This thought hung like a weight around his neck, breeding remorse.

His spirits lifted as he neared London, gazing delightedly from the postchaise window at the city approaching. No highwayman intercepted him on Blackheath. Yonder gleamed the domes of Greenwich, wooded and grand. The Thames stretched out, crowded with ships, and there stood the Tower of London. "Look, Gumbo! The Tower!" "Yes, master," Gumbo replied, unaware of

The morning light in London was already a commodity, bought and sold alongside the newsprint of the *Advertiser*, which arrived with breakfast. Mr. Warrington read the invitation to the trial of skill between Sutton and Figg, a declaration of war disguised as sport. He decided to attend. He drove his newly purchased horses to Marybone Fields, the Wooden House. The driver, a young charioteer unfamiliar with the geography of ambition, veered wildly through green lanes, tangling the route past Whitfield's tabernacle and the hospital grounds until the destination was finally reached.

Outside, the air was thick with the smell of unwashed bodies and anticipation. A crowd of London blackguards pressed against the doors of the temple of British valour. Among them, the horses and equipages of the fashionable few, including Mr. Warrington, sought patronage. Beggars and cripples hustled for coins; shoe-black boys scrambled for the privilege of polishing his boots; women selling flowers and fruit offered their wares to Mr. Gumbo. Piemen, tramps, and strollers filled the perimeter. A flag snapped in the wind. From the stage, accompanied by a drummer and a horn-blower, a manager announced the imminent beginning of the noble English sports.

Warrington paid his fee and secured a seat in a gallery offering a clear view of the platform. Below, in the amphitheatre, Mr. Gumbo settled in, or retreated to the outer world to drink beer or gamble with coachmen when fatigue set in. Around the venue, the old society moved with a heavy encumbrance of servitude. Gentlemen and women could scarcely walk without a retinue of liveried vassals. Every theatre possessed a footman's gallery; armies of servants crowded chapel doors, swarmed anterooms, sprawled on hallways, and guzzled in corners. They bullied visitors for tips, played cards, and debauched the peace of the streets. That noble, melancholy race—the footman—is nearly extinct. Only a few thousand remain, visible perhaps on levee days with their nosegays and buckles, their plush coats and powdered hair. Like the Red Indians of America, whose camps and villages once dotted the continent, they are passing out of the world where they once walked in glory. The fatal decree has gone forth. Uncas with his tomahawk and Jeames with his cocked hat are fading into the mist.

Before the principals appeared, minor warriors tested the air. A boxing match occurred, but neither fighter was particularly game, nor severely punished, yielding little pleasure to the spectators. Cudgel-play followed, resulting in broken heads of negligible note and wounds so trifling they provoked hissing and grumbling. "The masters!" the crowd shouted. Finally, the champions emerged.

First walked the intrepid Sutton, sword in hand, saluting the company with a warlike grace, making an especial bow to a private box occupied by a stout gentleman of apparent importance. He was swiftly followed by the famous Figg, to whom the stout gentleman waved approval. Both men were shirtless, their heads shaved clean, bearing the cracks and scars of numerous glorious battles. On their burly sword-arms, they wore ribbons of their respective colours. The gladiators shook hands. As a contemporary poet noted, "The word it was bilboe."

Figg struck first. The blow was tremendous, intended to shear Sutton's honest head off as cleanly as a knife chops a carrot. But Sutton intercepted the blade with his own sword. The force was so mighty that Figg's weapon broke in his hands, less constant than the heart of the man who wielded it. New swords were distributed. Blood spouted from Figg's panting side amidst a yell of delight from Sutton's supporters. Yet, the veteran appealed to his audience, specifically the stout individual in the private gallery, revealing that the wound stemmed from the broken sword of the previous encounter.

During the pause, a gentleman in a riding-frock and plain wig entered the stout personage's box. It was Lord March and Ruglen, Warrington's friend from Tunbridge Wells. Lord March, usually sparing with politeness, displayed singular deference to the stout gentleman, receiving bank-bills with a profound bow. Then, spotting the Virginian, the lord crossed to Warrington's gallery and greeted him warmly.

They watched the combat, which shifted from swords to quarter-staffs. Victory ultimately remained with the ancient champion, Figg. Throughout the battle, a thunderstorm broke over the building. Warrington gladly accepted a seat in Lord March's chariot, leaving his own phaeton to be driven home by his groom. Harry was in great delectation, pronouncing this the finest sport he had seen since arriving in England. He sighed, wishing his elder brother were there to share the amusement, then stopped himself.

"What do you wish and what don't you wish?" Lord March asked.

"I was thinking," Harry replied, "of my elder brother. We promised to have our sport together at home. Many times we talked of it. But he wouldn't have liked this rough sort of sport, nor fighting, though he was the bravest lad alive."

"Oh! he was the bravest lad alive, was he?" the lord queried, lolling on his cushion.

"You should have seen him in a quarrel with a very gallant officer," Harry continued. "It was hard to keep George off him. I never saw a fellow so cool, nor more savage and determined. Ah! For the honour of the country, I wish he could have come here instead of me, and shown you a real Virginian gentleman."

"Nay, sir, you'll do very well," the amused nobleman said. "What is this I hear of Lady Yarmouth taking you into favour?"

"I will do as well as another," Harry admitted. "I can ride, and I think I can shoot better than George. But then my brother had the head, sir, the head!" He tapped his own skull. "He had read almost every book ever written; could play the fiddle and harpsichord; could compose poetry and sermons most elegant. What can I do? I am only good to ride, play cards, and drink Burgundy." The penitent hung his head. "But them I can do as well as most fellows."

"By Gad, Mr. Warrington," Lord March said, relishing the young man's naivete like a jaded voluptuary enjoying wholesome mutton-chops, "you ought to be taken to Exeter Change and put in a show. A gentleman from Virginia who has lost his elder brother and absolutely regrets him. The breed isn't known in this country. Upon my honour, I believe you would like to have him back again."

"Believe!" cried the Virginian, turning red.

"That is, you believe you believe you would like him back. But depend on it you wouldn't. 'Tis not in human nature, sir."

As they approached fine houses—Sir Richard Littleton's, Lord Bingley's—they discussed the town's chaotic expansion. Montagu House had become a museum of stuffed birds and rhinoceroses; a new road had been cut behind Bedford House Gardens, spoiling the Duke's comfort. Where would the town stop? Would they dine at Pall Mall or with Warrington? Spend the evening with the Queen of Spades?

"With the Queen of Spades, if your lordship pleases," Warrington blushed.

The equipage drove to his hotel in Covent Garden. The landlord, recognizing Lord March, bowed his wig onto the nobleman's shoes in humble welcome. A rich young English peer in the reign of George the Second; a wealthy patrician in the reign of Augustus. Which would you prefer?

After the best English dinner possible, wine flowed in plenty, of quality sufficient for an epicurean earl. Talk turned to fireworks at Vauxhall or cards. Harry, having seen only a dozen squibs at Williamsburg on the fifth of November—a display he considered sublime—yielded to his guest's preference for piquet. They were soon absorbed in the game.

Harry began by winning, as usual. But within half an hour, luck turned to favour Lord March. Just then, Mr. Draper, Warrington's man of business, bowed into the room to accept an invitation to sit and drink. Warrington always invited everyone to partake of his best. If he had a crust, he divided it; a haunch, he shared it; a jug of water, he drank with a kindly spirit; a bottle of Burgundy, he consumed with a thirsty friend. Such virtues are rare in our selfish world. We may bring them from the country when young, but they mostly wither after transplantation, drooping and perishing in the stifling London air.

Draper cared little for wine but delighted to be in the company of a great man. He protested that nothing pleased him more than watching piquet played by two consummate players of fashion. Undismayed by the sidelong scowls of his lordship, he surveyed the game. Harry was no match for the experienced club player, especially with Lord March holding superior cards.

Their stakes were no concern of Draper's. The gentlemen agreed to play for shillings, counting gains and losses in an undertone with scarce conversation. A bow, a grave manner, and the game continued.

Then came a second interruption, drawing execrations from Lord March. Scuffling outside, whispers, then an outcry of a woman in tears. Finally, a female rushed in, producing an explosion of naughty language from the lord.

"I wish your women would take some other

The narrative begins not with a hero, but with a confession of familial rot. A narrator recounts his intimacy with a man from an unnamed island—a place deemed a jewel by impartial critics, yet populated by a lineage of pure depravity. This family tree was a forest of crimes: ten commandments shattered, murders committed with the casual efficiency of Thurtell or Turpin, and forgeries so brazen they defied invention. Brother Tim had inherited his father's gray hair along with his sorrow; Brother Mick had desecrated parish churches; Sister Annamaroia had jilted a captain, stolen a grandmother's will, and pilfered silverware until a knife-boy named Larry paid the ultimate price. Yet, amidst this catalogue of hellish acts, the family maintained a grotesque, amicable correspondence. They wrote to one another as "dearest brother" and "dearest sister," sharing drinks tainted by patricide and kisses stained by perjury. They wept at reunions, clutching memories of a castle that no longer existed, their grief masking the fact that they could hang each other if they chose.

This capacity for forgiveness struck the narrator as a peculiar, angelic virtue. After hurling insults, they apologized; after flinging decanters, they embraced. It was a performance of peace, a rationalization of chaos. The aunt and niece at Tunbridge Wells exemplified this. They played cards, shared wings of chicken, and scratched neighbors' eyes while sparing each other's. Their reconciliation was a theatrical display, a way to ignore the loaded firelocks resting quietly beside them. Lady Maria, holding Harry's unpaid bond in her pocket, treated him not as a debtor but as a pet, granting him leave of absence on parole. She allowed him to frisk through London like a mousekin, while her cat, Grimalkin, watched with green, judgmental eyes.

While Harry wandered, the household sought diversion. Jack Morris, a man who delighted in the company of the titled and the loss of his coin, became a convenient distraction. Lady Maria, eager to win money, secured permission to play cards with him at the Countess of Yarmouth's assembly. For forty-eight hours, the atmosphere at the Wells was cheerful, a suspension of tension until Friday arrived. That day demanded a sermon from Mr. Sampson, a preacher renowned for his eloquence and ability to entertain. Knowing the power of Lady Yarmouth—who held the keys to bishoprics and livings—the congregation waited expectantly. If there were no concerts or dancing bears, they would endure the word.

Sampson prepared his finest discourse, aware that the Great Man might attend. In the church, the dynamic shifted instantly upon the arrival of the aristocracy. The curate, Trotter, faltered, hoping for a living from the Great Pew. His wife and daughters watched anxiously, gauging the effect of his words on the assembled dignitaries. But the moment the Right Honourable Lord Naseby began to snore in the pew by the fire, the illusion of piety evaporated. The visionary mitre dissolved into the rhythm of sleep.

Sampson, the domestic chaplain to Madame Bernstein, entered the scene rosy and handsome, wearing a fresh wig and a new cassock bought on credit. He marched from breakfast to church, flanked by lacqueys carrying gilt prayer books. The crowd was a mix of high fashion and ragged desperation. Among them were two men in worn coats, one with carrot-colored hair peeking from a scratch-wig. As the organ ceased, the stranger crossed himself mechanically, whispering, "Bedad, Tim, I forgawt!" revealing his origin from the same cursed island. Nearby, two men with hooked noses and sallow complexions, smelling of spirits, managed to slip past the beadle. One whispered, "We wants to be conwerted, gov'nor!" and slipped a coin into the beadle's palm. The barrier fell, the doors closed, and the service commenced.

Sampson ascended the pulpit, buoyant and jolly. Then, disaster struck. He turned pale as death, his eyes darting to the western door where the two Hebrew strangers stood like silent sentinels. Elsewhere, two perfumed Irishmen slipped from their seats and positioned themselves near the vestry door, casting meek glances at the floor. The sermon continued, but its content was irrelevant; the preacher was already consumed by panic.

Afterward, the ladies praised the sermon effusively. Madame Walmoden claimed it made her tremble; Madame Bernstein called it excellent. Lady Maria waved to Sampson, seeking acknowledgment, but he barely registered her gesture. His gaze remained fixed on the font, where the two Hebrew men still stood alone as the tide of humanity swept past them. The congregation dispersed in a rush, a throng, a dribble of individuals. Only the two strangers remained by the door.

Lady Maria, distressed by the smell of spirits emanating from the figures at the exit, voiced her outrage to her aunt's maid. Meanwhile, Madame Bernstein engaged in polite small talk with a gentleman, ignoring the impending storm. As the old lady insisted that her niece, the earl's daughter, precede her from the pew, the two "horrible men" advanced. One produced a long strip of paper. Lady Maria started, turning pale, and fled toward the vestry, hoping to seal the door. But the men were faster. One seized her shoulder, introducing himself as Costigan, a poor gentleman of Ireland forced into a disagreeable profession.

"At the suit of Mistress Pincott... I have the honour of arresting your leedyship," Costigan declared.

Lady Maria shrieked three times and collapsed. "Keep the door, Mick!" Costigan shouted, politely instructing Madame Bernstein to exclude others. "Her ladyship has fallen in a feinting fit."

"Unlace her, Brett!" cried the Baroness, her eyes twinkling with a strange, predatory light. As the maid loosened the necklace, the Baroness snatched a small bag hanging around Lady Maria's throat and severed the chain. "Dash some cold water over her face!" she commanded. "You stay with her, Brett. How much is your suit?"

Costigan replied, "The debt we hold against her leedyship is one hundred and thirty-two pounds."

Amidst this chaos, where was Mr. Sampson? Like the opossum who surrenders to the hunter rather than fight, he yielded to his pursuers. "At whose suit, Simons?" he asked, resigned.

"Buckleby Cordwainer," came the reply.

The letter from Tunbridge Wells arrived on a Saturday morning, dated the 25th of August, 1756. It was written by Mr. Warrington, his hand moving with the brisk confidence of a man who believes he has solved the equation of life. He reported success: three bets won, a journey from Bromley executed in record time, and a safe arrival at the Wells. He mentioned the horses, the postilion, the black man driving Mrs. Betty, and the lack of trouble on Blackheath despite suspicious riders. He ended with a promise of repayment for a game of piquet played the previous night. The tone was one of triumph, a declaration that the chaos of the road had been tamed by skill and speed.

Inside the house of captivity, the atmosphere was thick with the scent of stale food and suppressed fear. Lady Maria and the chaplain had been playing cards past midnight, a futile distraction from the reality of their confinement. The sound of wheels breaking the silence was not a gentle approach; it was a mechanical intrusion, a disruption of the stagnant air. The wheels whirred, slowed, and halted directly before the door. A pause followed—a tense negotiation at the gate—before Mrs. Betty emerged. Her face was a mask of conflicting emotions, radiating joy while her eyes overflowed with tears. Behind her stepped a figure that shattered the remaining tension: a tall young gentleman.

The chaplain dropped his cards, the sound sharp against the table, a signal of total surrender to the moment. Lady Maria rose, her color draining until she resembled a sheet of paper, before collapsing into the arms of her cousin. The reunion was immediate and overwhelming, a physical release of months of pressure. Kisses were exchanged, a torrent of affection that seemed to defy logic, flooding the small room.
