He had come to rescue her. The narrative of her imprisonment, her shame, her isolation—all of it evaporated under the weight of his presence. She wept, a genuine flood of relief, and leaned heavily on his arm. For a moment, stripped of her captors and her fears, she appeared more vibrant than she ever had in the history of her suffering. She did not faint; instead, they walked out, a united front against the world.

"You are both free," Harry said, his voice steady, cutting through the emotional haze. "Betty, fetch my lady's hat and cloak. Chaplain, we shall smoke a pipe at our lodgings; the ride has tired me." The chaplain, a man constructed entirely of sensitivity, reacted with a violence of feeling. He seized Harry's hand, kissing it fervently, praying aloud for divine blessing upon his patron. Harry felt a surge of warmth, a chemical reaction in his blood that confirmed his worthiness. To save the suffering was the highest function of existence. He marched beside his rescued princess, his hat cocked, imagining himself crowned by invisible trumpeters. He saw himself as a conqueror, a hero entering a hall of comfort. Yet, the reality was simpler: he had simply used a key to unlock a door. He had driven away a dragon with a gesture and silenced a tyrant with a coin. His vanity inflated, puffing him up like a balloon filled with hot air, but beneath it lay a core of good nature. He had saved two lives, extracted tears of gratitude, and now, perhaps, he would swagger a bit about London and White's Club. Let him brag; it was harmless.

Sampson, however, was not merely relieved; he was transformed. His nature was a sponge, absorbing pain and pleasure with equal intensity. As a preacher, he had trained his emotions to be tools, and now they were unleashed. He wept, laughing, roaring, and offering money with reckless abandon. He was in a state of rapturous gratitude, viewing Harry not just as a savior but as a god. In London, such a companion would be dangerous, but tonight, he was merely a vessel for adoration.

"The younger branch of our family," Harry announced with a superior air, "have treated you poorly. But by Jove, Sampson, I shall stand by you!" He spoke of the splendor of his lineage, a topic that always ignited his eloquence. He offered a banknote to cover his sister's schooling, a tangible proof of his benevolence. Sampson accepted the gift, overwhelmed, swearing oaths of unworthiness.

"I do not deserve this!" Sampson cried, dropping his head. "By George, I am a villain!"

"Pshaw," Harry dismissed, waving a hand. "That pocket-book I lost last week was empty anyway."

"No, sir! There was no money!" Sampson insisted, his voice trembling.

"How do you know?" Harry demanded, startled.

"Because I am a thief, sir. I found the book that night at Barbeau's when you were drunk."

"And the letters?" Harry asked, rising, his face flushing red.

"They told me everything, sir. Spies surround you, young and simple as you are. They cannot hide their secrets."

"Stories about Lady Fanny? About my cousin Will?"

"Yes, they are true," Sampson sighed. "The House of Castlewood has been cursed since the elder branch departed."

Harry lunged forward. "Do you dare breathe a word against Lady Maria?"

"Not for worlds!" Sampson replied, glancing sideways. "Though I must confess, she seems older than you, and perhaps a match ill-suited to your youth. Still, she possesses every virtue."

"She is too old, I know," Harry declared with majestic certainty. "But she has my word. Bring me the letters, and I will forgive your theft."

"My benefactor, let me forgive myself!" Sampson cried, retreating to his chamber.

He returned moments later, pale and shaken. He produced the pocket-book. "It bears your name, sir. But the letters are gone. My trunk and valise were tampered with. I am a guilty man, unable to restore what is owed." He knelt, clasping his hands in a posture of pathetic despair.

Who had entered the room? The landlady swore no one had come in. Nothing else was missing from Sampson's sparse belongings. The theft was specific, targeted. Was it Lady Maria? She had been imprisoned all day, unaware of the breach. She could not have stolen the letters. The clues pointed elsewhere: Case, the house steward's secret trip to London, his knowledge of the shoemaker where Sampson lodged, his access to the Esmond family's private affairs. These threads suggested the Baroness Bernstein. But why arrest Lady Maria? The chaplain knew nothing of the specific letter she had lost; she had not trusted him with her deepest secret.

Harry, intoxicated by his own success—the three bets, the rescue, the fine supper of partridges and Burgundy sent by Monsieur Barbeau—accepted Sampson's confession. He forgave the theft, shook the chaplain's hand, and invited him to drink.

"To the House of Esmond-Warrington!" Sampson toasted, tears streaming down his face.

"We are the elder branch," Harry corrected, his voice noble but slightly slurred. "My grandfather was the Marquis of Esmond. Here's to you, Chaplain—I forgive you—and God bless you. If you had been caught for three times as much, I would have paid it. Look, the sun has risen! We need no candles." He dismissed the chaplain to bed, secure in his victory.

At noon, Madame de Bernstein summoned him. She noted the changes in his attire, evidence of his recent shopping sprees in London. She called him her dearest child, thanking him for his generosity toward Maria. She recounted the shock of the church seizure and her own gambling losses, explaining why she had sent Case to London for funds.

"So Case went to London?" Harry asked.

"Of course. The Baroness de Bernstein cannot remain in debt." She turned to him, eyes pleading. "Can you lend me some, child?"

"I can give you twenty-two pounds," Harry blushed, counting his coins. "I have forty-four left until my Virginian remittances arrive. I have been extravagant buying horses and clothes."

"And rescued your relations in distress, you prodigal good boy. No, child, I do not want your money. Take this note for fifty pounds. Spend it, be merry! Your mother will repay me, even if she does not love me." She held out her hand, affectionate and commanding.

"When you need me, come to me."

Beatrix Bernstein's anger bubbled beneath her grace. "I love you, child, and yet I am furious. You have engaged yourself to poor Maria, who is as old as your mother? What will Madam Esmond say? You will starve before she dies."

"I have ten thousand pounds from my father," Harry argued. "Now that my brother is gone."

"The interest will not buy enough cards."

"We must stop playing cards," Harry conceded.

"It is beyond Maria's capacity. She will pawn your coat to play. The madness runs in our family—in me too. I warned you. I prayed you not to gamble with them. A lad of twenty engaging a woman of forty-two! Writing letters on his knees, signing with his heart's blood, promising to marry no other woman than his adorable cousin, Lady Maria Esmond. Oh, it is cruel! Cruel!"

"Madam, who showed you my letter?" Harry asked, burning with shame.

"An accident. She fainted when the bailiffs took her. Brett cut her laces. When she was carried off, we found a sachet on the floor.

The autumn air carried the scent of damp earth and departing summer, signaling the end of the Tunbridge season. Madame de Bernstein's destination was clear: Bath. It offered cards, company, and the vibrant pulse of life that the countryside lacked. Harry agreed to escort Lady Maria and the chaplain to Castlewood, though the promise tasted bitter on his tongue.

They passed Oakhurst village, the site of Harry's recent hospitality. Lady Maria's commentary on the local young ladies—her observations regarding their attempts to capture Harry's attention and the mother's obvious eagerness to secure a match—irritated him. Mr. Warrington, emboldened by this perceived slight, declared he would bypass their hosts' door. That evening, at the inn, the atmosphere curdled. Lady Maria's smiles failed to thaw Harry's expression; her manufactured tears elicited no sympathy. To her querulous remarks, he offered only grunts. Eventually, she retired to her chamber, sighing heavily. Harry bowed her out with a rigid politeness, consigning her to the care of the landlady and her maid.

Ten minutes after her departure, a horse galloped out of the yard. Later, when Mrs. Betty entered to retrieve smelling salts, she found Parson Sampson smoking a solitary pipe. Mr. Warrington had vanished, ostensibly for a moonlit walk, but his whereabouts were unknown. Sampson confirmed that Mr. Warrington intended to depart the following morning, seated beside Lady Maria's carriage. Yet, the darkness lingered on his brow. During the journey, silence reigned between them. Lady Maria, convinced he knew of her theft, fretted silently.

The truth lay elsewhere. On a steep hill near Oakhurst, three miles from the road, Lady Maria, leaning heavily on Harry's arm, had poured out sentimental vows. She was genuinely in love, unaware that her affection only deepened his coldness. Her face, fresh despite the lines of twenty years, cast shadows in the moonlight. Harry felt the weight of her dependence as an unbearable burden. He cursed the walk, the heat, and the wine that had compelled him to make a fatal pledge.

Her praises became noise. She recited scraps of plays, calling herself his champion, her Henrico, her Molinda, vowing eternal faithfulness. She clung to him, asking if his precious image and hair, kept in a silken case, would accompany her to the grave. Harry realized then that Madame Bernstein had burned the contents of that case—the hair and the note—and Maria still held onto the fiction. It was at that moment, as he suddenly jerked his arm free, that she felt the sharp pang of remorse for her deception.

At Castlewood, Harry's mood did not improve. Lord Esmond was absent, as were the ladies. Only Mr. Will returned from partridge shooting, turning pale at the sight of his cousin and receiving a sulky scowl in return. Despite this, they met at supper. The conversation was civil but strained until Harry mentioned the brown horse.

"Your horse! What horse?" Will asked, confused.

"The brown horse," Harry stated curtly. "For which I played you, and which I won the night before you rode away."

Will feigned ignorance. Harry summoned Parson Sampson to act as umpire. The chaplain confirmed Harry had wagered fifty guineas against the horse. Will admitted he had won the other horse but dismissed the value of the brown steed, laughing at the low sale price of thirty shillings. Harry maintained a studied courtesy, offering chicken to Lady Maria, locking the servants out, and pocketing the key.

Once the door clicked shut, the tension snapped. Will, gripping a knife, demanded to know the meaning of the maneuver. Harry revealed the stakes: fifty guineas against the horse. Will denied the debt, claiming it cost Harry no more effort to lie than to cheat. Harry stepped closer, warning the chaplain to ensure fair play, before launching into an insult Will could not hear.

Will retaliated, hurling a wine bottle. It missed Harry's head, shattered against the wainscot, destroying a portrait of an Esmond ancestor, and splashed port wine over the chaplain's wig. The parson cried out for quiet, but the gentlemen ignored the Church's commands. Harry seized a large silver-handled knife, driving it toward his cousin. He intercepted the thrust, catching Will's wrist, and delivered a rapid blow to the nose. Will reeled backward, dropping his weapon, which Harry's opponent kicked under the table.

Will recovered, charging forward like a battering ram. Harry, recalling the brutal combat styles of Marybone and the plantations, sidestepped. He struck Will's right ear with tremendous force, knocking him unconscious against the heavy oak table.

"Chaplain, bear witness to a fair fight!" Harry shouted, drawing the key to unlock the lobby where servants had heard the commotion. They entered to find two victims: the chaplain drenched in port, and Will Esmond lying in his own blood.

Harry ordered the neckcloth undone and a fleam fetched. Will soon regained consciousness, his eye swollen shut, cursing freely. Harry noted his recovery with grim humor, asking if he desired more drink. Will, enraged, struck the black servant Gumbo, who had approached to offer assistance. Gumbo explained that Harry had nearly cut his tongue. The household servants, who loved Harry, refused to eject the guest, declaring the fight fair and noting that Will was accustomed to such treatment.

Betty mocked Will's departure, saying he had lost his zipper and was going to bed. Will retreated, leaving a trail of curses. The next morning, Harry sent a polite note inviting Will to join him in London or elsewhere, threatening to frighten highwaymen on Hounslow Heath. Will responded with violence, demanding Harry leave Hounslow.

Simultaneously, a letter arrived from Molinda, written in a mix of English and Greek, pleading for peace and forgiveness. Harry read it with weariness, reflecting on how the golden tablets of romance had faded into leaves. His dignity forbade a prolonged stay at Castlewood after blinding a son of the house. He drafted a formal explanation, supervised by Parson Sampson, ensuring the spelling remained impeccable. He informed Maria that remaining would be improper and that his absence was necessary. She wept profusely at the prospect of losing him, imagining him finding no one who loved him as she did.

Harry left, cheerful and singing to his horses, nodding at roadside beauties and chuckling at the landlady. He was not inconsolable; he craved the glitter of London, the crowds of St. James's, and the thrills of Newmarket. By the time he arrived, the forty-four pounds he had carried from Tunbridge were gone. He needed funds. With two sums of five thousand pounds in his and his brother's name, he felt secure. He would borrow, rely on a streak of luck at the gaming tables, and restore his capital. For now, he must maintain the appearance of a gentleman, proving that a man of his rank could not survive on a miserly two hundred a year.

The air in the Royal Library was stagnant, thick with the dust of dead arguments. I sat at a wooden desk, cataloging the lies of the past to construct a slightly different sequence of lies for the future. It was a mechanical task, the quiet rearrangement of history until it sang a flat, circular tune. One afternoon, a clerk brought me a volume. *Memoirs of M. d'Artagnan*. Printed in Amsterdam by Pierre Rouge, a man who understood that inconvenient honesty led to the Bastille, and so the author had fled to write in the safety of exile. Now, both the author and his jailers were equally forgotten.

I took the book to my rented room. I turned the pages. There were portraits of the period within it—kings, queens, cardinals, the interchangeable courtiers. They did not interest me. They were the usual statues, rigid and silent. But in the margins of this official history, the author described a mundane waiting room. It was the antechamber of M. de Tréville, a captain of the Musketeers. Here, d'Artagnan encountered three young men in uniform.

Athos. Porthos. Aramis.

The names were unnatural. Mythological remnants ending in *os* and *is*. They were obviously false. They were pseudonyms assumed by men who had likely donned a uniform out of financial ruin, social disgrace, or a profound exhaustion with their own given identities. They had erased themselves to become functions of the state. I felt a sudden, inexplicable kinship with this erasure.

I began to search for them. I spent weeks requesting catalogs, folios, and registers. I compiled lists of nothing. The effort was immense and entirely pointless. I was on the verge of abandoning the inquiry, ready to accept that these men were merely the inventions of a mediocre soldier, when, prompted by the idle suggestion of an acquaintance named Paulin Paris, I requested another manuscript.

Consciousness returned as a sequence of dull, rhythmic aches. The floorboards of the Jolly Miller smelled of stale wine and centuries of trampled mud. Above him, the innkeeper's mouth moved. The words were a ledger of inconveniences: a disturbance had been caused, a departure was required. The world, functioning on a brutal economy of space and quiet, had no use for a bleeding boy.

D'Artagnan assembled his limbs. It was a mechanical effort, the mere function of bone and bruised tissue. He dragged himself toward the light of the doorway. Outside, the gray geometry of the courtyard framed a heavy carriage. Two Norman horses stood breathing steam into the cold air. Inside the carriage sat a woman—pale, blonde, possessing a beautiful and terrifying blankness. Beside her stood the man in the violet doublet.

Observing them, D'Artagnan felt the sudden, exhausting obligation to perform his identity. He had been taught that he was a gentleman; therefore, he was required to be outraged. He pushed himself over the threshold. He formed a shout, a hollow sound against the vast, indifferent sky.

The man in violet did not draw his sword. He merely looked. It was a gaze of absolute detachment, a look that reduced D'Artagnan to a minor atmospheric disturbance. The carriage departed. The man mounted his horse and rode away. D'Artagnan tried to pursue, but the physical reality of his damaged body intervened. He collapsed into the dirt, muttering words of vengeance that dissolved immediately into the dust. He fainted, slipping back into the void.

When he woke again, the inventory of his existence had diminished. Eleven crowns remained in a velvet purse. He sat in the dim kitchen and mixed oil, wine, and rosemary. The recipe was his mother's, a domestic ritual applied to the senseless violence of the world. He rubbed the paste into his skin. The flesh sealed, but the underlying structure remained exactly as fragile as before.

The rain fell on London with a steady, indifferent rhythm, washing the grime from the cobblestones but doing little to cleanse the air of the city's inherent rot. Harry Warrington stood in the corner of a smoky room, watching the dice tumble across the green baize table. He was a man of bright lace and expensive velvet, a figure of golden hair and fair complexion, yet beneath the surface lay a calculation as cold as the winter wind outside.

He remembered the promise he had made to his cousin, the one extracted with such force that it now hung over him like a debt. It was a dismal engagement, one he would gladly shed if he could. Perhaps the beating he had administered to poor Will Esmond served this purpose. A boxing match would inevitably lead to a family quarrel; in that quarrel, Maria might take her brother's side. Harry would offer no retraction, no apology. Will, forced to call him to account, would discover which was the stronger man. In the chaos of the feud, the agreement would dissolve, and Harry would be free.

So honest Harry had laid his trap and lit the fuse. The explosion had occurred, leaving Will's nose swollen and his eye black for a week, but no blood had been spilled. Will had risen from the floor, shaken but standing. How many sensible men would settle their accounts in private, with no witnesses to record the score? Maria had not taken her family's side; she had declared for her cousin, as had my lord, when advised of the disturbance. Lord Castlewood claimed Will had struck the first blow, citing the chaplain's provocation. It was not the first time Will had been found quarreling in his cups. Mr. Warrington had shown proper spirit in resenting the injury, and it was for Will, not Harry, to ask pardon.

Harry refused any apology until his horse was returned or his bet paid. The chronicler cannot determine how the affair of the bet was finally resolved, but it is certain the cousins met in the houses of various friends, avoiding further mauling.

Maria's elder brother had initially been content to see his sister, who had remained unmarried for so many years, marry any gentleman who presented himself. If it were a Virginian, so much the better. She would retreat to his wigwam in the forest, where she could be disposed of. In the natural course of things, Harry would survive his elderly bride, consoling himself or not as he preferred after her departure.

But after an interview with Aunt Bernstein, which his lordship had conducted upon arriving in London, he changed his mind. He even attempted to dissuade Maria from the match, professing pity for the young fellow condemned to a life of misery because of a silly promise made at twenty-one.

Misery, indeed! Maria was at a loss to understand why he should be miserable. Pity, forsooth! My lord at Castlewood had seen no pity in it. Maria knew what pity meant. Her brother had visited Aunt Bernstein; she had offered money to break the match. She understood my lord's meaning, but Mr. Warrington was a man of honor, and she trusted him.

Away walked my lord to White's, or whatever haunt he frequented. It is probable his sister had guessed too accurately the nature of his conversation with Madame Bernstein.

"And so," he thought, "the end of my virtue is likely to be that the Mohock falls a prey to others, and there is no earthly use in my sparing him. 'Quem deus vult'—what was that schoolmaster's adage? If I do not have him, somebody else will. That is clear. My brother has had a slice; my dear sister wants to swallow the whole of him bodily. Here have I been at home respecting his youth and innocence, declining to play beyond the value of a sixpence, acting as guardian and mentor. Why am I but a fool to fatten a goose for other people to feed upon? Not many good actions have I done in this life, and here is this one that benefits whom? Others. Talk of remorse! By all the fires and furies, the remorse I feel is for things I have not done and might have done! Why did I spare Lucretia? She hated me ever after, and her husband went the way for which he was predestined. Why have I let this lad off? Those Marches and the rest, who do not want him, may pluck him! And I have a bad reputation; I am the man people point at, calling the wicked lord, against whom women warn their sons! Pardi, I am not a penny worse, only a great deal more unlucky than my neighbors, and it is only my cursed weakness that has been my greatest enemy!"

Here, manifestly, in recording a speech which a gentleman only thought, the chronicler overdraws his account with the patient reader, who has a right not to accept this draft on his credulity. Yet have not Livy, Thucydides, and a score of other historians made speeches for their heroes which the latter never thought of delivering? How much more may we, knowing my Lord Castlewood's character so intimately, declare what was passing in his mind and transcribe his thoughts onto this paper? What? A whole pack of wolves hunts this lamb, and will make a meal of him presently, while one hungry old hunter stands by, denied a single cutlet? Who has not admired that noble speech of my Lord Clive, when reproached on his return from India for making rather too free with jaghires, lakhs, gold mohurs, diamonds, pearls, and what not? "Upon my life," said the hero of Plassy, "when I think of my opportunities, I am surprised I took so little!"

To tell disagreeable stories of a gentleman until one is forced to impart them is always painful to a feeling mind. Hence, though I have known, before the very first page of this history was written, what sort of a person my Lord Castlewood was, and in what esteem he was held by his contemporaries, I have kept back much that was unpleasant about him, allowing only the candid reader to perceive that he was a nobleman who ought not to be liked. It is true that my Lord March and other gentlemen of whom he complained would have thought no more of betting with Mr. Warrington for his last shilling and taking their winnings than they would scruple to pick the bones of a chicken. They would take any advantage of the game, their superior skill, or their private knowledge of the horses. In so far, they followed the practice of all gentlemen: but when they played, they played fair; and when they lost, they paid.

Madame Bernstein was loath to tell her Virginian nephew all she knew to his family's discredit. She was even touched by my lord's forbearance regarding Harry on his first arrival in Europe, and pleased with his compliance with her wishes in this particular. But in the conversation she had with her nephew Castlewood concerning Maria's designs on Harry, he had spoken his mind with his usual cynicism. He voted himself a fool for sparing a lad whom no sparing would eventually keep from ruin. He pointed out Mr. Harry's undeniable extravagances, his spendthrift associates, his nights at faro and hazard, and his rides to Newmarket. He asked why he alone should keep his hands from the young fellow.

In vain Madame Bernstein pleaded that Harry was poor. Bah! he was heir to a principality which ought to have been his, Castlewood's, and might have set up their ruined family. Indeed, Madame Bernstein thought Mr. Warrington's Virginian property much greater than it was. Were there not money-lenders in the town who would give him money on post-obits in plenty? Castlewood knew as much to his cost: he had applied to them in his father's lifetime, and the cursed crew had eaten up two-thirds of his miserable income. He spoke with such desperate candor and ill-humor that Madame Bernstein began to be alarmed for her favorite and determined to caution him at the first opportunity.

That evening she began to pen a billet to Mr. Warrington. But all her life long she was slow with her pen and disliked using it. "I never knew any good come of writing more than bonjour or business," she used to say. "What is the use of writing ill, when there are so many clever people who can do it well? And even then, it were best left alone." So she sent one of her men to Mr. Harry's lodgings, bidding him come and drink a dish of tea with her the next day, when she proposed to warn him.

But the next morning she was indisposed and could not receive Mr. Harry when he came. She kept her chamber for a couple of days, and the next day there was a great engagement, and the next day Mr. Harry was off on some expedition of his own. In the whirl of London life, what man sees his neighbor, what brother his sister, what schoolfellow his old friend? Ever so many days passed before Mr. Warrington and his aunt had that confidential conversation which the latter desired.

She began by scolding him mildly about his extravagance and madcap frolics (though, in truth, she was charmed with him for both). He replied that young men will be young men, and that it was in dutifully waiting in attendance on his aunt that he had made the acquaintance with whom he mostly lived at present. She then, with some prelude, began to warn him regarding his cousin, Lord Castlewood. On this, he broke into a bitter laugh and said the good-natured world had told him plenty about Lord Castlewood already.

The air in Bond Street was thick with the scent of beeswax and expensive wool, a static weight that seemed to press against the lungs of anyone unfortunate enough to breathe it. Harry Warrington sat within this atmosphere, a figure of considerable girth and even greater fortune, drinking bumpers of vintage wine as if the liquid were merely water. To the fashionable world, he was the Fortunate Youth, a title worn as comfortably as a tailored coat. He was young, handsome, and possessed a specific brand of luck that allowed society to treat him with a deference usually reserved for saints or kings.

Do not mistake this for hypocrisy. Let us not cry out against the selfishness of the world for welcoming the young and the beautiful while ignoring the old and the broken. Society is an aggregate of choices, a collection of preferences where the lively, the well-dressed, and the amusing naturally crowd out the slouching, the ill-washed, and the misanthropic. Harry was liked because he was likeable; because he was rich, jovial, and brave; because he sang with drunkards and hunted with gentlemen; because he possessed a modest, blushing timidity around ladies that made him intriguing; and because he was magnificently generous to those beneath him. He was high and mighty, certainly, but in an era where rank dictated the very posture of one's spine, distance from the inferior was not a flaw but a feature. The Secretary of State knelt to the King; the Under-Secretary stood while his superiors reviewed papers. Such lines must be drawn. Yet, there remains a peculiar friction when Bob Bowstreet or Tom Garbage, men of obscure literary connections, slap one on the back and demand to be called "old boy" or by one's Christian name. It is a breach of protocol, a small rebellion against the necessary hierarchy.

Winter 1756–57 offered the town its usual fare of distractions. There were operas, assailed by satirists as foolish and popish, yet attended nonetheless. There were theaters hosting Garrick and Pritchard, and masquerades frequented by lords and ladies. There were private assemblies ending in cards, though Harry preferred White's Club, where the stakes were higher and the fairness more absolute.

One day, his kinsman Lord Castlewood escorted him to court, presenting him to the Sovereign, who had recently descended from Kensington. The reception was icy. The King, turning to his lordship in German, remarked that the Earl played too high at White's, then turned his royal back. Lord Castlewood recoiled, frightened by the cold dismissal.

"What does he say?" Harry asked.

"The Majesty thinks they play too high," the nobleman whispered, trembling.

"If he does not want us, we had better not come again," Harry replied simply. "I never considered that German fellow a real King of England."

"Hush!" cried Lord Castlewood, panic rising in his voice. "Hold your confounded colonial tongue! Do you not see the walls have ears?"

"And what then?" Harry countered, observing the courtiers. "Look at them! Just moments ago they shook my hand, bowed, and flattered me. Now they avoid me as if I carried the plague."

A broad-faced gentleman in a scarlet-laced waistcoat and an old-fashioned wig stepped forward. "Shake hands, nephew," he said, extending a large brown hand. "I heard what you said. I have ears like the wall. Since others show you the cold shoulder, I offer my hand." He grasped Harry firmly. "There is something of my brother in your eyes. Though I suppose in your island you grow more wiry and thin. I am your uncle, child. Sir Miles Warrington."

Lord Castlewood looked yellow with fright. "Yes, my dear Harry. Your paternal uncle."

"Might as well have come to see us in Norfolk as dangle about playing the fool at Tunbridge Wells," the Baronet grunted. "The old lady calls herself Madam Esmond, doesn't she?"

"My mother is not ashamed of her father's name, nor am I, uncle," Harry said, pride coloring his tone.

"Well said, lad! Come home and eat mutton with Lady Warrington at three, unless you cannot do without your White's kickshaws." The Baronet turned to Lord Castlewood. "You need not look frightened. I shall tell no tales."

"I—I am sure Sir Miles Warrington will act as a gentleman!" the lord stammered.

"Belike, he will," the Baronet growled, turning on his heel. "And thou wilt come, young man, at three. Mind, good roast mutton waits for nobody. Thou hast a great look of thy father. Lord bless us, how we used to beat each other! He was smaller, younger, yet many a time he had the best of it. Take it he was henpecked when he married, and Madam Esmond took the spirit out of him when she got him in her island. Virginia is an island. Ain't it an island?"

Harry laughed. "No!"

The jolly Baronet chuckled. "Island or not, thou must come and tell all about it to my lady. She'll know whether 'tis an island or not."

"My dear Mr. Warrington," Lord Castlewood pleaded as they exited the King's apartments onto Saint James's Street, "in this great city, every man has enemies. Detraction and scandal abound. I never mentioned Sir Miles to you precisely because we have had differences. Should he make remarks to my disparagement, you must receive them *cum grano salis*, remembering they come from an enemy."

By the time Harry reached White's, the news of his rejection had already circulated. The King's jealousy regarding Harry's favor with Lady Yarmouth was a settled fact. A score of gentlemen congratulated him on the conquest. By nightfall, the cold shoulder at court was merely another victory for the Fortunate Youth.

That evening, Harry appeared at the Baronet's dinner table. Sir Miles rallied him in his simple, rustic fashion. Lady Warrington, initially a grand and stately figure, admitted that the world's bad reputation had given her a frigid welcome. Her daughters greeted him with stiff, prim curtseys—"How d'ye do, cousin?" and "No, thank you, cousin"—before departing. Their little boy, the heir, dined under the watchful eye of his governor. After dinner, with a glass of port, the child loosened his tongue. Looking hard at Harry, he asked, "Are you wicked, cousin Harry? You don't look very wicked!"

"My dear Master Miles!" the tutor exclaimed, turning red.

"But you said he was wicked!" the child insisted.

"We are all miserable sinners, Miley," the father explained gently. "Have you not heard the clergyman say so every Sunday?"

"Yes, but not so very wicked as cousin Harry. Is it true that you gamble, drink all night with wicked men, and frequent wicked women? You know you said so, Mr. Walker—and mamma said so, too, that Lady Yarmouth was a wicked woman."

"And you are a little pitcher," the father cried, dismissing the child to his sisters. "Take Miles to his sisters, Mr. Walker, and Topsham shall give thee a ride in the park on thy little horse." The idea of the horse consoled the boy, who had begun to cry bitterly, bawling that he would rather stay with his wicked cousin.

"They have made you a sad reputation among 'em, nephew!" Sir Miles noted later, pouring wine. "My wife, since the death of my poor eldest son, has taken to Tottenham Court Road and Mr. Whitfield's preaching. We have had a ward recount sad stories about you and your brother."

"About me, Sir Miles, as much as he pleases," Harry declared, warm with port. "But I'll break any man's bones who dares say a word against my brother! That fellow was not fit to buckle my dear George's shoe. If I find him repeating what he dared say in our house in Virginia, I promise him a second caning."

"You seem to stand up for your friends, nephew Harry," the Baronet observed. "Fill thy glass, lad. Thou art not as bad as thou hast been painted. I always told my lady so. I drink Madam Esmond Warrington's health, of Virginia, and will have a full bumper for that toast."

Harry drained his glass, refilled it, and toasted Lady Warrington and Master Miles.

"Thou wouldst be heir to four thousand acres in Norfolk did he die, though," the Baronet added.

"God forbid, sir, and be praised that I have acres enough in Virginia of my own!" Harry replied.

He moved to take coffee with Lady Warrington, talking easily to the young ladies. One resembled Fanny Mountain and became his special favorite. When he left, they agreed their wicked cousin was not nearly as wicked as imagined. Lady Warrington hoped to rescue him from the pit. That evening, while Harry was at White's, she sent him a copy of *Law's Call* with a note hoping it would serve him well. Once dispatched, she and her daughters went to a rout at a minister's house.

Before heading to White's, Harry drove to Tavistock Street to visit his friend Mr. Sparks. He purchased more trinkets for his female cousins, claiming they were gifts "from their aunt in Virginia." He was full of kindness, kindled and warmed by his prosperity. Not all men respond to wealth with such grace

The rejection of his peace offerings ignited a fury in the young American chief that burned hotter than any battlefield rage. His wrath was not merely directed at Colonel Lambert, but consumed the entire lineage of that gentleman's family. In his mind, the humiliation was total: he had been displayed before the girls as a spectacle of failure. Mr. Wolfe and the Colonel, those perpetual preachers of morality, had treated him with the condescension of handlers managing a stray dog, offering protection that felt more like a cage. They held him up to the family as a scapegrace, a prodigal son whose very existence seemed to mock their virtue. Their refusal to shake his hand was a final severance; when he attempted to offer even a scrap of common gratitude, they threw his gifts back in his face, treating his generosity as an insult.

"Sir," Parson Sampson observed, his voice rising with a mixture of piety and greed, his eyes fixed on the two morocco boxes resting on white satin cushions. "These things must be worth a small fortune."

"They cost some money, Sampson," the young man replied, his tone weary. "Not that I would grudge ten times the amount to people who have shown kindness."

"No, faith, sir, not if I know your honour!" Sampson interjected, seizing the opportunity to praise his patron, his face a mask of servile adoration.

"The repeater, they told me, was a bargain in Paris, worth a hundred pounds. Little Miss Hetty mentioned she longed for a repeating watch."

"Oh, what a lovely sentiment!" cried the chaplain, his imagination running wild. "With a circle of pearls on the back and a diamond knob for the handle! It would win any woman's heart, Sir!"

"There passes an apple-woman with a basket," Mr. Warrington shouted suddenly, his temper flaring. "I have a mind to fling the thing out to her!"

When Harry stepped out onto business, heading toward the City and the Temple, his parasite did not follow him far into the Strand. Fear of Chancery Lane, its labyrinthine inhabitants, and its oppressive precincts held him back. Instead, Mr. Warrington proceeded to his broker, and they walked to the Bank. There, amidst the clatter of coins and the murmur of commerce, they conducted some trivial business. After signing a few insignificant documents, Harry departed with a handful of crisp banknotes tucked safely in his pocket. The broker then escorted Mr. Warrington to one of the great dining houses, famous in the City then as now. Afterwards, he guided him along the Virginian walk upon 'Change. Harry passed through it with a distinct sense of shame. He wondered what a certain lady in Virginia would think if she knew he was carrying off, in that bottomless gambler's pocket, a significant portion of his father's patrimony. He imagined the whispers among the Virginia merchants: *"That is young Esmond, of Castlewood, on the Potomac, Madam Esmond's son; and he has been losing his money at play, selling out so much, and so much, and so much."*

His spirits did not lift until he had passed beneath the traitors' heads of Temple Bar and emerged fully from the City. From the Strand, Mr. Harry walked home, glancing into St. James's Street, but the streets were empty; the company did not gather at the Chocolate-House until a later hour.

Upon arriving home, Mr. Harry produced his bundle of banknotes. He selected three, placed them inside a sheet of paper, and sealed it carefully. Within the sheet, he had previously written: *"Much good may they do you. H. E. W."* He directed this packet to the Reverend Mr. Sampson, leaving it on the chimney-glass with instructions for his servants to deliver it to the divine upon his arrival.

Now, his honour's phaeton was brought to the door. He stepped in, intending to drive around the park, but the rain began to fall, or perhaps the east wind blew, or some other reason arose. His honour turned his horses' heads down St. James's Street and returned to White's at about three o'clock. Scarcely anyone had arrived yet; it was the hour when folks were at dinner. However, there was his cousin Castlewood, lounging over the *Public Advertiser*, having just returned from his duties at Court nearby.

Lord Castlewood yawned over the newspaper. "What shall they do?" he asked. "Shall we have a little piquet?" Harry had no objection to a little piquet. "Just for an hour," said Lord Castlewood. "I dine at Arlington Street at four." "Just for an hour," agreed Mr. Warrington. They called for cards.

"Or shall we have them upstairs?" suggested my lord. "Out of the noise?"

"Certainly, out of the noise," said Harry.

By five o'clock, a half-dozen gentlemen had arrived after dinner, engaging in cards, coffee, or talk. The regulars from the ordinary had not yet cleared their tables. Often, the gentlemen of White's would remain seated until past midnight.

One toothpick pointed over the coffee-house blinds into the street. "Whose phaeton?" asked Toothpick 1 of Toothpick 2.

"The Fortunate Youth's," said No. 2.

"Not so fortunate the last three nights. Luck confoundedly against him. Lost, last night, thirteen hundred to the table. Mr. Warrington been here today, John?"

"Mr. Warrington is in the house now, sir. In the little tea-room with Lord Castlewood since three o'clock. They are playing at piquet," said John.

"What fun for Castlewood!" said No. 1, with a shrug.

The second gentleman growled an execration. "Curse the fellow!" he said. "He has no right to be in this club at all. He doesn't pay if he loses. Gentlemen ought not to play with him. Sir Miles Warrington told me at court the other day that Castlewood has owed him money on a bet for these three years."

"Castlewood," said No. 1, "doesn't lose if he plays alone. A large company flurries him, you see—that's why he doesn't come to the table." The facetious gentleman grinned, showing all his teeth, polished perfectly clean.

"Let's go up and stop 'em," growled No. 2.

"Why?" asked the other. "Much better to look out the window. A lamplighter is going up the ladder—a famous sport. Look at that old putt in the chair: did you ever see such an old quiz?"

"Who is that just gone out of the house? As I live, it's Fortunatus! He seems to have forgotten that his phaeton has been here, waiting all this time. I bet you two to one he has been losing to Castlewood."

"Jack, do you take me to be a fool?" asked one gentleman of the other. "Pretty pair of horses the youth has got. How he is flogging 'em!" They saw Mr. Warrington galloping up the street, with scared coachmen and chairmen clearing the way before him. Presently, my Lord Castlewood was seen entering a chair and departing.

Harry drove up to his own door. It was but a few yards, and those poor horses had been beating the pavement all this while in the rain. Mr. Gumbo was engaged at the door in conversation with a country-looking lass, who tripped off with a curtsey. Mr. Gumbo was always engaged with some pretty maid or other.

"Gumbo, has Mr. Sampson been here?" asked Gumbo's master from his driving seat.

"No, sar. Mr. Sampson have not been here!" answered Mr. Warrington's gentleman. Harry bid him go upstairs and bring down a letter addressed to Mr. Sampson.

"Addressed to Mr. Sampson? Oh yes, sir," said Mr. Gumbo, who could not read.

"A sealed letter, stupid! On the mantelpiece, in the glass!" said Harry; and Gumbo leisurely retired to fetch that document. As soon as Harry had it, he turned his horses' heads toward St. James's Street. The two gentlemen, still yawning out of the window at White's, beheld the Fortunate Youth, in an instant, back again.

As they passed out of the little tea-room where he and Lord Castlewood had played piquet, Mr. Warrington saw that several gentlemen had entered the play-room and that there was a bank there. Some were already steadily at work, wearing their gaming jackets—they kept such coats at the club, putting them on when they intended to sit down to a regular night's play.

Mr. Warrington went to the clerk's desk, paid his account of the previous night, and, sitting down at the table, called for fresh counters. This had been decidedly an unlucky week for the Fortunate Youth, and tonight was no more fortunate than previous nights. He called for more counters, and more presently. He was a little pale and silent, though very easy and polite when spoken to. But he could not win.

At last, he got up. "Hang it! Stay and mend your luck!" said Lord March, who was sitting by his side with a heap of counters before him, green and white. "Take a hundred of mine, and go on!"

"I have had enough for tonight, my lord," said Harry, and he rose and went away. He

The air in the drawing room was thick with the scent of beeswax and suppressed anxiety. It was a room designed for performance, where every gesture was calculated to project a specific moral weight. Yet, beneath the surface of this carefully constructed piety, a different current flowed—one of desperation, of accounts balancing in the dark.

We have all encountered the theater of religion. The Sunday procession: the maid-servants trailing ribbons, the apprentices following, the schoolboys reciting Latin verses they do not understand, the clerks droning psalms while worrying about their daughters' debts. They carry prayer books like shields, hiding behind the cover of devotion. They are not hypocrites in the theatrical sense; they simply possess a religion stored in a mental lockbox, a remedy for illness to be dispensed when the body fails. They trade in spiritual prescriptions with the same casual indifference as apothecaries trading in laudanum.

Lady Warrington was the chief dispenser in this household. She held the conscience of the servants' hall and the digestion of the family in her hands. Her authority was absolute, her belief in her own virtue total. She believed herself a saint among sinners, a wisdom incarnate, and she fed this delusion to everyone around her until they swallowed it whole. At dinner, amidst the clatter of silver and the sight of liveried men, she maintained the illusion of abundance even as the beer grew thin and the joints of meat dwindled. When Sir Miles toasted the King, his eyes gleaming with the nectar of the grape, he spoke of Virginia wines as if they were ambrosia. He asked Harry if such port existed in the New World, boasting that Harry tasted nothing compared to the vintage of Norfolk. Harry, watching his uncle's performance, found himself almost believing the lie, blinking into his own glass to catch a reflection of the merit his uncle projected onto the ruby liquid.

In this house of mirrors, two figures emerged as the true idols. The elder daughter, the Beauty, was betrothed to Tom Claypool, a fact announced with the gravity of a royal decree. The younger, the Genius and the Angel, was reserved for Harry. She sang to him at the harpsichord, though the notes were often discordant, a flaw in the instrument of perfection. She offered advice, instruction, conversation—a relentless barrage of correction that Harry found suffocating. He preferred the silence of the nursery, the presence of Miles, a small cousin who reminded him of Fanny Mountain. Yet, after dinner, the house settled into a strange rhythm. The Beauty retreated to her own orbit; the mother attended to correspondence or the ailments of the household; the father dozed in his chair. Only the Genius remained, a sentinel guarding the young man against the chaos of the outside world.

This calm was intoxicating. Harry, accustomed to the riot of dissipation, found refuge here. The doors stood open at all hours. If Flora was absent, Dora waited. Within days, they had ridden together in the park, and Harry, generous to a fault, planned to buy a horse for his cousin. But fate, that cruel accountant, intervened. The circumstances that followed stripped Harry of his coaches and horses, reducing the young Virginian prince to a state of sudden destitution.

While the ladies discussed geography with earnest curiosity, listening as Harry drew maps of his estate—log huts disguised as towns, rivers flowing across parchment—they failed to notice the erosion of his reality. Harry described the Potomac and the James River with the pride of a traveler recounting foreign lands. He pointed to hills and forests, imagining himself a prince describing realms to a queen. In truth, he possessed miles where Englishmen owned acres. Even Dora, usually serene, began to lose her temper, twisting Flora's hair in jealousy. Claypool, in his red waistcoat, sat silent before the splendor of Harry, unable to compete with the charm of the American stranger.

Mama's affection deepened, transforming into a maternal obsession. She worried for the souls of the Indians and the Negroes in Virginia, seeking ways to aid Madam Esmond. She praised Gumbo's musical gifts and the butler's piety. Then came the plea: *"Ah! Harry, Harry! You have been a wild boy! Why did you not come sooner? 'Tis not too late. We must reclaim thee!"* The three ladies looked upward, invoking a divine witness to their mission. Who would claim him first? Dora watched Flora; Mama prepared to adopt him in the absence of his own parents. She handed him a hymn book, kissed his forehead, and poured out a love that was pure, tender, and utterly blind to the wreckage accumulating in the corner of the room.

Meanwhile, Mr. Warrington kept his own counsel. The fortune that had favored him for weeks had vanished overnight. Lord Castlewood, his kinsman, had played him for everything, winning every shilling and threatening the reversions. Harry, refusing to fight further, had surrendered. He gambled away the last reserve meant for Sampson, leaving himself nearly naked on the shore of his misfortune.

Desperate, Harry turned to the only resources left. He pawned his trinkets to raise funds for Sampson's landlord. The broker recognized the diamond-handled repeater Harry had intended for his Oakhurst friends. The recognition was immediate, the judgment swift. Before Harry could react, the net closed. Bailiffs, familiar with the mechanics of debt, descended upon him. They tracked him through the gossip of Gumbo, finding him in the sanctuary of the Warringtons.

Never had Uncle Miles been more cordial, never had Aunt Warrington been more gracious. Flora looked radiant; Dora was amiable beyond measure. At parting, Lady Warrington pressed both hands into Harry's, calling down blessings from the ceiling. Sir Miles, jovial and oblivious, laughed at the blush on Harry's cheeks, suggesting that the girls were used to such affection. *"A kiss won't do no harm,"* he declared, pressing Harry's hand and repeating the benediction at the door.

As the door closed, cutting off the warm light of the hall, two figures emerged from the shadows on the opposite side of the street. One produced a strip of paper, placed a hand on Mr. Warrington's shoulder, and declared him a prisoner. A hackney coach awaited. Poor Harry slept in Chancery Lane, a Virginian prince slapped by a ragged follower, destined for a spunging house in Cursitor Street.

Let us imagine the sting of that night. The remorse that keeps a man awake on a dingy pillow, the raucous laughter of hardened inmates drifting through the wall, the rage and shame of a fall from grace. No pity for him, perhaps, for those who have never indulged in such folly cannot truly understand the reckoning.

The silence of the lock-up was not empty; it was heavy, a physical weight pressing against the ribs of Harry Warrington. Remorse for past follies coiled in his gut, sharp and familiar, but beneath it lay a hotter, more immediate irritation: the indignity of arrest itself. Yet, he dismissed the present misery as transient. His mind, trained to calculate probabilities rather than feel despair, swept through a roster of potential saviors. There was Mr. Draper, the obsequious businessman; his uncle the Baronet, who had once offered his estate as a sanctuary; Cousin Castlewood, the gambler who had drained his pockets; the noble friends of the Chocolate House; even Aunt Bernstein. Any of them could intervene, though some might append a lecture on imprudence to their aid. The primary objective, however, remained singular: secrecy. Mr. Warrington insisted that the public knowledge of a gentleman's arrest was a vulgar stain, a detail to be scrubbed from the narrative of society. He reasoned with a detached cynicism that the world, ultimately, cared for nothing. Scandal was merely noise, a temporary irritation that the masses endured with a weary, blasé indifference. Whether one was imprisoned, bankrupt, or caught in a foolish bargain, the outcome was identical: vanity. The world would continue its rhythm of dinners, laughter, and yawning, indifferent to the private collapse of individuals. To assume otherwise was a conceit of the ego.

Yet, Harry Warrington chafed. He imagined the atmosphere of the club, the sharp edges of Mr. Selwyn's mockery, the trembling of March regarding their wager, the raucous congratulations at the Savage Arms. He could not face the world again. He resolved against contacting his relations or his peers at White's. Instead, he summoned Sampson, the chaplain, a man who understood the mechanics of crisis. Dawn broke slowly over Cursitor Street, delayed as if the sun had forgotten its rounds. Once the inmates stirred, Harry dispatched a messenger to Long Acre, entrusting the chaplain with his plight and seeking his counsel.

Unaware that sending for Sampson was akin to inviting a lion into a pit, Harry believed his ally was free to act. The message sent, the captive's tension eased fractionally. Breakfast arrived, served by an attendant who inquired if he wished to dine alone or join the other gentlemen at Mrs. Bailiff's. Harry declined. He intended to leave before dinner, informing the chamberlain of his imminent departure. The man, skeptical and experienced, noted the frequency of such announcements among young gentlemen and closed the door, leaving Harry to his solitude.

Time dragged, measured in agonizing increments. Ten o'clock passed, then eleven, then noon. Still, no Sampson. Just after midday, a figure entered: Gumbo, carrying a portmanteau of his master's clothes. The boy threw himself at Harry's feet, roaring with grief, offering his life, his freedom, anything to restore his beloved master. Harry, touched by the display, commanded him to rise and dress him, strictly ordering silence. "Not a word," Harry warned, his voice severe.

"Oh no, sir, never to nobody!" Gumbo declared, his face a mask of solemn devotion. He proceeded to groom his master with meticulous care, applying powder and arranging the finest attire, transforming Harry into a figure of splendor fit for St. James's. Gumbo performed every act of love and servitude within his power, yet he possessed limitations. He could not reverse the past, nor could he suppress the truth when pressed. The language of slaves, whether black or white, is built on subterfuge; they hide like hunted animals, retreating behind veils of fiction. These strange relics of feudalism persist because society demands hypocrisy from its dependents. We expect them to lie, to withhold the whole truth, and when they fail, we are indignant.

But Gumbo's oath of silence was already broken. Before he could utter a single word, he had broadcast the catastrophe to a prodigious audience: gentlemen of the shoulder-knot and worsted lace, members of the footman's club, patrons of Sir Miles Warrington's butler, and finally, the household of Lord Castlewood. By the time he knelt before his master, promising eternal silence, the news had already rippled through the corridors of power.

While Gumbo finished the toilette, the hours stretched endlessly. Finally, a letter arrived from Sampson, sealed with a damp wafer, the ink barely dry. The chaplain wrote that he had done all he could, unaware of the peril he faced. He revealed that he had been seized by villains holding a writ against him and was currently incarcerated in a cell three doors away, separated only by garden walls. He confessed he had much to say but refused to commit his patron's affairs to paper.

Now, as Sampson remained silent, the truth emerged from the shadows of his omission. The chaplain's loquacity had not yet reached Long Acre; he was ignorant of the full extent of Harry's disaster until receiving the messenger. Driven by gratitude and desperation, he sought out Lord Castlewood, hoping to arouse pity. He delivered an eloquent plea, speaking with genuine sympathy of his cousin's generosity. My Lord Castlewood, however, cut him short with peevish brevity. "Did I not tell you two days ago," he snapped, "that I was as poor as a beggar? Has anyone left me a fortune since? The little I won from my cousin was swallowed by others. Not only cannot I help Mr. Warrington, but I had just written to him asking for assistance." Indeed, a letter to this effect had reached Harry's lodgings via the penny post.

"I must get him money, my lord," Sampson cried, desperate. "If I pawn my cassock and bands..."

"Amen," Castlewood replied, dismissing him with a wave of his hand. "Go and pawn whatever you please. Your enthusiasm does you credit." He returned to his paper while the chaplain departed in deep despondency.

Meanwhile, Lady Maria had sensed the gravity of the situation. Seizing the chaplain as he exited his futile meeting with her brother, she drew him into the dining room, her face etched with grief. "What has happened?" she demanded. "I have been in mortal anxiety since his servant brought the news last night." She claimed to have learned of the arrest from Lady Yarmouth's box at the opera.

"Then my lord knew?" Sampson asked, confused.

"Benson told us at tea," Lady Maria confirmed.

Patience evaporating, the chaplain swore and recounted the cruel irony of his encounter with Lord Castlewood—the nobleman's refusal to aid his cousin despite having won large sums from him. Enraged by the duplicity, Lady Maria launched into a furious tirade against her own family. Though rarely truthful, when familial bonds fracture, the floodgates of honesty open wide. With tears and imprecations, she denounced her brothers, sisters, and parents, questioning why God had fashioned such creatures. Emboldened by wrath, she stormed toward her brother's library door, her shrieks filling the hall.

Castlewood looked up from his book, surprised by her ferocity, and prepared a strong oath to check her advance. But Maria, usually afraid of him, was now fearless.

"So, my lord!" she shouted. "You sit down with him in private to cards and pigeon him! You get the poor boy's last shilling, and you won't give him a guinea out of his own winnings now he is penniless!"

"That infernal chaplain has been telling tales!" Castlewood retorted.

"Dismiss him! Pay him his wages and let him go—he will be glad enough!" Maria cried.

"I keep him to marry one of my sisters, in case he is needed," Castlewood glared.

"What can the women be in a family where there are such men?" she asked.

"Effectivement!" he shrugged.

"What can we be, when our fathers and brothers are what they are? We are bad enough, but what are you? I say, you neither have courage, nor honour, nor common feeling. As your equals won't play with you, my Lord Castlewood, you must take this poor lad out of Virginia, your own kinsman, and pigeon him! Oh, it's a shame—a shame!"

The air in the lodging house tasted of stale beer and damp wool. Harry's second night was less violent than the first, a dull ache replacing the sharp sting of humiliation, but the hardness in his chest remained intact. He viewed the world through a lens of absolute rejection. His aunt, his friend March, his cousin Castlewood—they were all failures, selfish creatures who had stripped him bare. To beg them again would be to admit defeat, to acknowledge the lie of their friendship. There was no loyalty in this cold, selfish country. He would leave. He would trust no Englishman, great or small. Perhaps he would march with the King in Germany, or retreat to Virginia, bury himself in the woods until he became indistinguishable from the soil, hunting all day, tending tobacco fields, marrying Polly or Fanny, doing anything to exist outside the orbit of these fine gentlemen.

Morning broke with a cheerfulness that did not reach his eyes. Gumbo stood ready, having traveled from Bond Street to deliver letters. "I sought to bring more clothes," the servant said, his tone honest and deferential, "but Mr. Ruff, the landlord, refused entry."

Harry ignored the letters initially, opening them mechanically. Bills. More bills. The fourth revealed the landlord's ultimatum: no more goods from Mr. Warrington's estate would leave the premises until payment was made. If unpaid, the goods would be sold, and the black man would be expelled. Gumbo hesitated, clutching Harry's own trunk. "I found refuge elsewhere," Gumbo lied smoothly, "at Lord Wrotham's house. With Colonel Lambert's people."

Lambert. The name curdled Harry's blood. Hetty had fainted upon hearing of his arrest; Lambert, a decent man, had offered shelter. Yet Harry knew better. He translated Gumbo's polished narrative with skepticism, aware of the servant's tendency to embellish. But the mention of the Colonel extinguished any remaining warmth. "Send to Lambert?" Harry ground out, his teeth clenched. "The man who spat my gifts back in my face? If I starved, I would not ask him for a crust!"

Dressed, Harry summoned breakfast and dispatched Gumbo with a note to Mr. Draper in the Temple. The instruction was written with the haughty brevity reserved for inferiors, a tone Harry usually reserved for his own servants.

"The note reads as if addressed to a negro, not a freeborn English gentleman," Draper observed later, his voice dripping with suppressed rage. "For a man in a spunging-house! Hang him! I have a mind not to attend." Yet he went. He found Mr. Warrington seated on his bed like a monarch, draped in a splendid gown, his hair meticulously coiffed, commanding a chair for his attendant.

"Excuse me, madam," the outraged attorney muttered, addressing the Baroness who watched with amusement. "Such airs I am not accustomed to."

"Take a seat, Mr. Draper," the Baroness smiled, delighted by her nephew's insolence in adversity.

The arrangement between the Baroness and her lawyer had been set in stone the day prior. Draper was competent, usually effective, but today he was blinded by pride and confusion. How does one offend a non-gentleman without understanding the mechanics of the offense? The creature acts, provoking scorn; the creature feels the scorn and rages; the observer speaks naturally, and the creature interprets it as mockery. Indifference breeds hatred.

"Gumbo, fetch a chair for Mr. Draper!" Warrington commanded, wrapping his brocade robe around his legs. "Sit, if you please. We discuss my business. Thank you for your prompt response. Have you heard of this misfortune?"

Draper nodded. "News travels fast, Mr. Warrington. I was eager to offer my services. Your friends and family will be pained that a gentleman of your rank finds himself in such a position."

"I have been imprudent," Warrington admitted, bowing stiffly. "I lived beyond my means. I gambled with wealthier men, and a cursed streak of bad luck has drained my cash, leaving me liable for five hundred pounds and more."

"Five hundred currently outstanding," Draper noted.

"It is a trifling sum," Warrington insisted, staring intently at the lawyer. "I thought sending to friends yesterday would suffice. I was mistaken. Please assist me in raising the funds."

"Hm," Draper grunted, pulling a long, grave face.

"Why, sir, it can be done!" Warrington challenged.

It could be done. Indeed, Draper had already proposed to the Baroness immediate payment and release. She intended to make him her heir. Assuming, as everyone else did, that Harry's Virginia inheritance matched its geographical extent in monetary value, Draper possessed the necessary notes and the Baroness's conditional order. Yet, when Harry declared, "It can be done!", Draper's expression hardened. "It can be done in time, sir, but it requires duration. To access the property in England belonging to Mr. George Warrington, we must prove the event and release the trustees. Who will do this? Lady Esmond Warrington in Virginia will not permit her son to rot in prison, but we must await six months for correspondence. Does your Bristol agent hold authority to honor your drafts?"

"He is authorized for two hundred pounds annually," Warrington replied. "I suppose I have no recourse but my aunt, Madame de Bernstein. She will guarantee it."

"Your ladyship will do anything," Draper confirmed. "If she gives the word now, you walk out of here."

"Then go to her, Mr. Draper. I wished not to trouble my relations, but rather than endure this horrible, unnecessary imprisonment, I must speak to her. State my location and plight. Disguise nothing. Tell her I confide in her affection to release me from this... disgrace," Warrington's voice trembled, and he wiped his eyes.

"Sir," Draper countered, fixing the young man with a stare, "I was with her ladyship yesterday discussing this most unpleasant affair—I won't say, as you do, disgraceful."

"What do you mean? Does Madame de Bernstein know of my misfortune?" Harry asked.

"Every detail, sir. The pawning of the watches, everything."

Harry flushed crimson. "An unfortunate business," the lawyer continued, "pawning items you never purchased."

Harry sprang from the bed, his eyes fierce enough to alarm Draper. "This may lead to litigation and unpleasant courtroom remarks. Those barristers respect nothing. When they get a fellow in the box—"

"Great Heaven, sir, do you suppose a gentleman of my rank cannot borrow a watch on credit without intending to defraud the tradesman?" Harry cried, agitated.

"Of course you meant honorably," Draper winked, enjoying the sting. ("Hang the supercilious beast; I hit him there.") "Only the law may not perceive it so. Your aunt calls it the most imprudent act she has ever witnessed—to call it by no worse name."

"You call it by no worse name yourself, Mr. Draper?" Harry asked, slowing his speech, attempting to maintain control.

Draper disliked the look. "Heaven forbid I speak thus between gentlemen—but between client and advisor, it is my duty to say, 'Sir, you are in a very unpleasant scrape,' just as a doctor tells a patient, 'Sir, you are very ill.'"

"And you cannot help me pay this debt? You come only to accuse me of roguery?"

"Obtaining goods under false pretenses? Undoubtedly, yes. Do not look as if you intend to strike me. (Curse him, I make him wince.) A young gentleman with two hundred a year from his mother ordering diamonds and watches, taking them to a pawnbroker. What do people think of such behavior? I tell you honestly. Do not be angry, Mr. Warrington."

"Go on, sir!" Harry groaned.

Draper sensed victory. "Can I not help pay the debt? Yes—and here is the money in my pocket to settle it now, if you wish—not mine, sir, but your aunt's, Lady Bernstein's. However, she imposes conditions, which I have brought."

"State them, sir," Harry said.

"They are not harsh, merely for your own good. If you agree, we summon a hackney coach and proceed to Clarges Street, which I promised to visit regardless. Mr. Warrington, I need not name names, but a marriage question existed between you and a certain party."

"Ah!" Harry's face brightened.

"My noble client, the Baroness, is most averse to this union," Draper continued. "She holds other views, believing it your ruin to marry someone of noble birth and title, yet lacking first-rate character and significantly older than yourself. You gave an imprudent promise."

"Yes; and she retains it," Warrington stated.

"It has been recovered. She dropped it accidentally at Tunbridge," Draper explained. "Indeed, her ladyship showed it to me. Written in blue ink—"

"Never mind, sir!" Harry interrupted, turning red as the ink of his own foolish promise, which had smitten him with shame a thousand times.

"At the same time, letters compromising a noble family were recovered," the lawyer pressed. "You lost them. No fault of yours; you were absent when they were found. You may claim that noble family, and yourself, possess a friend rare among young men.

The anger left Mr. Draper's throat, but the silence that followed was heavier than the words he had swallowed. Behind him, Harry had risen. The young prisoner's voice cut through the air, a sharp, half-formed exclamation meant to recall the lawyer. Pride, however, acted as a dam. Harry held his tongue; Draper chose not to break. To humble oneself before a man trapped by poverty felt like a violation of the natural order.

"An hour hence will do," Harry thought, sinking back onto the straw mattress. The movement was sluggish, defeated. He did not care for Maria Esmond. The name tasted like ash. He was ashamed of the trap, the way his boyish ardour had been harvested by a wily woman who played the game as her brother played cards. They were flesh and blood, yet they had treated him as prey, using his vulnerability to feed their own selfish ends. They had violated the sanctity of hospitality, turning the young kinsman who sought refuge into a victim. His heart was a wound that refused to clot. He leaned his head back against the rough pillow, and bitter tears soaked the fabric.

"If they had come to Virginia," he whispered to the gloom, "I would have offered a different welcome."

This desolation was interrupted by the grin of Gumbo at the door. "A lady is come to see Master Harry," the servant announced. Following him entered the lady in the capuchin.

Harry sat up, his face pale and haggard, illuminated by the flickering candlelight. The lady, overcome with a sob that broke instantly, crossed the room. Before the servant could retreat, she threw her arms around the young prisoner's neck. It was a gesture of raw, maternal tenderness. She buried her face in his pale cheek, kissing it amidst a torrent of tears.

"Oh, my Harry!" she cried. "Did I ever, ever think to see thee here?"

He recoiled, startled by the intimacy of her presence, but she sank to her knees beside the bed, seizing his feverish hand and clutching his legs. Her regard was genuine, her pity absolute. The wretchedness of his surroundings, the decay of his appearance, struck her heart with the force of a physical blow.

"I—I thought none of you would come!" Harry groaned, his voice cracking.

More tears, more kisses on his hot, young hand, more desperate clasping. This was her reply for a moment.

"Oh, my dear! My dear! I cannot bear to think of thee in misery," she sobbed.

Even hardened hearts possess cracks where light enters. That heart was not all marble; that life was not all desert. His mother could not have loved him more, nor spoken with greater tenderness than this kinswoman now kneeling at his feet.

"Some of the debts, I fear, were owing to my extravagance!" she admitted, and the statement carried the weight of truth. "You bought trinkets and jewels to give me pleasure. Oh, how I hate them now! I little thought I ever could! I have brought them all with me, and more trinkets—here! and here! and all the money I have in the world!"

She poured a cascade of brooches, rings, a watch, and a score of guineas into Harry's lap.

The sight agitated him, stirring something deep and ancient within him.

"Dearest, kindest cousin!" he sobbed. Words failed him, yet his expression conveyed the depth of his gratitude, his affection, his overwhelming emotion. Soon, the despair lifted. He smiled, putting away some of the gifts intended for Maria, recounting the dangers he had faced selling goods on credit and the insult the lawyer had just delivered regarding his finances. He would not accept her money—he claimed he had enough—but he valued her twenty guineas as if they were twenty thousand. He vowed never to forget her love and kindness. By all that was sacred, he would not! He must tell his mother of her goodness. It had buoyed him when he stood on the brink of total collapse under the weight of disgrace. Might Heaven bless her for it!

There is no need to prolong the cousins' conversation. After Maria's visit, the dark day seemed brighter to Harry; the imprisonment felt less like a tomb. The world was not entirely selfish and cold. Here was a creature who truly loved him. Even Castlewood was not as terrible as he had imagined.

He expressed his deepest grief at his inability to assist his kinsman. He was hopelessly in debt. Every shilling he had won from Harry was lost the next day to others. Yet, anything within his power, he would do. He promised to visit Mr. Warrington soon; the man was in waiting today, imprisoned alongside Harry.

Thus, the pair talked cheerfully and affectionately until the shadows lengthened across the room. Then, with a sigh, Maria bade Harry farewell.

The door had barely closed when it swung open again to admit Draper.

"Your humble servant, sir," the attorney said.

His voice grated on Harry's nerves; his presence was an offense.

"I had expected you some hours ago, sir," Harry replied curtly.

"A lawyer's time is not always his own, sir," Draper retorted, fresh from a consultation with a bottle of port at the Grecian. "Never mind, I'm at your orders now. Presume it's all right, Mr. Warrington. Packed your trunk? Why, now there you are in your bedgown still. Let me go down and settle whilst you call in your black man and tidbit yourself. I've a coach at the door, and we'll be off and dine with the old lady."

"Are you going to dine with the Baroness de Bernstein, pray?"

"Not me—no such honour. Had my dinner already. It's you are a-going to dine with your aunt, I suppose?"

"Mr. Draper, you suppose a great deal more than you know," Mr. Warrington said, folding his brocade dressing-gown around himself, looking fierce and tall.

"Great goodness, sir, what do you mean?" Draper asked.

"I mean, sir, that I have considered, and, having given my word to a faithful and honourable lady, it does not become me to withdraw it."

"Confound it, sir!" shrieked the lawyer. "I tell you she has lost the paper. There's nothing to bind you—nothing. Why she's old enough to be—"

"Enough, sir," Mr. Warrington stamped his foot. "You seem to think you are talking to some other pettifogger. I take it, Mr. Draper, you are not accustomed to have dealings with men of honour."

"Pettifogger, indeed!" Draper cried in a fury. "Men of honour, indeed! I'd have you to know, Mr. Warrington, that I'm as good a man of honour as you. I don't know so many gamblers and horse-jockeys, perhaps. I haven't gambled away my patrimony, and lived as if I was a nobleman on two hundred a year. I haven't bought watches on credit, and pawned—touch me if you dare, sir," and the lawyer sprang toward the door.

"That is the way out, sir. You can't go through the window, because it is barred," Mr. Warrington said calmly.

"And the answer I take to my client is No, then!" Draper screamed.

Harry stepped forward, his hands clenched. "If you utter another word," he said, "I'll—"

The door slammed shut. The sentence died unfinished. Draper departed, furious, heading to Madame de Bernstein. Though he gave her the best version of his story, he received fiercer language from her than he had from Mr. Warrington himself.

"What? Shall she trust me, and I desert her?" Harry stalked up and down his room, his brocade rustling like dry leaves. "Dear, faithful, generous woman! If I lie in prison for years, I'll be true to her."

With his lawyer dismissed after a stormy interview, the desolate old woman sat down to the meal she had hoped to share with her nephew. The chair remained empty before her, the glasses shining beside the silver. Dish after dish was laid before her by the silent major-domo, tasted, and pushed away. Finally, the man pressed his mistress.

"It is eight o'clock," he said. "You have had nothing all day. It is good for you to eat."

She could not eat. She wanted her coffee. Let Case go get it. The lacquies cleared the table, leaving their mistress sitting alone before the vacant chair.

Presently, the old servant re-entered without the coffee, his face etched with a strange, terrified expression. "MR. WARRINGTON!"

The old woman gasped, rising from her armchair only to sink back down, trembling violently.

"So you are come, sir, are you?" she said, her voice a shaky tremolo. "Bring back the—Ah!" Here she screamed, "Gracious God, who is it?"

Her eyes widened, staring wildly. Her white face looked ghastly beneath the rouge. Clinging to the arms of her chair for support, she watched as the visitor approached.

A gentleman whose face and figure exactly mirrored Harry Warrington, whose voice possessed strangely similar tones, had followed the servant into the room. He bowed toward the Baroness.

"You expected my brother, madam?" he said.

The coach wheels bit into the mud of the Strand, a rhythmic, grinding complaint that echoed the weariness of the journey itself. From Piccadilly to Fleet Street, the distance stretched unnaturally long, measured not in miles but in the slush of winter quagmires and the uneven, treacherous pavement. It was a landscape of shadows and lantern-light, where the only illumination came from the flickering torches of link-boys guiding the weary traveler through the dark. For George Warrington, this drive felt as interminable as the march from Marlborough to London earlier that day, a circular progression of fatigue and anticipation.

His mind replayed the correspondence that had precipitated this moment. He had written to Harry, announcing his safe arrival in Bristol, and prior to that, the great news of his own survival and return from captivity. Yet, the letter carrying the intelligence of George's restoration had vanished, swallowed by the chaos of war. Between England and France, privateers swept the seas, seizing merchant vessels within sight of port. That specific missive, bearing the weight of a brother's life restored, had likely fallen into the hands of a French ship among the American fleet. The letter confirming George's actual arrival in England lay unread in Harry's apartments, delivered on the third morning after Harry's imprisonment, only to remain sealed because the angry Mr. Ruff refused to surrender any further items of his lodger's property.

Now, George stood before the door of those very apartments, seeking his brother. The maid-servant who answered recoiled, screaming and retreating to her mistress, terrified by the uncanny resemblance between the two brothers. The mistress, unwilling to admit the truth—that Harry was a prisoner held by her husband—devised a lie. Mr. Warrington had left; she knew nothing of his whereabouts.

George knew the geography of London better than the map suggested. Clarges Street bordered Bond Street. His aunt, Mrs. Bernstein, lived nearby. Perhaps Harry was there, under her care. He recalled the kindness Harry had described in his letters to Virginia, the way even Madam Esmond had been softened by tales of her, particularly by a letter from the Baroness that had once spurred George to rush to Europe. He hoped, with a fervent prayer, that Madam Beatrix had finally found reason to repent of her past transgressions. At her age, surely there was ample cause for contrition. Yet, society remained inexorable; female virtue, especially, forgave no sins committed decades prior, regardless of the order for remission or the handwriting on the floor of the Temple.

Perhaps the Virginian agent in Bristol had fed George fearful stories of Harry's conduct.
