Gumbo, the negro servant, had emerged from the shock of recognizing his master, recovering quickly enough to stammer out fragments of information regarding Harry's location and pitiable state. This explanation accounted for George's stern demeanor as he presented himself to the old lady. It seemed natural to him that a brother in distress should be rescued by kin. But George, how little he understood the labyrinthine ways of London! A philanthropist attempting to rescue every impoverished soul he met would require the combined wealth of all American provinces.

Yet, as the coach jolted through the dark streets toward the house of his brother's captivity, George reconsidered. He thought of his aunt's behavior. *She feels my poor Harry's misfortune,* he reasoned, chastising his own haste. *I judged too quickly.* Throughout his life, George had frequently rebuked himself for this exact error—the crime of premature judgment. How many shared this flaw? And once the damage was wrought, no repentance could fully mend it.

"Quick, coachman!" George urged, though the vehicle moved with the sluggish inertia of the season. "We are almost as slow as you are getting from Clarges Street to the Temple."

Gumbo knew the path to the bailiff's house intimately. The bell rang again. The first door yielded to George and his attendant, then locked warily behind them, sealing them in a narrow passage guarded by a Jewish janitor. A second door swung open, admitting them into the house proper. The janitor stared, his face illuminated by a flaring tallow-torch, as he beheld a second Mr. Warrington.

"To see that gentleman?" the janitor asked.

"Yes," George replied. "But wait. This is Mr. Warrington's brother from America."

Gumbo retreated to prepare his master, instructing the porter that another gentleman awaited upstairs concerning Mr. W.'s affairs. The room George entered was a small apartment known as Mr. Amos's office. By the light of a guttering candle, a stout gentleman in a cloak and laced hat sat talking to the bailiff. The young porter preceded George, casting sufficient light into the cramped space.

"We are not angry anymore, Harry!" the stout gentleman declared, rising with a cheery voice and extending a hand toward the newcomer. "Thank God, my boy! Mr. Amos assures me there will be no difficulty about James and me acting as your bail. We shall settle your business by breakfast tomorrow. Why... angels and ministers of grace! Who are you?"

He recoiled as the other seized his hand. But the stranger gripped it tighter.

"God bless you, sir!" George said. "I know who you are. You must be Colonel Lambert, whose kindness to my poor Harry my brother wrote of. I am the brother you have heard of, sir—the one left for dead in Mr. Braddock's action, revived after eighteen months among the French, living now to thank God and you for your kindness to Harry." His voice faltered.

"James! James! News!" cried Mr. Lambert to a gentleman in red who entered the room. "The dead are alive! Here comes Harry Scapegrace's brother, and with his scalp intact!" George had removed his hat, standing by the light. "This is my brother-bail, Mr. Warrington! This is Lieutenant-Colonel James Wolfe, at your service. There has been a slight difference between Harry and me, Mr. George. He is pacified, is he, James?"

"He is full of gratitude," Mr. Wolfe responded, bowing to Mr. Warrington.

"Harry wrote home about Mr. Wolfe, too, sir," George added. "I hope my brother's friends will extend the same kindness to me."

"I wish he had none other but us, Mr. Warrington. Poor Harry's fine folks have been too fine for him, ending by landing him here."

"Nay, your honors, I have done my best to make the young gentleman comfortable. Knowing your honor before, when you bailed Captain Watkins, and that your security is impeccable—if your honor wishes, the young gentleman can leave tonight. I shall rectify matters with the lawyer in the morning," said Harry's landlord, recognizing the rank of the two officers offering bail.

"The debt is five hundred and odd pounds, I believe?" George asked.

"With a hundred thanks to these gentlemen, I can pay the amount instantly into the officers' hands, taking the usual acknowledgment and caution. But I can never forget, gentlemen, that you aided my brother in his need. For this, I say thank you, and God bless you, in my mother's name and mine."

Meanwhile, Gumbo ascended to Harry's apartment. Harry, seated over his pipe of Virginia tobacco in a somber mood, would likely have scolded the negro for returning so late. Maria's recent goodness and affection had offered little solace; instead, they had stirred a dread of the consequences to which such fidelity bound him. Then, Mr. Wolfe's homely features and eager, outstretched hand broke the gloom. Hearing of Lambert's presence below and the purpose of their visit, Harry's resistance crumbled. Despite his ill-temper and needless suspicion, the good gentleman was resolved to help, even against Wolfe's counsel.

"For you were wrong, Mr. Warrington," the Colonel said bluntly. "And you would not be set right. You, a young man, used hard words and unkind behavior to your senior, one of the best gentlemen who walks God's earth. Consider his answer to your wayward temper. Will you bear with a friend who speaks frankly? Martin Lambert acts thus always—as the best Christian, the best friend, the most kind and generous of men. If you seek another proof of his goodness, here it is: He has converted me. Though I do not care to disguise that I was angry with you for your treatment of him, he has absolutely brought me down here to be your bail. Let us both cry *Peccavimus!* Harry, shake hands with our friend! He sits in the room below. He would not come until he knew how you would receive him."

"I think he is a good man!" Harry groaned. "I was very angry and wild when we met last, Colonel Wolfe. Perhaps he was right in sending back those trinkets; I was hurt by it. Go down to him, will you be so kind, sir? Tell him I am sorry, ask his pardon—and, God bless him for his generosity."

Turning his head away, Harry rubbed his hand across his eyes.

"Tell him all this yourself, Harry!" the Colonel insisted, taking the young man's hand. "No deputy will say it half so well. Come with me now."

"You go first, and I'll—I'll follow. On my word, I will. See! I am in my morning gown! I will but put on a coat and come to him. Give him my message first. Just—just prepare him for me!" pleaded Harry, aware he must act yet

The drawing room at St. James's Palace possessed a specific gravity, a weight of silence that settled upon the shoulders of anyone entering. I had expected Sir Miles Warrington to be a monument to something solid, perhaps a statue carved from oak and gold leaf, representing the pinnacle of earthly felicity. Instead, he was merely a man with a round face and a round waistcoat, radiating a heat that felt less like nobility and more like a fever.

To be a good old country gentleman, the narrative suggested, was to occupy a position nearest the gods. It was a role defined by arithmetic: a large rent-roll, adoring farmers, and the occasional tenant withheld only to demonstrate the landlord's sublime charity. It was a life of hunting, health, and dinners where the prayer for mercy was recited solely for form, a script read aloud to ensure the actors knew their lines. If one possessed five thousand a year, the adoration of a parish, and the worship of a family, dissatisfaction became impossible. Misery, in this context, was a luxury reserved for the gout-ridden aristocrat in solitude, or the ploughman with an ague and seven children. For the healthy, the rich, the jolly, misery was merely a word, a formality stripped of its sting.

Yet, beneath the varnish of this perfection lay a machinery of indifference. The Warringtons performed their benevolence with the same detached precision as a merchant counting coins. Their coach, a rolling fortress of spotless daughters and sublime ladies, traversed the countryside like a royal progress. From its cushions, they smiled down upon the poor, buying yards of ribbon or ounces of salts with an affability that felt calculated rather than generous. They purchased virtue as one might purchase a bargain, honoring it by the act of acquisition alone. When Harry, their cousin, fell into distress, the response was not relief but a theatrical display of refusal. Aunt sent a tract; Uncle cited business. The performance of piety continued uninterrupted, a seamless loop of "Amen!" and polite rejection, allowing them to maintain their self-image while sending hunger away empty-handed.

Inside the house, the dynamic shifted from external performance to internal friction. The sisters, Dora and Flora, engaged in a battle of mirrors, reflecting each other's flaws back with the sharp clarity of polished steel. They argued over engagements, over looks, over the distribution of attention. Their father, Sir Miles, observed this chaos with the amusement of a spectator watching a trivial game, while their mother, the majestic matron, attempted to weave a tapestry of morality over the fray. She spoke of unity, of the danger of misplaced affection, of the contamination brought by gamblers and play-actors. Her voice carried the weight of established authority, yet it was easily broken by the simple mechanics of childhood.

Miles Junior, the artless child, existed outside this hierarchy of adult anxieties. He wound his top, a small, spinning universe of wood and string, indifferent to the sermons that tore at his mother's heart. He laughed when the top struck his sister's toe, finding joy in the collision of bodies and objects, a joy that the adults struggled to comprehend. When the mother laid her hand on his head, speaking of poverty and the necessity of withholding aid, he simply repeated the logic back to her, a mirror reflecting her own harshness. The top spun, staggered, and expired in its usual tipsy manner, a cycle of motion and stillness that mirrored the futile efforts of the adults to impose meaning on a chaotic world.

Then the door opened, breaking the rhythm. Sir Miles returned, followed by Tom Claypool, whose face bore the marks of a storm. The atmosphere in the room tightened, the air growing thick with the scent of impending revelation. Sir Miles announced the arrival of a stranger, a man who had dined at their table, kissed their wife's hand, and claimed a principality. The revelation was delivered not with fanfare, but with the blunt force of a hammer blow: the man was an impostor, a beggarly second son masquerading as an heir.

The reaction was immediate and visceral. The matron sank to the earth, overwhelmed by the hypocrisy of the situation, questioning whether she had entrusted her son to a pretender. The child, Miles Junior, paused in his play, his face clouded by a confusion that transcended understanding. He wore a puzzled look, a silent acknowledgment of the gap between the stories adults told and the reality they created. Then, the dam broke. The little boy burst into tears, not out of malice, but out of a profound, unarticulated sorrow for the lie that had consumed them all. The top lay still on the floor, the spin ceased, and the room was filled with the sound of a single, honest grief amidst the wreckage of their constructed virtues.

The air in the dining room was still, a vacuum created by the absence of Lieutenant-Colonel James Wolfe. He had declined the invitation, preferring the company of Miss Lowther to the finest provisions prepared by Lord Wrotham's household. In his stead, the reader occupies the sixth chair. Across the table, the women appear radiant, their happiness a stark contrast to the recent turmoil involving Harry Warrington. Mrs. Lambert, having secured the release of her son through the intervention of her husband, Major-General Martin Lambert, sits with a fervor that borders on religious ecstasy. Her gratitude is not merely spoken; it is performed. When her husband enters late, bearing news of Harry's freedom and Theo's recovery, the domestic sphere erupts into a chaotic liturgy of tears and embraces. Mrs. Quiggett, the housekeeper, observes this display with a mixture of astonishment and amusement, her laughter sharp and metallic, echoing like the jingle of keys against old ribs. She notes the absurdity of the situation—a quarrel resolved instantly, a husband elevated to sainthood overnight. Yet, beneath the surface of this domestic bliss, a darker current flows.

After supper, the family gathers in prayer, a moment of solemn unity broken only by the arrival of George and Harry Warrington. They arrive from Aunt Bernstein's, unable to sleep without offering thanks. The atmosphere shifts from familial warmth to something more tense, more expectant. The General dismisses the trivialities of the evening meal, signaling that the purpose of this gathering is not sustenance but testimony. The cloth is removed. The servants withdraw. The room falls silent, save for the rustle of fabric and the shifting weight of bodies leaning forward.

George Esmond Warrington begins his statement. His voice carries the weight of memory, recounting a scene far removed from the cozy parlor where they sit. He speaks of the table of the General, of the Philadelphia agents who dismissed the dangers of the campaign with arrogant confidence. He describes the terrain: woods almost untrodden, roads forced through dense forests, a line of troops stretched thin and vulnerable. The General had scoffed at the threat of the savages, confident in the superiority of regular troops. But the reality of the battlefield was different. On that fatal ninth of July, the arrogance of command collided with the ferocity of ambush.

George recounts the chaos of the engagement. The initial cheer of the advancing column quickly curdled into terror as hidden rifles tore through the ranks. Men dropped faster than they could reload. The orderly formation disintegrated into a panicked retreat. Amidst the carnage, George, wounded in the leg, lay exposed. A hybrid figure, half-French, half-Indian, approached him with a clubbed musket, intent on killing and plundering. With a desperate grasp, George seized a fallen fusil and felled the attacker. But he was not safe. An Indian warrior, scalp in hand, advanced upon him, knife dripping. George was paralyzed, helpless, until a French officer emerged from the shadows.

It was Florac, his dueling partner from Quebec. Recognizing George amidst the blood and confusion, Florac intervened. He killed the Indian with a swift thrust of his sword, saving George's life. With pragmatic cruelty, Florac bound George's leg with his sash, disguised him in the clothes of a French volunteer, and urged him to feign allegiance to survive the rout. Before departing to face the collapsing British lines, Florac revealed a personal connection: a gambling debt owed by George, settled long ago. He stayed briefly, ensuring George was hidden among the trees, before rushing forward to join the flight.

As dusk fell, the pursuit ended. Florac returned with two Indians, claiming George was a wounded comrade. They carried him back to the fort, placing him in a blanket beside the officer's log-house. George lay on Florac's own bed, the pain of his wound acute, his consciousness fading in and out. The narrative hangs suspended here, the story of survival cut short by the darkness of the night, leaving the listener in the parlor to wonder at the cost of glory and the fragility of life in the shadow of empire.

The silence inside the fort was not empty; it was heavy, a thick suspension of grease, smoke, and the low, wheezing drone of the commandant's flute paired with my own cracked guitar. For months, we existed in this suspended animation, cycling through quarrels and reconciliations, playing cards with sticky fingers, and enduring the Fawn's dual role as nurse and victim. She cared for the papoose, the gout-stricken master, and the feverish prisoner, absorbing the blows and the liquor dispensed by the whims of her lord. This rhythm held until the autumn of the previous year, when a hunter arrived carrying news that shattered the garrison's lethargy.

The Marquis de Montcalm was moving. A significant detachment was descending the St. Lawrence and the lakes in bateaux, laden with artillery and provisions, to reinforce the French hold and push deeper into British territory. The men dreamed of advancing into Pennsylvania and New York, of seizing Albany and Philadelphia, of driving the Rosbifs into the sea. Yet, amidst this collective fantasy of conquest, Commandant Museau found no satisfaction. His eyes remained fixed on the invisible horizon of Normandy, on the cider pots and the tripe dishes he would never taste again.

"Eh, commandant," I said, breaking the tension. "'Tis fort bien, but where are the farm, the cider, and the trippes?"

"Yes," he replied, his voice flat. "But consider your position when Museau is replaced. Your successors lack my humanity. Will they grant you the indulgences I have? No. You will be kept in a sty, a pig awaiting slaughter. If an officer falls into the hands of your brigands, you pay with your skin. You will be handed to the red allies—the brethren of La Biche. Do you recall what they did to your countrymen at Braddock? Roasting was the least of their punishments."

He launched into a catalogue of horrors: eyes burned out, teeth wrenched loose, limbs gashed. I felt the color drain from my face, but La Biche remained unmoved. She had witnessed such savagery firsthand among the Senecas, whose villages lay near the great cataract. In the wars between whiteskins and redskins, it was impossible to declare which race possessed greater ferocity.

"They may chop me into cutlets," I said calmly. "But you will never have your farm in Normandy."

"Fetch the whisky, La Biche," Museau commanded.

"And it is not too late," I pressed. "I will reward the guide who brings me home. I promise ten thousand livres to anyone who returns my watch and seal, the ones with my grandfather's arms, hidden somewhere in this fort."

"Ah, scelerat!" Museau roared, laughing hoarsely. "You have eyes! All is good prize in war."

"Imagine a house in your village, a field with six cows, an orchard heavy with fruit," I urged. "Javotte at the door with her wheel, children with cheeks red as apples. O my country! O my mother!"

That night, Museau sat in deep thought, and so did La Biche. She sat apart, nursing her child, her gaze occasionally locking onto mine. When the infant cried, Museau shoved it away with foul language toward the building where La Biche resided. Once alone, I laid out my reasoning.

"How do you know this hunter will serve you?" he asked.

"That is my secret," I lied smoothly. "But since we are not bound by honor, let me explain. Hunters stop at settlements for rest and drink. Our new friend loved these things. He gambled with the men, traded furs for liquor, sang, danced, and listened to my songs. When the drums signaled retreat, interrupting our concerts, he seemed content."

The hunter had attended a couple of these gatherings. I tested him with an English song, setting doggerel words to the tune of "Over the hills and far away"—a melody my grandfather once hummed in Marlborough's camp. *"This long, long year, a prisoner drear... I'll give a hundred guineas gay, To be over the hills and far away."*

"What is it?" the hunter asked, confused.

"'Tis a girl to her lover," I answered. But I saw the understanding twinkle in his eye.

The next day, when the men were absent, the trapper confirmed my suspicion. Passing me, he hummed the refrain in perfect English. "If you are ready," he said, "I am ready. I know your people and the way. Talk to the Fawn." He produced cards, mocking my pretense of nobility, and walked away, offering to play elsewhere.

The plan was set. La Biche acted as the agent. She had long been my confidante, tending to my wounds and managing my small chamber. Free to move between the fort and the fields where the garrison's food was grown, she executed her part with faithfulness. Having gambled away most of his earnings, the trapper packed his flints, powder, and blankets, departing three days later.

Then came the signal. When first wounded, I had been placed in the officers' room by Florac. Now, with the fort stripped of officers save for the old lieutenant, my status fluctuated between relative freedom, confinement on prisoner's rations, and shared meals with my tipsy gaoler. My quarters were a log cabin, one of several mounting four small-caliber guns. From the bastion behind my cabin, I could see the confluence of the Ohio and Monongahela rivers, where Duquesne stood on a small island. Opposite the island, across the water, stood a cluster of trees.

"You see those trees?" La Biche whispered in her broken French the day before. "He waits for you behind them."

During the day, the door to my quarters stood open. On the eve of my escape, she returned from the fields with a pickaxe and a basket of vegetables. Sitting on the bench outside, she rested the pick against the doorframe. I stood talking to her, oblivious to the danger. Perhaps because I was so foolish, I never considered using the tool she offered unless she forced it into my open door. Instead, she simply said, "Hide it. Want it soon," pointing to the trees.

The following day, she feigned anger, shouting, "Why do you not come to the commandant's dinner? He is very bad!" Peering into the room, she threw a coil of rope at me.

"I am coming, La Biche," I hobbled after her on my crutch. As we approached the commandant's quarters, she whispered, "For tonight."

Museau knew nothing. He grumbled about cold soup, stared me down while smoking and playing piquet, and watched La Biche cower in the corner. When his bottle ran dry, he demanded another glass, rising to retrieve the firewater from his locked inner room, deliberately excluding the poor Fawn.

As he turned his back, La Biche sprang forward. Kissing my hand, pressing it to her heart, she burst into tears at my knees. Overcome by the depth of her silent devotion—a bond I had barely suspected—I lost my composure. When Museau returned, finding me distracted, he merely remarked on the behavior of neither of us.

"I win everything from you tonight, milor," he said grimly. "We play upon parole."

"And you may count upon mine," I replied.

"'Tis all you have!" he scoffed.

"Monsieur," I insisted, "my word is good for ten thousand livres."

Finally, claiming a headache, he retired to bed, signaling for me to follow. "Good night, mon petit milor," he said, adding, "Stay, you will fall without your crutch." His eyes twinkled with sarcasm. In my agitation, I forgot my lameness and strode away with the gait of a grenadier.

"What a vile night!" he muttered, observing the storm raging outside. "Bring a lantern, La Tulipe, and lock my lord comfortably into his quarters!" Standing in his doorway, he glanced back, catching a glimpse of La Biche hiding behind him.

The rain was torrential. The sentries retreated to their boxes, undisturbed. My log house was constructed of upright posts sunk deep into the earth, with horizontal logs laid across them. I had to dig beneath these supports to create a passage wide enough for my body. Working in the dark shortly after tattoo, I labored until well past midnight, feeling the rain fall upon my hand as I lifted it under the log. With trembling caution, I worked for two more hours before creeping through to the parapet and flinging my rope over the gun. Fear gripped me, lest a sentry spot me and pour lead into my body.

The wall was only twelve feet high; the drop into the ditch was manageable. I waited there, staring under the gun, trying to discern the river and the island. The darkness cleared as the moon rose, illuminating the shimmering water and the dark rocks and trees of the island rising from it.

I moved swiftly toward the marked clump of trees. Hearing a low voice humming "Over the hills and far away" from the shadows, I felt a wave of pure relief wash over me.

Our path led through a level forest tract familiar to my guide. By daylight, we reached a clearer landscape. My trapper, Silverheels, asked if I recognized the spot. It was the fatal field where Braddock had fallen, the place from which I had

George Warrington had repeated the tale to Madame de Bernstein the night before, though the narrative suffered a disjointed rhythm under her attention. The old lady nodded off frequently, her consciousness drifting only when George paused to declare the story fascinating, prompting her to demand its continuation. In response, the young gentleman faltered, his voice trembling, cheeks flushing, compelled to speak despite his reluctance. He spoke far less fluently than he did for his small audience in Hill Street, where Hetty's wide-eyed wonder, Theo's sympathetic gaze, and their parents' gentle expressions provided applause sufficient to sustain any modest youth. Regarding his mother's conduct, the General remarked it resembled Mr. Addison's trunk-maker; she possessed the capacity to elevate any tragedy simply by engaging in the front boxes to weep. This was precisely why they selected Lord Wrotham's house as the theater for George's debut performance, hoping he might speak to advantage rather than to the sleepy, cynical ear of the old lady.

"Very good and most interesting, I am sure, my dear sir," Madame Bernstein murmured, raising three delicate fingers encased in lace to conceal a convulsive twitch of her mouth. "And your mother must have been delighted to see you." George offered a slight shrug and a low bow as his aunt lifted her keen, aged eyes. "Delighted indeed," she continued dryly, "to see you, and to kill the fatted calf—and that sort of thing. Though why I invoke the calf, nephew George, I cannot say; you were never the prodigal son. I may call it a calf to thee, my poor Harry! Thou hast certainly dwelt amongst the swine. Evil companions have stripped the coin from thy pocket and the coat from thy back."

"He came to his family in England, madam," George replied with rising heat, "and his friends were your ladyship's."

"He could not have chosen worse advisers," the old lady retorted, tossing her head. "Had she condescended to write to me through him, I would have informed my sister sooner." Later that night, arranging herself for the rout, she whispered to her maid, "This young gentleman's mother is half sorry he has returned to life; I saw it in his face. Half sorry, and I am perfectly furious! Why did he not lie still beneath the tree? Why did that young Florac carry him to the fort? I knew those Floracs in Paris, under Monsieur le Regent. They were of the Floracs of Ivry. No great house prior to Henri IV. His ancestor was the King's favorite. His ancestor—he! he!--his ancestress! Brett! Entendez-vous?" She demanded her card-purse. "I dislike the grand airs of this Monsieur George, yet he bears a striking resemblance to his grandfather—the same look, sometimes the same tone. Have you heard of Colonel Esmond when I was young? This boy possesses his eyes. I suppose I favored the Colonel's because he loved me."

Engaged in a card party—an amusement she never missed, weekday or Sabbath, so long as she retained the strength to hold trumps or occupy a chair—she soon dismissed her two nephews. To the elder, she offered a couple of fingers and a stately curtsy; to Harry, two hands and a kindly pat on the cheek. "My poor child, now thou art disinherited, thou wilt see how differently the world treats thee!" she declared. "There is only, in all London, a wicked, heartless old woman who will treat thee as before. Here is a pocket-book for you, child! Do not lose it at Ranelagh tonight. That suit becomes your brother far better than it did upon you! Present your brother to everyone, and pace the room for at least two hours, child. Were I you, I would retire to the Chocolate-House and play as if nothing had occurred. While you are there, your brother may return to me and consume a morsel of chicken. My Lady Flint serves wretched suppers, and I wish to discuss your mother's letter with him. Au revoir, gentlemen!" She departed toward her toilette, her chairmen and flambeaux already awaiting at the door.

The gentlemen proceeded to Ranelagh, where only a handful of Mr. Harry's acquaintances were present. They walked the circular path, encountering Mr. Tom Claypool with country friends; they listened to the music and drank tea in a box. Harry served as master of ceremonies, introducing his brother to the curiosities of the venue, while George displayed even greater excitement than Harry had on his initial arrival at this palace of delight. George loved music more profoundly than Harry ever did; he heard a full orchestra for the first time, witnessing a satisfactory performance of a piece by Mr. Handel. A notable instance of Harry's humility and deference to his elder brother emerged: he held George's love for music in high regard at a time when fiddling was deemed effeminate and unmanly in England, and when patriotic prints daily urged Britons to sneer at the frivolous accomplishments of Squallinis and Monsieurs.

Nobody in Britain prides itself on ignorance anymore. Conceit has vanished. Dulness is nonexistent. Arrogance is entirely unknown. Art has secured her letters of naturalization, residing here on terms of near-equality. If Mrs. Thrale chooses to marry a music-master today, her friends need not shudder. If she possessed a good fortune and maintained a competent cook, people would dine with her despite the misalliance, treating Mr. Piozzi with civility.

After Ranelagh, following Madame Bernstein's counsel, George returned to her ladyship's house, while Harry presented himself at the club, where gentlemen assembled nightly to sup and gamble. Naturally, no one alluded to Mr. Warrington's brief absence. Mr. Ruff, his former landlord, visited him with utmost gravity and civility, acting as if no rift had ever existed between them. Mr. Warrington had instructed his trunks and belongings to be removed from Bond Street that morning; he and his brother were now established in separate apartments. Yet, once supper concluded and the gentlemen prepared to ascend to the macco-table, Harry announced he would not play further. He had already burned his fingers and could afford no further extravagance.

"Why," Mr. Morris asked in a flippant tone, "must you not have won more than you lost, Mr. Warrington, after all is said and done?"

"And of course I do not understand my own affairs as well as you, Mr. Morris," Harry replied sternly, remembering the other's behavior upon hearing of his arrest. "But I have another reason. A few months or days ago, I was heir to a great estate and could afford to lose a little money. Now, thank God, I am heir to nothing." He glanced around, blushing noticeably, at the knot of gentlemen—his gaming associates—who lounged at the tables or gathered by the fire.

"How do you mean, Mr. Warrington?" cried Lord March. "Have you lost Virginia, too? Who has won it? I always fancied playing you myself for that stake."

"And grow an improved breed of slaves in the colony," another added.

"The rightful owner has won it. You have heard me tell of my twin elder brother?"

"Who was killed in that affair of Braddock's two years ago! Yes. Gracious goodness, my dear sir, I hope in heaven he has not come to life again?"

"He arrived in London two days since. He was a prisoner in a French fort for eighteen months; he escaped only a few months ago and left our house in Virginia shortly thereafter."

"You haven't had time to order mourning, I suppose, Mr. Warrington?" Mr. Selwyn asked with good-natured simplicity, though Harry struggled to grasp the jest until his brother explained it.

"Hang me, if I don't believe the fellow is absolutely glad of the reappearance of his confounded brother!" Lord March exclaimed as they continued discussing the matter after the young Virginian had taken his leave.

"These savages practice the simple virtues of affection—they are barely civilized in America yet," yawned Selwyn. "They love their kindred, and they scalp their enemies," simpered Mr. Walpole. "It is not Christian, but natural. Wouldn't you like to attend a scalping match, George, and watch a fellow skinned alive?"

"A man's elder brother is his natural enemy," Mr. Selwyn stated placidly, arranging his money and counters.

"Torture is like broiled bones and pepper. You wouldn't relish simple hanging afterwards, George!" continued Horry.

"I'm hanged if there's any man in England who would like to see his elder brother alive," said Lord March.

"No, nor his father either, my lord!" cried Jack Morris. "First time I ever knew you had one, Jack. Give me counters for five hundred."

"I say, 'tis all mighty fine about dead brothers coming to life again," Jack continued. "Who is to know that this wasn't a scheme arranged between these two fellows? Here comes a young fellow calling himself the Fortunate Youth, claiming to be a Virginian Prince and the devil knows what else, getting into our society..." A great laugh erupted at Jack's phrase "our society."

"Who is to know that this wasn't a cross?" Jack pressed. "The younger one comes first. He marries an heiress, secures her, and then the elder brother rises! When did this elder brother appear? Only when the younger's scheme failed and everything collapsed! Who can tell me that the fellow hasn't been living in Seven Dials or a cellar, dining on tripe and cow-

The air in the dining room was thick with the scent of old paper and the lingering smoke of a pipe that Harry had not yet extinguished. Outside, the world moved in a rhythm of decay and renewal, but inside, time had settled into a static, dusty suspension. George Warrington watched his aunt, Madame de Bernstein, move with the deliberate, heavy grace of a woman carrying the weight of a lifetime behind her eyes. She was a ruin of a former splendor, a palace reduced to rubble, yet she still wore her diamonds as if they were the final, defiant jewels of a crown.

"Beauty," George thought, watching her adjust her rouge. "Once a firework, now a skeleton wheel." He recalled the explosion at Williamsburg, the dazzling Roman candles that had blinded everyone before leaving only sockets and ash. His aunt's early career had been that same brilliant, terrifying flash, burning bright enough to blind the crowd, only to leave them staring at the empty sky where the light had vanished. Now, she was the aftermath. The buds of prime had fallen; the roses were withered; the naked branches shivered in the winter wind of her own making.

He wondered if it was sad or pleasant for a woman to recall her bloom when the heart was already withered. Did she love remembering how it once beat? Or did she simply endure the silence of the present, waiting for the memory to fade completely?

"And that was a beauty once!" George murmured, the thought forming involuntarily as she entered from the rout. "And that ruin was a splendid palace."

She stopped, her cane tapping a sharp, rhythmic note against the floor. She did not turn immediately. Instead, she pointed with the tip of the cane toward her escritoire, a gesture of command that belied her frailty. "Bring the letter," she said to her attendant. "The one under the inkstand."

George recognized the handwriting instantly. It was from home. From Richmond.

"It appears," the old lady said, her voice dropping to a register that sounded like dry leaves skittering on stone, "that since your return, there have been differences between you and my sister."

"Differences?" George asked, feigning surprise. "I did not know Madam Esmond had alluded to them."

The Baroness pulled a pair of spectacles onto her nose. Through the lenses, her eyes shot sparks, kindling passions that had long since cooled in the damp Virginia air. She glanced at the letter, then handed it to George. He unfolded the paper.

The words were formal, stiff, trying to impose order on chaos. The letter spoke of Harry returning from the Valley of the Shadow, of the anguish of captivity, of the false colors under which he had appeared in England. It detailed the tragedy of George's death, the injustice of Harry inheriting the title while his brother lay buried. But beneath the formalities, the raw emotion seeped through.

"My Harry's is the tenderest of any man's now alive," the letter read. "In the joy of seeing Mr. Esmond Warrington returned... he will forget the worldly misfortune which befalls him. He will return to comparative poverty without a pang."

George listened as his aunt read aloud, her voice rising and falling with the cadence of a recitation she had heard a thousand times. She paused at the mention of the lost opportunity—the land, the negroes, the noble estate that had slipped away because of legal forms and her elder son's scruples.

"I had a fancy to pay that debt myself," she interrupted, her tone shifting from reading to argument. "Why not have kept it to buy a pair of colours for him? Or to help towards another estate?"

"Nay, sir," George replied softly. "I ask leave to be my brother's banker in this matter. I have borrowed so much from my mother; it must be paid back to Harry."

"You are an extravagant fellow!" she laughed, a brittle sound. "Read on. Your mother thinks so too. Drink to your health, nephew George! 'Tis good Burgundy. Your grandfather never loved Burgundy. He loved claret."

George smiled faintly, taking the glass. He continued reading, letting the words wash over him. The letter spoke of Harry's obedience, of the danger of youth, of the unsuitability of Lady Maria Esmond. It praised Harry's polish gained in Europe, his refinement, his ability to be beloved by everyone. It was a portrait of perfection, painted by a grieving mother who saw only the virtues of her favorite son.

"There is a scratch with a penknife," George noted, pointing to a smudge near the bottom. "As if water had fallen on it. Your mother writes well, Aunt. I suppose you and she had a difference?"

"Yes, ma'am, many," George answered, his face falling into a shadow of sadness. "The last was about money. About ransom. I promised an old lieutenant who helped me escape. He had a mistress—a poor Indian woman—who helped me. She came to Richmond six weeks later, bringing the token from Museau. But at home... they received her sternly. They hardly gave her a welcome. She fell to drinking. She became the jest of our negroes, the subject of scandal. Even the Governor, usually sensible, could not stop the gossip. A fellow preached against me from the pulpit; I had to treat another with a cane. And I had a violent dispute with Madam Esmond because I insisted on paying the heirs Museau pointed out."

"I have suffered from it myself, my dear!" Madame de Bernstein said, her demeanor softening instantly. She poured a little cherry-brandy into his glass. "Fill thy glass, child! 'Twill do thee all the good in the world."

"As for my poor Harry's marriage," the letter continued, detailing the rejection of Lady Maria and the decision to let Harry return to his mother rather than face a lonely future. It ended with a postscript: *No need to tell him or your ladyship that his mother hopes to increase his small fortune. The match is altogether unsuitable.*

George laid down the paper. "My mother's letter conveys no news to me," he said quietly. "I always knew that Harry was the favorite son. He has a hundred good qualities which I have not the good fortune to possess. Better looks..."

"Nay, that is not your fault," the old lady said, looking at him slyly. "And, but that he is fair and you are brown, one might almost pass for the other."

George bowed, a faint blush tinging his pale cheek. "His disposition is bright, and mine is dark. Harry is cheerful, and I am otherwise. He knows how to make himself beloved by everyone. It has been my lot to find but few friends."

"My sister and you have pretty little quarrels," the Baroness mused, leaning forward. "There were such in old days in our family. If Madam Esmond takes after our mother—"

"My mother has always described hers as an angel upon earth," George interposed quickly.

"Eh! That is a common character for people when they are dead!" the Baroness cried, waving her fan. "Rachel Castlewood was an angel, if you like—at least your grandfather thought so. But let me tell you, sir, that angels are sometimes not very commodes à vivre. They are too good to live with us sinners. The air down below doesn't agree with them. My poor mother was so perfect she never could forgive me for being otherwise. Ah, mon Dieu! How she used to oppress me with those angelical airs!"

George cast down his eyes. He thought of his own melancholy youth, the isolation that had defined him since childhood. He did not care to submit more of his family secrets to the cynical inquisition of this old worldling, who seemed, paradoxically, to understand him perfectly despite her reticence.

"I quite comprehend you, sir, though you hold your tongue," the Baroness continued, her voice dropping to a conspiratorial whisper. "A sermon in the morning: a sermon at night: and two or three of a Sunday. That is what people call being good. Every pleasure cried *fie* upon; all us worldly people excommunicated; a ball an abomination of desolation; a play a forbidden pastime; and a game of cards perdition! What a life! Mon Dieu, what a life!"

"We played at cards every night, if we were so inclined," George said, smiling despite himself. "And my grandfather loved Shakespeare so much that my mother had not a word to say against her father's favorite author."

"I remember. He could say whole pages by heart. Though, for my part, I like Mr. Congreve a great deal better. And then, there was that dreadful, dreary Milton, whom he and Mr. Addison pretended to admire!" The old lady tapped her fan sharply against the table.

"If your ladyship does not like Shakespeare, you will not quarrel with my mother for being indifferent to him, too," George said gently. "And indeed I think—and I am sure—you don't do her justice. Wherever there are any poor, she relieves them; wherever there are any sick, she—"

"She doses them with her horrible purges and boluses!" the Baroness snapped. "Of course, just as my mother did!"

"She does her best to cure them! She acts for the best, and performs her duty as far as she knows it."

George sat back, the pipe cooling in

The air in the room was thick with the scent of tobacco and unresolved business. Harry and George sat facing one another, the silence between them filled only by the rustle of papers and the occasional clink of a glass. They had spoken of home, dissecting the landscape of their childhoods with the clinical precision of surgeons examining a corpse. Harry detailed the condition of his dogs, the health of little Dempster, and the enduring presence of Nathan. He spoke of Mountain, whose well-being seemed to hinge entirely on the weather, and Fanny, who had blossomed into a girl of considerable pretension.

Parson Broadbent's daughter, Polly, was engaged to Tom Barker of Savannah, a union destined to carry them to Georgia. Harry confessed, with a flush of shame, that he had once been infatuated with her, squandering pocket money at cards and consuming strong waters with the parson solely to secure proximity to the girl. Yet, the barrier of class remained impregnable; Madam Esmond would never permit him to throw himself away on Polly Broadbent.

George, conversely, described his wife, the widow of Colonel Washington. She was a woman of pleasing features and ample fortune, a prize he had successfully paraded before Madam Esmond in a display of rigid ceremonial courtesy. His mother had greeted the new wife with a politeness so stiff it bordered on the hostile. "Never mind, George," Mrs. Mountain had remarked, her mind already racing toward the next matrimonial arrangement. "The Colonel has taken another wife, but I feel certain that at one time two young gentlemen I know of ran a very near chance of having a tall stepfather six feet two in his boots." It was she, the relentless matchmaker, who had circulated the rumor concerning the Indian woman. Madam Esmond had dismissed the insinuation with scornful hauteur, comparing the idea of Mr. Esmond marrying a negro woman to the theft of silverware. Though she spared the woman, Biche, from direct accusation, her demeanor was so chillingly aloof that the Indian woman retreated to the servants' quarters, drowning her disappointment in alcohol.

George's account of his two months at home painted a portrait of isolation. "The birthright is mine, Harry," he said, his voice dropping. "But thou art the favourite, and God help me! I think my mother almost grudges it to me. Why should I have taken the pas, and preceded your worship into the world? Had you been the elder, you would have had the best cellar, and ridden the best nag, and been the most popular man in the country, whereas I have not a word to say for myself, and frighten people by my glum face." He lamented the inversion of natural order, invoking Shakespeare's curse: "O cursed spite, that ever I was born to set it right!"

Harry laughed, finding the sentiment theatrical. "Georgy, you are talking verses," he protested. "Somebody else talked those verses before me." George smiled, acknowledging the borrowed nature of his despair. Harry then recounted his pilgrimage to Tunbridge, where he had bowed to the authors of the books George revered, admitting he cared little for the literature itself but cherished the memory of his brother's admiration. "I say, dear, it's like a dream seeing you over again," Harry whispered, his voice catching. "Think of that bloody Indian with his knife at my George's head! I should like to give that Monsieur de Florac something for saving you—but I haven't got much now, only my little gold knee-buckles, and they ain't worth two guineas."

"You have got the half of what I have, child," George replied calmly. "And we'll divide as soon as I have paid the Frenchman."

At this, Harry erupted into a torrent of blessing and imprecation, his love manifesting as a violent need to assert his bond. He swore that no brother in the world compared to George. For days following his arrival, Harry's eyes tracked his sibling's movements. He would pause mid-meal, drop his knife, and laugh silently at the sight of George. Walking together on the Mall or in Hyde Park, Harry would gesture broadly at the passersby, presenting George as a miracle: "Look here, gentlemen! This is he. This is my brother, that was dead and is alive again! Can any man in Christendom produce such a brother as this?"

Harry insisted that George fulfill his promise to pay Museau's heirs. This demand had been a source of deep friction at home. With Museau dead, Madam Esmond had eagerly seized the opportunity to void the bargain, arguing that paying twelve thousand livres to peasant priests in Normandy—who would undoubtedly funnel the funds to Rome—was an act of wickedness. She refused to subsidize the wages of iniquity. While she acknowledged George's age and independence, she viewed the expenditure as a dangerous extravagance. Instead, she urged him to give the money to Harry, who had been stripped of his inheritance. The dispute had raged throughout George's final days in Virginia, remaining unresolved until the morning of his departure. Madam Esmond had visited his bedside, her face pale and tearless, asking if he still intended to waste his father's property. George, overwhelmed by grief and perplexity, had affirmed his oath of honor. Her final image of him was her standing by his bed, turning away slowly, leaving the room in silence.

"Where didst thou learn the art of winning over everybody to thy side, Harry?" George asked, shifting the focus to his own social failures. "How is it that you and all the world begin by being friends? Teach me a few lessons in popularity."

He recalled his initial reception in Richmond. As the sole survivor of Braddock's defeat, he had been a favorite, receiving private and public kindness. The parson had preached his escape; neighbors had visited; the family coach had been deployed. But his mother had routed off the admirers with prodigious haughtiness, extinguishing his popularity before the scandal of the Indian woman could fully take root. He was too somber for the officers' drinking bouts and dice games; too sarcastic for the ladies' tea and gossip. He felt profoundly lonely amidst the scandal, regretting his imprisonment and longing for the silent ease of Duquesne. He was shy, speaking unreservedly to few, sitting in utter silence before most. At home, Harry had been the talker, drawing smiles from their mother; with George, they shared no common ground save for disputes over law and divinity.

The gentlemen deemed him supercilious and dull; the ladies found him cold and enigmatic, unable to discern his sincerity. Their verdict was unanimous: he was a disagreeable fellow. This judgment hardened when the appearance of the Indian woman confirmed their suspicions. Boys hooted her in the streets; ladies dropped half-curtseys and crossed to the opposite side of the road. A clergyman preached sermons on the horrors of seduction and the lax principles imported from papist lands. Even the return of Fawn was interpreted as a calculated scheme between her and George. Officers bantered at the coffee house, cracking clumsy jokes about the woman he had chosen. Enraged, George considered retreating to Castlewood to live alone, seeking refuge in his aunt's ministry, until news of Harry's distress provided a pretext for his journey to Europe.

"I should like to see any of the infernal scoundrels who said word against you," Harry roared, pacing the room. "I had to do something similar for Bob Clubber."

"That little sneaking, backbiting, toad-eating wretch," Harry spat, describing the man who hung around Lord Greenway, subsisting on the charity of every gentleman in the country. "If you whipped him, I hope you whipped him well, George?"

"We were bound over to keep the peace," George explained. "I offered to go to Maryland with him to settle our differences, and the good folk remarked that, having made free with the seventh commandment, I was inclined to break the sixth. By this logic—and being as innocent of the crime imputed to me as you are—I left home with a reputation worse than any young gentleman ever earned."

There was a temptation here to moralize, to compile a memoir of the lies that surrounded them. The world knew everything about them, and far more besides. A recent critique had corrected the date of Mulligan's birth; another warned against mentioning New South Wales due to a brother residing there. These falsehoods formed a vast, noble tapestry of misinformation. Perhaps, George mused, a society of literati should collaborate on a series of biographies written by friends who did not know the subjects.

Having aired his grievances as a champion and martyr, Harry opened his own chest. He revealed the collapse of the Castlewood family dynamics. He had refused to aid the chaplain in his necessity, dismissing him with false pretexts. Regarding Mr. Will, he had made his judgment clear after the horse-dealing incident, marking his displeasure on the man's eyes and nose. His comments on the Countess and Lady Fanny were guarded but unfavorable; he had heard tales of a card-playing old cat and a desperate flirt, stories relayed by Maria herself, who had freely confided anecdotes about her stepmother and half-sister that were hardly flattering.

Yet, regarding Lady Maria, Harry was staunch and hearty. "It may be imprudent," he admitted, his voice trembling. "I may be a fool. I know there will be a dreadful piece of work at home, and that Madam and she will fight. Well! We must live apart. Our estate is big enough to live on without quarrelling, and I can go elsewhere than to Richmond or Castlewood. When you

The house in Kensington Square was a vessel designed for containment, holding the weight of the Castlewood name and the necessary machinery of its service. It was a fortress of proximity, built to keep the family close to the Palace when the King resided there, yet geographically distant from the heart of London itself. A century prior, the distance was a chasm; today, it was merely a measure of inconvenience. George Selwyn, observing the geography of his own anxiety, admitted he dared not venture forth, fearing the theft of a single night—by footpads draped in crape or by ladies powdered in rouge at the quadrille table. The threat was ambiguous, the danger statistical, but the isolation was absolute.

At noon, the day following Harry's reappearance at White's, the household convened for breakfast. The gathering was a mechanical assembly of duty: the virtuous kinsfolk, Mr. Will included, who was scheduled to depart into waiting later that afternoon. The ladies arrived first, bringing the ritual of chocolate. Mr. Will joined them in his court suit, a performance of deference. Finally, the head of the house entered, languid and unmade, clad in bedgown and nightcap, his wig still absent from his head.

Here lay the news. Will had brought it home from the Star and Garter the previous evening, sourced from whispers at White's and confirmed by sight at Ranelagh.

"Heard what? Seen what?" Lord Castlewood asked, picking up the *Daily Advertiser*, his voice flat against the silence of the morning.

"Ask Maria," Lady Fanny replied, her tone suggesting the answer was written in her expression alone. She wore a face of portentous sadness, pale as a tablecloth, a mask of grief that seemed to absorb the light of the room.

"'Tis one of Will's usual elegant and polite inventions," Maria dismissed, her voice smooth.

"No," Will swore, punctuating the denial with several oaths. "It was no invention of his. Tom Claypool of Norfolk saw them both at Ranelagh; and Jack Morris came out of White's, where he heard the story from Harry Warrington's own lips. Curse him, I'm glad of it!" Will roared, slapping the table with a force that shook the china. "What do you think of your Fortunate Youth, your Virginian, whom your lordship made so much of, turning out to be a second son?"

"The elder brother not dead?" Lord Castlewood asked, his brow furrowing.

"No more dead than you are. Never was. It's my belief that it was a cross between the two."

"Mr. Warrington is incapable of such duplicity!" Maria cried, her composure cracking.

"I never encouraged the fellow, I am sure you will do me justice there," the Lady interjected, her voice steady. "Nor did Fanny: not we, indeed!"

"Not we, indeed!" echoed Lady Fanny, her voice joining hers in a chorus of feigned innocence.

"The fellow is only a beggar, and, I dare say, has not paid for the clothes on his back," Will continued, his satisfaction palpable. "I'm glad of it, for, hang him, I hate him!"

"You don't regard him with favourable eyes; especially since he blacked yours, Will!" Lord Castlewood grinned, the humorist in him surfacing despite the gravity of the situation. "So the poor fellow has found his brother, and lost his estate!" He turned to Maria, who, though looking the picture of woe, must have suggested something ludicrous to the man beside her. For a minute, he gazed at her, then burst into a shrill laugh. The sound cut through the room, causing the poor lady's face to flush, her eyes suddenly pouring over with tears. "It's a shame! It's a shame!" she sobbed, hiding her face in her handkerchief. Maria's stepmother and sister exchanged glances, silent observers of the performance.

"We never quite understand your lordship's humour," the stepmother remarked, her voice grave.

"I don't see there is the least reason why you should," Lord Castlewood said, coolly. "Maria, my dear, pray excuse me if I have said—that is, done anything, to hurt your feelings."

"Done anything! You pillaged the poor lad in his prosperity, and laugh at him in his ruin!" Maria rose from the table, glaring around at her family, her voice rising.

"Excuse me, my dear sister, I was not laughing at him," Lord Castlewood said, gently.

"Oh, never mind at what or whom else, my lord! You have taken from him all he had to lose. All the world points at you as the man who feeds on his own flesh and blood. And now you have his all, you make merry over his misfortune!" She rustled from the room, flinging looks of defiance at the assembled party.

"Tell us what has happened, or what you have heard, Will, and my sister's grief will not interrupt us." Will recounted the tale at great length, with immense exultation at Harry's discomfiture, the story now buzzing through all of London regarding George Warrington's sudden apparition. Lord Castlewood expressed sorrow for Harry; Harry was a good, brave lad, and his kinsman liked him, as much as certain worldly folks like each other. To be sure, he played Harry at cards and took the advantage of the market upon him; but why not? The peach which other men would certainly pluck, he might as well devour. If that were all his conscience had to reproach him with, he need not be very uneasy.

"Where does Mr. Warrington live?"

Will expressed himself ready to enter upon a state of reprobation if he knew or cared.

"He shall be invited here, and treated with every respect," Lord Castlewood declared.

"Including piquet, I suppose!" Will growled.

"Or will you take him to the stables, and sell him one of your bargains of horseflesh, Will?" Lord Castlewood asked. "You would have won of Harry Warrington fast enough, if you could; but you cheat so clumsily at your game that you got paid with a cudgel. I desire, once more, that every attention may be paid to our cousin Warrington."

"And that you are not to be disturbed, when you sit down to play, of course, my lord!" cried Lady Castlewood.

"Madam, I desire fair play, for Mr. Warrington, and for myself, and for every member of this amiable family," Lord Castlewood retorted, fiercely.

"Heaven help the poor gentleman if your lordship is going to be kind to him," the stepmother said, with a curtsey. There is no knowing how far this family dispute might have been carried, had not, at this moment, a phaeton driven up to the house, in which were seated the two young Virginians.

It was the carriage the prodigal had purchased in the days of his prosperity. He drove it still. George sat by his side; their negroes were behind them. Harry had been meekly giving the whip and reins to his brother, ceding the whole property to him. "What business has a poor devil like me with horses and carriages, Georgy?" Harry had humbly said. "Beyond the coat on my back, and the purse my aunt gave me, I have nothing in the world. You take the driving-seat, brother; it will ease my mind if you will take the driving-seat." George laughingly said he did not know the way, and Harry did; and that, as for the carriage, he would claim only a half of it, as he had already done with his brother's wardrobe. "But a bargain is a bargain; if I share thy coats, thou must divide my breeches' pocket, Harry; that is but fair dealing!" Again and again Harry swore there never was such a brother on earth. How he rattled his horses over the road! How pleased and proud he was to drive such a brother! They came to Kensington in famous high spirits; and Gumbo's thunder upon Lord Castlewood's door was worthy of the biggest footman in all St. James's.

Only Lady Castlewood and her daughter Lady Fanny were in the room into which the young gentlemen were ushered. Will had no particular fancy to face Harry; my lord was not dressed; Maria had her reasons for being away, at least till her eyes were dried.

When we drive up to friends' houses nowadays in our coaches-and-six, when John carries up our noble names, when, finally, we enter the drawing-room with our best hat and best Sunday smile foremost, does it ever happen that we interrupt a family row? That we come simpering and smiling in, and stepping over the delusive ashes of a still burning domestic heat? That in the interval between the hall-door and the drawing-room, Mrs., Mr., and the Misses Jones have grouped themselves in a family tableau; this girl artlessly arranging flowers in a vase, let us say; that one reclining over an illuminated work of devotion; mamma on the sofa, with the butcher's and grocer's book pushed under the cushion, some elegant work in her hand, and a pretty little foot pushed out advantageously; while honest Jones, far from saying, "Curse that Brown, he is always calling here!" holds out a kindly hand, shows a pleased face, and exclaims, "What, Brown my boy, delighted to see you! Hope you've come to lunch!" I say, does it ever happen

The air in the drawing room was thick with the scent of stale tobacco and the dust of settled grievances. Madame de Bernstein sat, her posture rigid, watching the aftermath of a battle fought not with swords, but with silence and withheld glances. The game was over. The pieces were reset. Yet, the board remained scarred.

George Warrington entered, bringing with him the weight of a victory that tasted of ash. He spoke to his aunt of the interview with Lady Maria, detailing the transaction with a casual brutality that suited the man. He had seen the move clearly: the sacrifice of Harry, the acceptance of the lesser prize, the preservation of the family line through the elder son. It was logic stripped of emotion, a clean equation where Harry was the variable eliminated to balance the sum.

"My sister," George said, his voice smooth, devoid of tremor, "has secured her future. She will not waste another moment on a dream that cannot feed itself. I stood by my blood, as duty demands."

Madame de Bernstein nodded, her expression unreadable. "Your candour is... adequate."

"Sans rancune," George replied, offering a bow that was more of a dismissal than a greeting. "We lost the wager, but the stakes remain ours."

They dissected the scene with the clinical detachment of surgeons examining a corpse. The visit to the spunging-house, the desperate offering of gifts, the tearful farewell—it was all analyzed as a tactical error, a necessary concession to circumstance. "Had George not appeared," Eugene mused, taking a pinch of snuff as if to dry the sweat of the conversation, "we might have held the field longer. But nature abhors a vacuum, and the elder brother filled it."

The discussion drifted to the specifics of the loss. George's admission that he had advised his sister to see Harry, only to withdraw support when the older brother emerged, hung in the air. It was a confession of calculated cruelty, wrapped in the language of familial necessity. "No bread except at his brother's side-table," George explained, his tone matter-of-fact. "That was the reality. The elder said as much."

"How hard-hearted!" Madame de Bernstein exclaimed, though her voice lacked conviction.

"Audacity," George corrected gently. "Everyone plays the role assigned to them. Even Molly admitted it once the initial shock wore off. She retreated with dignity, yes—a masterly withdrawal—but she was starved out of her position."

The narrative shifted to the memory of the encounter itself. The image of the young man, muddy-stocked, hair disheveled, throwing himself among the diners with a frankness that bordered on desperation. He had offered himself, naked and vulnerable, to a woman who had already chosen the path of least resistance. The comparison to Mrs. Woffington in the new Scotch play served to elevate the tragedy into something theatrical, something distant enough to be observed without pain. They had lost, but they had lost with the honors of war.

Yet, beneath the surface of this polite exchange lay a current of genuine bitterness. The reconciliation was superficial, a diplomatic maneuver to restore the facade of the great world. Differences were smoothed over; estranged branches of the family were allowed to reunite as if no chasm had ever existed. A stray prodigal, a stray puppy-dog, welcomed back under the blanket of amnesty, despite the ugly company he had kept.

For six months, the Castlewoods and Madame de Bernstein had been locked in a silent war for possession of Harry Warrington. Now, the question was settled. The coach rolled to Lady Castlewood's rout, and the Esmond ladies smiled at Madame de Bernstein's drums, pretending that the previous animosity had never tainted the air.

"So, sir," the Baroness said, her voice rising to a playful menace, brandishing her fan like a weapon, "you acted like a hard-hearted monster regarding your poor brother."

"I acted upon your ladyship's hint," George replied, his cheeks flushing a shade of red that betrayed his composure. "To test whether it was for himself or his money they sought him."

"Nay, Maria could not marry a pauper," George insisted, the argument rehearsed and delivered with practiced ease. "And so thou didst, O thou hypocrite!" the old lady cried, her French slipping through the cracks of her indignation.

"Hypocrite?" George drew himself up, adopting a pose of mock grandeur. "Why? I know all, my infant! Thou art very like thy grandfather. Come, embrace me! Harry has told me all—that thou hast divided thy little patrimony with him!"

"It was natural," George said, his eyes filling with tears that were clearly manufactured. "Common hearts and purses since birth. I feigned hardness to try those people yonder."

"And Virginia?" the Baroness pressed.

"The land goes to the eldest," George conceded, the logic unyielding. "Though half of what is mine is his. He was extravagant because he was mistaken about his station."

Madame de Bernstein retired to her chambers, whispering to her attendant, "A knight of old! A Bayard! The grandfather come to life!" That evening, when the lads finally left, she bestowed her favors: two fingers to Harry, a rouged cheek to George. George blushed, a deep, artificial crimson, at the mark of approval from his ancient kinswoman.

Harry Warrington, however, felt none of this warmth. Though he honored his brother as his guide and superior, a profound sense of humiliation settled over him. He had been deposed from the throne of the Fortunate Youth, stripped of the boundless Virginian territories that had been his inheritance. His friends at Kensington promised love in his fall, but Harry saw the truth: George was the center of the universe. All the talk, the compliments, the *petits soins* belonged to the elder brother. Only Maria followed him, her eyes conveying a silent, dismal reproach. She had rejected him, yet she could not quite forgive him for accepting her rejection.

"Oh, my young friends," the narrator reflected, observing the absurdity of it all, "how delightful the beginning of love, and how undignified the end! Young Damon and Phillis twine their arms, surveying the *pays du tendre*, believing they can cross any river, slay any dragon. They imagine resting together under the stars, safe in the woods. But they have not walked a mile before they hit a great wall. They argue with the post-boy at Barnet; Papa appears with a whip; the girl is locked in the schoolroom crying. Years later, when Strephon meets Delia again, is it not ridiculous? Still, one should not fall in love unless one believes in the eternity of passion. Some women expect their hearts to break from eighteen to eighty-five. When the King of France marches up the hill with twenty thousand men and colors flying, only to march down again, he and you are in an absurd position."

This was Harry's position. He felt the melancholy of looking into a house once lived in, now dark and empty. He passed No. 13, where someone else grinned from the window, on friendly terms with the landlady. The sight of other boots on the sofa where he had once reclined provoked a visceral rage. He kicked the air, imagining the boots as muddy, vulgar highlows.

His period of occupation was over. Maria's rooms were to be let. He found himself unable to converse with her; her words were dull, commonplace, far less agreeable than the red lip of a white-necked pipe of Virginia. When George went to Kensington, Harry stayed behind, pleading other engagements.

At his uncle's house in Hill Street, the treatment was colder still. The virtuous people there paid him scant attention. The Ladies did not scruple to deny themselves when he arrived, a betrayal that shocked the naive Harry. Sitting with the Lamberts, he watched his aunt's chairmen arrive with an empty seat. She stepped out, entered the vehicle, and did not even blush when he bowed from the opposite window.

"To be denied by one's own relations!" Harry cried, his impetuosity breaking the tension. "To have that door slammed in my face! Did you ever see such cold-heartedness, Aunt Lambert?"

"Do we make any difference to you, my dear Harry?" Aunt Lambert asked, casting a sidelong glance at her youngest daughter. "The world may look coldly at you, but we do not belong to it. You may come to us in safety."

"In this house, you are different," Harry replied, seeking solace in the familiar. "I always feel quiet and happy."

*"Quis me uno vivit felicior? aut magis hac est Optandum vita dicere quis potuit?"* General Lambert quoted, addressing his son from college who had come to spend Easter.

"You got them out of Catullus, sir," the scholar answered.

"I got them out of no such thing," the General declared proudly. "Out of my favorite Democritus Junior—old Burton, who has provided many indifferent scholars with learning; and Montaigne."

The General's voice boomed, filling the room with the authority of a man who believed he understood the world, unaware that outside, the world had quietly closed its doors on the young man standing in the corner, holding a pipe that burned brighter than any conversation.

The air in the drawing-room was thick with the scent of beeswax and the low hum of a thousand polite anxieties. For a hundred years, the King's door had swung open daily, a ritual of loyalty where nobility fed on the sight of the sovereign. But now, the war had changed the geometry of things. To avoid the enemy's ships, to prevent the catastrophic loss of the royal person at sea, the monarch had been forced to abandon his native Hanover. He remained here, amongst the Britons he loved reluctantly, a captive of geography rather than choice.

Madam Esmond viewed this displacement with a pragmatic clarity that bordered on cynicism. Her sons, the Virginians, were Jacobites in spirit, yet bound by the hard facts of survival. The King *de facto* was the King; he had been crowned, anointed, and had defeated the claimant *de jure* in battle. Domestic morality mattered less than the throne. If the monarch offered a post or a ribbon, Madam Esmond accepted it as inevitable. She had instructed her sons to present themselves, unaware that George had already stolen Harry's laced coat for the journey to Kensington.

Mr. Warrington arranged the introduction through his uncle, an assiduous courtier, while General Lambert accompanied him to thank the Crown for his promotion. They traveled in a hackney-coach, a modest vessel compared to the grandeur they sought, arriving at the gate of Kensington Palace. The sentries recognized the General, and the pair walked on foot through the portico, past galleries lined with tapestries and busts, until they reached the great black marble staircase. From there, they entered the King's drawing-room, a cavern of art housing Titian's Venus and Rubens' St. Francis.

Here, amidst the murmur of visitors, George Warrington stood transfixed. He had never seen a palace before. The paintings seemed to hold a power that transcended their frames: Van Dyck's portrait of Charles I and his family, Tintoret's Esther before Ahasuerus, all rendered in magnificent Venetian hues. George drank in these images with a hunger that drowned out the whispers of General Lambert listing the names of the powerful surrounding them—the Lords of the Admiralty, the Paymaster Fox, whose waistcoat was rumored to be worth a fortune, the Chief Justice Willes, the learned Doctor Sherlock.

"Yonder," Lambert whispered, guiding George's gaze through the crowd, "are the Admiralty. That stout gentleman belted with gold is Mr. Fox. He carries the *auri fames* on his person; his waistcoat is a perfect Potosi."

"Aliena appetens," George murmured, trying to recall the Latin. "How goes the text?"

"He loves to get money and to spend it," Lambert corrected, his tone flat. "And there is Lord Chief Justice Willes, talking to Lord Salisbury and Doctor Headley. He belongs to your grandfather's time. Loved by Dick Steele, hated by the Dean. Beside him stands Lord London, Doctor Sherlock. The lawn-sleeves have lost half their honors. Remember when I was a boy, kneeling to the Bishop of Rochester? That handsome fair man is Admiral Smith, President of Byng's court-martial. The French broke him when he was a lieutenant for lowering topsails to a French ship, yet the King made him a captain the next day. And that tall, haughty figure? Lord George Sackville. Once I was a Major-General, he might treat me better than a footman. I wish my old Blakeney were here; he is the soldier's darling."

As Lambert spoke, a young gentleman approached, mistaking the General for someone else. "I have the honor of speaking to Mr.—?"

"To Major-General Lambert, at your lordship's service," Lambert replied smoothly. "That, Mr. Warrington, is the first commoner in England, Mr. Speaker Onslow. Where is your uncle? I shall have to present you myself if Sir Miles delays."

Lambert turned his attention entirely to George, ignoring his colleague who stalked away with a startled expression. In another age, a nobleman demanded admiration; here, the hierarchy shifted with the arrival of the sovereign.

Sir Miles Warrington appeared, his red waistcoat bright against the gloom. Cordial greetings erupted between him, his nephew, and the General. The Baronet was drowning in emotion, choked by the sheer generosity of his feelings. "We miss you, we positively miss you!" he cried, wiping his eyes. "My daughters were in love with those lovely young ladies... We feel as if we were old friends already... We love you, on account of your friendship and kindness to our dear nephews." He paused, overwhelmed. "My own flesh and blood, sir; my dear, dear brother's boys!"

While Sir Miles poured out his heart, George restrained his laughter with admirable gravity. Suddenly, the door to the King's apartments opened. Pages swept in, announcing the monarch. He was followed by his son, the Duke of Cumberland, a corpulent prince in a coat of blazing scarlet. Behind them trailed the ministers of state, including the towering figure of Mr. Secretary Pitt, whose eagle eye and beak cut through the room. Even a century later, the image of Pitt struck awe—a colossus striding the narrow island, his gouty shoes silent, his hand wrapped in flannel like a thunderbolt.

Perhaps anger simmered beneath his dark looks. Hours of eloquent, repulsive speeches had likely bored the small, shrewd old gentleman who disliked fine broderie and fine brose alike. The sublime Minister passed solemnly through the crowd; the company ranged along the walls. The King walked the circle, his royal son lagging behind to engage select individuals.

The monarch was a little, keen, fresh-colored man with protruding eyes, dressed in plain, snuff-colored clothes and brown stockings, his only ornament the blue ribbon of the Garter. He spoke with a German accent, addressing those he chose to notice before moving on. He knew General Lambert, who had served under him at Dettingen and in Scotland.

"It is not always," the King said, his voice easy and simple, "that we can do as we like; but I was glad when, for once, I could give myself that pleasure in your case, General; for my army contains no better officer as you."

Lambert blushed, deeply gratified. The King's gaze then fell on Sir Miles Warrington and the young man beside him.

"Who is this?" the Defender of the Faith asked, pointing to George, who stood in a respectful posture wearing Harry's best embroidered suit.

"With the deepest reverence," Sir Miles informed the King, "this young gentleman is my nephew, Mr. George Warrington, of Virginia, who asks leave to pay his humble duty."

"This, then, is the other brother?" the Prince observed. "He came in time, else the other brother would have spent all the money." Turning to Lord Salisbury, he added, "Why do you come out in this bitter weather? You had much better stay at home!"

The King moved on, leaving Sir Miles shaken by the royal condescension. He clapped his nephew's hands. "God bless you, my boy," he cried. "I told you that you would see the greatest monarch and the finest gentleman in the world. Is he not so, my Lord Bishop?"

"That, that he is!" cried the Bishop, clasping his ruffled hands and turning his eyes to the sky. "The best of princes and of men."

Lambert pointed out another young gentleman, Louis, the favorite nephew of Lady Yarmouth. Soon, the Duke of Cumberland approached their group. He held out his hand to Lambert.

"Congratulate you on your promotion, Lambert," the Prince said good-naturedly.

"I owe it, sir, to your Royal Highness's good offices," Lambert replied gratefully.

"Not at all; not at all: ought to have had it a long time before. Always been a good officer; perhaps there'll be some employment for you soon. This is the gentleman whom James Wolfe introduced to me?"

"His brother, sir."

"Oh, the real Fortunate Youth! You were with poor Ned Braddock in America—a prisoner, and lucky enough to escape. Come and see me, sir, in Pall Mall. Bring him to my levee, Lambert." The broad back of the Prince turned, dismissing them.

"It is raining!" Sir Miles shouted, seizing the moment. "You came on foot, General Lambert? You and George must come home in my coach. You must and shall come home with me, I say. By George, you must! I'll have no denial."

He drove them back to Hill Street, presenting the General to his wife and daughters, Flora and Dora, insisting they share a collation. "What, there is only cold mutton? Well, an old soldier can eat cold mutton. And a good glass of my Lady Warrington's own cordial, prepared with her own hands, will keep the cold wind out."

"Delicious cordial! Capital mutton!" Sir Miles declared, explaining the scarcity. "Our own, my dear General, our own from the country, six years old if a day. We keep a plain table; but all the Warringtons since the Conqueror have been remarkable for their love of mutton; and our meal may look a little scanty, and is, for we are plain people, and I am obliged to keep my rascals of servants on board-wages. Can't give them seven-year-old m

The narrative machinery of fiction requires fuel beyond the mundane. A writer dealing with the profession of arms—the clash of steel, the display of courage, the specific geometry of danger—can afford to recount actual affairs. But the lives of the rest? The sugar merchant, the lawyer buried in black-letter texts, the tailor measuring breeches on a board? To chronicle their daily grind, the rustical history of eighteenpence a day, would be unbearable. Even the poets of the age, in their desperate flight from reality, dressed shepherds in pink breeches and chintz waistcoats, tying flageolets with blue satin ribbons. They invented a pastoral that denied the labor of the soil.
