So, the novelist is forced to the periphery. We wander the theatre-lobbies, the coffee-houses, the pleasure-haunts. Meanwhile, the great serious world plods in its chambers, toils at its looms, jogs on its accustomed labors. We see our characters only when they are disengaged, only when they are away from their work. Corydon carts litter and threshes barley before he can make love to Phillis; Ancillula dresses the nursery and waits on her misses before she can speak through the area-railings to Boopis, the policeman. All day, heels beat the stale pavement until the moment arrives to snatch a hasty kiss or a furtive cold pie. It is only in these stolen intervals that we can bring the character and the reader together.

Madame de Bernstein found her nephew, Harry Warrington, increasingly invisible. The macaronis and fine gentlemen at White's and Arthur's had shown him a cold shoulder; the Ring, the Mall, and the gaming-tables knew him no more. Yet the old lady, who had once loved him with such eager intensity, now took the side of the world against him. She vowed that the indifference of the world would be conquered if he possessed the courage to face it. But Harry was too honest to wear a smiling face when discontented; too genuine to disguise mortification or anger with the adroit flatteries required of society.

"You pull a long face," the old woman said, her tone shifting from warmth to patronizing dismissal. "Everybody has to put up with impertinences. If you get a box on the ear now that you are poor and cast down, you must say nothing, bear it with a smile, and revenge it ten years after. Moi qui vous parle! Do you suppose I have had no humble-pie to eat? All of us in our turn are called upon to swallow it. Win back the place you have lost. Go about more than ever. Be civil to everybody—to all women especially. Show your spirit. With economy and your brother's generosity, you can still make a genteel figure. Look out amongst the merchants in the City. They won't know you are out of fashion at the Court end. When did you go to see my Lady Yarmouth? Improve that connexion!"

Instead of the smiles and kisses of the past, she received him with coldness. She became peevish, casting gibes and scorn at him before her guests, making his honest face flush with humiliation. Her servants, once eager, barely acknowledged him. My Lady was often indisposed; her people did not press him to wait, did not volunteer to ask if he would stay to dine. Harry carried his woes to Mrs. Lambert. In a passion of sorrow, he told her of his aunt's cruelty. He sat glum in a window-seat, heart-sick and silent, while the good lady and her daughters moved to and fro, busy with the cares of the house.

"I know you are the best people alive," he would say to the ladies, "and the kindest, and that I must be the dullest company in the world."

"Well, you are not very lively, Harry," said Miss Hetty, beginning to command him, perhaps asking herself, "Is this the gentleman I took to be such a hero?"

"If he is unhappy, why should he be lively?" asked Theo, gently. "He has a good heart, and is pained at his friends' desertion. Surely there is no harm in that?"

"I would have too much spirit to show I was hurt," cried Hetty, clenching her little fists. "And I would smile, though that horrible old painted woman boxed my ears. She is horrible, mamma. You think so yourself, Theo! Own, now, you think so yourself!"

"I mayn't like her," said Theo, turning very red. "But there is no reason why I should call Harry's aunt names before Harry's face."

"You provoking thing; you are always right!" cried Hetty. "Indeed, Harry, it was very wrong of me to make rude remarks about any of your relations."

"I don't care about the others, Hetty; but it seems hard that this one should turn upon me. I had got to be very fond of her; and you see, it makes me mad, somehow, when people I'm very fond of turn away from me."

"Suppose George were to do so?" asked Hetty.

Harry stood up, looking resolute and angry. "You don't know my brother as I know him—or you wouldn't take such a liberty as to suppose my brother George could do anything unkind or unworthy!" His face flushed.

Hetty turned very white. Then she looked up at Harry, and then she did not say a single word.

Before taking leave, Harry said in his simple way, "I'm very sorry, and I beg your pardon, Hetty, if I said anything rough... but I always fight up if anybody says anything against George."

Hetty did not answer, but gave him her hand and dropped a prim little curtsey.

That night, making curl-paper confidences, Hetty whispered to Theo, "Oh! I thought it would be so happy to see him every day. And now I do see him, I go on offending him. I can't help offending him; and I know he is not clever, Theo. But oh! isn't he good, and kind, and brave? Didn't he look handsome when he was angry?"

"You silly little thing, you are always trying to make him look handsome," Theo replied.

General Lambert chose to apply the monosyllable "Bo" to his wife as a rebuke for her inveterate love of sentiment and propensity to match-making—a trait belonging to every woman whose heart is worth a fig. He hinted that Madam Lambert was a goose if she fancied the two Virginian lads would fall in love with the young women of the house. Little Het might have her fancy; little girls will, but they get it over. "And you know, Molly," the General noted, "you fancied somebody else before you fancied me." But Harry had evidently not been smitten by Hetty; he was superseded by his elder brother, and could not even claim the coat upon his back as his own. Master Harry was no great catch.

"Oh yes: now he is poor we will show him the door, as all the rest of the world does, I suppose," said Mrs. Lambert.

"That is what I always do, isn't it, Molly? Turn my back on my friends in distress?" asked the General.

"No, my dear! I am a goose, now, and that I own, Martin!" said the wife, having recourse to the usual pocket-handkerchief.

"Let the poor boy come to us and welcome: ours is almost the only house in this selfish place where so much can be said for him. He is unhappy, and to be with us puts him at ease; in God's name let him be with us!" said the kind-hearted officer. Accordingly, whenever poor crestfallen Hal wanted a dinner or an evening's entertainment, Mr. Lambert's table had a corner for him. So was George welcome, too.

George went among the Lamberts, not at first with the cordiality Harry felt, for George was colder in his manner, more mistrustful of himself and others. But there was a goodness and friendliness about the family which touched almost all who came into frequent contact with them. George soon learned to love them for their own sake, as well as for their constant regard for his brother. He saw how sad Harry was and pitied his brother's depression. In his sarcastic way, George would often take himself to task before his brother for coming to life again.

"Dear Harry, I am George the Unlucky, though you have ceased to be Harry the Fortunate. Florac would have done much better not to pass his sword through that Indian's body, and to have left my scalp as an ornament for the fellow's belt. I say he would, sir! At White's the people would have respected you. Our mother would have wept over me, as a defunct angel, instead of being angry with me for again supplanting her favourite—you are her favourite, you deserve to be her favourite: everybody's favourite: only, if I had not come back, your favourite, Maria, would have insisted on marrying you; and that is how the gods would have revenged themselves upon you for your prosperity."

"I never know whether you are laughing at me or yourself, George," said the brother. "I never know whether you are serious or jesting."

"Precisely my own case, Harry, my dear!" said George.

"But this I know, that there never was a better brother in the world; and never better people than the Lamberts."

"Never was truer word said!" cried George, taking his brother's hand.

The theater emptied into the cool, damp air of the street. The performance was done, the artificial passions extinguished, and the friends piled into the coach for Mr. Warrington's lodging. There, a supper awaited—an elegant arrangement funded by the Virginian's generosity. Mr. Warrington sought to impress; the General and his wife, pleased to extend kindness to their younger charges, accepted. The party assembled: the General, his wife, their son from college, two blooming daughters, Mr. Spencer of the Temple—a new acquaintance from the coffee-house—and the hosts. They ate the landlady's fare with cheerful indifference to the menu. The seating arrangement remains a mystery to the narrator, save for one detail: Miss Theo sat beside the chickens, while Mr. George Warrington found himself paired with Miss Hetty and a ham, sharing the attention of Mr. Harry. Mrs. Lambert presumably occupied George's right hand, leaving the General, his son, and the Templar to fill the remaining spaces.

Conversation drifted back to the play. Some insisted it was delightful; others, more skeptical, held a different view.

"As for what our gentlemen say," Mrs. Lambert interjected sharply at Mr. Spencer, "believe none of it. 'Tis a delightful piece, and my husband and Mr. George behaved as ill as possible."

"We laughed in the wrong place, and when we ought to have cried," the General admitted. "That is the truth."

"You caused the people in the boxes to turn and cry 'Hush!' You made the pit demand silence!" Mrs. Lambert continued, her voice rising. "Such behavior I have never witnessed, and I blushed for you, Mr. Lambert!"

"Mamma thought it was a tragedy, and we thought it was fun," the General replied, turning to his daughter. "George and I behaved perfectly well, didn't we, Theo?"

"Not when I was looking your way, Papa!" Theo retorted.

"Was there ever such a saucy baggage seen?" the General demanded.

"I spoke only when bid," Theo said, lowering her eyes modestly. "I was moved by the play, by the beauty and acting of Mrs. Woffington. I was sorry for the mother losing her child. I am sorry, Papa, if I ought not to have been sorry!" she added, offering a small, knowing smile.

"Women are not so clever as men," Hetty chimed in from across the table, casting a sly glance at Harry. "Next time, Brother Jack, pinch us when we must cry, or nudge us when we must laugh."

"I wish we'd seen the fight," the General mused. "Norval versus the gigantic Norwegian—that would have been rare sport. Write to Jack, suggest it to Mr. Rich."

"I haven't seen that," Harry said gravely, wondering if his comment landed with wit as the company chuckled. "But I saw Slack and Broughton at Marybone Gardens! It requires no giant to knock over the little fellow in the red boots. I could throw him over my shoulder."

"Mr. Garrick is a little man," Mr. Spencer observed. "Yet there are moments when he appears a giant. Remember his Macbeth, Mr. Warrington? That dagger scene! You should have seen our host, ladies! I introduced you in the greenroom to Mr. Garrick and Mrs. Pritchard. Lady Macbeth even took a pinch from his box."

"Did the wife of the Thane of Cawdor sneeze?" the General asked, adopting a theatrical, awful tone.

"She thanked Mr. Warrington in tones so hollow and tragic he nearly dropped his rappee, for Macbeth sneezed thrice."

"Macbeth, Macbeth, Macbeth!" the General cried.

"And the great philosopher standing by Mr. Johnson said, 'Mind, Davy, lest thy sneeze awaken Duncan!' Who, by the way, was conversing with the three witches as they sat against the wall."

"What! Behind the scenes?" Theo exclaimed. "I would give worlds to go behind the scenes!"

"And see the ropes pulled, smell the tallow candles, examine the pasteboard gold and tinsel jewels, and study the painted old women?" the host replied, demurely lifting a glass of hock. "No. Do not look too close."

"Nay, George!" Theo protested.

"Nay? I say yes! You were angry with your papa and me," George corrected gently. "If I may speak for you, sir, as well as myself," he bowed to the General, "I think we were not inclined to weep like the ladies because we stood behind the author's scenes. Looking close up at the young hero, we saw how much was rant and tinsel. As for the pale, tragical mother, her pallor was white chalk, and her grief merely a pocket-handkerchief. Admit it, Theo—you thought me unfeeling?"

"If you find it out without my confession, what is the point of admitting it?" Theo countered.

"Suppose I died," George pressed. "And you saw Harry in grief? You would witness genuine affliction, a real tragedy, and grieve too. But you would not be affected if you saw the undertaker in weepers and a black cloak!"

"Indeed, I should," Mrs. Lambert declared firmly. "Any daughter of mine would."

"Perhaps we might find weepers of our own, Mr. Warrington," Theo suggested.

"Would you?" George asked, his cheeks flushing alongside hers. Hetty's bright eyes watched them both.

"The elder writers understood little of the pathetic," Mr. Spencer remarked, the Temple wit dispensing snuff to the right and left.

"Sophocles and Antigone?" John Lambert called out.

"Our wits trouble themselves little about him unless an Oxford gentleman reminds us," Spencer replied. "I meant to stop at Shakespeare, who, as you agree, does not grasp the elegant and pathetic as well as the moderns. Has he approached Belvidera, Monimia, or Jane Shore? Can you find the elegance of Congreve in his comic females?"

"Must Mr. Spencer have tried his hand?" someone asked.

"Many gentlemen of leisure have. Mr. Garrick returned a piece of mine."

"And I confess I have four acts of a play in one of my boxes," George said.

"It's as good as any of theirs," Harry whispered to his neighbor.

"A tragedy or comedy?" Mrs. Lambert asked.

"Oh, a tragedy, with two or three dreadful murders," George answered.

"Let us play it! Let the audience guard their eyes! Yet my chief humor is for a tyrant," the General stated.

"The tragedy! Fetch it, Gumbo!" Mrs. Lambert commanded the black servant.

Gumbo bowed low. "Tragedy? Yes, madam."

"In the great cowskin trunk," George instructed gravely.

"Yes, sir," Gumbo replied with superior gravity.

"But my tragedy is buried under linen, packages, books, and boots," Hetty noted.

"Fling the linen out the window!" Hetty cried.

"The trunk is at our agent's in Bristol. Gumbo must secure post-horses; we can wait until he returns the day after tomorrow," George decided.

The ladies groaned a comical "Oh!" The General, perhaps more seriously, proposed gratitude for their escape. "Let us think of going home. Our young gentlemen have treated us nobly. We will drink a parting bumper to Madam Esmond Warrington of Castlewood, in Virginia. Suppose, boys, you find a tall, handsome stepfather when you arrive? Ladies as old as she have married before now."

"To Madam Esmond Warrington, my old schoolfellow!" Mrs. Lambert toasted. "I shall write to tell her what a pretty supper her sons gave us. And, Mr. George, I won't say how ill you behaved at the play!" With this final toast, the company dispersed. The General's coach, accompanied by a servant with a flambeau, waited to carry his family home.

After such an entertainment, a visit from the host seemed natural, a courtesy to ensure his guests had reached home safely. Their coach might easily have been waylaid in the open country behind Montague House; the ladies might have caught cold or slept poorly after the emotional exertion of the tragedy. There was no excuse needed for the inquiry. Mr. Warrington closed his books early at the Sloane Museum, perhaps realizing he remembered little of what he had read, and walked away.

What drove this eagerness? This hesitation? This knocking at the door of Mr. Lambert's lodgings in Dean Street, surveying the footman summoned to answer? Did any young man read? Did any old man remember? Did any weary, pulseless heart recall the time of its full beat? It had been hundreds of years since some were young; we forget, but we do not all forget. No, madam, we remember with advantage, as Shakespeare's Harry promised his soldiers at Agincourt. Worn old chargers turned to grass, yet if the trumpet sounds over the hedge, may we not kick up our old heels and gallop briefly in the paddock? I care not for clowns or fairies now; I like to see children at a pantomime. I do not dance or eat supper anymore, but I like to watch Eugenio and Flirtilla twirl, or Lucinda and Ardentio pull crackers. Burn your little fingers! Blaze kindly flames from each other's eyes! The air in the drawing room was thick with the scent of snuff and suppressed rage. Sir Miles Warrington and his wife sat in a silence that felt heavier than the accusation hanging between them. Their nephew, George, stood near the window, a figure of polite indifference amidst the storm.

They understood the indignation of loyal Britons when they read of Mr. George Warrington's conduct toward a gallant Prince, the beloved son of the best of monarchs. What an inestimable favor had the young man slighted! What a chance of promotion had he thrown away!

Will Esmond, whose tongue was always rich in blasphemies, employed his strongest curses to describe his cousin's behavior. He expressed delight that the confounded young Mohock was cutting his own throat. Cousin Castlewood offered a charitable interpretation: perhaps a savage gentleman had a right to scalp himself if he liked; or maybe our cousin Mr. Warrington had heard enough of the war-whoop in Braddock's affair and simply lacked the stomach for fighting.

Mr. Will rejoiced that the younger brother had gone to the deuce, and he rejoiced further to think the elder was following him. The first time he met the fellow, Will said, he should take care to let Mr. George know what he thought of him.

"If you intend to insult George, at least you had best take care that his brother Harry is out of hearing!" cried Lady Maria.

More curses followed from Mr. Will regarding his twin kinsfolk.

"Ta, ta, ta!" said my lord. "No more of this squabbling! We can't be all warriors in the family!"

"I never heard your lordship laid claim to be one!" said Maria.

"Never, my dear; quite the contrary! Will is our champion, and one is quite enough in the house. So I dare say with the two Mohocks;—George is the student, and Harry is the fighting man. When you intended to quarrel, Will, what a pity it was you had not George, instead of t'other, to your hand!"

"Your lordship's hand is famous—at piquet," said Will's mother.

"It is a pretty one," said my lord, surveying his fingers with a simper. "My Lord Hervey's glove and mine were of a size. Yes, my hand, as you say, is more fitted for cards than for war. Yours, my Lady Castlewood, is pretty dexterous, too. How I bless the day when you bestowed it on my lamented father!"

In this play of sarcasm, as in some other games of skill, his lordship was not sorry to engage, having a cool head and being able to beat his family all round.

Madame de Bernstein, when she heard of Mr. Warrington's bevue, was exceedingly angry. She stormed and scolded her immediate household; she would have scolded George but she was growing old and had not the courage of her early days. Moreover, she was a little afraid of her nephew and respectful in her behavior to him.

"You will never make your fortune at court, nephew!" she groaned, when, soon after his discomfiture, the young gentleman went to wait upon her.

"It was never my wish, madam," said Mr. George, in a very stately manner.

"Your wish was to help Harry? You might hereafter have been of service to your brother, had you accepted the Duke's offer. Princes do not love to have their favors refused, and I don't wonder that his Royal Highness was offended."

"General Lambert said the same thing," George confessed, turning rather red. "And I see now that I was wrong. But you must please remember that I had never seen a court before, and I suppose I am scarce likely to shine in one."

"I think possibly not, my good nephew," said the aunt, taking snuff. "And what then?" asked George.

"I never had ambition for that kind of glory, and can make myself quite easy without it. When his Royal Highness spoke to me—most kindly, as I own—my thought was, I shall make a very bad soldier, and my brother would be a very good one. He has a hundred good qualities for the profession, in which I am deficient; and would have served a Commanding Officer far better than I ever could. Say the Duke is in battle, and his horse is shot, as my poor chief's was at home, would he not be better for a beast that had courage and strength to bear him anywhere, than with one that could not carry his weight?"

"Au fait. His Royal Highness's charger must be a strong one, my dear!" said the old lady.

"Expende Hannibalem," muttered George, with a shrug.

"Our Hannibal weighs no trifle."

"I don't quite follow you, sir, and your Hannibal," the Baroness remarked.

"When Mr. Wolfe and Mr. Lambert remonstrated with me as you have done, madam," George rejoined, with a laugh, "I made this same defense which I am making to you. I said I offered to the Prince the best soldier in the family, and the two gentlemen allowed that my blunder at least had some excuse. Who knows but that they may set me right with his Royal Highness? The taste I have had of battles has shown me how little my genius inclines that way. We saw the Scotch play which everybody is talking about t'other night. And when the hero, young Norval, said how he longed to follow to the field some warlike lord, I thought to myself, 'how like my Harry is to him, except that he doth not brag.' Harry is pining now for a red coat, and if we don't mind, will take the shilling. He has the map of Germany for ever under his eyes, and follows the King of Prussia everywhere. He is not afraid of men or gods. As for me, I love my books and quiet best, and to read about battles in Homer or Lucan."

"Then what made a soldier of you at all, my dear? And why did you not send Harry with Mr. Braddock, instead of going yourself?" asked Madame de Bernstein.

"My mother loved her younger son the best," said George, darkly. "Besides, with the enemy invading our country, it was my duty, as the head of our family, to go on the campaign. Had I been a Scotchman twelve years ago, I should have been a—"

"Hush, sir! or I shall be more angry than ever!" said the old lady, with a perfectly pleased face.

George's explanation might thus appease Madame de Bernstein, an old woman whose principles we fear were but loose: but to the loyal heart of Sir Miles Warrington and his lady, the young man's conduct gave a severe blow indeed!

"I should have thought," her ladyship said, "from my sister Esmond Warrington's letter, that my brother's widow was a woman of good sense and judgment, and that she had educated her sons in a becoming manner. But what, Sir Miles, what, my dear Thomas Claypool, can we think of an education which has resulted so lamentably for both these young men?"

"The elder seems to know a power of Latin, though, and speaks the French and the German too. I heard him with the Hanover Envoy, at the Baroness's rout," said Mr. Claypool. "The French he jabbered quite easy: and when he was at a loss for the High Dutch, he and the Envoy began in Latin, and talked away till all the room stared."

"It is not language, but principles, Thomas Claypool!" exclaimed the virtuous matron. "What must Mr. Warrington's principles be, when he could reject an offer made him by his Prince? Can he speak the High Dutch? So much the more ought he to have accepted his Royal Highness's condescension, and made himself useful in the campaign! Look at our son, look at Miles!"

"Hold up thy head, Miley, my boy!" said papa.

"I trust, Sir Miles, that, as a member of the House of Commons, as an English gentleman, you will attend his Royal Highness's levee to-morrow, and say, if such an offer had been made to us for that child, we would have taken it, though our boy is but ten years of age."

"Faith, Miley, thou wouldst make a good little drummer or fifer!" said papa. "Shouldst like to be a little soldier, Miley?"

"Anything, sir, anything! a Warrington ought to be ready at any moment to have himself cut in pieces for his sovereign!" cried the matron, pointing to the boy; who, as soon as he comprehended his mother's proposal, protested against it by a loud roar, in the midst of which he was removed by Screwby.

In obedience to the conjugal orders, Sir Miles went to his Royal Highness's levee the next day, and made a protest of his love and duty, which the Prince deigned to accept, saying: "Nobody ever supposed that Sir Miles Warrington would ever refuse any place offered to him."

A compliment gracious indeed, and repeated everywhere by Lady Warrington, as showing how implicitly the august family on the throne could rely on the loyalty of the Warringtons.

Accordingly, when this worthy couple saw George, they received him with a ghastly commiseration, such as our dear relatives or friends will sometimes extend to us when we

The air in the Lambert household was thick with the static of a continent at war, yet inside, the atmosphere remained suspended in a peculiar, suffocating stillness. Harry Warrington existed in this space as a monument to potential energy that refused to discharge. While Europe burned under the hammer of Frederick the Great, and the world held its breath awaiting the thunder of Rossbach or the slaughter of Leuthen, Harry remained anchored to the floorboards of London. It was a mercy, perhaps, that he stayed away; for if he had marched alongside the Prussian hero, the narrative would have shifted from domestic comedy to the grim chronicle of death, a genre reserved for other pens and far more terrible realities.

Instead, Harry lived a life of deliberate stagnation. He drifted through the military coffee-houses, a ghost among men who spoke of glory he would not claim. His interests were narrow, almost pathological: newspapers, and nothing else. Literature bored him; the sentimental weeping of society over Richardson's novels seemed a performance of exquisite stupidity. He possessed a capacity for laughter, but it was always late, arriving after the joke had already expired, a reflex rather than a response. Why should a gentleman possess a literary soul? Was the worth of a man measured by the couplets he could spin? He was ruined, idle, dependent on his brother's charity, sleeping over books he despised. Yet, in this paralysis, there was a strange safety. To be idle was to be safe. To be active, as Achilles once was, was to invite catastrophe. Had he been sent to the court of some distant king, surrounded by women and distaffs, what fate awaited him? Where would the mighty Pyrrhus have emerged from such softening? A young man of his mettle, dawdling by the hearth, was a contradiction waiting to explode.

Two women, the elder sisters of the Lambert family, offered him the only comfort available: a pity that bordered on the maternal. They were the gentle physicians of his suffering, soothing his wrath, amplifying his joys, and bandaging the invisible wounds inflicted by Fortune. But Theo and her mother were not alone in their concern. Hetty, however, viewed Harry differently. Her heart was harder, edged with a savage impatience. The world was in arms; harvest was underway with its thousand reapers; why could he not wield a musket or a sickle? To her, his dependence was not a tragedy but a crime against nature.

"Why, how savage the little thing is!" her father observed later, watching Hetty fire verbal volleys at Harry, who sat like a quivering target in the drawing-room.

"Abominable," her mother cried, envisioning a whip and a bed.

Theo, observing the scene, offered a softer interpretation. Perhaps Hetty's anger stemmed from affection. If one loved someone, one desired their perfection, their constant elevation above the rest. "My father is so much better than the world," Mrs. Lambert admitted, her voice tinged with pride. "He has spoiled us."

"He is good-tempered," Theo conceded. "Brave?"

"Mr. Wolfe, Harry, George—they are all brave."

"Witty?"

"George is learned, in his way. His manners are fine. Madame de Bernstein confirms it. He possesses a loftiness, a choice for noble things even amidst his satire. He treats the wicked with bitterness, but his heart remains tender. Yesterday, he bought apples for a crowd of children. The day before, he gave coin to a beggar. He reads Moliere beautifully, though I dare not recite the verses myself."

"And you know them by heart?"

"Oh yes, mamma! Nonsense!"

Theo fled the room, her cheeks flushed, leaving her mother staring after her with an odd, searching intensity. Why did she watch for George? Why did her cheeks burn? Time moved on, indifferent to these small dramas, cycling through seasons of bloom and decay, while the characters remained trapped in the amber of their own perceptions.

When George finally arrived, Theo descended with a fluttering heart, carrying a bouquet culled specifically for his honor. She watched from the window, noting his generosity to the sweep, his purchases from the fruit-seller. But if it was Harry who knocked, she retreated to her sanctuary of work and books, delegating the reception to her sister or her brothers. Theo's eyes were sharp instruments, distinguishing the golden hair of Harry from the dark locks of George, despite their striking resemblance. Hetty, conversely, showed no excitement, no rudeness, when the black-haired gentleman entered.

The visitors were welcome to stay as long as they pleased. One afternoon, Harry sat on a couch, radiating a despondency deeper than usual. Hetty appeared.

"What makes your honour look so glum this morning?" she asked.

"Nothing but looking glum," Harry replied. "Do you recall when we were boys? I was always asking for holidays, passing them swinging on gates or making ducks and drakes. Those days were the most melancholy. What have I to do now? Breakfast, walk, dinner, walk, tea, supper, and a pipe of Virginia."

"When I returned to the Chartreux last night," Harry continued, "I wanted to tell the master: 'Teach me. This boy knows more Latin and Greek at thirteen than I do at twenty. I have nothing to do, and I might as well try to repair my childhood's negligence.'"

"I laugh to imagine you at the head of a class," Hetty said.

"I wouldn't be," Harry admitted humbly. "George could lead any class. I am not a bookman. Neglect in youth, idleness—it might have been cured by a cane. Your account of Tom's schooling makes me realize I wasted my prime. For what am I good? Spending my patrimony abroad, lounging in coffee-houses, racing dogs. I am good for nothing."

"Such a great, brave, strong fellow, good for nothing?" Hetty exclaimed. "I would not confess that to any woman, even if she were twice as useless!"

"What am I to do? I seek leave for the army, but Madam Esmond ignores me. It is the only path for me. I have no money. I have spent mine and my brother's. If my mother would send me, I would jump."

"A gentleman of spirit does not need a woman to buckle his sword or clean his firelock," Hetty declared. "Recall Sir John Armytage, the noble young lord from the North, engaged to Miss Howe. Is marriage an obstacle to glory?"

"To what?"

"To glory!" Hetty shouted, pacing the room with a sudden, fierce energy. "No woman of spirit says 'Stay!' when her country says 'Go!' Sir John volunteered. The King asked when he would depart. 'Tomorrow,' he replied. That is a soldier's answer. Even my father longs to go, leaving us brats behind. Oh, why wasn't I a man? Both my brothers are destined for the Church. As for me, I would have made a famous little soldier!"

She strode about, adopting a military posture, looking as bold as Joan of Arc. Harry watched her with a tender, protective admiration. "I think," he said softly, "I would hardly like to see a musket on that little shoulder, nor a wound on that pretty face, Hetty."

"Wounds! Who fears wounds?" she cried. "If I could carry one, I would use it. Men think we are only for puddings and samplers. Why wasn't I a man? George read Tasso to us yesterday. Look, here is the book, marked where we left off."

"With the mark?" Harry asked dutifully.

"Yes! About a woman disappointed because her brother does not go to war. She says: 'Alas! why did not Heaven these members frail / With lively force and vigour strengthen, so / That I this silken gown...'"

"Silken gown?" Harry queried.

"Well, it is Calimanco, but the book says 'silken gown': '...this silken gown and slender veil / Might for a breastplate and a helm forgo; / Then should not heat, nor cold, nor rain, nor hail, / Nor storms that fall, nor blust'ring winds that blow, / Withhold me; but I would, both day and night, / In pitched field or private combat, fight—'"

"Fight? Yes, that I would! Why are both my brothers to be parsons? One of my papa's children ought to be a soldier!"

Harry laughed, a gentle, kind sound. He looked at her, realizing he would not wish to strike such a tender little warrior.

"Why," he said, extending a finger, "here is a digit nearly as large as your arm. How would you stand before a great, strong man? I should like to see a man try to injure you. Just to see him! You little, delicate, tender creature! Do you suppose any scoundrel would dare be unkind to you?"

Excited by this fantasy of protection, Harry began to pace the room himself, chafing at the mere possibility of a rogue Frenchman daring to insult Miss Hester Lambert.

The ink on the page was dry, but the silence in the room was heavy. George Warrington sat at his desk, the quill hovering over a sheet of paper that smelled of fresh linen and old ambition. Outside, London hummed with a noise that was mostly just people trying to be important. Inside, the only thing that mattered was the weight of the name Warrington, a stone dropped into a deep well, echoing with a history he was barely keeping up with.

He had been told, in letters that arrived with the smell of rain and parchment, that he must not forget himself. He was not a drudge in a gown; he was a gentleman with an ancient lineage. So he studied, but he studied like a man playing a game he intended to win, not a servant learning a trade. He treated the law with the polite distance one reserves for a strict aunt. He respected Themis, but he loved the ghosts of Chaucer far more. The black-letter script of the old poets felt warmer than the cold, jagged edges of Hale and Coke.

Those were strange times, when the dead spoke louder than the living. Men in courts and parliaments argued their points by citing Virgil or Lucan, asking what the ancients would have done. Now, the gods were silent. The worship of Jupiter was as outdated as the fashion of the previous century. Economists and calculators ruled the day, filling rooms with numbers that meant nothing to the soul. Tooke's Pantheon was empty, a monument to a faith that had evaporated. Occasionally, a politician like Stanley or Gladstone would burn a small amount of incense to the old gods, a gesture of nostalgia, but no one cared anymore. Who needed the Muses? Who asked about the length of Thalia's 'i'? Or whether Melpomene's name stretched across three or four syllables?

George knew the answer because he had stolen the figure of Tragedy to adorn the initial 'G' of this very chapter. He remembered the long afternoons in his youth, the idleness of his imprisonment on the banks of the Monongahela, where he had paid court to Melpomene. The result was a tragedy, a piece so ambitious it had been omitted from Bell's Theatre, though it held no greater sins than the plays that filled the stages. Young men always fell in love with the Tragic Muse first, just as they fell for women older and wiser than themselves. Ambition was a fever in the green years; later, one settled for translating an ode of Horace or turning a song of Waller into neat, manageable verses. But then, there was only the highest prize, the epic, the tragedy.

So George went to London. He walked through the theaters, listening to the critics in the coffee-houses, sitting between the acts at the Bedford, supping with the wits at the Cecil. He became known, not for his wealth—which was real, but secondary—but for his wit and his modesty. The actors drank punch with him; the critics ate supper at his table. To hob-nob with Bobadil or Henry the Fifth, to share a pinch of snuff with Aristarchus, to hand Juliet into her coach—it was the dream of every poet. George lived in that dream. He gave entertainments to the players, and rumors spread that distinguished geniuses had borrowed money from him, a sign of a patronage that flowed both ways.

As he polished his masterpiece, adding new beauties to the skeleton of his plot, he sought the approval of friends. Mr. Spencer, a new acquaintance from the Temple, hosted a breakfast in Fig Tree Court. George read aloud. The gentlemen listened, nodding. "Uncommon merit," they said. Even Mr. Johnson, the learned critic, offered praise. "It shows talent," he admitted. "Though it wars against the unities." George smiled. Other authors had broken the rules; he could too.

His tragedy was full of blood and iron. The hero was Carpzow, a companion of Frundsberg, a man carved from the wars of Pavia and the campaigns of the Constable Bourbon. The first act opened before a convent on the Rhine, besieged by Lutherans. These were godless men, pulling beards and tearing veils. Inside the walls, a dozen nuns trembled. Outside, soldiers gambled on the grass. Then the bell rang. Noon. No succor had come. The gates swung open.

Enter Colonel Carpezan. Clad in complete steel, hammer over shoulder, flags unfurled, trumpets blaring. He was the terror of the Danube, the scourge of the Papists. "No rudeness," he commanded his men. "The wine is yours. The cellar is good. The church plate shall be melted. Any of the garrison wishing to serve under Gaspar Carpezan are welcome. But no insult to the religious ladies! I have promised them safe conduct. He who touches a finger on them hangs!"

Mr. Spencer explained the scene to his guests. "An agreeable acquaintance," Mr. Johnson remarked, wagging his head over his tea. "Novel and stirring."

Inside the convent, the Abbess—a lady of violent prejudices and noble birth—faced the conqueror. She knew his reputation. His hammer had shattered sacred images; his greed had melted pounds of silver. She told him her mind, sharp and clear. Carpezan, though a conqueror, was momentarily shaken by the force of her words. But an old soldier is not easily beaten by a woman.

"How many ladies are there?" he asked.

"Twenty-three," the Abbess replied, correcting herself from twenty-four.

"Why this hesitation?" asked Captain Ulric, one of Carpezan's gayest officers.

Carpezan pulled a letter from his pocket. "I require the body of the noble lady Sybilla of Hoya. Her brother was my favorite captain, slain by my side in the Milanese. By his death, she inherits his lands. It is said a greedy uncle locked her here. She shall decide her fate: remain a sister of Saint Mary's, or return to home and liberty."

The Abbess bristled. "There is no Lady Sybil here. Every inmate is under your protection, sworn to freedom. Sister Agnes was a professed nun; her wealth reverts to the Order."

"Give me the body of Lady Sybil of Hoya!" Carpezan roared, his face twisting in wrath. "Or I signal my Reiters, and I burn this convent to the ground."

Captain Ulric grinned. "If I lead the storm, I won't choose your Lady Abbess. I'd pick a plump, smiling, red-lipped maid." As he looked under the veils of the attendant nuns, the Abbess cried out, "Silence, fellow! The lady you seek is dead. Three days ago, Sister Agnes died."

The Chaplain confirmed it, ghastly and pale.

Too much. In the pocket of his coat of mail, Carpezan found a letter from Sister Agnes herself. She wrote that she was going to be buried, but in an oubliette, where she would either starve or live on water and bread. Seizing the Abbess by the arm, Carpezan grabbed the Chaplain by the throat. He blew a blast on his horn. The Lanzknechts rushed in. Crash, bang! The walls shook. Flames licked the rafters. Screams rose into the smoke.

Bringing Sybilla out, fainting on his shoulder, Carpezan revealed the truth. A little sister nun—the one with the red lips—had shown them the way to the dungeon.

"We end the first act with the burning," Mr. Warrington declared to his circle. "Roars of triumph, outcries of nuns. They will need to change their costumes immediately; they will be court ladies in the next act."

They cast the roles. Mrs. Pritchard as the Abbess, Miss Pritchard as Sybilla, Miss Gates as the nun. Mr. Garrick, though scarce tall enough, would be Carpezan when excited. Mr. Johnson agreed that Woodward would make a fine Ulric. Already, in their minds, the play was on the boards.

In Act II, Carpezan married Sybilla. Enriched by war, ennobled by the Emperor, he lived in splendor on the Danube. But happiness was elusive. Perhaps the crimes of his wild life gnawed at him. Perhaps his rough manners clashed with his imperious, high-born bride. She taunted him with his low birth, his vulgar companions. There were moments when he wished he had never rescued the lovely, quarrelsome vixen from the oubliette. The act was quiet, passing in quarrels until horns blew, announcing the arrival of the young King of Bohemia and Hungary.

Act III took place in Prague. The King offered preferment: Baron to Count, Colonel to General-in-Cheif. His wife was the brightest jewel of the court. And as for Carpzoff...

"Oh, stay—I have it," Mr. Johnson interrupted, his eyes lighting up. "'Tis in 'Meteranus,' in the Theatrum Universum. I read it in Oxford as a boy. Carpezanus or Carpzoff..."

The silence in the kitchen was heavier than the grief Gumbo allowed himself to wear. To the outside observer, the negro's reaction to Harry's enlistment was theatrical—a performance of tears and howls designed to convince anyone listening that his heart had physically shattered at the prospect of separation. Yet, beneath the melodrama lay a desperate, singular need: proximity to female attention. Gumbo retreated to the quarters of the Lambert household, seeking solace in the presence of the maid-servants. There, amidst the soft curves of their bosoms, he found a sanctuary where his dark complexion ceased to be a mark of difference and became merely a feature, as unremarkable as Othello's to Desdemona. Europe, it seemed, possessed a peculiar capacity for squeamishness regarding Africa, a bias that vanished instantly once the context shifted to romance. Even the Chevalier de St. Georges, a figure of aristocratic renown, had proven himself a favorite of the fair sex, suggesting that the barrier was not skin, but circumstance.

In this humbler theater, Gumbo flourished. The servants wept openly in his company, elevating him from a mere attendant to a brotherhood of shared sorrow. Hetty, usually a creature of sharp wit and pert laughter, found herself transformed. Witnessing Gumbo roar over the injustice of his master's departure—his refusal to take the boy along despite promises of saving Harry's life at any cost, even if it meant being cut into twenty thousand pieces—triggered a shift in Hetty's demeanor. She laughed, but it was a brittle sound, quickly swallowed by a newfound humility. She moved through the house like a ghost of her former self, meek and silent. To her mother, she was submissive; to John and Betty, grateful; to Polly, enduring the pain of her hair being combed; to Charley, accepting the crushing of her toes and the disarray of her workbox without complaint. Even in Papa's presence, the girl who once wagged her tongue with impudence now offered only a curtsey and a whispered, "Yes, if you please, dear papa!"

The contrast was stark. What sort of meal was a leg of mutton compared to the rations of a volunteer facing cannonballs? What purpose did church serve when the prayer was for survival? Hetty knelt, her knees knocking together, hiding her face as the parson read the commandment against murder, convinced he was staring directly at her sin. Her mind raced through the geography of danger, tracking travelers by land and sea, scanning newspapers for whispers of the Expedition. Every glance at her father returning from the Ordnance Office was a forensic examination of his face, searching for the signs of a general's promotion or the tragedy of a prisoner's wound. She imagined the pensioner in Chelsea Garden, whose legs had been severed by a single shot, and resolved that she would remain on her knees forever unless Harry walked through that door intact. "Haven't you heard of people, Theo," she asked, her voice trembling, "whose hair turns grey in a single night?" She checked the mirror, searching for the phantom silver strands, terrified that the war had already aged her soul.

"Yet you were not so nervous when Papa was away in Minorca," Theo observed gently.

"Ah, Theo," Hetty replied, her cheeks flushing with a mixture of shame and relief. "One sees clearly enough that George is not with the army, but safe at home."

George blushed, looking pensive. If Harry had been in the ranks, the dynamic of the family would have shifted irrevocably; another pair of boots would have been added to the ledger of loss. But for now, the narrative held a temporary peace. Harry Warrington was, for the moment, as secure as any officer stationed at Regent's Park Barracks.

His first engagement, however, offered no such security. It was a campaign devoid of glory, a series of maneuvers that felt less like war and more like a fable of a lion chasing a goose. The British Lion, emboldened by the absence of a worthy adversary, found itself confronting nothing but empty forests and retreating shadows. Imagine a beast charging into the wild, only to discover six tigers emerging from the brush. The rational choice, the only choice for a creature of instinct, is to tuck the tail between the legs and retreat to the den. To fight six tigers would be to admit oneself an ass.

Harry's experience mirrored this absurdity. On June 1st, he embarked with thirteen thousand men from the Isle of Wight. By dawn on the fifth, the fleet anchored in the Bay of Cancale, Brittany. For hours, the volunteers idled, peering at the French coast, watching cattle graze and dragoons trot away into the distance. A small fort, displaying a reckless audacity, fired upon the cutter carrying Marlborough and the Commodore. By two o'clock, the fleet was settled, and the grenadiers prepared to disembark in flat-bottomed boats. Lord George Sackville and General Dury commanded the landing party, including the gentlemen volunteers.

There was no heroism to be written here. The French, anticipating a trap or simply unwilling to engage, melted away. The frigates silenced the lone fort that had dared to interrupt the reconnaissance, and the army marched forward to capture the garrison. The entire force was taken prisoner, save for one old gentleman who, firing his two remaining cannons, declared that if every Frenchman had acted with such gallantry, the British would never have landed. He was shot in the leg, a casualty of his own stubborn pride.

The volunteers were mocked by their comrades for their eagerness to collect scalps, but none fell to Harry's tomahawk. The only death recorded was that of a French gentleman riding with his servant, foolishly refusing quarter from Lord Downe. Both men and their horses were dispatched instantly.

The following day, the force advanced from Cancale to St. Malo. The villages were stripped bare, the roads narrowed to single file by the sheer volume of men, creating a procession vulnerable to ambush from behind the tall hedges. Though no enemy awaited them, the tension of potential slaughter hung in the air. At nightfall, the army reached St. Malo, greeted by a barrage of artillery that did little damage in the dark. Seizing the opportunity, the British ignited the harbor, setting ablaze ships, wooden structures, and magazines of pitch and tar. The resulting inferno consumed the night.

Yet, the French, confident in their unseen strength within the town, did not emerge to stop the destruction. Marlborough and Sackville, satisfied with the display of power, decided not to besiege the city. Instead, they marched back to Cancale and boarded their ships. The expedition concluded not with a triumph, but with a return to Portsmouth exactly four weeks after departure.

George Warrington, receiving the news, burst into the Lambert dining room with exaggerated enthusiasm. "Glorious news, ladies!" he cried. "Our champion returns. He has faced dragons—upon my word, he says so."

"Dragons?" Hetty asked, her anxiety spiking.

"But not killed any," George clarified, reading the letter aloud. "'Dearest Brother—I think you will be glad to hear that I am returned, without any commission as yet; without any wounds or glory; but, at any rate, alive and hearty.' He describes the ship as crowded as the Black Hole of Calcutta, the weather rough, and the food inedible. 'My gentlemen's stomachs are dainty,' he writes, complaining of plain sailor's rum and biscuit after the delicacies of Braund and White. 'But I, who have been at sea before, took my rations and can of flip very contentedly... showing that a Virginia gentleman is as good as the best of 'em.'"

He continued, recounting the journey to Cancale Bay, the sighting of "a few dragons on a hill" who rode away without engaging, the sleeping under canvas, and the burning of privateers at St. Malo. "Better luck next time," Harry concluded. "This hasn't been very much nor particularly glorious: but I have liked it for my part. I have smelt powder, besides a good deal of rosin and pitch we burned. I've seen the enemy; have slept under canvas, and been dreadfully crowded and sick at sea. I like it."

The mention of dragons sent Hetty into fresh distress, imagining the seven champions and the fate of Andromeda. Papa dismissed the fear, attributing the creatures to the imagination of the Seven Champions, noting that seeing St. George's flag must have caused them to slink off. The little boy from Chartreux solemnly confirmed the lore, recalling the monster guarding the apple tree.

Despite the lack of actual combat, the atmosphere in the Lambert household shifted. Harry's return, though marked by boredom rather than bloodshed, served to quell Hetty's qualms. His announcement that he was unhurt and alive provided a temporary respite. Later, during an official visit to the troops at Portsmouth, George accompanied his father and found Harry transformed. The listless, moping youth of London coffee houses was gone, replaced by a figure brimming with energy and health. The excitement of the campaign had forged a new vigor in him. He was a ready pupil in the open-air school of war, eager to learn his new duties.

George watched his brother pace the streets of the warlike city, arm-in-arm, marveling at the scale of the army compared to the disastrous force he had witnessed two years prior. The nation, too, was intoxicated by the narrative of victory. The humiliation of the proud Gaul was proclaimed complete, even if the reality was a skirmish

The noise of London was a mechanical thing, a grinding of wheels and shouting that sought to prove something to itself. They spoke of St. Casimir, a rear-guard action where five hundred men were cut down by ten thousand on a beach. They fired cannons at the Invalides until the powder ran dry, celebrating a disaster as if it were a harvest festival. It was a vulgar arithmetic of glory, adding up losses to make a sum called "victory."

Harry sat in the room, listening to the echo of his own absence. He had returned. The sea air had stained his cheeks brown, a permanent mark of survival. The ball that had whistled past his side-curl had missed, sparing the flesh beneath. He had shown the lapels of his coat to the enemy, yes, but only for the briefest instant, withdrawing into the shadow of the retreat. Lapels were reverses, he reminded himself; coats had them, and men wore them with a certain stoic indifference.

"We all had to run," Harry said, his voice flat, devoid of the drama the room demanded. "When the line broke, luck belonged to those who reached the boats first."

He recounted the slaughter. The French horse and foot had poured down onto the sand, mingling with the fleeing English, bayonets finding soft targets on the ground. Armytage had fallen ahead of him. Harry had picked up the dying man and staggered through the surf, carrying weight that threatened to crush his legs. The sailors in the boat had not flinched; the shots whistled around their ears, snapping oars and shredding flags, yet the officer commanded with the calm of a man drinking punch in Portsmouth.

"Sir John never made the ship," Harry finished. "The service lost a fine soldier. Miss Howe lost a true gentleman."

Hetty watched him, her eyes fixed on the gap between his words and the silence that followed. She thought of the King, of the command that had sent Armytage to his death. "Why did the King encourage him?" she asked, her voice trembling with a sentimentality that felt like a wound. "A gentleman cannot refuse such a quarter. Now he is dead!"

Her father nodded, a slow, rhythmic motion. "His Majesty will be in a deep state of grief."

"Now you are laughing!" Hetty cried out, the accusation sharp. "Do you mean the King feels nothing when a gentleman dies almost at his feet?"

"If I thought that, I would be for the Pretender," Harry replied, the words automatic, a shield raised against the storm of her emotion.

"The sauce-box would make a pretty little head for Temple Bar," the General murmured, watching Hetty's face. He saw the turmoil there—the remorse, the consternation, the gratitude that the danger was finally over. "No, my dear," he said gently. "Were kings to weep for every soldier, what a life you would make for them! I think better of his Majesty than to suppose him so weak. If Miss Hester Lambert got her Pretender, I doubt she would be any the happier. That family was never famous for too much feeling."

"But if the King sent Harry... actually to the war..." Hetty pressed.

"If Harry had fallen, the court would have gone into mourning," the General noted, observing the shift in the room's atmosphere. "As it is, gentlemen and ladies wore colored clothes yesterday."

"Why should we not make bonfires for a defeat and put on sackcloth after a victory?" George asked, trying to lighten the mood.

"I protest I don't want to thank Heaven for helping us burn the ships at Cherbourg," Harry said, his tone grave.

"Yes, you do, George," Hetty interjected, though she knew she had no right to speak. "Not that I have a right, and you aren't ever so clever. But when your country wins, you're glad—I know I am. When I run away before Frenchmen, I'm ashamed. I can't help it, though I did it."

She paused, looking at Harry. "'Tis a bad business," she continued, her voice dropping. "But 'tis lucky 'twas no worse."

"The story about the French is that their Governor, the Duke of Aiguillon, was rather what you call a moistened chicken," Harry said, turning his attention back to the mechanics of the war. "Our retreat might have been cut off entirely, only we were in a mighty hurry to move. The local militia behaved famously. There were many gentlemen volunteers with them, showing as they ought to do, in the front. They say the Chevalier of Tour d'Auvergne engaged in spite of the Duke's orders."

Officers had brought lists of prisoners and wounded to General Bligh and Lord Howe. Howe was a lord now, elevated by the news of his brother's death. He was a brave fellow, whether lord or commoner.

"And his sister, who was to have married poor Sir John Armytage..." Hetty sighed, the image of the future collapsing into the present. "Think what her state must be!"

"And his mother!" Mrs. Lambert cried.

Mrs. Lambert read the address in the papers aloud, her voice rising. "'Lord Howe being now absent upon the publick service, and Lieutenant-Colonel Howe with his regiment at Louisbourg, it rests upon me to beg the favour of your votes and interests that Lieutenant-Colonel Howe may supply the place of his late brother as your representative in Parliament.'"

"A laconic woman," George observed.

"How can sons help being brave who have been nursed by such a mother as that?" the General asked.

The two young men looked at each other.

"If one of us were to fall in defense of his country, we have a mother in Sparta who would think and write so too," George said.

"If Sparta is anywhere Virginia way, I reckon we have," Harry remarked.

"And to think that we should both of us have met the enemy, and both of us been whipped by him, brother!" he added, his penitence evident.

Hetty looked at him, and in her mind, she saw only the other day: Harry tottering through the water, his comrade bleeding on his shoulder, the enemy in pursuit, the shot flying round. It was she who had driven him into that danger. Her words had provoked him. Yet, now that he was returned, he never rebuked her. Except when asked, he scarcely spoke of his adventures at all. He was very grave and courteous with Hetty; with the rest of the family, especially frank and tender. But those taunts of hers had wounded him.

"Little hand!" his looks and demeanor seemed to say. "Thou shouldst not have been lifted against me! It is ill to scorn anyone, much more one who has been so devoted to you and all yours. I may not be over quick of wit, but in as far as the heart goes, I am the equal of the best, and the best of my heart your family has had."

This magnanimous endurance served him to regain in Miss Hetty's esteem the place he had lost during the previous months of inglorious idleness. The respect she paid to the brave transformed her. She was no longer pert in her answers, or sarcastic in her observations regarding his conduct. In a word, she was a humiliated, an altered, an improved Miss Hetty. And all the world seemed to change towards Harry, as he changed towards the world.

He was no longer sulky and indolent; he no more desponded about himself or defied his neighbors. The colonel of his regiment reported his behavior as exemplary and recommended him for one of the commissions vacated by the casualties. Unlucky as the expedition's termination was, it was fortunate to him.

His brother-volunteers, returning to St. James's Street, reported highly of his behavior. These volunteers and their actions became the theme of everyone's praise. Had he been a general commanding, slain in the moment of victory, Sir John Armytage could scarce have had more sympathy than that which the nation showed him. The papers teemed with letters about him; men of wit and sensibility vied with each other in composing epitaphs in his honor. The fate of his affianced bride was bewailed. She was, as we have said, the sister of the brave Commodore who had just returned from this unfortunate expedition and succeeded to the title of his elder brother, an officer as gallant as himself, who had just fallen in America.

My Lord Howe was heard to speak in special praise of Mr. Warrington, and so he had a handsome share of the fashion and favor which the town now bestowed on the volunteers. Doubtless there were thousands of men employed who were as good as they, but the English ever love their gentlemen, and love that they should distinguish themselves; and these volunteers were voted Paladins and heroes by common accord. As our young noblemen will, they accepted their popularity very affably. White's and Almack's illuminated when they returned, and St. James's embraced its young knights. Harry was restored to full favor amongst them. Their hands were held out eagerly to him again.

Even his relations congratulated him. There came a letter from Castlewood, whither Aunt Bernstein had by this time betaken herself, containing praises of his valor and a pretty little bank-bill as a token of his affectionate aunt's approval. This was under my Lord Castlewood's frank, who sent his regards to both his kinsmen and an offer of the hospitality of his country-house if they were minded to come to him.

Besides this, there came to him a private letter through the post

The glass tank in Regent's Park held a suspended silence, a curated ecosystem where finned and feathered creatures traded their freedom for bread and observation. Yet, amidst the swirling currents and the deliberate grace of the goldfish, my attention drifted, as it often does when the mind wanders from the immediate to the distant. I watched a flatfish, a flounder, execute a series of impossible arcs through the water. Its movements were fluid, a study in hydrodynamic elegance, displaying a white chest and a glistening black back with a rhythm that suggested music rather than biology. It was a creature of profound beauty, until gravity reclaimed it.

Remove the flounder from the water, and the illusion shatters instantly. The beauty vanishes, replaced by a grotesque flapping of fins against the gravel, a desperate gasping that signals the rapid decay of life. Out of its element, the creature becomes ridiculous, a caricature of itself. This is the condition of Harry Warrington for years. He had been adrift, floundering in a sea that offered him no buoyancy. But the moment he stepped onto solid ground—the moment the epaulet touched his shoulder—he transformed. The water receded, and the true form emerged. He possessed an innate aptitude for the military machine, mastering its details with a hunger that bordered on obsession. To witness him marching, dining in the mess, or standing rigid on the parade ground would have been a spectacle of vitality. Since I lack the descriptive power of my friend Lorrequer to paint the full canvas of his campaign life, the reader must supply the colors: imagine Harry in his scarlet coat and yellow facings, radiating a joy derived solely from the discipline of war, eager to learn and perform every duty of his new trade.

While Harry found his purpose in the chaos of battle, George discovered his in the quiet architecture of sentiment. He viewed his brother's ascent with a pride that was almost painful, a recognition of the superior qualities he admired in Harry. In a letter to his mother in Virginia, George recounted the praises Harry had earned, withholding the specifics because he knew Harry's modesty would never allow him to boast. George detailed how Harry had secured his first commission, and then he turned the page of his own life. He spoke of the Lamerts, the only true friends the brothers had found in England, contrasting their generosity with the cold indifference of their biological relatives. George confessed a deep attachment to the elder Miss Lambert, a feeling he believed constituted the axis upon which his future happiness turned. He had not yet made formal proposals, but the sentiment was clear, the reception favorable. He painted Miss Lambert as a paragon of virtue, a model daughter whose approval he trusted implicitly. He assured his mother that her son's destiny hung in the balance, dependent entirely on winning her hand.

Why withhold this letter? Some might ask, seeking the raw data of youthful infatuation. We refuse. It is a cruelty to dissect the incoherent vows of a young man, to catalogue the sighs exchanged at twilight, to listen with clinical detachment to the beating of a heart that knows only devotion. Love is a sacred geometry; to map it precisely is to destroy its shape. We prefer to salute the signal whenever it appears—a glance between lovers, a mother's watchful eye, the simple presence of an old couple holding hands. To expose these moments to the public gaze is sacrilege. Let the reader trust our word: the letters exchanged between George and Miss Theo were modest, pure, and steeped in a tenderness that demands privacy.

George's pursuit was a gentle dance compared to Harry's martial fervor. He penned sonnets to the curve of a brow, paced the shadows beneath her window watching a single lamp glow, and cultivated pretexts to send notes that demanded no answer yet received one anyway. He plucked verses from favorite poets and flowers from Covent Garden markets, arranging them for her adornment. He attended services at St. James's, his mind wandering so completely he heard none of the sermon, consumed instead by the memory of her smile. He was affectionate to her entire circle: the siblings at school, the brother at college, the witty Miss Hetty, and even his mother, who, according to Martin Lambert, had fallen in love with him anew. For if fathers grow stern at the prospect of a son-in-law, mothers often become sentimental, reliving their own youth through the eyes of their children's affections.

Meanwhile, Gumbo and Sady kept a constant circuit between Southampton Row and Dean Street, orchestrating a calendar of diversions. Summer brought endless invitations to Ranelagh, Hampstead, Vauxhall, and Marylebone Gardens. George desired a specific tragedy transcribed in Miss Theo's exquisite Italian hand, and the exchange of manuscripts became a ritual of gratitude and admiration. These packets, marked neatly in Warrington's script, contained whispers meant for ears alone. To publish them would be to violate a vow. We assure the reader that the correspondence reflects the highest standards of respect and purity, a testament to a love that flourished in the dark.

George had decided he had found the woman of women. She may not possess the striking features of cousins Flora, Coelia, or Ardelia, nor the intellectual brilliance of a dozen rivals. Her face simply pleased him more than any other; her voice was the most agreeable sound in the world. In her presence, his own intellect sharpened, his words gained eloquence. He felt generous, noble, and witty, elevated by her company. Conversely, he believed Miss Theo endowed him with every virtue imaginable: wit, wisdom, beauty, valor, and goodness. It was a mutual projection, a perfect mirror reflecting the idealized version of oneself.

When George's letter arrived at Castlewood, the atmosphere around Madame Esmond's fireside shifted. The news of his engagement was received with relief; marriage was seen as a stabilizing force, a shield against the dangers of the world. Madame Esmond accepted the union with a well-bred Christian woman without hesitation.

"They will certainly be trying to capture him," observed Mountain, his tone suggesting a calculation of assets. "They assume his wealth matches his estate. He hasn't mentioned whether the young lady possesses funds. I suspect she does not."

"People would try to catch Mr. Esmond Warrington for their daughters here," Madame Esmond replied, her gaze sharp as she looked at her dependent. "Even if they are no richer than Miss Lambert."

"Perhaps you mean me?" Mountain interjected, feigning offense. "My Fanny is poor, as you say, and it is kind of you to remind me!"

"I stated that people would attempt to secure him. If the cap fits you, so be it," Madame Esmond retorted, invoking a phrase from her father.

"You imply I am scheming to keep George for Fanny? On my word, I thank you! After years of cohabitation, you hold a surprisingly low opinion of us!"

"My dear Mountain, I know you better than to believe you would consider your daughter a suitable match for a gentleman of Mr. Esmond's rank," Madame said, maintaining her dignity.

"Fanny Parker was as good as Molly Benson at school, and Mr. Mountain's daughter is as good as Mr. Lambert's!" Mrs. Mountain cried out, her ambition surfacing despite her protests.

"Then you intended to marry her to my son! I shall inform Mr. Esmond Warrington of your disappointment," the mistress of Castlewood declared. We may infer that Mrs. Mountain harbored ambitions for her daughter, hence her anger at the prospect of George's marriage elsewhere.

Madame Esmond's response to her son acknowledged the fraternal bond George displayed. She noted it was fitting that he compensate Harry, whose mistaken belief regarding his inheritance had led to unnecessary expenditures. Regarding the cost of buying Harry's promotion, she agreed to share the burden, grateful that her son's gallantry had already secured his entry into the army. She contrasted this sacrifice with other extravagances she refused to condone, specifically the ransom paid to the French captain's family—a sum George's mother, sharing her son's stubborn spirit, had steadfastly refused to pay. She labeled his persistence as pride, though her biographer, writing from a century later, cannot claim to understand the nuances of such obstinacy. People, it seems, remain remarkably similar across generations, driven by the desire to impose their will upon the world.

After settling his own ransom, clearing his brother's debts, and funding half of Harry's promotion, George realized a significant portion of his private patrimony would vanish. Nevertheless, he made the sacrifice with a clear conscience. His mother's letters constantly reminded him of his grandfather's legacy and the necessity of maintaining family dignity. She commissioned purchases in England, sent trifling sums, yet spoke incessantly of the exalted rank of the Esmonds. She urged him to frequent the courts his ancestors had visited, to inhabit the highest circles of society, and to conduct himself in a manner worthy of his name. George, confident that his mother's resources would eventually replenish his coffers, obediently adopted this expensive lifestyle. The Esmond papers from this era record bills for genteel entertainments, tailoring accounts for court suits, liveries for his negro servants and chairmen, and receipts from horse dealers. Thus, the elder of our Virginians, like his brother, learned to spend lavishly, believing that the performance of status was as vital as the substance of it.

The air in the theater was thick with the scent of rosin, sweat, and the metallic tang of anticipation. It was a small, claustrophobic world where the only law was the rhythm of the curtain rising and falling. Outside, the city of London hummed with a restless, hungry energy, desperate for a hero to fill the vacuum left by failures in France. The Duke of Marlborough had proven himself a shadow of his ancestor; Lord George Sackville's genius had been extinguished at Minden. The nation craved a general who could turn the abstract desire for glory into concrete victory. Into this vacuum stepped James Wolfe, returning from America, a man whose name was already being whispered as destiny.

Wolfe had been promoted to Colonel, a second battalion forged in the fires of Cape Breton. Harry, the Virginian, approached his new commander with a deference that was genuine, stripped of the theatrical posturing common among peers. To Wolfe, Harry was not just a subordinate; he was a mirror reflecting the romantic simplicity that defined the era's best soldiers. Wolfe possessed a transparency that bordered on naivety. He spoke of battles as if recounting personal confessions, his heart laid bare on the table of conversation. While the polished circles of St. James's might have sneered at such candor, within their private orbit, Wolfe was revered. He was a boy grown large, intoxicated by music and poetry, a man who claimed on his final day that writing Gray's Elegy held more value than winning a battle.

This literary inclination drew him inevitably to George. Both men were lovers, both seekers of happiness, bound by a shared hunger for sentiment. Their conversations, lost to history, must have been rich with the texture of their souls. Warrington, observing this dynamic from afar, wrote of Wolfe's "glorious bravery," noting how the General hungered for laurels to offer his mistress. To Warrington, Wolfe was not merely a soldier fulfilling duty; he was a knight charging dragons, a figure of chivalric ardour that contrasted sharply with the stoic, Roman-like constancy of the Virginian.

While Wolfe and Lambert navigated the political currents of London, the quieter drama unfolded in the domestic sphere. Warrington's family remained rooted in the rural quiet of Southampton Row, finding solace in the gardens of Bedford. Yet, the pull of the city was irresistible. Warrington visited Castlewood briefly, stepping into rooms once occupied by Colonel Esmond, but his true allegiance lay elsewhere. He preferred the vibrant chaos of London, specifically the company of a young woman whose presence offered a pleasure the aristocratic circle of Lady Maria could not replicate.

Lady Maria, gracious and enchanted, listened to the tragedies read aloud by George and Parson Sampson. It was the Chaplain's enthusiasm, however, that propelled Warrington's play, *Carpezan*, onto the boards. Though Mr. Johnson had attempted to secure a venue at Drury Lane, the competition with Thomas Holcroft's *Douglas* forced a shift. The play found its home at Covent Garden under the management of Mr. Rich.

The night of the premiere was a convergence of fate and fortune. Warrington hosted an elegant gathering at the Bedford Head, a prelude to the journey to the theatre. As the crowd spilled into the house, the atmosphere crackled with nervous excitement. Theo, Warrington's daughter, sat in the box, her heart fluttering with a mixture of pride and dread. Harry, watching from the shadows, was pale, his complexion draining of color until he matched the chalk-white intensity of the lead actor, Barry.

Barry commanded the stage as Carpezan, delivering lines of terror and pathos that silenced the audience. The role of the King of Bohemia fell to a young Dubliner, Hagan, whose grace and elegance won immediate acclaim. There were moments of friction—the introduction of an elephant in the Janissary march, a whimsical touch that confused the historical gravity—but the core of the tragedy held firm. The execution of Sybilla was the pivot point. With her head severed, the tension broke. Friends in the box exhaled, a collective breath released after holding it for hours. Messengers raced through the coffee-house, announcing the triumph.

The applause was deafening. Barry declared the play worthy of repetition, crediting it to a young gentleman from Virginia. In the box, Theo's anxiety transformed into radiant sparks in her eyes. Harry, usually composed, erupted in cheers, his voice joining the roar of the crowd alongside Wolfe and Lambert. Even Mrs. Lambert, typically reserved, wept openly. Her daughter Hetty mocked the tears, claiming the villains deserved their fate, yet the girl herself was swept up in the delirium, sharing the joy with the children and the servants alike.

The celebration continued long past the closing of the curtains. Suppers were held at the Star and Garter and the Bedford Head, fueled by wine and the intoxicating belief in a masterpiece. Toasts were raised, and Lady Maria, in a moment of inspired gallantry, proposed "The King of Hungary." The actor playing the role, a man of fire and eloquence, responded with a flourish, vowing eternal service to her ladyship, regardless of his mortality. His gestures were excessive, bordering on the ridiculous, fueled by punch and the heat of the moment. Theo and her sisters, inexperienced in such displays, recoiled slightly, but Lady Maria absorbed the energy with practiced ease.

By midnight, the festivities wound down. Coaches waited to carry the ladies home. The men lingered, exhausted but exhilarated. The King of Hungary retired with a pounding headache, and the Poet, Warrington, found himself astonished the following morning by the arrival of half a dozen guests he had barely seen the night before, eager to repeat the experience.

The aftermath settled into a new normal. As Mrs. Lambert prepared to send her daughters home, the household buzzed with the echoes of the play. George remained close to Theo, discussing the nuances of the performance, the lighting, the timing. Mrs. Lambert, wary of the dangers lurking in the greenroom, urged caution. George smiled, revealing a secret talisman in his gaze—a look fixed on Theo's face that suggested she alone could ward off all peril.

"Why should he be afraid?" Theo asked, her innocence untouched by the cynicism of the world.

Her mother kissed her, dismissing the fear. Hetty, however, seized upon the opportunity to mock, suggesting the actress was old enough to be George's mother. The rebuke was sharp, maternal, invoking the fifth commandment and the sanctity of age. George, in a display of sentimental affection, declared his mother's age to be ideal.

Yet beneath the surface of this polite exchange ran a strange current. Passion had a way of twisting logic. Before his marriage, a man might find himself strangely attached to his future mother-in-law. Mrs. Lambert treated Theo with a tenderness that bordered on obsession, dressing her carefully, questioning her constantly about George's appearance, his health, his fatigue. She imagined nursing him with gruel, sending recipes for his recovery. When he was absent, she spoke of him incessantly to her daughter.

Theo, in turn, became a vessel for this attention. Whenever George entered the room, she was subtly displaced, told to attend to him while she retreated. She whispered instructions to servants and children outside the door, creating a network of surveillance around the young couple. When she reappeared, George maintained a respectful distance, except for one fleeting moment when he stooped to retrieve her dropped scissors. In that brief contact, a blush colored her cheeks. Was it shame? Or was it the natural blooming of youth? Mrs. Lambert, blind to the significance of the flush, sat down to her work, her heart full of happiness, unaware that the tragedy had ended, but the farce of courtship had only just begun.

The narrative arc of departure is usually reserved for the tragic, yet here, amidst the weight of empire and the fragility of life, it unfolds with a quiet, almost mechanical inevitability. Harry Warrington leaves. The text refuses to dwell on his internal landscape; instead, it maps the geography of absence. Thousands of men stand in the same position, suspended between the solid earth of home and the fluid uncertainty of the Atlantic. Mr. Wolfe carries the burden of a mother's kiss and a sweetheart's embrace, while Admiral Holmes, commanding a flotilla of iron-hulled ships—the Somerset, the Terrible, the Northumberland—must navigate the emotional turbulence of bidding farewell to Mrs. Holmes and her daughters. Even Admiral Saunders, sailing the following day, is not immune to the gravity of separation. The visual of the ship shrinking into the sunset is not a romantic trope but a mathematical reduction of presence until the vessel becomes a speck, then a thought, then a silence.

George accompanies his brother to Portsmouth, witnessing the final ritual: the clash of bells, the roar of cannon, the violent punctuation of departure. Once back in Dean Street, George retreats into the domestic sphere, a realm defined by routine and the absorption of trivialities. He observes the empty room where Harry slept, noting the half-burned papers in the grate—a residue of interrupted labor. This moment of stillness is quickly overwritten by the arrival of supper and the family unit, shifting the focus from the epic scale of naval warfare to the intimate theater of dinner conversation.
