The news of the fleet's destination circulates effortlessly; the strategic assessment is clear: Wolfe possesses the soul necessary to correct the blunders of past campaigns, despite his frail body.

Time moves forward with a rhythmic indifference. Days dissolve into weeks, marked by the turning of pages and the resumption of work. George, the dramatist, finds solace in the fickleness of the theatrical world. The success of *Carpezan* brings him a steady income from Dodsley and the theatre managers, allowing him to pivot to new compositions with philosophical detachment. He accepts the transience of fame, watching as other geniuses seize the stage. His correspondence reveals a mind sharp enough to dissect the political failures of Lord George Sackville and the arrogant constancy of the Duke, finding a perverse admiration in the losers of history. He critiques the pomposity of figures like Mr. P----, preferring the grounded manner of Barry, and engages in the mundane logistics of costume design for *Pocahontas*, debating whether the titular character should bear tattoos or the refined attire of Hampton Court.

Yet, beneath these observations of professional and political maneuvering lies a current of personal vulnerability. George writes to his brother of Cousin Maria, a figure who haunts the galleries of the playhouse, sparking a chain of protective instincts and suppressed confessions. He burns a letter, then whispers its contents, caught between the desire to protect and the compulsion to reveal. This leads to a reflection on their upbringing in Virginia, contrasting the dullness of their childhood with the moral clarity they retained compared to Harry, who was stripped by highwaymen. George projects a future of quiet domesticity—a library, a gentle companion, a small estate—against the backdrop of Harry's destined military glory. He fears this peace, fearing its elusiveness, yet feels no ambition for the sword of Mars.

Finally, the letter turns to the arrival of Miss Lydia, a young woman of seventeen who commands the room with an ease that belies her age. She treats the older generation as peers, discussing politics and fashion with a confidence that suggests she belongs in Mayfair rather than Monument Yard. George's description of her is a cascade of adoration, a torrent of poetic rapture that he ultimately decides to withhold from the reader, citing respect for privacy and memory. In doing so, he acknowledges the universal nature of youthful infatuation, inviting the reader to recall their own lost springs, while gently dismissing the current generation's need to linger on such follies. The narrative concludes not with a resolution, but with the natural cycle of seasons, leaving the characters adrift in the flow of time, their lives continuing regardless of the distance separating them.

The correspondence from George Warrington confirms a peculiar stability: despite the Lord's prior maneuvers involving Harry, the bond between the Earl and his cousin remained intact, governed by a rigid code of kinsmanship. George required nothing more than access, introduction, or a seat in the House of Lords to witness the debates; his cousin was perpetually available to facilitate these desires, provided the transaction did not impinge upon the Earl's own standing. This utility extended to securing a position for George himself, a prospect the Virginian embraced with fervor. His reluctance to return to Virginia was not merely sentimental. The idleness of a planter's life held no allure unless coupled with autonomy—a condition impossible in the colony where he ranked as the most neglected among his mother's subjects. He foresaw a domestic trajectory defined by wearisome routine, tedious subordination, and the inevitable friction of shared quarters with a young bride. In contrast, London offered congenial society and the faint, intoxicating possibility of distinction. Thus, the Earl's patronage was accepted, even if the Earl's own provision for his brother had historically been scant.

Lord Castlewood, however, displayed a grace toward George's engagement to Miss Lambert that belied his reputation. He attended the Lamberts, praising the union to the parents and the young lady alike, cultivating an atmosphere so agreeable that the family's charitable nature filtered out his notorious past. They constructed a narrative wherein his presence was a temporary reprieve from the storm, a harbor where a worldling sheds his coat of wickedness to breathe. Perhaps he was genuinely better in their company, shedding the cynicism of his trade until the moment he stepped back into the hall. The Lambert elders, observing this performance, seemed satisfied. When Castlewood spoke of his inability to compete with innocence, claiming he could not learn goodness at his age, he did so with a melancholy precision that resonated with the matrons. He posited that Fate held the reins, that some souls were predestined angels while others, like him, were condemned to vacillation. Yet, Mrs. Lambert, reading the prayers in his eyes, refused to accept this fatalism. She argued that there was always an hour left for hope, a sentiment that softened the hard edges of his confession. He acknowledged the truth of her kindness, bowing with a grace that suggested a man aware of his own fragility, knowing that tears were often a debauch of sentiment, yet unable to suppress the genuine feeling that arose in their proximity.

This dynamic evolved into a sustained intimacy. The Ladies of Castlewood frequented the Lamberts' drawing rooms, engaging in card assemblies and games that the aristocracy deemed acceptable precisely because bishops and peers participated. Mrs. Lambert, finding solace in the admiration her daughters garnered, became a regular guest at these routs. Meanwhile, the Lambert matriarch, ever the architect of alliances, turned her attention to her son, Jack. With Harry absent in the colonies, she envisioned a new arrangement: a wealthy, handsome wife for Jack from the Dutch family recommended by Madam Esmond. She pressed this upon the General, ignoring his dismissal of her as a goose, arguing that breaking Hetty's heart was preferable to letting her son remain unmarried. The General, however, saw only the absurdity of sacrificing his daughter's happiness for a political convenience.

Parallel to these domestic machinations was the arrival of the Dutch family, specifically Miss Lydia. Initially confined to the lower circles of London, she ventured outward with her grandfather, seeking to expand her sphere. She encountered Mr. Draper of the Temple, who viewed her as a prize, and Mr. Drabshaw, a Quaker nephew who regarded her arrival with alarm. George Warrington, wishing to honor his mother's neighbors, hosted an entertainment where Miss Lydia's presence commanded immediate attention. She possessed the foot of a fairy and the flashing eyes of a huntress, carrying a plaintive accent from home that, rather than marking her as foreign, added a layer of charm to her speech. Whether one favored the Cockney imitation, the northern burr, or the Irish warble, the consensus was that beauty dictated the value of the voice. After six months of instruction at the Kensington Academy, Miss Lydia returned to her grandfather's care, eager to navigate the narrow world of society. The Lambert family welcomed the benevolent old man, unaware that the intricate web of engagements and ambitions was tightening around them, with George and Theo already bound, rendering Madam Esmond's matchmaking efforts moot, and leaving Mrs. Lambert to devise her own schemes amidst the shifting tides of London life.

The silence in the library was not empty; it was a held breath, a suspension of judgment waiting for a verdict. I sat at the mahogany desk, the wood cool beneath my palms, tracing the grain of a history written by men who feared the Bastille more than they loved the truth. My task was to catalogue the dead, to arrange the lies of Louis XIV into a sequence that looked like order. It was a mechanical act, a rearrangement of shadows.

Then, a volume arrived. *Memoirs of M. d'Artagnan*. Printed in Amsterdam by Pierre Rouge, a name whispered in corners where honesty was punished. The title caught my eye, not with the fanfare of epic poetry, but with the quiet click of a key turning in a lock. I took it to my room.

Inside, the portraits of kings and cardinals stared back—statues of power, interchangeable and mute. They meant nothing to me. But in the margins, in the spaces between the grand narratives, lay a different story. A mundane waiting room. The antechamber of M. de Tréville. Here, amidst the dust of bureaucracy, d'Artagnan met three young men.

Athos. Porthos. Aramis.

Names ending in *-os* and *-is*, sounding like gods, yet belonging to men who had likely traded their real names for uniforms bought with borrowed money or sold dreams. They were not myths; they were functions of the state, erasing their individuality to serve a corps. I felt a sudden, sharp kinship with this erasure. To be a hero was to cease being a man.

My attention drifted, however, to a letter found tucked within the binding—a correspondence from Mr. George Warrington to his brother. It spoke of a woman, Lady Maria Esmond, and a marriage that defied the rigid architecture of society. A woman of noble blood, mature experience, recently in love, marrying a man of low origin, years her junior, behind the closed doors of a chapel in Mayfair.

How could anyone divine such a secret? How could respectable minds conceive that a woman of her station would forget herself? Yet, the letter argued, with a logic that felt dangerously close to madness, that nature operates on a cycle of consumption. Men change partners freely; women are rebuked for doing the same. Is there not a hunger that cannot be satisfied by one feast? The letter cited examples of women who clung to two men simultaneously, suggesting that marriage tables follow funeral feasts.

"Your natural fine appetite," the writer mused, "shows that tomorrow evening you will be in want of your dinner."

He painted a picture of Lady Ogresham, who had devoured Tom Kydd and discarded his bones, only to seize Ned Suckling next. He saw the pattern clearly: we are served *à la russe*, gobbled up one dish at a time, destined to disappear into the omnivorous maws of fate. He imagined Lady Maria losing her heart repeatedly, only for Harry Warrington to finally secure it, believing he owned the fee-simple of her affection forever.

"But this Maria," the letter continued, "was a woman of an ill-regulated mind."

Her current enslaver was Mr. Geoghegan, a young actor from George's tragedy. His voice, his bright eye, his noble bearing in gilt leather armor—he had captured her heart completely, just as Ariadne surrendered to Bacchus. The Irishman, touched by her partiality, accepted her despite her age and status. She retreated to his humble lodgings in Westminster, fleeing the fury of her relatives who had disowned her.

General Lambert, reading the account, found amusement in the chaos. In those homely times, a joke was sharpened by its breadth. He mocked the union, imagining the child named after a tragic character, the family forced to rent a chariot with pasteboard dragons from Mr. Rich's theatre. Little Hetty, unaware of the depths of her aunt's past follies, defended the match with innocent logic: "Why should he not love her because she is a little old?"

George Esmond Warrington, the narrator's cousin, confirmed the scene. He had visited the couple in their dirty Westminster quarters, finding them surrounded by poverty and dignity. Mr. Hagan supported his wife, his old mother, and a younger brother, all on a salary that barely covered bread and cheese. The theater community, particularly the macaronis, threatened riots; Mr. Rich feared for his reputation. The family had severed ties; Lady Castlewood wore mourning; Mr. Will vowed to tear the actor's ears.

Yet, amidst the scorn, there was a strange tenderness. George admitted he had developed a liking, even a respect, for the improvident man. He had taken money from his sister Theo to send to the starving couple. The General scoffed at George's generosity, noting that George had left his purse behind, but George smiled, attributing the sacrifice to Theo's influence.

"The town was excited," the letter noted, describing the scandal that rippled through Kensington and the courts. Whispers, titters, and sneers filled the air until a victory in Germany or a new opera singer from Italy diverted the public's gaze. Lady Maria was forgotten in her garret, eating the hard-earned meal of the actor's family, while the world moved on.

The final image lingered: George writing to his brother, describing the scene of the newlyweds. He found them in a loose undress, hair unkempt, playing cribbage with a neighbor from the second floor. Good Mrs. Hagan sat by the fire with a glass of punch and *The Whole Duty of Man*, a stark contrast to the frivolity of the game.

It was a life stripped of pomp, reduced to the essentials of survival and love, observed by a narrator who understood that the greatest dramas often played out not in the spotlight of history, but in the dim light of a cramped room, where the only audience was the fire and the silence.

The silence in the room was heavier than the shouting that had preceded it. George Warrington sat apart, the air thick with the residue of a quarrel he had barely fought. His cousin, Will, had withdrawn his visits to Bloomsbury, perhaps wisely, perhaps out of spite. In his absence, Mr. Will's attentions had grown assiduous, a relentless pressure that made George feel the urge to strike out, even though both parties were legally bound to peace. The law demanded restraint; George's hands demanded release.

Lydia, however, offered no resistance. Her manner had shifted with startling velocity. Six weeks prior, she had viewed George's attention to another with a jealousy that bordered on madness. Now, she greeted Theo with a warmth that felt engineered. "Miss Theo is such a quiet, gentle creature," Lydia declared, her voice soft, almost musical in its rhythm. "George is just the husband for her. How fortunate that horrible quarrel had been prevented!"

She spoke of the constables arriving just in time, saving George's life, framing the interruption as a mercy. "You won't stop and meet Mr. Esmond at dinner today?" she asked, her smile a closed loop of innocence. "He can't do you any harm; and I am sure you will do him none." These words, delivered by a small girl to a gentleman in the presence of strangers, created a wall around George. They were polite, they were kind, and they were utterly devoid of the intimacy he sought. They rendered him an outsider in his own home.

For several days, the incident remained buried in Dean Street. George avoided disturbing the household with the memory of his near-duel. But once the ladies learned of it, the atmosphere fractured.

"You were actually going to take a fellow-creature's life," Theo exclaimed, her face pale. "And you came to see us, and said not a word! Oh, George, it was shocking!"

"My dear, he had insulted me and my brother," George pleaded, the logic of defense clashing with the chaos of emotion. "Could I let him call us both cowards, and sit by and say, Thank you?"

The General sat by, his expression grave, a statue of stoicism amidst the turmoil. "You know you think, papa, it is a wicked and un-Christian practice," Theo continued, seeking validation. "And have often said you wished gentlemen would have the courage to refuse!"

"To refuse? Yes," Mr. Lambert murmured, his face gloomy. "It must require a prodigious strength of mind to refuse."

Jack Lambert nodded, matching his father's somber tone. "And I think if any man were to call me a coward, I should be apt to forget my orders."

"You see brother Jack is with me!" George cried, seizing the alliance. "I must not be against you, Mr. Warrington."

"Mr. Warrington!" George snapped, turning red. "Would you, a clergyman, have George break the Commandments, and commit murder, John?"

"I am a soldier's son, sister," Jack replied dryly, dismissing the moral weight. "Besides, Mr. Warrington has committed no murder at all. We must soon be hearing from Canada, father. The great question of the supremacy of the two races must be tried there ere long!"

He turned his back on George, and the younger man watched him with a mixture of wonder and confusion. Hetty, looking pale, was excused under a pretext. George rose and followed the retreating girls to the door, returning moments later with a sudden clarity.

"Great powers, gentlemen!" he said, stepping back into the center of the room. "I believe, on my honour, you are giving me the credit of shirking this affair with Mr. Esmond!"

The clergyman and his father exchanged a glance. "A man's nearest and dearest are always the first to insult him," George flashed out, the anger rising again.

"You mean to say, 'Not guilty?' God bless thee, my boy!" the General roared. "I told thee so, Jack." He rubbed his hand across his eyes, blushing, and wrung George's hand with all his might.

"Not guilty of what, in heaven's name?" Mr. Warrington demanded.

"Nay," the General said, "Mr. Jack, here, brought the story. Let him tell it. I believe 'tis a ------ lie, with all my heart." Uttering this wicked expression, the General walked out of the room.

The Reverend J. Lambert looked foolish. "And what is this--this d----d lie, sir, that somebody has been telling of me?" George asked, grinning at the young clergyman.

"To question the courage of any man is always an offence to him," Mr. Lambert said stiffly. "And I rejoice that yours has been belied."

"Who told the falsehood, sir, which you repeated?" Mr. Warrington bawled. "I insist on the man's name!"

"You forget you are bound over to keep the peace," Jack warned.

"Curse the peace, sir! We can go and fight in Holland. Tell me the man's name, I say!"

"Fair and softly, Mr. Warrington!" the young parson interjected. "My hearing is perfectly good. It was not a man who told me the story which, I confess, I imparted to my father."

"What?" George stopped. The truth occurred to him. "Was it that artful, wicked little vixen in Bloomsbury Square?"

"Vixen is not the word to apply to any young lady, George Warrington!" Lambert exclaimed. "Much less to the charming Miss Lydia. She artful--the most innocent of Heaven's creatures! She wicked--that angel! With unfeigned delight that the quarrel should be over--with devout gratitude to think that blood consanguineous should not be shed--she spoke in terms of the highest praise of you for declining this quarrel, and of the deepest sympathy with you for taking the painful but only method of averting it."

"What method?" George stamped his foot.

"Why, of laying an information, to be sure!" Jack said.

On this revelation, George burst forth into language too violent to repeat, highly uncomplimentary to Miss Lydia.

"Don't utter such words, sir!" the parson cried, his patience snapping. "Do not insult, in my hearing, the most charming, the most innocent of her sex! If she has been mistaken in her information regarding you, and doubted your willingness to commit what, after all, is a crime--for a crime homicide is, and of the most awful description--you, sir, have no right to blacken that angel's character with foul words: and, innocent yourself, should respect the most innocent as she is the most lovely of women! Oh, George, are you to be my brother?"

"I hope to have that honour," George answered, smiling. He began to perceive the other's drift. "What, then, what--though 'tis too much bliss to be hoped for by sinful man--what, if she should one day be your sister? Who could see her charms without being subjugated by them? I own that I am a slave. I own that those Latin Sapphics in the September number of the Gentleman's Magazine, beginning Lydicae quondam cecinit venustae (with an English version by my friend Hickson of Corpus), were mine. I have told my mother what hath passed between us, and Mrs. Lambert also thinks that the most lovely of her sex has deigned to look favourably on me. I have composed a letter--she another. She proposes to wait on Miss Lydia's grandpapa this very day, and to bring me the answer, which shall make me the happiest or the most wretched of men! It was in the unrestrained intercourse of family conversation that I chanced to impart to my father the sentiments which my dear girl had uttered. Perhaps I spoke slightingly of your courage, which I don't doubt--by Heaven, I don't doubt: it may be, she has erred, too, regarding you. It may be that the fiend jealousy has been gnawing at my bosom, and--horrible suspicion!--that I thought my sister's lover found too much favour with her I would have all my own. Ah, dear George, who knows his faults? I am as one distracted with passion. Confound it, sir! What right have you to laugh at me? I would have you to know that risu inepto..."

"What, have you two boys made it up?" the General cried, entering at that moment, in the midst of a roar of laughter from George.

"I was giving my opinion to Mr. Warrington upon laughter, and upon his laughter in particular," Jack Lambert said, in a fume.

"George is bound over to keep the peace, Jack! Thou canst not fight him for two years; and between now and then, let us trust you will have made up your quarrel. Here is dinner, boys! We will drink absent friends, and an end to the war, and no fighting out of the profession!"

George pleaded an engagement as a reason to run away early from dinner. Jack must have speedily followed him, for when the former, after transacting some brief business at his own lodgings, came to Mr. Van den Bosch's door in Bloomsbury Square, he found the young parson already in parley with a servant.

"His master and mistress had left town yesterday," the servant said.

The fire crackled, a low, rhythmic percussion against the silence of the room. I watched the flames climb, casting shadows that danced with a logic of their own, detached from the rigid order of the outside world. Across the hearth, seated in the chair that had held my wife for half a century, she observed the same spectacle. We are middle-aged now, a stage where repetition becomes a form of prayer; we repeat our jokes, our inquiries, our stories until the edges blur.

"Do we recall," I asked, the words hanging in the warm air, "how foolishly we wed?"

Miles, my son, paused in his contemplation of the gaps and rivers he had recently traversed with such perilous ease. He spoke of the terror that followed his triumphs, the astonishment at his own audacity. Yet, he remains the most eager sportsman in the two counties, his love for the hunt consuming everything else. He has broken his collarbone and suffered a hundred falls, to his mother's horror. And yet, so did I. I recall falling from my mare, Sultana, slipping on a stone while we dreamed along a park road at four miles per hour. For Miles, reckless sport is the delight of life; for me, marriage was the blessing. I never think of it without thanking Heaven. Do not mistake my devotion for an example. I do not advise young couples to marry on twopence a year; the vestry meetings would turn black against me. But my wife is a desperate matchmaker. When Hodge and Susan appear in my justice room discussing allowances, we urge them to spend their half-crown at home, add a contribution of our own, and summon the vicar.

Now, when I pose a question to my dear oracle, I know the answer before I speak. This certainty is why I consult her so often. I wear a particular expression, and my Diana reflects it instantly. If I say, "My dear, do you not think the moon was made of cream cheese tonight?" she replies, "Well, Papa, it did look very like cream cheese, indeed—there's nobody like you for droll similes." Or if I suggest, "My love, Mr. Pitt's speech was fine, but I doubt he equals his father," she answers, "Nobody was equal to my Lord Chatham." Then one of the girls cries out, "Why, I've often heard our Papa say Lord Chatham was a charlatan!" And Mama retorts, "How like she is to her Aunt Hetty!"

As for Miles, *Tros Tyriusve* means nothing to him. He reads only the sporting announcements in the Norwich paper. As long as the scent is good, the state of the country is irrelevant. I suspect the rascal has never read my poems, let alone my tragedies. I mentioned *Pocahontas* to him recently, and the dunce believed she was a river in Virginia. Regarding my Latin verses, how can he comprehend them when he cannot construe Corderius? This notebook lies openly on the little table at my corner of the fireside. Any passerby could lift my spectacles from their cover and read it. Yet Miles never has.

Inside the loose pages lie caricatures of Miles: jokes against him. He never knows, nor does he heed them. Only once, replacing a neat drawing of myself in China ink—a portrait of Miles asleep after dinner, a work my friend Bunbury would not disown—I found a crude sketch of myself going over my mare Sultana's head, titled "The Squire on Horseback, or Fish out of Water." The fellow roared with laughter, and all the girls tittered when I discovered the page. My wife confessed she had never been in greater fright than when I approached the book. I can bear a joke against myself; I have heard many, though strangely, for one who lived among the chief wits of the age, I never heard a truly good one in my life.

Never mind, Miles. Though thou art not a wit, I love thee none the worse. There has never been any love lost between two wits in a family. Thou hast no great beauty, yet thy mother thinks thee as handsome as Apollo, or his Royal Highness the Prince of Wales, born in the very same year. Indeed, she believes Coates's picture of the Prince resembles her eldest boy, keeping the print in her dressing-room to this day.

*[Note, in a female hand: "My son is not a spendthrift, nor a breaker of women's hearts, as some gentlemen are; but that he was exceeding like H.R.H. when they were both babies, is most certain, the Duchess of Aneaster having herself remarked him in St. James's Park, where Gumbo and my poor Molly used often to take him for an airing. Th. W."]*

In that same year, with vastly different prospects, my Lord Esmond, son of Lord Castlewood, appeared to adorn the world. My Lord C. and his humble servant had already fallen into a coolness at that time. Heaven knows, my honest Miles's godmother brought no gold pap-boats to his christening! Matters have mended since, *laus Deo*—indeed, *laus Deo*, for I suspect neither Miles nor his father would ever have achieved much by their own wits.

Castlewood House wears a quite different face now from the venerable visage it bore in my youth. Back then, it was covered with the wrinkles of time, the scars of old wars, the cracks and blemishes marked by years on its hoary features. I love best to remember it in its old shape, as I saw it when young Mr. George Warrington descended at the owner's invitation to attend his lordship's marriage with Miss Lydia Van den Bosch—"an American lady of noble family of Holland," as the county paper announced. Then the towers stood as Warrington's grandfather, the Colonel—the Marquis, as Madam Esmond would like to call her father—had seen them. The woods, thinned considerably, still stood; nay, some of the very same rooks may have cawed over them which the Colonel had witnessed sixty years prior. His picture hung in the hall, a frame he might have owned had he not preferred love and gratitude to wealth and worldly honor.

Mr. George Esmond Warrington—that is, *Egomet Ipse* writing this page—as he paced the long corridors, the smooth dew-spangled terraces, and the cool, darkling avenues, felt momentarily as if he were one of Mr. Walpole's cavaliers, complete with ruff, rapier, buff-coat, and gorget. He imagined an Old Pretender or a Jesuit emissary emerging from behind any tall tree-trunk or antique carved cupboard. I harbored strange, sad, pleasant, old-world fancies walking the place: imagining tragedies, intrigues, serenades, escaladoes, Oliver Cromwell's Roundheads battering the towers, or bluff Hal's Beefeaters pricking over the plain before the castle.

I was then courting a certain young lady. Your ladyship's eyes needed no spectacles then, and on the brow above them there was never a wrinkle or a silver hair. I remember writing a ream of romantic description, under my Lord Castlewood's franks, to the lady who never tired of reading my letters. She says I now send her only three lines when I am away in London or elsewhere. 'Tis that I may not fatigue your old eyes, my dear!

Mr. Warrington thought himself authorized to order a genteel new suit of clothes for my lord's marriage. With Mons. Gumbo in attendance, he made his appearance at Castlewood a few days before the ceremony. I may mention it had become expedient to send my faithful Sady home aboard a Virginia ship. A great inflammation attacking the throat and lungs, fatal in many cases that year of Wolfe's expedition, had seized and nearly killed my poor lad, for whom his native air was pronounced the best cure. We parted with abundant tears, and Gumbo shed as many when his master went to Quebec. But he possessed attractions in this country and none for the military life, so he remained attached to my service.

We found Castlewood House full of friends, relations, and visitors. Lady Fanny was there upon compulsion, a sulky bridesmaid. Some virgins of the neighborhood also attended the young Countess. A bishop's widow herself, the Baroness Beatrix, brought a holy brother-in-law of the bench from London to tie the holy knot of matrimony between Eugene Earl of Castlewood and Lydia Van den Bosch, spinster. For some time before and after the nuptials, the old house in Hampshire wore an appearance of gaiety to which it had long been unaccustomed. Country families came gladly to pay their compliments to the newly married couple. The lady's wealth was the subject of everyone's talk, and no doubt did not decrease in the telling. Those naughty stories rife in town, spread by her disappointed suitors, took some time to travel into Hampshire. When they reached the country, they found it disposed to treat Lord Castlewood's wife with civility, not inclined to be too curious about her behavior in town. Suppose she had jilted this man and laughed at the other? It was her money they were anxious about, and she was no more mercenary than they. The Hampshire folks were determined that it was a great benefit to the country to have Castlewood House once more open, with beer in the cellars, horses in the stables, and spits turning before the kitchen fires.

The new lady took her place with great dignity, certain she possessed uncommon accomplishments and

The air inside Castlewood held the weight of a settled grievance, a static charge that Will Esmond carried like a second coat. He had sworn vengeance upon the news of his brother's engagement to the colonial heiress, fleeing the house with the fervor of a man excommunicating his own blood. Yet, oaths are merely the currency of temporary exits; no one, not even the speaker, believes them to be permanent laws. Within a day or two, the prodigal returned. The fumes of the marriage feast—the smell of roast meat and clinking silver—proved too potent a lure for his stomach to resist. He came back to drink and eat at the Castlewood table, performing his revenge through the act of consumption. He raised a bumper to toast the young bride, whispering perdition beneath his breath, offering smothered maledictions as her father handed her over in the chapel.

He was not alone in his resentment. Mr. Warrington heard the abuse flow from every corner of the estate. The old gentleman's City friends, his legal adviser, the Dissenting clergyman who had welcomed them to England, and even poor Jack Lambert, the orthodox young divine, were bitter. Each claimed to have been enslaved, encouraged, and then jilted by the American girl. The lawyer, who possessed an exhaustive inventory of her properties, estates, and slaves, declared the entire account a fabrication—that New York and Virginia were myths, that Mr. Van den Bosch owned no land, and that the Guinea trade was a fiction invented by a wily old planter. The Dissenting pastor mourned his lost flock, calling the girl a monster disguised as a lamb. Jack Lambert wept over a lock of black hair, bedewing it with tears and speaking in language unfit for a cleric. And Will Esmond, indulging in his customary shrieks and curses even under his sister-in-law's nose, invoked Acheron to testify that no such artful devil existed as Miss Lydia. He labeled her an infernal Cerberus, calling down wrath upon his swindling brother who had filched his prize with fair words.

"Why," Mr. Warrington asked when Will expatiated on these grievances, "if the girl is such a she-devil, you are all the better for losing her. If she intends to poison her husband, how lucky you are not to be the victim! You should thank the gods for robbing you of such a fury; you cannot be better revenged on Castlewood than by allowing him her sole possession."

"All this was very well," Will replied, noting that his brother was still a scoundrel for cheating him out of the fortune he had risked his life to win.

George Warrington struggled to understand how his cousin had risked his existence on such a venture. Will explained with candor how the little Cerbera had orchestrated their meeting, how she had provoked George by claiming he was a coward for sneering at Will, and how Will had doubled down on his taunts until George ran away and nearly died of fright in Braddock's expedition. "Deuce take me," Will said, "I was never more surprised than when you stood so coolly in Tottenham Court Fields, for me and my second offered to wager you would never come!"

Mr. Warrington laughed, thanking Will for this assessment. "Though," he added, "it was lucky the constables arrived, or you would have whipped your sword through my body. Didn't you see how clumsy I was standing before you? You actually turned white and shook with anger!"

"Yes, curse me," Will said, turning red, "that is my way of showing rage. I was confoundedly angry with you, cousin! But now it is my brother I hate, and that little devil of a Countess—a countess! A pretty countess, indeed!" With another rumbling cannonade of oaths, he saluted the reigning member of his family.

"Well, cousin," George said, looking him queerly in the face, "you let me off easily. I dare say I owe my life to you, or at least a whole waistcoat. I admire your forbearance. What a pity such courage is wasted as a mere court usher! You are a loss to His Majesty's army."

"I never know whether you are joking or serious," Will growled.

"Few gentlemen would dare joke with you if they valued their lives or ears!" cried Mr. Warrington, enjoying the sight of his noble kinsman choking back curses, grinding his teeth, and smothering his cowardly anger. "And you should moderate your expressions regarding the dear Countess and my lord your brother. Of you, they speak most tenderly. Her ladyship has told me everything."

"What everything?" Will cried, aghast.

"As much as women ever do tell," Warrington replied. "She admitted she thought you had been a little enamored with her. What woman can help liking a man who has admired her?"

"She hates you, and says you were wild about her!" Will insisted.

"Spretae injuria formae, cousin!" Warrington quoted.

"For me? What's for me?"

"I never cared for her, and hence, perhaps, she does not love me. Don't you remember the case of the wife of the Captain of the Guard?"

"Which Guard?"

"My Lord Potiphar," Warrington said.

"Lord Who? My Lord Falmouth is Captain of the Yeomen of the Guard..." Will began, listing titles until Warrington clarified he meant the Captain of the Guard of the King of Egypt, whose wife had persecuted Joseph for not returning her affection. Will dismissed Egypt as a confounded long way off and concluded that if Lord What-d'ye-call's wife lied, it was simply the nature of her sex, which he supposed was the same everywhere.

The truth, however, was that when Warrington visited Castlewood, the little Countess had shared her version of the differences between Will and herself. Her tale differed from Will's ingenuous narrative, though Warrington suspected neither was wholly authentic. The lady was grieved to think she had been deceived in her brother-in-law. She feared his life at court and town had injured the high principles inherent to the Esmonds. She suggested Will's words were not to be trusted, that a loose life and pecuniary difficulties had made him mercenary, blunting his honor.

"We Esmonds, cousin," the little lady said, tossing her head, "must always possess... leastways, you and me, and my lord, and my cousin Harry have it, I know!" She confessed that she had doubted George's courage, without which a man of ancient family was unworthy to be called a man. She forgave George, stating she could afford to despise the slanders Will had spoken. She noted that Will had paid her court, as others had, and might have prospered better had he known her lord's qualities. But she found Will deficient in courage. "No man who lacks that can ever have the esteem of Lydia, Countess of Castlewood," she declared. She accused Will of sending for constables to prevent the match between George and himself, calling him a "mean skunk."

"I say, noblesse oblige," she continued, snapping her little fingers. "A man of our family who hasn't got courage, I don't care not this pinch of snuff for him." She gestured to the ancestral effigies painted by Lely and Kneller lining the walls of her drawing-room. "Haven't the Esmonds always fought for their country and king? If my eldest son showed the white feather, 'My Lord Esmond!' I would say to him, 'I disown your lordship!'"

Over the apartment, the domain, and the village, the new Countess soon ruled with unlimited sway. Surprisingly quick to learn the ways of command, she invented regulations for herself and made others submit. Bred a Dissenter and unfamiliar with the Established Church service, she initially made blunders regarding the direction of prayer, but by Warrington's second visit, she sailed into chapel on his arm, her footmen bearing her great Prayer-book behind her with demure precision. She seemed to patronize not only the new chaplain but the service and the church itself, as if she had never heard a Ranter in a barn.

She elevated the oldest established families, grave baronets and their wives, to the status of humble squires who rode over to pay homage, knowing their distance and giving her the pass. She acquired an old heraldry book and tutored herself with a learned old maiden lady from Winton, mastering terms like *gules*, *sables*, *bends*, and *saltires* with wonderful volubility. She made little progresses in her gilt coach-and-six or village chair, asserting a quasi-regal right of homage from her tenants. She lectured the parson on divinity, the bailiff on farming, and instructed the astonished housekeeper in preservation. She attempted to teach the great London footmen to jump behind the carriage, though the height proved too great for her to mount. She gave the village gossips instructions on nursing children long before she had one herself. In matters of physic, Madam Esmond in Virginia was not more resolute about her pills and draughts than Miss Lydia, the Earl's new bride. One wondered how such a prodigious genius could be corked down into such a little bottle as her body.

Upon returning to London, she sent presents to her young friends in Dean Street: a doll for Theo and a nursery-book with single-syllable words for Hetty. For Will, her younger brother-in-law, she reserved a

The silence in the castle was heavy, a physical weight that pressed against the ribs of the young man who had just returned. Two weeks had elapsed since his departure, a duration that stretched into an eternity for someone consumed by obsession. He stepped back into the orbit of Madame de Bernstein, finding her relieved to shed the constraints of her father's household. As the carriage wheels rattled away from the estate, the dam broke. She spoke with a newfound freedom, though the specific contours of her confrontation with her niece remained locked behind a veil of silence.

Her tone shifted, however, becoming brittle and nervous whenever the subject of the niece arose. The old lady laughed, a sharp, jagged sound devoid of malice, yet tinged with a frantic energy. She dismissed the notion of molding the girl, speaking instead of Eugene, her nephew, whom she declared doomed to eternal subservience under a wife's rule. Order, she insisted, was the only medicine available. Her assessment of the American gentleman, Mr. Van den Bosch, was pragmatic: a shrewd steward, a man of small means but large utility. She treated the Countess's lineage with a casual indifference, brushing aside rumors of a convict mother transported from England, a woman who had supposedly beaten hemp in various prisons. To the old lady, birth was a trivial variable; the Countess possessed a name of sufficient magnitude to purchase entry into any salon. Whether her father was a grocer, a smuggler, or a dealer in human souls mattered less than the fact that the Countess of Castlewood could afford to be anyone's daughter. Once the nephew presented her, the duty was clear: stand by her.

Mr. Warrington, observing the deep currents binding his aunt to her nephew, hoped she would extend similar warmth to the niece. He mentioned his recent visits to Mr. Hagan and his wife, seeking his aunt's blessing. The request hit a wall of obstinate refusal. Madame de Bernstein demanded that Lady Maria's name remain buried, then launched into a monologue lasting two hours, populated exclusively by ghosts of the past. She recounted anecdotes regarding her niece that, given the innocence of the current generation, I shall omit. Yet, I must note the complexity of the creature: if she sinned, she was not alone in her transgression; if she repented, others ought to follow suit. Hagan, after shedding the stage, lived a life of exemplary piety, distinguished by elegance and eloquence in the pulpit. His wife, conversely, adopted extreme sectarian views, earning great respect within her fold. When I last saw her, she spoke of a peculiar spiritual illumination—a glow I suspected at the time stemmed from the liberal consumption of spirits. Still, I remembered the kindness they showed me and mine during a season when sympathy was scarce, when many a Pharisee turned away.

My standing with my fickle aunt fluctuated like the tides, dependent on the whims of the moment. Each brother, in turn, was embraced and then cast adrift. My zenith of favor coincided with the success of my play. Introduced to the town wits, I held my ground among them, deemed sufficiently bred by the macaronis and the fashionable set. Had my purse been deeper, or my ambition sharper, I might have carved a career among them. Instead, I loved a pair of kind eyes more than the brightest orbs of the Gunnings or Chudleighs, or all the painted beauties of the Ranelagh ring. Because I favored your mother, children, my tastes were branded low, a source of lamentation for my genteel family. My godly Lady Warrington and my worldly Madame Bernstein united in their disapproval, laying their elderly heads together in sorrow.

"Why, with his name, he might marry anybody," murmured Meek Religion, keeping one eye on Heaven and the other on the main chance. "I meddle with no man's affairs, and admire genius," replied Uncle, "but it is a pity you consort with poets and authors... and that, when you might have had a lovely creature with a hundred thousand pounds, you let her slip and make up to a country girl without a penny-piece."

"But if I had promised her, uncle?" I asked.

"Promise, promise!" he retorted, spinning on his heel. "These things are matters of arrangement and prudence, demanding a careful lookout. When you first committed yourself to little Miss Lambert, you had not yet seen the lovely American lady your mother wished you to marry, as a good mother naturally would. Your duty to your mother, nephew—your duty to the Fifth Commandment—would have warranted your breaking with Miss L. and fulfilling your excellent mother's intentions regarding Miss... What was the Countess's Dutch name? Never mind. A name is nothing; but a plum, Master George, is something to look at! Why, I have my dear little Miley at a dancing school with Miss Barwell, Nabob Barwell's daughter. I do not disguise my wish that the children may contract an attachment enduring through their lives! I tell the Nabob so. We went from the House of Commons one dancing day and saw them. 'Twas beautiful to see the young things walking a minuet together! It brought tears into my eyes, for I have a feeling heart, George, and I love my boy!"

"But if I prefer Miss Lambert, uncle, with twopence to her fortune, to the Countess, with her hundred thousand pounds?"

"Then, sir, you have a singular taste, that's all," the old gentleman said, dismissing me. I understood his vexation; he could not comprehend my inability to view the world through his lens of calculation.

Neither did Aunt Bernstein approve of my engagement or the family I frequented. Their simplicity wore thin on the old woman of the world, annoying her as much as holy water annoys a certain person who is not quite as black as he is painted. She chafed at my constant presence at my sweetheart's side. Rising mightily in her favor, I began to fall again. Once more, Harry became the favorite, and his brother, heaven knows, was not jealous.

Harry was now our family hero. He wrote brief dispatches from the seat of war, simple accounts that Madame Bernstein initially ignored, finding them lacking in drama. We had learned in London of the action on the glorious first of August at Minden, where Wolfe's old regiment was one of the British six securing victory. At that same hour, the young General lay in his bed near Quebec, stricken by fever, perhaps compounded by rage and disappointment at the setback his troops had just suffered.

Arriving in the St. Lawrence in June, the fleet carrying Wolfe and his army disembarked on the last day of the month onto the Island of Orleans. Opposite rose the great cliff of Quebec. Following the great action where his General fell, the dear brother who accompanied the chief wrote home a simple letter. He described his modest role in that glorious day but offered no new details on the action of the 13th of September, save for a recollection from a brother aide-de-camp: the General never spoke after receiving his death wound. Thus, the famous phrases attributed to the dying hero are no more authentic than an oration of Livy or Thucydides.

From his vantage point on the island, situated in the great northern channel of the river, the General hungered for a chance to strike. He landed above the city and below it, shifting constantly, bent on attacking wherever an opening appeared. It was surely a prodigious error on the part of the Marquis of Montcalm to accept battle on equal terms, for the British General lacked artillery. When we executed our famous scaling of the heights and reached the Plains of Abraham, we were closer to the city, certainly, yet as distant as ever from entering it.

The game played between the brave chiefs of those two gallant little armies, spanning from July until Mr. Wolfe won the crowning hazard in September, must have been as riveting as any match between eager players. On the very first night after landing, the sport commenced. At midnight, the French sent a squadron of fireships down upon the British vessels discharging stores at Orleans. Our seamen found it good sport to tow the fireships clear of the fleet and grind them ashore, where they burned out.

Upon learning our ships had entered the river, the French commander marched to Beauport, ahead of the city, establishing a strong position. Once our stores and hospitals were secured, the General crossed from his island to the left shore, drawing nearer to the enemy. He had the ships behind him, but the entire countryside facing him was armed. Indians in the forest seized our advanced parties as they cleared the path, murdering them with horrible tortures. The French matched their Indian allies in savagery. The Montmorenci River rushed between Wolfe and the enemy, blocking direct assault. Neither the Indians nor the city could be taken directly.

Determined to find another point of vulnerability, the General circled the town of Quebec, skirting the left shore beyond. Everywhere was guarded, including his immediate front. Having run the gauntlet of the batteries up and down the river, he returned to his post at Montmorenci. To the right of the French position, across the Montmorenci River—which was fordable at low tide—stood an enemy redoubt. He wanted it. Perhaps defending it would force the French chief out of his lines, bringing on a battle. Wolfe decided to gamble these odds. He planned to ferry the bulk of his army from the Island of Orleans to attack via the St. Lawrence. Timing was critical; at shallow water, his lieutenants, Murray and Townsend, would cross the Montmorenci. On the last day of July, he played this desperate game.

The silence in the room was not empty; it was heavy with the arithmetic of regret. Had your father possessed the commonest share of prudence, this chapter of his history would remain unwritten, and you, the chaotic variables of his later years, would never have entered the equation to plague him. You shouted in the passages when he sought solitude for his books; you shattered his post-dinner slumber with the biological imperative of health; you misplaced his spectacles and stole his newspapers with the casual cruelty of youth. You ruined him with the relentless accumulation of tailors', mantua-makers', and tutors' bills—a debt incurred not by malice, but by the sheer weight of your existence. When illness struck, you broke his rest; when Joan could not sleep, the utility of Darby's nightcap vanished. Every trifling ailment terrified her until tranquility became a foreign concept. Were I not the most long-suffering creature in the world, I would have discarded the entire pack.

Now, having outgrown childhood, long petticoats, chicken-pox, small-pox, whooping-cough, and scarlet fever—the delectable accidents of puerile life—what proposal could that unconscionable woman make but to convert the south rooms into a nursery for potential grandchildren? She proposed setting up the Captain with a wife, urging early marriage simply because we had done it. She claimed he was too fond of Brookes's and Goosetree's when in London. She hinted, with perverse logic, that while an entrée to Carlton House was pleasant, it was dangerous for a young gentleman; thus, Miles must live away from temptation, sow his wild oats, and marry, as we did.

"Marry!" I replied, my voice cutting through the domestic fog. "My dear creature, we had no business to marry at all! By the laws of common prudence and duty, I ought to have backed out of my little engagement with Miss Theo—who would have married someone else—and taken a rich wife. Your Uncle John was a parson and unable to fight; poor Charley was a boy at school; your grandfather was too old to hold me to account with sword or pistol. I repeat: there never was a more foolish match in the world than ours, and our relations were perfectly right in being angry with us. What are relations made for, indeed, but to be angry and find fault?"

"When Hester marries," I continued, addressing Master George, "do you mind quarreling with her if she does not take a husband of your selecting? When George has secured his living, after being senior wrangler and fellow of his college, Miss Hester, do you toss up your little nose at the young lady he shall fancy? As for you, my little Theo, I cannot part with you. You must not quit your old father; for he likes you to play Haydn to him and peel his walnuts after dinner."

On the blank leaf opposite this paragraph, written in a large, girlish hand:

"I never intend to go.—THEODOSIA."

They both married, as the note in the family Bible confirms: Miss Theodosia Warrington to Joseph Clinton, son of the Rev. Joseph Blake, subsequently Master of Rodwell Regis Grammar School; and Miss Hester Mary, in 1804, to Captain F. Handyman, R.N.

While they enjoyed the blessing of meeting daily, billing and cooing in a fool's paradise, the two young people, your parents, drifted through the world, little heeding the chatter and meddling around them. Rinaldo was as brave a warrior as ever slew a Turk, yet he loved dangling in Armida's garden. Pray, my Lady Armida, what did you mean by flinging your spells over me in youth? Not glory, not fashion, not gaming-tables, not the society of men of wit whom I encountered, could keep me long from your apron-strings or out of reach of your dear, simple prattle.

Pray, my dear, what did we say to each other during those endless hours? I never slept after dinner then. Which of us was so witty? Was it I or you? How did our conversations become so delightful? I recall a year when I cared not to attend the trial and hanging of my Lord Ferrers, while all the world ran after his lordship. The King of Prussia's capital was taken; had the Austrians and Russians been encamped round the Tower, there could have been no greater stir in London. Yet Miss Theo and her young gentleman felt no inordinate emotion of pity or indignation. What to us was the fate of Leipzig or Berlin? The truth is, that dear old house in Dean Street was an enchanted garden of delights. I have been as idle since, but never as happy. Shall we order the postchaise, my dear, leave the children to keep house, and drive up to London to see if the old lodgings are still to be let? You shall sit at your old place in the window and wave a little handkerchief as I walk up the street. Say what we did was imprudent. Would we not do it over again? My good folks, if Venus had walked into the room and challenged the apple, I was so infatuated I would have given it your mother. And had she had the choice, she would have preferred her humble servant in a threadbare coat to my Lord Clive with all his diamonds.

Once, to be sure, and for a brief time in that year, I harbored notions of going on the highway to be caught and hung as my Lord Ferrers; or of joining the King of Prussia, requesting some of his Majesty's enemies to knock my brains out; or of enlisting for the India service to perform a desperate exploit ending in my bodily destruction. Ah me! That was indeed a dreadful time! Your mother scarce dares speak of it now, save in a whisper of terror; or think of it—it was such cruel pain. She was unhappy years after on the anniversary of the day, until one of you was born on it.

Suppose we had been parted: what had come to us? What had my lot been without her? As I think of that possibility, the whole world is a blank. I do not say were we parted now. It has pleased God to give us thirty years of union. We have reached the autumn season. Our successors are appointed and ready; and that one of us who is first called away knows the survivor will follow ere long. But we were actually parted in our youth; and I tremble to think what might have been, had not a dearest friend brought us together.

Unknown to myself, and very likely meaning only my advantage, my relatives in England chose to write to Madam Esmond in Virginia, representing what they were pleased to call the folly of the engagement I had contracted. Every one of them sang the same song. I saw the letters and burned the whole cursed pack of them years afterwards when my mother showed them to me at home in Virginia. Aunt Bernstein was forward with her advice. A young person, with no wonderful good looks, of no family, with no money—was ever such an imprudent connexion, and ought it not for dear George's sake to be broken off? She had several eligible matches in view for me. With my name and prospects, 'twas a shame I should throw myself away on this young lady; her sister ought to interpose—and so forth.

My Lady Warrington must write, too, in her peculiar manner. Her ladyship's letter was garnished with scripture texts. She dressed her worldliness out in phylacteries. She pointed out how I was living in an unworthy society of player-folks and the like, who she could not say were absolutely without religion (Heaven forbid!), but who were deplorably worldly. She would not say an artful woman had inveigled me for her daughter, having in vain tried to captivate my younger brother. She was far from saying any harm of the young woman I had selected; but at least this was certain: Miss L. had no fortune or expectations, and her parents might naturally be anxious to compromise me. She had taken counsel, etc. etc. She had sought for guidance where it was, etc. Feeling what her duty was, she had determined to speak. Sir Miles, a man of excellent judgment in the affairs of this world (though he knew and sought a better), fully agreed with her in opinion, nay, desired her to write and entreat her sister to interfere, that the ill-advised match should not take place.

And who besides must put a little finger into the pie but the new Countess of Castlewood? She wrote a majestic letter to Madam Esmond, stating that having been placed by Providence at the head of the Esmond family, it was her duty to communicate with her kinswoman and warn her to break off this marriage. I believe the three women laid their heads together previously; and packet after packet, they sent off their warnings to the Virginian lady.

One raw April morning, as Corydon goes to pay his usual duty to Phillis, he finds not his charmer with her dear smile ready to welcome him, but Mrs. Lambert, with very red eyes, and the General as pale as death. "Read this, George Warrington!" he says, as his wife's head drops between her hands; and he puts a letter before me, of which I recognized the handwriting. I can hear now the sobs of the good Aunt Lambert, and to this day the noise of fire-irons stirring a fire in a room overhead gives me a tremor. I heard such a noise that day in the girls' room where the sisters were together

The air in the room was thick with the scent of stale linen and suppressed hysteria. The little maid, Hetty, stood trembling, her hands clutching the hem of her dress as if it were the only anchor keeping her from floating away into the chaos of her own emotions.

"So you are come at last," she began, her voice rising and falling in a jagged rhythm of accusation and despair. "To ask after Theo. To feel sorry that your neglect has made her so ill? For six weeks she has been unwell, and you have never asked a word about her!"

"Kind!" Mr. Warrington gasped, the word escaping him like a deflating balloon. "I suppose you call it kind to be with her every day and all day for a year, and then to leave her without a word?"

"My dear, you know my promise to your father?" I replied, attempting to steady the ground beneath my feet.

"Promise!" Miss Hetty shrugged, the movement sharp and brittle. "A very fine promise, indeed, to make my darling ill, and then suddenly, one fine day, to say, 'Good-bye, Theo,' and walk away forever. I suppose gentlemen make these promises because they wish to keep them. I wouldn't trifle with a poor child's heart, and leave her afterwards, if I were a man. What has she ever done to you, but be a fool and too fond of you? Pray, sir, by what right do you take her away from all of us, and then desert her, because an old woman in America doesn't approve of her? She was happy with us before you came. She loved her sister—there never was such a sister—until she saw you. And now, because your mamma thinks her young gentleman might do better, you must leave her forsooth!"

"Great powers, child!" I cried, exasperated by the sheer weight of her wrongheadedness. "Was it I that drew back? Is it not I that am forbidden your house? And did not your father require, on my honour, that I should not see her again?"

"Honour!" she spat the word, as if it were poison. "And you are the men who pretend to be our superiors; and it is we who are to respect you and admire you! I declare, George Warrington, you ought to go back to your schoolroom in Virginia again; have your black nurse to tuck you up in bed, and ask leave from your mamma when you might walk out. Oh, George! I little thought that my sister was giving her heart away to a man who hadn't the spirit to stand by her; but, at the first difficulty, left her! When Doctor Heberden said he was attending you, I determined to come and see you, and you do look very ill, that I am glad to see; and I suppose it's your mother you are frightened of. But I shan't tell Theo that you are unwell. She hasn't left off caring for you. She can't walk out of a room, break her solemn engagements, and go into the world the next day as if nothing had happened! That is left for men, our superiors in courage and wisdom; and to desert an angel—yes, an angel ten thousand times too good for you; an angel who used to love me till she saw you, and who was the blessing of life and of all of us—is what you call honour? Don't tell me, sir! I despise you all! You are our betters, are you? We are to worship and wait on you, I suppose? I don't care about your wit, and your tragedies, and your verses; and I think they are often very stupid. I won't set up of nights copying your manuscripts, nor watch hour after hour at a window wasting my time and neglecting everybody because I want to see your worship walk down the street with your hat cocked! If you are going away, and welcome, give me back my sister, I say! Give me back my darling of old days, who loved every one of us, till she saw you. And you leave her because your mamma thinks she can find somebody richer for you! Oh, you brave gentleman! Go and marry the person your mother chooses, and let my dear die here deserted!"

"Great heavens, Hetty!" I cried, amazed at the logic of the little woman. "Is it I who wish to leave your sister? Did I not offer to keep my promise, and was it not your father who refused me, and made me promise never to try and see her again? What have I but my word, and my honour?"

"Honour, indeed!" she retorted, her voice cracking under the strain. "You keep your word to him, and you break it to her! Pretty honour! If I were a man, I would soon let you know what I thought of your honour! Only I forgot—you are bound to keep the peace and mustn't... Oh, George, George! Don't you see the grief I am in? I am distracted, and scarce know what I say. You must not leave my darling. They don't know it at home. They don't think so but I know her best of all, and she will die if you leave her. Say you won't! Have pity upon me, Mr. Warrington, and give me my dearest back!"

Thus the warm-hearted, distracted creature ran from anger to entreaty, from scorn to tears. Was my little doctor right in thus speaking of the case of her dear patient? Was there no other remedy than that which Hetty cried for? Have not others felt the same cruel pain of amputation, undergone the same exhaustion and fever afterwards, lain hopeless of anything save death, and yet recovered after all, and limped through life subsequently? Why, but that love is selfish, and does not heed other people's griefs and passions, or that ours was so intense and special that we deemed no other lovers could suffer like ourselves;—here in the passionate young pleader for her sister, we might have shown an instance that a fond heart could be stricken with the love malady and silently suffer it, live under it, recover from it.

What had happened in Hetty's own case? Her sister and I, in our easy triumph and fond confidential prattle, had many a time talked over that matter, and, egotists as we were, perhaps drawn a secret zest and security out of her less fortunate attachment. 'Twas like sitting by the fireside and hearing the winter howling without; 'twas like walking by the *maxi magno*, and seeing the ship tossing at sea. We clung to each other only the more closely, and, wrapped in our own happiness, viewed others' misfortunes with complacent pity. Be the truth as it may. Grant that we might have been sundered, and after a while survived the separation, so much my sceptical old age may be disposed to admit. Yet, at that time, I was eager enough to share my ardent little Hetty's terrors and apprehensions, and willingly chose to believe that the life dearest to me in the world would be sacrificed if separated from mine. Was I wrong? I would not say as much now. I may doubt about myself (or not doubt, I know), but of her, never; and Hetty found in me quite a willing sharer in her alarms and terrors.

I was for imparting some of these to our doctor; but the good gentleman shut my mouth.

"Hush," says he, with a comical look of fright. "I must hear none of this. If two people who happen to know each other chance to meet and talk in my patients' room, I cannot help myself; but as for match-making and love-making, I am your humble servant! What will the General do when he comes back to town? He will have me behind Montagu House as sure as I am a live doctor, and alive I wish to remain, my good sir!" and he skips into his carriage, and leaves me there meditating.

"And you and Miss Hetty must have no meetings here again, mind you that," he had said previously.

Oh no! Of course we would have none! We are gentlemen of honour, and so forth, and our word is our word. Besides, to have seen Hetty, was not that an inestimable boon, and would we not be for ever grateful? I am so refreshed with that drop of water I have had, that I think I can hold out for ever so long a time now. I walk away with Hetty to Soho, and never once thought of arranging a new meeting with her. But the little emissary was more thoughtful, and she asks me whether I go to the Museum now to read? And I say, "Oh yes, sometimes, my dear; but I am too wretched for reading now; I cannot see what is on the paper. I do not care about my books. Even Pocahontas is wearisome to me. I..."

I might have continued ever so much further, when, "Nonsense!" she says, stamping her little foot. "Why, I declare, George, you are more stupid than Harry!"

"How do you mean, my dear child?" I asked.

"When do you go? You go away at three o'clock. You strike across on the road to Tottenham Court. You walk through the village, and return by the Green Lane that leads back towards the new hospital. You know you do! If you walk for a week there, it can't do you any harm. Good morning, sir! You'll please not follow me any farther." And she

The morning light filtered through the window, illuminating the dust motes dancing above the mahogany table where I sat. Across from me, Captain Miles Warrington adjusted his cravat, his reflection catching the edge of the mirror. He was handsome, yes, but his posture suggested a man already weary of the performance required of his station.

"You possess the rare fortune of winning the affections of a lady," I began, my voice steady despite the weight of the subject. "Of several, in fact. The Duchess of Devonshire, Mrs. Crew, the Queen of Prussia... the list is endless. Yet, when such a triumph occurs, do you retreat to the mess hall to recount the particulars?"

"Not such a fool, Squire," Captain Warrington replied, his tone flat, devoid of the expected bravado. He surveyed his sideburns in the glass, a gesture of mundane vanity that belied the gravity of the situation.

"Have you, Miss Theo, confessed every syllable spoken to Mr. Joe Blake, junior, in the shrubbery this morning?"

"Joe Blake, indeed!" Theo Junior cried out, his voice sharp with indignation.

"And you, Mademoiselle? That scented billet-doux delivered under Sir Thomas's seal—have you disclosed its contents? Observe her flush. It rivals the crimson of the theater curtains. No, Mademoiselle, we all harbor our secrets." I executed a bow, mimicking the French affectations of the era, though the sentiment felt increasingly archaic. "No, Theo, the shrubbery yielded nothing but acorns. No, Miles, my son, we do not disclose everything, not even to the most indulgent of fathers. Should I recount the incident involving the landau on the Hampstead Road on the twenty-fifth of May, 1760, may the Chevalier Ruspini extract every tooth from my skull!"

"Please, Father," my mother interjected, her voice rising with genuine urgency. "Or, since Jobson now serves you, perhaps summon him from the stables! I demand you speak!"

"What is this mystery?" Mademoiselle asked, her French accent lending a melodic quality to her confusion.

"Eh, ma fille," my wife whispered, leaning close. "If you inquire what I said, I replied 'Yes.' Behold, that is the totality of my discourse." Thus, it was my wife who had betrayed us, not I. This was the summary of our exchange as the carriage, moving faster than I deemed prudent, thundered toward Hampstead and retreated. Theo had not chosen to defy her honored parents; no such rebellion existed in her nature. Yet, we could not wed anyone else—not if we lived to the age of Methuselah, not even the Prince of Wales. Her heart had been surrendered with her father's consent, nay, his command. It was not hers to reclaim. Perhaps, eventually, such a benevolent father would soften; and if George kept his promise—whether today, in twenty years, or in the next life—she knew she would never breach hers.

Hetty's face lit with joy when, following my brief interview, she observed Theo's serene expression. "All the physician's remedies have achieved less for her," the devoted sister noted. After their act of defiance, the girls departed. I relinquished the chair I had occupied beside my invalid sister during a fleeting period of contentment. Hetty skipped back to her seat, and Charley ascended to the box. Later, he informed me it had been a tedious, obtuse sermon, noting that the boy was too orthodox to appreciate the rhetoric of dissenting preachers.

Hetty was not the sole family member to notice her sister's transformed visage and elevated spirits. Upon their return, I learned that their mother embraced both, particularly the invalid, with an intensity surpassing the norm. "Nothing compares to a country ride," Aunt Lambert declared. "She traveled to Hampstead, did she? She requires another tomorrow. Blessed be Heaven, Lord Wrotham's horses await instruction three or four times weekly, and the sweet child might avail herself of them!" Regarding the notion that Mr. Warrington might have intercepted the children on their drive, Aunt Lambert never entertained it—or at least, never voiced it. I leave it to the curious observer to deduce whether Mrs. Lambert could possibly have imagined her daughter and her sweetheart reuniting. Do women assist one another in matters of romantic entanglement? Do they scheme, plot, weave small deceptions, arrange clandestine encounters, lower the rope ladder, distract the guardians, and create opportunities while Strephon and Chloe whisper in the twilight or flee to Gretna Green? Some do. Some, having loved tenderly in their own time, retain a disposition of kindness toward their young successors as they commence similar games.

Miss Prim does not. If she learns of two youths attached to one another, she scoffs at them as fools or imagines every conceivable malice. Possessing a hunchback herself, she bites at everyone else's heels. I suspect if she witnessed turtles cooing in a forest, she would cast a stone to startle them. However, I address you, young ladies, regarding your grandmother, Aunt Lambert, who was a vessel of pure human kindness. Furthermore, concerning the current affair, how am I to determine if she possessed knowledge of it?

Thus, her greeting to Theo upon arrival was, "My child, the country air has benefited you immensely. I trust you will ride again tomorrow, and the day after, and so forth."

"Do you not think, Papa, that the ride has wrought wonders upon the child, and ought she not be compelled to breathe the fresh air?" Aunt Lambert queried the General as he entered for supper.

"Yes, certainly. If a coach-and-six benefits little Theo, she shall have it," Lambert affirmed. "Or he shall drag the landau up Hampstead Hill himself if horses are unavailable." The good man would willingly spend his guineas, his breath, or his blood to procure his child's pleasure. He was enchanted by her changed appearance; she ate a piece of chicken with appetite, drinking a negus he prepared for her—a beverage seemingly superior to the physician's best concoctions, which, God knows, had offered little relief thus far. Mamma was gracious and happy. Hetty radiated joy. It resembled an evening at home at Oakhurst. For months past, never since that fatal, cruel day—the one no one mentioned—had they shared such a delightful night.

Yet, if the other women chose to coax and cajole the simple, good-hearted father, Theo herself was too honest to sustain that sweet delusion for long. When, for the third or fourth time, he returned to the topic of his daughter's restored health, asking, "What has effected this change? Is it the country air? The Jesuit's bark? The new medicine?"

"Cannot you surmise, dear, what it is?" she asked, placing a hand upon her father's, her voice trembling, though her eyes remained clear and bright.

"And what is it, my child?" the General inquired.

"It is because I have seen him again, Papa!" she declared.

The other two women turned pale. Theo's heart began to pound, her cheeks flushing white as she continued to gaze into her father's terrified face.

"It was not wrong to see him," she continued, more urgently. "It would have been wrong not to inform you."

"Great God!" groaned the father, recoiling, his countenance etched with such profound grief that Hetty ran to her nearly fainting sister, clutching her to her bosom and shouting, "Theo knew nothing of it, sir! It was my doing—it was entirely my doing!"

Theo lay upon her sister's neck, kissing it dozens of times.

"Women, women! Are you trifling with my honor?" the father cried, erupting in a fierce exclamation.

Aunt Lambert sobbed wildly, "Martin! Martin! Speak not a word to her!" Hetty called out again, collapsing backward, staggering toward the wall, for Theo had fainted onto her shoulder.

I took my breakfast the following morning with whatever appetite I retained. My door opened, and my faithful black servant announced, "General Lambert." Instantly, by the General's expression, I discerned that the previous day's transaction was known to him. "Your accomplices did not confess," the General stated as soon as my servant departed, "but sided with you against their father—a testament to the desirability of clandestine meetings. It was from Theo herself I learned she had seen you."

"Accomplices, sir!" I replied, perhaps unwillingly shifting the focus from the core issue. "You know how fondly and dutifully your young people regard their father. If they oppose you in this instance, it must be because justice lies against you. A man of your stature cannot establish *sic volo, sic jubeo* as the sole law within his household!"

"Pshaw, George!" cried the General. "Though we are separated, heaven forbid I wish to cease loving one another. I held your promise that you would not seek her out."

"Nor did I go to her, sir," I said, turning red; for while this was truth, I admitted it was also a falsehood.

"You imply she was brought to you?" Mr. Lambert asked, agitated. "Will you hide behind Hester's petticoat? What a splendid defense for a gentleman!"

"Well, I shall not shield myself behind the poor child," I responded. "To speak as I did was an attempt at evasion, and I am ill-acquainted with diss

The document labeled "From London, April, 1760" burned quietly in the fire, reducing my mother's neat script and my own declaration of rebellion to ash. I chose this finality to spare future generations the spectacle of a son's domestic treason, just as I had previously incinerated her reply. Her letter, dignified and cold, had referred our conflict to the silent tribunal of God, asking if I could answer for my duty as she had for hers. We both appealed to the heavens, demanding judgment for our grievances, yet the great world moved on regardless. We screamed, we wept, we raced to win or lose, only to fade into the morning that shines on strangers.

My revolt was formalized in a duplicate shown to Mr. Lambert, ensuring he understood that my separation from Theo was a forced exile, not a choice. He knew the depth of my commitment; he also knew the limits of his own power. He could not force the gentle creature to drink poison, nor could he keep the bars of the wall raised forever. The wall stood, yes, but Pyramus and Thisbe whispered through the cracks. The lime and rough plaster offered resistance, but eventually, the lovers pressed through until the barrier became obsolete.

I ceased returning to Dean Street, opting instead for the open road where the air was deemed healthful. I acquired a fine horse to ride beside her carriage. The old woman at Tottenham Court recognized us, nodding with a familiarity that suggested she had hosted many such pairs before. The country air and the doctor's regimen produced a prodigious cure. Hetty served as duenna with exemplary patience, listening to the confessions of young love that filled the closed carriage. Letters flew back and forth; Gumbo's bandy legs carried messages between my quarters and Dean Street, while Mrs. Molly returned answers from Bloomsbury. By autumn, when the leaves turned pale, Miss Theo's roses bloomed again, and the cure was declared complete.

During this interlude, Mr. Warrington completed his tragedy of *Pocahontas*, accepted by Garrick, with my cousin Hagan cast as the hero. My aid to Hagan's family, disproportionate to my means, stemmed partly from the feud with Madam Esmond. Her response to my April missive arrived in autumn, matching my anger with threats of war and control. Yet her thunder reached me with no weight; my conscience supplied alternative interpretations of her scripture, rendering her oracles harmless. I told no one of my new loves, preferring to carpe diem while the railers grumbled at their ease.

I had also sent notes of sarcasm to Uncle Warrington, Aunt Madame de Bernstein, and various Lords, thanking them for obstructing my happiness. When the jovial Baronet and his family arrived in London early, they found me cheerful and assumed the match was broken. The Baroness, eager to secure a rich match, praised my wit while warning me against using it among the roturiers. Miss Badge married a gentleman of shattered fortune, and I hope Mrs. Mackshane forgives my levity. Among the guests at my aunt's house was a lady who remembered James the Second, a figure of great worldly experience. I loved her company, though I withheld the stories of the great old folks she knew—the quarrels of George the First and the Prince of Wales—for my modest muse speaks only to boys and virgins.

Yet I cannot suppress the scandal of my aunt's ambition. My Lady Warrington, tall and virtuous with "Heaven in her eye," possessed another eye fixed on the world's advancement. When my marriage seemed broken, she attempted the arts she had unsuccessfully tried on my brother Harry.
