It was not the Beauty, Miss Flora, who captivated me, but Dora, the Muse, who languished over me, reading my godless tragedies and offering consolation. Meanwhile, the Beauty was occupied elsewhere. My aunt presented her boy to Lady Yarmouth and placed Miles under her protection. Whether she sent her daughter to stay at the lady's house with ulterior views remains uncertain, though Aunt Bernstein hinted that without a fit of apoplexy, a Countess might soon join the Warrington family. My neighbor, Lady Claypole, is now buried, and I imagine Miles in his militia uniform, led by his parents to the lady the King honored, while the good-natured old Jezebel laid her hand upon his curly pate.

Life proceeded in pleasant occupation. Being happy myself, I could afford reconciliation with those who had merely added zest to my joy through brief obstacles. No specific plans were formed, but Theo and I knew a day would come when we needed say farewell no more. Whether a year or ten years hence, we waited. On our drives, we spied cottages suitable for young people of small means, devising schemes of delightful poverty. A cot and a brown loaf should suffice. Gumbo and Molly would serve us, as they have since. Who fears poverty at twenty? Our trials would only confirm our attachment. The "sweet sorrow" of daily parting made the next meeting more delightful. We exchanged precious letters, secrets whispered to the penny-post or delivered by Molly and Gumbo. Though my wife keeps them in a tin sugar-box and I have glanced at them once, I hereby command my heirs and executors to burn them all, unread, at our demise. My son the Captain, to whom manuscripts are unpleasant, shall perform this duty.

News arrived in successive brief letters of our dear Harry continuing as Mr. General Amherst's aide-de-camp following the death of his own glorious general. By mid-October, news of the Capitulation of Montreal and the loss of Canada reached us. A postscript announced that Hal would ask for leave to visit the old lady at home, who wrote as sulky as a bear. Captain Warrington remarked on this, though the claws could not reach me. I had written fully to my brother regarding my affairs in England, knowing the distance rendered his grievances powerless.

The guilt settled upon us not as a single weight, but as a treble chord, resonating whenever we glanced at the man we had broken. He was the object of our deepest love and highest respect, yet he was the victim of our collective treachery. The shock he endured was a peculiar, pitiful thing to witness for those of us holding the hammer. Our child of the heart had deceived him; our entire family had conspired against him. In the silence of heaven, where he dwells among spirits purified by earthly lessons in love and pardon, surely he forgives. With such a man, beneficence was instinctual, humility a gentle smile; to wound him was to commit a savagery akin to torturing a child or striking a nursing mother. Once the deed was done, we all groveled in the dust before the man we had injured.

I omit the scenes of forgiveness, the reconciliation, the shared worship, and the final separation when he departed for his government. The ship sailed away before us, leaving me and Theo stranded on the shore. We stood there, hand in hand, horribly abashed, silent, and guilty. My wife did not join me until her father had gone. During the interval between the ceremony of our marriage and his departure, she had remained at home, occupying her old place by her father's side and beside her sister. He was as kind as ever, but the women were almost speechless among themselves. Aunt Lambert, for once, was unkind and fretful in her temper; little Hetty was feverish and strange, murmuring, "I wish we were gone. I wish we were gone." Though admitted to the house and forgiven, I slunk away during those last days, seeing my wife only for a minute or two in the street or with her family. She was not truly mine until they were gone.

We traveled to Winchester and Hampton for what may be called our wedding. It was a dismal business. For a while, we felt utterly lonely, grieving for our dear father as if we had buried him or driven him to the grave by our undutifulness.

I instructed Sampson to announce our marriage in the newspapers. (My wife used to hang her head low before the poor fellow afterward.) I took Mrs. Warrington back to my old lodgings in Bloomsbury, where there was ample room for us, and our modest married life began. I wrote home to my mother in Virginia, revealing no particulars save that Mr. Lambert was departing for his government and that, bound in honor, I intended to fulfill my promise to his dearest daughter. I also stated my intention to complete my studies for the Bar, qualifying myself for employment at home or in any colony. My good Mrs. Mountain answered this letter, acting on the desire of Madam Esmond, who deemed it best for the sake of peace that my communications be conducted through her.

I found my relatives in a fury that was perfectly amusing to witness. The butler's face, as he said, "Not at home," at my uncle's house in Hill Street, was a blank tragedy that might have been studied by Garrick. My poor little wife was on my arm, and we were tripping away, laughing at the fellow's accueil, when we came upon my lady in a street stoppage in her chair. I took off my hat and made her the lowest possible bow. I affectionately asked after my dear cousins. "I—I wonder you dare look me in the face!" Lady Warrington gasped out. "Nay, don't deprive me of that precious privilege!" I replied. "Move on, Peter," she screamed to her chairman. "Your ladyship would not impale your own husband's flesh and blood!" I retorted. She rattled up the glass of her chair in a fury. I kissed my hand, removed my hat, and performed another of my very finest bows.

Shortly thereafter, walking in Hyde Park with my dearest companion, I met my little cousin exercising on horseback with a groom behind him. As soon as he saw us, he galloped up to us, the groom powdering afterwards and bawling out, "Stop, Master Miles, stop!"

"I am not to speak to my cousin," said Miles, "but telling you to send my love to Harry is not speaking to you, is it? Is that my new cousin? I'm not told not to speak to her. I'm Miles, cousin, Sir Miles Warrington Baronet's son, and you are very pretty!" "Now, duee now, Master Miles," said the groom, touching his hat to us, and the boy trotted away laughing, glancing back over his shoulder. "You see how my relations have determined to treat me," I said to my partner. "As if I married you for your relations!" said Theo, her eyes beaming with joy and love into mine. Ah, how happy we were! How brisk and pleasant the winter! How snug the kettle by the fire, where the abashed Sampson sometimes came to make the punch; how delightful the night at the theatre, for which our friends brought us tickets, and where we daily expected our new play of *Pocahontas* would rival the successes of all former tragedies.

The fickle old aunt of Clarges Street, who had received me on my first coming to London with my wife in a burst of scorn, mollified presently. As soon as she came to know Theo—who she had pronounced to be an insignificant little country-faced chit—she fell utterly in love with her, inviting her to tea and supper every day when there was no other company. "As for company, my dears," she would say, "I don't ask you. You are no longer *du monde*. Your marriage has put that entirely out of the question." So she would have us come to amuse her, entering and exiting by the back stairs. My wife was fine lady enough to feel only amused at this reception; and I must do the Baroness's domestics the justice to say that, had we been duke and duchess, we could not have been received with more respect. Madame de Bernstein was very much tickled and amused with my story of Lady Warrington and the chair. I acted it for her, giving her anecdotes of the pious Baronet's lady and her daughters, which pleased the mischievous, lively old woman.

The Dowager Countess of Castlewood, now established in her house at Kensington, gave us that kind of welcome which genteel ladies extend to their poorer relatives. We went once or twice to her ladyship's draws at Kensington; but, losing more money at cards and spending more money in coach-hire than I liked to afford, we speedily gave up those entertainments. I dare say we were no more missed or regretted than other people in the fashionable world, who are carried by death, debt, or other accident out of the polite sphere. My Theo did not in the least regret this exclusion. She had made her appearance at one of these draws, attired in some little ornaments which her mother had left behind, by which the good lady set some store; but I thought her own white neck was a great deal prettier than these poor twinkling stones. There were dowagers whose wrinkled old bones blazed with rubies and diamonds, which, I am sure, they would gladly have exchanged for her modest parure of beauty and freshness. Not a soul spoke to her—except, to be sure, Beau Lothair, a friend of Mr. Will's, who prowled about Bloomsbury afterwards and even sent my wife a billet. I met him in Covent Garden shortly after and promised to break his ugly face if ever I saw it in the neighborhood of my lodgings, and Madam Theo was molested no further.

The only one of our relatives who came to see us (Madame de Bernstein never came; she sent her coach for us sometimes, or made inquiries regarding us by her woman or her major-domo) was our poor Maria, who, with her husband, Mr. Hagan, often took a share of our homely dinner. Then we had friend Spencer from the Temple, who admired our Arcadian felicity and gently asked our sympathy for his less fortunate loves; and twice or thrice the famous Doctor Johnson came in for a dish of Theo's tea. A dish? A pailful! "And a pail the best thing to feed him, sar!" said Mr. Gumbo, indignantly; for the Doctor's appearance was not pleasant, nor his linen particularly white. He snorted, he grew red, and sputtered in feeding; he flung his meat about and bawled out in contradiction, annoying my Theo, whom he professed to admire greatly, by saying every time he saw her, "Madam, you do not love me; I see by your manner you do not love me; though I admire you, and come here for your sake. Here is my friend Mr. Reynolds that shall paint you: he has no ceruse in his paint-box that is as brilliant as your complexion." And so Mr. Reynolds, a most perfect and agreeable gentleman, would have painted my wife; but I knew what his price was and did not choose to incur that expense. I wish I had now, for the sake of the children, that they might see what yonder face was like some fifty-three years ago. To me, madam, 'tis the same now as ever; and your ladyship is always young!

What annoyed Mrs. Warrington with Dr. Johnson more than his contradictions, his sputterings, and his dirty nails, was, I think, an unfavorable opinion which he formed of my new tragedy. Hagan once proposed that he should read some scenes from it after tea

The air in the theater was thick with the scent of unwashed bodies and impending failure. I stood on the stage, watching the darkness swallow the lights, feeling no shock, only a detached observation of mechanics gone wrong. The audience was not a collection of souls but a shifting mass of indifference, punctuated by the sharp, ugly sounds of rejection.

Miss Pritchard had entered earlier, dressed as a Red Indian. She wore the costume with a terrible accuracy, a fidelity to history that seemed to invite mockery rather than reverence. When she rushed to the stake, clutching the captive, the house did not hold its breath. Instead, a voice from the pit erupted—a crude, repetitive chant about the Belle Savage. It was a joke, trivial and malicious, designed to break the spell of the moment. The laughter followed, fitful and explosive, turning the sacred drama into a potboiler. I watched the actors shrink under the weight of the noise. They were professionals, trained to endure, yet here they were, stripped of their dignity by the ignorance of the crowd.

My friend Sampson, usually so buoyant, had retreated into the shadows. He claimed the house swarmed with enemies, men who wanted the galleries washed with brimstone. He spoke of conspiracies involving managers and actors, weaving a narrative of betrayal that explained everything and nothing. He threatened to throw a rival over the edge, a gesture of loyalty that felt increasingly absurd in the face of such a diffuse, invisible enemy. We had hoped to sell our eggs at market; instead, they lay broken on the floor of our expectations.

Earlier, before the curtain fell, I had noted the signs. The manager, a man of flippant demeanor, had dismissed my presence with a wave, telling me to fetch punch elsewhere lest my countenance darken the room further. His face was grim, matching the mood of the greenroom where everyone looked doubtful. Miss Pritchard suffered from a headache; the wig on Hagan's head was powdered poorly; the atmosphere was one of quiet dread. I had argued with him then, asserting my right to comment on his face while denying his authority over mine. It was a petty exchange, a small rebellion against the machinery of the theater, but it highlighted the distance between us. He was made for the stage; I was merely the ghost haunting it.

As the second act ended, the news arrived. Not from the battlefield, but from the box office. The tide had turned decisively against us. I walked out, calm as a captain steering a sinking ship, accepting the gales and the broken masts as inevitable forces of nature. No one could blame me for the clamor of the crowd; I could not stop the wind, nor the voices. I carried my defeat with a stoicism that perhaps bordered on arrogance, refusing to show the tremor that must have been inside me.

Back at home, the silence was heavier than the noise of the theater. My wife, Theo, waited for me. Her face was pale, drained of color, betraying the knowledge of the disaster before I could speak. On the table lay the gifts from the gardener: flowers arranged with care, tiny painted standards meant to celebrate a victory. They looked ridiculous now, artifacts of a hope that had evaporated. I looked at her, at the artless trophies she had set up for a hero who had failed, and the dam broke. The courage I had maintained on stage crumbled instantly. The pain was sharp, a physical blow that echoed the loss of the night, proving that despite the calm exterior, the heart remains vulnerable to the collapse of dreams.

The air in the room was still, thick with the scent of old paper and the quiet accumulation of years. I sit here, an elderly gentleman, reflecting on a rash decision made when we were barely out of our teens—a leap into matrimony that my dear partner encouraged despite the odds. As a man and a father, burdened by the arithmetic of mutton chops and the baker's bill, surrounded by children who might flee to Scotland tomorrow citing our own examples, I know I should adopt a demure posture. I should offer myself as a warning, a cautionary tale for the hot-headed youth.

Yet, the truth is that the life my relatives predicted so gloomily has defied their prudence. It has had its trials, certainly, but I recall them without bitterness. Time, by divine ordinance, acts as a benign consoler for passionate griefs. We endured days of poverty to the astonishment of our sympathizing kin, yet the rewards were precious, too great to whisper aloud except to the One Ear that hears the prayers of all men. To marry without a competence is a crime against social codes, no doubt, but do not thousands commit this annually, trusting only in heaven, health, and labor? Why must young people wait for a fully furnished cottage, a stocked cellar, and a strong box full of money before beginning housekeeping? Were the laws governing the genteel class made universal, the increase of the world would cease. Our gentlefolk tremble in silk stockings, waiting for a bridge or a gilt barge; the poor do not fear to wet their bare feet in the brook, trusting fate and strength to carry them over. Who would wish to consign a daughter to poverty? Yet, I look at my wife and ask her pardon for imposing such a task on one so gentle. I think of the trials she endured and am thankful for the unfailing love and constancy with which God strengthened her to bear them.

My own judgment on marriage is flawed; mine was imprudent and yet so happy that I dare not counsel the young. I have endured poverty and found it tolerable, perhaps even delightful. Had I not undergone it, I could never have known the kindness of friends, the delight of gratitude, or the surprising joys that accompany a scanty meal and a narrow fire. Superfluous pity is often wasted on the decent poor. Good-natured folk stepping from sunshine into narrow obscurity stumble over obstacles invisible to us, surprised that we drink small beer cheerfully and say grace over cold mutton.

General Lambert, my father-in-law, married his Molly as a subaltern with a purse nearly empty as my own. They had their ups and downs. I suspect, though my wife will never admit it, they married without parental leave, as young people did in their time. They were so pleased with their luck that they did not grudge their children's hardships. When I presented my pecuniary statements to Mr. Lambert, believing him to be master of two thousand pounds, he departed for Jamaica at ease regarding his daughter's future. After outfitting the family, he left no richer than his son-in-law. His portion for Theo consisted of trinkets, lace from Aunt Lambert, and twenty new guineas sewn into a purse by her mother and sisters. In valuing my stock, I counted as a good debt the sum my honored mother refused to acknowledge until her death. She argued those funds improved the estate destined for me, saving them for my poor brother, who, lacking inheritance, would have spent his own means had he not imagined himself sole heir to the Virginian property—a point she emphasized in many letters. He distinguished himself in the service of his king. To purchase his promotion was, she supposed, his brother's duty. Once I finished my bar studies and dramatic amusements, Madam Esmond invited me home to take the colonial place to which my birth entitled me, communicating this through Mountain before news of my marriage reached her.

Her expressions of maternal indignation upon learning of my step were clear. Upon the pacification of Canada, my dear Harry sought leave and visited Virginia. He wrote of the splendid entertainments my mother staged in his honor. Castlewood, uninhabited since our European departure, opened its doors to colonial friends. The friend of Wolfe and soldier of Quebec was received with becoming honors. Dismal quarrels ensued, however, as Harry persisted in his friendship with Colonel Washington of Mount Vernon, whose praises Harry never tired of singing. I allow the gentleman every virtue; in the struggles that ended fatally for England, I admire General Washington's constancy and success as well as his warmest friends.

If these battles between Harry and our mother were frequent, as his letters described, I wondered why he remained at home. One reason suggested itself, which I hesitated to share with Mrs. Warrington. We had discussed Hetty's romantic attachment to my brother, wondering why he had never discovered it. Naturally, my gentleman had found a young lady at home more to his taste than our dear Hester, accounting for his prolonged stay.

Soon, a letter from him admitted this fact without full confession. A person was described, unnamed, a being of beauty and perfection. My wife asked to see the letter; I showed it to her with a very sad face. To my surprise, she read it without corresponding sorrow.

"I have thought of this before, my love," I said. "I feel with you for your disappointment regarding poor Hetty."

"Ah! poor Hetty," said Theo, looking down at the carpet.

"It would never have done," I replied.

"No—they would not have been happy," sighed Theo.

"How strange he never should have found out her secret!" I continued.

She looked me full in the face with an odd expression. "Pray, what does that look mean?" I asked.

"Nothing, my dear—nothing! Only I am not surprised!" said Theo, blushing.

"What," I asked, "can there be another?"

"I am sure I never said so, George," the lady said hurriedly. "But if Hetty has overcome her childish folly, ought we not all to be glad? Do you gentlemen suppose that you only are to fall in love and grow tired, indeed?"

"What!" I said, with a strange commotion of my mind. "Do you mean to tell me, Theo, that you ever cared for any one but me?"

"Oh, George," she whimpered. "When I was at school, there was—one of the boys of Doctor Backhouse's school, who sate in the loft next to us; and I thought he had lovely eyes, and I was so shocked when I recognised him behind the counter at Mr. Grigg's the mercer's, when I went to buy a cloak for baby, and I wanted to tell you, my dear, and I didn't know how!"

I went to see this creature with the lovely eyes, having made my wife describe the fellow's dress. I saw a little bandy-legged wretch in a blue camlet coat, with red hair tied with a dirty ribbon. About him, I forbore even to reproach my wife; nor will she ever know that I have looked at the fellow, until she reads this confession. If our wives saw us as we are, I thought, would they love us as they do? Are we as much mistaken in them as they in us? I look into one candid face at least, and think it never has deceived me.

Lest I encourage my young people to imitate my own imprudence, I will not detail the small capital with which Mrs. Theo and I commenced life. The unfortunate tragedy brought us nothing, though recent reviewers have spoken favorably of its merits, and Mr. Kemble himself commended it. Our kind friend Lord Wrotham proposed publishing it by subscription, sending a banknote for a hundred copies, but I was averse to that method of levying money. Preferring my poverty sine dote, I locked up the manuscript, with my poor girl's verses inserted at the first page. I know not why the piece offended at court, except that an actor who ran off with an earl's daughter performed a principal part. Sentiments placed in the mouths of Indian characters, declaiming against ambition and British rule, were pronounced dangerous and unconstitutional, taking away any hope of royal favor.

What was to be done? A few months after the tragedy's failure, counting the remains of my fortune, I concluded I must beat a retreat from my pretty apartments in Bloomsbury. I gave warning to our good landlady, stating my wife's health required country lodgings. We went no farther than Lambeth, our faithful Gumbo and Molly following. Though as poor as might be, we were waited on by a maid and a lackey in livery, like folks of condition. Kind relatives cried out against our extravagance; indeed, are they not the people who find our faults and proclaim them to the world?

Returning home from London, where I had visited booksellers, I recognized the family arms and livery on a grand gilt chariot before a public-house near our lodgings. Loitering inhabitants gazed with awe at the footmen, resplendent in the sun, quaffing blazing pots of beer. I found my Lady Castlewood seated opposite my wife in our little apartment, whence we enjoyed a bright prospect of the river, covered with barges and wherries, and the ancient towers and trees of the Archbishop's palace and gardens. Mrs. Theo, with her droll way of describing persons and scenes, narrated the particulars of her ladyship's conversation upon departure.

"I have been here this ever-so-long," said the Countess, "gossiping with cousin Theo, while you have been away at the coffee-house, I dare say,

The Royal George was born amidst illuminations and cannonade, a spectacle witnessed by multitudes behind gilt railings. In contrast, the cradle of our little Prince rested in silence, attended only by Gumbo and Molly, whose loyalty to the heir of my poverty matched the fervor of any courtier. We named him Miles, not George, honoring a distant, beloved figure of my paternal line rather than the paragon of the throne. Theo and I agreed on this deviation, seeking a connection to a singular friend in a world where affection from one's own blood was increasingly scarce. We announced the birth to the world via the *Daily Advertiser*, citing Church Street, Lambeth, as our humble precinct.

Aunt Bernstein replied with a fine silk coverlid edged in lace fit for royalty, though she questioned why we pointed out our lodging in such a thoroughfare. The lace was useless to us; the cost alone would have fed us, yet Theo and Molly cherished the gift, draping our eldest son's cradle in finery surpassing that of many nobles. Dr. Heberden visited frequently, recommending a local surgeon, though my wife required little more than the care of our landlady and Molly. Soon, the gilded wheels of Lady Castlewood's chariot adorned our gate once more. She delivered a pot of jelly, undoubtedly served previously at her own banquets, and recounted the court's splendor regarding the new heir.

Mr. Johnson, visiting on the same day, bowed respectfully to her magnificence. She addressed me as cousin with affable benignity, transferring the jelly from her silver dish to our crockery pan. Dr. Heberden tasted the confection, declaring it excellent and noting that such delicacies must have graced tables of the great, quivering under the gaze of celebrated beauties and shared with distinguished company. He consumed a considerable portion of Lady Castlewood's benefaction. Yet, as for the epithet "delicious," my wife rejected it, finding the taste disagreeable, while Molly, flinging up her head, declared it mouldy.

Our boy possessed the privilege of an Earl's daughter as godmother; Lady Maria, our cousin, performed the office with a tenderness born of her own lost maternal happiness. Captain Miles, a fine gentleman in the Prince's Hussars, ensured that the little garments prepared for our child were not wasted, mindful of his own infant past. Sampson christened him in the Southwark chapel where our marriage had taken place. The celebrant read the Prayer-book with noble impressiveness until his voice failed near the end, leaving the small congregation to wipe their eyes. Hagan remarked that even Garrick could not have read the words more tenderly.

The origin of the name Miles occurred days prior to the christening. Just as we intended to name our firstborn after his father, a patter of horse hooves galloped up to our gate. Young Miles, our cousin, pulled the bell. He had likely disobeyed his parents to undertake this undutiful journey. "Cousin Harry gave me my little horse," he explained, his voice breathless. "I can't help liking you because you are so like Harry, and because they're always saying things of you at home, and it's a shame. I have brought my whistle and coral that my godmamma Lady Suckling gave me for your little boy. And if you're so poor, cousin George, here's my gold moidore. It's worth ever so much, and it's no use to me, because I mayn't spend it, you know."

We lifted the boy to Theo in her room. He climbed the stairs in his little tramping boots, proud of them. Theo kissed him and accepted the coin. That moidore has remained in her purse since that day. Later, my mother, writing through her ambassador, expressed royal surprise and displeasure at the christening name, noting it was unknown to the Esmond family. I withheld the reason at the time. Years later, recounting the tale to Madam Esmond, I saw a tear roll down her wrinkled cheek. She subsequently asked Gumbo countless questions about the boy who gave his name to our Miles—the Miles Gloriosus of Pall Mall, Valenciennes, Almack's, and Brighton.

The silence of the nursery was broken only by the rhythmic ticking of the clock and the distant, muffled roar of London traffic, a sound that seemed to press against the glass, demanding entry. I sat in the shadows, watching the fire die down to embers, contemplating the absurdity of my own trajectory. How could a boy who once skipped lessons with Harry, a boy whose tutor had resigned in despair, possibly ascend to the dizzying height of becoming a Governor? The irony tasted bitter, like the moldy jelly my aunt had once sent, yet it was the only currency I possessed.

My mother avoided the subject entirely. When we reunited, she spoke of "that terrible time," of "dreadful years," her voice tightening around the memory like a noose. She referred to Mr. Esmond Warrington only as "your friend in England" or "your wealthy Lambeth friend," never by name, and she refused to touch a single drop of his beer. Yet, at Warrington Manor, we brewed our own, and Mr. Foker, a man of shrewd business and surprising kindness, shipped barrels of his ale to Ipswich annually. His son, a fashionable sprig married to an earl's daughter, was a contrast to his father, who remained a brewer, a man of the earth. It was this connection, this accidental intersection of worlds, that delivered the guineas I needed.

But the money was secondary. It was the office itself, the Governorship I had seized, that offered a tangible anchor in a drifting sea. Mr. Foker's brewery stood near Pedlar's Acre, a site later altered by the relentless march of development. The surgeon who attended my wife, a Bavarian named Voelker, had introduced me to the brewer. Through shared pipes of Virginia tobacco and my ability to sing "Prinz Eugen the noble Ritter"—a tune inherited from my grandfather's campaigns—the barrier of class dissolved. Voelker's family embraced us; his daughter favored our infant, a child who, though now described by observers as ugly, had once been a beautiful thing. Even his son, Master Foker, maligned at Westminster for his father's trade, became my ward. I accepted the responsibility, the payment sufficient to sustain the illusion of dignity.

Mr. Foker listened to my accounts with a mixture of amusement and genuine affection. He mocked the trivial aid of my relatives—a coverlid, a jar of jelly, Lady Warrington's disdain—but his eyes filled with tears when I recounted the loss of little Miles's moidore. "I vow," he declared, "they shall have as much beer as they can drink." He extended his network to Lady Maria, treating her with obsequious deference, and funded Hagan's journey to Dublin. When Hagan departed, Lady Maria stayed with Mr. Foker, her needs met from the brewer's overflowing purse.

Foker viewed my restraint regarding my brother Harry's funds as evidence of exalted virtue. Harry, spending money he believed to be his own, sent a remittance for his promotion, urging me to draw on his agents if necessary. He was unaware of the depth of my depletion, how my capital had evaporated into thin air. To touch his money would delay his ascent; to ask General Lambert, a man of modest means, for support would crush him further. These acts of forbearance, silent and unseen, were celebrated by Foker in private letters to Harry in America, where he lauded us as paragons of humanity. He even sought an audience with Madame de Bernstein, a woman I considered selfish and worldly, only to discover, through her subsequent respectful treatment of us, that he knew the Baron from his apprenticeship days in Munich. Perhaps our family should avoid such inquiries into our uncle's past.

Life, in these moments, transformed from a melancholy exile into something resembling comfort. The bear I led was docile, dancing to my piping without resistance. It was preferable to loitering in booksellers' anterooms, waiting for the whims of managers. We spent evenings with these benefactors, sharing music and readings of my play, *Pocahontas*, performed with a conviction that Mr. Foker claimed surpassed Hagan's own delivery.

Then came the silence surrounding Miles Warrington, Jr., and the rare mentions of my paternal relatives. Sir Miles thrived at court, a figure of obedience to whatever Minister held power, much like a servant at Nero's table. I recalled the day Mr. Foker mentioned hearing on the Exchange that they intended to make my uncle a "Beer." "A Beer?" I asked, bewildered. "Can't you understand the word, ven I say it?" the testy old gentleman retorted. "Vell, veil, a Lort!" Indeed, Sir Miles was the humble servant of the state, surviving the First Duke's reign with a strange resilience. I remembered seeing him and his wife emerging from court, Mrs. Claypool presented to the Queen. On my shoulder sat my little boy. From the gilt coach window, my uncle and aunt stared resolutely at me, blind to the connection, while the footmen looked blankly over their flowers. Had I worn the Fairy's cap and rendered myself invisible, my father's brother could not have passed me with less notice.

We seldom availed ourselves of Lady Castlewood's invitations to tea, unless accompanied by the formidable Van den Bosch. Though shrewd, he lacked conversational charm, save for his daughter, who matched his intellect in discussing markets, stocks, and agriculture. Lord Castlewood was rarely present, or missed by his wife, existing in a perpetual state of unease among the old father. The Countess explained the dynamic simply: her husband desired money, her father guarded the keys. Without the father's vigilance, the Lord would have melted the plate. We paid for everything—cooks, horses, tailors—while ensuring the Lord retained a guinea in his pocket to maintain the facade of nobility. It was a delicate dance, a performance of respectability that cost us dearly.

Lord Castlewood, in exchange for clearing his estates and debts, had become a slave to the little wife and her father. No wonder he fled his own fine house, seeking refuge in clubs or any society that did not demand the presence of his captors. Leading a bear was unpleasant enough; waiting in an anteroom for an audience was harder for a man of my name and pride. Would I have traded my poverty for Castlewood's ignominy? Could I prefer his miserable dependence to my own? I earned my wage, however meager, and I never flattered my patrons. In fact, I dealt with them as sulky, overbearing, and intolerable as possible.

Yet, there was another partner in fate, a woman who bore her poverty with a smiling sweetness that softened even the hardest hearts. Fortune, usually a savage ogre, melted before her uncomplaining innocence. Despite her lack of wealth, she commanded the respect of tradesmen and neighbors alike. Mrs. Foker remarked that she looked like the mistress of the carriage, reducing the driver to a mere maid. Landladies adored her; shopkeepers executed her commands with the eagerness reserved for a duchess. I often thought of the lady in *Comus*, moving serenely through the rout and rabble, untouched by the chaos.

The Fokers lent us their carriage frequently, allowing us to drive abroad or visit the few friends we retained. Once, we visited the Protestant Hero, where the hostess insisted on addressing my wife as "her ladyship" throughout the afternoon. We also called on Mr. Johnson, taking tea in the company of the ingenious Mr. Goldsmith, and the Doctor waited upon my wife at her coach. But our most frequent destination was Aunt Bernstein. I confess, I was not jealous when she expressed a profound fondness for Theo. This affection grew into a dependency; she wished to spend her days with us, viewing Theo's child and husband as plagues that kept her favorite from her side. She hated us, in the most amusing way, for withholding her beloved.

Mrs. Bernstein flooded us with notes, pouring out heartrending appeals that resembled love letters. Her servants patrolled our kitchen. If my wife failed to visit, the Baroness wrote severe scoldings. When the child fell ill—a trial of temperaments that Fate seemed determined to inflict—Madame Bernstein arrived three days in succession, declaring the baby perfectly healthy and accusing us of feigning sickness solely to vex her.

Even the reigning Countess of Castlewood maintained an affable demeanor with her old aunt, dismissing the Baroness's status with characteristic bluntness. "Scraping and bowing to that old woman!" she exclaimed. "She is no finer than any other lady... Take away her wig, her rouge, her teeth, and what remains but a shriveled old woman?" And she was right. Beauty inevitably decays, whether underground or on the tree. Here was age without reverence, grey hairs hidden beneath paint, a woman tottering on the world with a crutch. For eighty years she had walked this path, eating from the forbidden and permitted trees, accumulating beauty, pleasure, and flattery alongside secret rages and humiliations.

"You are not a beauty, my dear," she told my wife, "and may thank your stars that you are not." If she contradicted herself, perhaps we all did. "Every woman would rather be beautiful than be anything else," she continued, gesturing to a portrait by Kneller, a painting that failed to capture her eyes, hair, or complexion. "Why have we such a short time of our beauty? I remember Mademoiselle de l'Enclos at a much greater age, quite fresh and well-conserved. We

The silence in the house was not empty; it was heavy, a physical weight composed of the absence of a heartbeat. Hard times had ended, not with a fanfare, but with the arithmetic of inheritance. My little kinsman's death had stripped the world of its uncertainty, leaving me as the next heir to a good estate. The logic was simple, the outcome inevitable: my uncle and his wife were unlikely to produce further heirs. Yet, even in this clarity, there was a treacherous undercurrent. Sampson, my uncle, vowed that the woman was capable of any crime to disappoint me, a fear rooted in the superstition of the age. But Lady Warrington committed no such treachery. Instead, she was crushed by the stroke, taught by her religious advisers to view the tragedy as a chastisement of Heaven, a submission to the Divine Will. Her clergyman told her that whilst her son lived, her heart had turned away from the better world, obsessed with rank and title, asking for an earthly coronet. Now, he argued, what avail was it to one who had but a few years to pass upon earth? What importance compared to the heavenly crown for which she was an assured candidate?

This accident caused no little sensation. In the chapels of that enthusiastic sect, towards which she now inclined with desperate fervor, many sermons were preached bearing reference to the event. Far be it from me to question the course which the bereaved mother pursued, or to regard with other than respect and sympathy any unhappy soul seeking that refuge whither sin and grief and disappointment fly for consolation. Lady Warrington even tried a reconciliation with myself. A year after her loss, being in London, she signified that she would see me, and I waited on her; and she gave me, in her usual didactic way, a homily upon my position and her own. She marvelled at the decree of Heaven, which had permitted, and how dreadfully punished! her poor child's disobedience to her—a disobedience by which I was to profit. It appeared my poor little man had disobeyed orders, and gone out with his gun, unknown to his mother. She hoped that, should I ever succeed to the property, though the Warringtons were, thank Heaven, a long-lived family, except in my own father's case, whose life had been curtailed by the excesses of a very ill-regulated youth—but should I ever succeed to the family estate and honours, she hoped, she prayed, that my present course of life might be altered; that I should part from my unworthy associates; that I should discontinue all connexion with the horrid theatre and its licentious frequenters; that I should turn to that quarter where only peace was to be had; and to those sacred duties which she feared—she very much feared that I had neglected. She filled her exhortation with Scripture language, which I do not care to imitate. When I took my leave she gave me a packet of sermons for Mrs. Warrington, and a little book of hymns by Miss Dora, who has been eminent in that society of which she and her mother became avowed professors subsequently, and who, after the dowager's death, at Bath, three years since, married young Mr. Juffles, a celebrated preacher. The poor lady forgave me then, but she could not bear the sight of our boy. We lost our second child, and then my aunt and her daughter came eagerly enough to the poor suffering mother, and even invited us hither. But my uncle was now almost every day in our house. He would sit for hours looking at our boy. He brought him endless toys and sweetmeats. He begged that the child might call him Godpapa. When we felt our own grief (which at times still, and after the lapse of five-and-twenty years, strikes me as keenly as on the day when we first lost our little one)—when I felt my own grief, I knew how to commiserate his. But my wife could pity him before she knew what it was to lose a child of her own. The mother's anxious heart had already divined the pang which was felt by the sorrow-stricken father; mine, more selfish, has only learned pity from experience, and I was reconciled to my uncle by my little baby's coffin.

The poor man sent his coach to follow the humble funeral, and afterwards took out little Miles, who prattled to him unceasingly, and forgot any grief he might have felt in the delights of his new black clothes, and the pleasures of the airing. How the innocent talk of the child stabbed the mother's heart! Would we ever wish that it should heal of that wound? I know her face so well that, to this day, I can tell when, sometimes, she is thinking of the loss of that little one. It is not a grief for a parting so long ago; it is a communion with a soul we love in Heaven.

We came back to our bright lodgings in Bloomsbury soon afterwards, and my young bear, whom I could no longer lead, and who had taken a prodigious friendship for Charley, went to the Chartreux School, where his friend took care that he had no more beating than was good for him, and where (in consequence of the excellence of his private tutor, no doubt) he took and kept a good place. And he liked the school so much, that he says, if ever he has a son, he shall be sent to that seminary.

Now, I could no longer lead my bear, for this reason, that I had other business to follow. Being fully reconciled to us, I do believe, for Mr. Miles's sake, my uncle (who was such an obsequious supporter of Government, that I wonder the Minister ever gave him anything, being perfectly sure of his vote) used his influence in behalf of his nephew and heir; and I had the honour to be gazetted as one of his Majesty's Commissioners for licensing hackney-coaches, a post I filled, I trust, with credit, until a quarrel with the Minister (to be mentioned in its proper place) deprived me of that one. I took my degree also at the Temple, and appeared in Westminster Hall in my gown and wig. And, this year, my good friend, Mr. Foker, having business at Paris, I had the pleasure of accompanying him thither, where I was received a bras ouverts by my dear American preserver, Monsieur de Florac, who introduced me to his noble family, and to even more of the polite society of the capital than I had leisure to frequent; for I had too much spirit to desert my kind patron Foker, whose acquaintance lay chiefly amongst the bourgeoisie, especially with Monsieur Santerre, a great brewer of Paris, a scoundrel who hath since distinguished himself in blood and not beer. Mr. F. had need of my services as interpreter, and I was too glad that he should command them, and to be able to pay back some of the kindness which he had rendered to me. Our ladies, meanwhile, were residing at Mr. Foker's new villa at Wimbledon, and were pleased to say that they were amused with the "Parisian letters" which I sent to them, through my distinguished friend Mr. Hume, then of the Embassy, and which subsequently have been published in a neat volume.

Whilst I was tranquilly discharging my small official duties in London, those troubles were commencing which were to end in the great separation between our colonies and the mother country. When Mr. Grenville proposed his stamp-duties, I said to my wife that the bill would create a mighty discontent at home, for we were ever anxious to get as much as we could from England, and pay back as little; but assuredly I never anticipated the prodigious anger which the scheme created. It was with us as with families or individuals. A pretext is given for a quarrel: the real cause lies in long bickerings and previous animosities. Many foolish exactions and petty tyrannies, the habitual insolence of Englishmen towards all foreigners, all colonists, all folk who dare to think their rivers as good as our Abana and Pharpar, the natural spirit of men outraged by our imperious domineering spirit, set Britain and her colonies to quarrel; and the astonishing blunders of the system adopted in England brought the quarrel to an issue, which I, for one, am not going to deplore. Had I been in Virginia instead of London, 'tis very possible I should have taken the provincial side, if out of mere opposition to that resolute mistress of Castlewood, who might have driven me into revolt, as England did the colonies. Was the Stamp Act the cause of the revolution?—a tax no greater than that cheerfully paid in England. Ten years earlier, when the French were within our territory, and we were imploring succour from home, would the colonies have rebelled at the payment of this tax? Do not most people consider the tax-gatherer the natural enemy? Against the British in America there were arrayed thousands and thousands of the high-spirited and brave, but there were thousands more who found their profit in the quarrel, or had their private reasons for engaging in it. I protest I don't know now whether mine were selfish or patriotic, or which side was in the right, or whether both were not. I am sure we in England had nothing to do but to fight the battle out; and, having lost the game, I do vow and believe that, after the first natural soreness, the loser felt no rancour.

What made brother Hal write home from Virginia, which he seemed exceedingly loth to quit, such flaming patriotic letters? My kind,

The silence in the house was not empty; it was a calculated absence, a deliberate withdrawal of resources designed to starve me into submission. My mother had severed my allowance, expecting me to fold and return to Virginia, to kneel beneath her roof and her authority. We never resolved the pecuniary dispute that lay between us until her final breath. She withheld her gold; I withheld my voice, managing a precarious existence without her aid. Perhaps, in her dying moments, she harbored a repentance she never voiced aloud. Years later, when we met, her behavior shifted—a gentle deference, an exaggerated respect for my wife, Madam Esmond. It was a language of tacit admission, suggesting she understood her error. Yet, she offered no apology, and I demanded none.

Harry was secure, his welfare a fact I could not begrudge. Consequently, every penny of my mother's hoarded economy flowed to my advantage, enriching the heir she had tried to disinherit. Time, that great equalizer, had rendered the struggle moot. When we finally stood face to face, the era of necessity was a distant memory. I possessed abundance; she could offer only surplus. No doubt she carried the weight of guilt—that my children had hungered while her pride turned away, that strangers had filled the void her stinginess created. Was she truly prouder than I? A single word of explanation might have healed the rift decades prior. But I chose silence. When I recognize a fault in myself, I seek pardon, yet it is rarely for the victim's sake; it is a transaction with my own ego, a way to absolve the shame of failing myself. This obsession with self-preservation prevents me from performing the lowly acts of humility. Consider the men whose lives are constructed entirely of lies and subterfuge. What company do they keep when the masks slip? Every smile is a performance, every wink a deception. Do they wear a mask even in their solitude, facing their own consciences? If I overlook an injury, it is not from a spirit of Christian charity, but because I possess the luxury of remitting the debt and despise the idea of settling accounts. I have known rare souls for whom forgiveness is effortless, growing naturally from the soil of their hearts. I know how to remit; I do not know how to forgive. Are we proud men proud simply to be proud?

Thus, I showed no sign of submission to my parent in Virginia. For years, we existed in a state of polite estrangement, exchanging only the barest announcements—the birth of a child, a seasonal greeting. Then, the storms of the Stamp Act broke upon us in America, and shortly thereafter, the political tempests of London claimed me. Though I aligned myself with the Tories, knowing full well I would lose, I published a letter to a member of the House of Burgesses that exposed the habitual insolence of the mother country. The sentiments were unacceptable to those in power. I was stripped of my position as hackney-coach licenser. My uncle recoiled in terror and horror. He could never endure a man in disgrace. While he cultivated an extreme affection for my wife and my son, viewing them with protective tenderness, he regarded me with a pitying contempt that amused me endlessly. He possessed a natural scorn for poverty and a corresponding, insatiable love for success. Any deviation from the conventional path shocked him; truth-telling made him pale. Yet, despite the dreadful blows I dealt him, he continued to see Theo, the child, and me. He viewed me as a melancholy creature, lost beyond redemption, past all worldly hope.

Never mind. I must devise a new scheme for living. Fortunately, the repayment of Harry's debt allowed me to survive, perhaps for months, certainly for years. Oh, the strange fatuity of youth! How could we dare to be so poor, so utterly cast down?

Then came the death of His Majesty's royal uncle, the Duke of Cumberland. It was a singular event that altered the trajectory of my fortune. My poor Sir Miles Warrington attended every court ceremony imaginable. Drawing-rooms, christenings, balls, funerals—he was everywhere. If a prince or princess fell ill, his carriage waited at the door. Whether at Leicester Fields, Carlton House, or Gunnersbury, nothing satisfied him now but attending the stout duke's funeral. Standing bareheaded in the rain, he contracted a severe cold and an inflammation of the throat. Before I had even heard of his illness, a lawyer arrived at my lodgings in Bloomsbury to inform me that I was now Sir George Warrington.

The anxieties of party and the fear of the future evaporated. We laid the poor gentleman beside his young son in the family churchyard, where so many of his lineage rested. Little Miles and I were the chief mourners. The tenantry bowed and curtseyed with obsequious fervor, striving to conciliate my honor and my worship. The dowager and her daughter retreated to Bath; I and my family took possession of the estate, a home I have mastered for thirty years. Be not too eager, my son. Exercise a little patience, and soon I too shall sleep beneath those yew trees, while the populace tosses their caps for Sir Miles.

The chronicles of a prosperous rural life are concise. The steward's books record the rents paid and forgiven, the crops harvested and rotated. They list the visitors, their duration of stay, the pensioners my wife supported, their treatments, and their deaths. They note the year I served as sheriff and the frequency of the hounds' meetings. All these details reside in our house journals, available to any heir willing to consult them. We could not afford the magnificent mansion in Hill Street occupied by my predecessor. Instead, we selected a smaller dwelling, yet we expended more money within its walls. We lacked the grandeur of liveries, equipages, and silver plate for which my uncle was renowned. Our beer was stronger, however, and my wife's charities were perhaps more costly than those of the Dowager Lady Warrington. She undoubtedly believed there was no sin in spoiling the Philistines. Indeed, she charged us exorbitant sums for the goods she left behind in our country house, and I submitted to most of her extortions with unutterable good humor. What valuation did she place upon the potted plants in her greenhouses? What price did she assign to that detestable old spinet in her drawing room? And the framed pieces of worsted work, executed by the accomplished Dora and the lovely Flora—if they were masterpieces of Titian or Van Dyck, surely the dowager could not have valued them higher. Despite our generous payments, and though we were, without boasting, far kinder to the poor than she had ever been, we initially held the worst reputation in the county. Tales of various kinds circulated to my discredit.

I had hoped to inherit my uncle's seat in Parliament alongside his lands. Yet, I discovered, through unknown mechanisms, that I was deemed a person of dangerously radical opinions. I refused to bribe. I would not coerce my own tenants into voting for me in the election of '68. A gentleman descended from Whitehall carrying a pocketbook full of banknotes, and I realized I had no chance against my competitor.

Bon Dieu! Now that we were secure in worldly means, now that obedient tenants bowed and curtseyed as we walked to church, now that we traveled to visit friends or neighboring towns in the great family coach drawn by four fat horses, did we not often regret our former poverty? Did I not long to return to bear-leading, vowing that translating for booksellers was not such arduous drudgery? When we journeyed to London, we made sentimental pilgrimages to our old haunts. I dare say my wife embraced every landlady we knew. You may be certain we invited all the friends of our youth to share the comforts of our new home. The Reverend Mr. Hagan and his lady visited us repeatedly. His appearance in the pulpit at B------, where he preached with such finesse in our estimation, caused an awful scandal. Sampson also came, another unfortunate Levite, welcomed as long as he remained among us. Mr. Johnson spoke of visiting but put us off on one or two occasions. I suppose our house was dull. I know that I myself would remain silent for days, and I fear my moodiness often tested the sweetest-tempered woman in the world who lived with me. I cared little for field sports. Killing one partridge seemed indistinguishable from killing another; I wondered how men could spend days pursuing such slaughter. Their fox-hunting stories began at four o'clock, when the tablecloth was removed, and lasted until supper. I sat silent, listening. Day after day, I fell asleep. No wonder I was unpopular with my company.

What confession is this? Here was the storm passed, the rocks avoided, the ship in port, and the sailor not entirely content? Was Susan, whom I sighed for during the voyage, not the beauty I expected to find? First, Susan and the entire family can inspect William's logbook; thus, madam, I shall not record my secrets there. No, Susan, I never kept secrets from thee. I never coveted another woman. I have seen greater beauties, but none suited me as well as your ladyship. I met Mrs. Carter, Miss Mulso, Mrs. Thrale, Madame Kaufmann, the angelical Gunnings, Her Grace of Devonshire, and a host of beauties who were, by no means, angelic. None dazzled me. Nay, young folks, I may have led your mother a weary life and acted as a very Bluebeard over her, but then I had no other

The letter from our mother possessed a gravity that would have melted iron, yet it hardened our resolve instead. We discussed the immediate journey to Virginia, the logistics of securing the entire cabin of the *Young Rachel*, and the mechanics of departure. But the calculus of travel demanded a variable we could not remove: our child. Illness was a certainty for her on the high seas, and the mother could not sever the tether. Thus, the *Young Rachel* sailed that year without us.

Another year brought another obstruction. My worship suffered his first assault of the gout—a condition that occupied me significantly, though it subsequently clarified my wits and enlivened my spirits. Then came a sorrow deeper than either: news from Jamaica. Two years after establishing ourselves at the Manor, our General returned from his government. He carried a modest increase in worldly goods, yet he bore a loss for which no accumulation of wealth could provide solace. Afterward, he ceased to wish to remain in the West Indies. My Theo's mother—the most tender and affectionate friend I have ever known, barring one exception—had died abroad of the fever. Her final regret was that she would not live to witness our children and ourselves in prosperity.

"She sees us, though we do not see her," her husband said, his voice flat. "And she thanks you, George, for having been good to her children."

We believed he would not long join her. His love for her had constituted the entirety of his happiness and business; to exist away from her was to cease living. Watching the good man sit with us was pitiable. My wife, through her manner and countless gestures, constantly resurrected the memory of the bereaved widower's mother in his heart. Whatever cheer we could muster, we offered. Yet, above all, little Hetty became his primary support and consolation. She had refused more than one advantageous match in the island, according to the General, and upon returning to England, the heir of Lord Wrotham threw himself at her feet. But Hetty preferred to remain with her father. "As long as he is not tired of me," she stated, "I care for no husband."

"Nay," we replied when the proposal of this final great match arose. "Let the General stay six months with us at the Manor, and you may have him at Oakhurst for the remaining six."

Hetty declared her father could never endure Oakhurst again now that her mother was gone. She would marry no man for his coronet or his money—not she! The General, discussing the matter gravely, suggested the child's lack of desire for marriage stemmed from some early-life disappointment she never articulated. Respecting her feelings, we fell equally silent. By this time, my brother Lambert held a college living near Winchester and a wife to adorn his parsonage. We professed a moderate liking for this lady, welcoming her when she visited, though her opinion of Hetty's determined celibacy differed sharply from mine. Mrs. Jack was a chatterbox, accustomed to speaking her mind freely and taking pride in her ability to inflict pain upon her friends.

"My dear Sir George," she announced, "I have often told our dear Theo that I would not have a pretty sister in my house to make tea for Jack when I am upstairs, always available when summoned to the kitchen or nursery, and perpetually dressed neatly in her best while I bake pies or tend to the children. I have every confidence in Jack, of course. I should like to see him look at another woman, indeed! And so I feel about Jemima, but they do not converge in my house when I am upstairs—that I promise you! And so I informed my sister Warrington."

"Am I to understand," the General asked, "that you have done my Lady Warrington the favor of warning her against her sister, my daughter Miss Hester?"

"Yes, pa, of course I have. A duty is a duty, and a woman is a woman, and a man is a man, as I know very well. Do not tell me! He is a man. Every man is a man, with all his sanctified airs!"

"You yourself possess a married sister, with whom you were staying when my son Jack first had the happiness of making your acquaintance?" the General remarked.

"Yes, of course I have a married sister; everyone knows that, and I have been as good as a mother to her children, that I have!"

"And am I to gather from your conversation that your attractions proved a powerful temptation for your sister's husband?"

"Law, General! I don't know how you can go for to say I ever said any such a thing!" cried Mrs. Jack, turning red and voluble.

"Do you not perceive, my dear madam, that it is you who have insinuated as much, not only regarding yourself, but regarding my own two daughters?"

"Never, never, never, as I'm a Christian woman! And it's most cruel of you to say so, sir. And I do say a sister is best out of the house, that I do! And as Theo's time is coming, I warn her, that's all."

"Have you discovered, my good madam, whether my poor Hetty has stolen any of the spoons? When I came to breakfast this morning, my daughter was alone, and there must have been a score of pieces of silver on the table."

"Law, sir! Who ever said a word about spoons? Did I ever accuse the poor dear? If I did, may I drop down dead at this moment on this hearth-rug! And I ain't used to be spoke to in this way. And me and Jack have both remarked it; and I've done my duty, that I have." With this, Mrs. Jack flounced from the room, in tears.

"And has the woman had the impudence to tell you this, my child?" the General asked when Theo, who was somewhat delicate, approached the tea-table.

"She has told me every day since she has been here. She enters my dressing-room to inform me. She visits my nursery and declares, 'Ah, I wouldn't have a sister prowling about my nursery, that I wouldn't.' Ah, how pleasant it is to have amiable and well-bred relatives, say I."

"Thy poor mother has been spared this woman," the General groaned.

"Our mother would have made her better, papa," Theo said, kissing him.

"Yes, dear." I observed that both of them were already at prayer.

Yet it must be acknowledged that loving one's relatives is not always an easy task; living with one's neighbors is sometimes not amusing. From Jack Lambert's demeanor the following day, I saw that his wife had delivered her version of the conversation. Jack was sulky, though not dignified. He was angry, yet his anger did not suppress his appetite. He preached a sermon to us that was entirely stupid. Little Miles, clad once more in sable, sat at his grandfather's side, his small hand placed in that of the kind old man.

Would he stay to keep house during our Virginian trip? The housekeeper should be placed under the full domination of Hetty. The butler's keys should be handed over to him; for Gumbo, I thought with excessive grace, was to accompany us to Virginia: having, it must be noted, united himself with Mrs. Molly in the bonds of matrimony and populated a cottage in my park with sundry tawny Gumbos. Under the care of our good General and his daughter, we left our house. We traveled to London, and thence to Bristol, where our obsequious agent declared he would offer up prayers for our prosperity and vowed that children so beautiful as ours—we had an infant by this time to accompany Miles—were never seen on any ship before. We made a voyage without accident.

How strange the feeling was as we landed from our boat at Richmond! A coach and a host of negroes waited to receive us; nearby, a gentleman on horseback, accompanied by negroes in our livery, dismounted and rushed forward to embrace us. Both of us were greatly charmed to see our dearest Hal. He rode with us to our mother's door. There she stood on the steps to welcome us; Theo knelt to ask her blessing.

Harry rode in the coach with us as far as our mother's house but declined to enter further, claiming he would spoil the sport. "She sees me," he admitted, "and we are pretty good friends; but Fanny and she are best apart, and there is no love lost between them, I can promise you. Come over to me at the Tavern, George, when thou art free. And tomorrow I shall have the honor to present her sister to Theo. 'Twas only from happening to be in town yesterday that I heard the ship was signaled, and I waited to see you. I have sent a negro boy home to my wife, and she will be here to pay her respects to my Lady Warrington." After this brief greeting, Harry jumped from the carriage, leaving us to meet our mother alone.

Since parting from her, I had seen much fine company, and Theo and I had paid our respects to the King and Queen at St. James's; yet we had seen no stater person than this individual who welcomed us. Raising my wife from her knee, he embraced her and led her into the house. It was a plain, wood-built place with a gallery around it, typical of our Virginian houses; but even if it had been a palace housing a little empress, our reception could not have been more courteous. There was old Nathan, still the major-domo, and a score of kind black faces grinning in welcome

The air inside the room was thick with the scent of claret and the metallic tang of impending civil war. I sat at the table, watching my mother, a solitary figure in a sea of conflicting loyalties. To the Patriots, I was a Tory; to the Tories, a dangerous Republican. The realm was out of joint, a broken clockwork where every gear turned against the others. I envied the banks of the Waveney, wishing I could retreat from this Virginia chaos, yet duty held me fast. My mother could not face the storm alone, nor could a man of spirit abandon his post. Around us, the well-affected raised their glasses, vowing allegiance to the Crown, their voices rising in a chorus of "God save the King," a mantra that seemed to suspend the gravity of the coming catastrophe.

Among the acquaintances of Virginia, Madam Esmond stood apart, a constant in a shifting landscape. While the gentlemen of Williamsburg prepared for a ball following the news of the Boston Port Bill—a celebration of defiance against Parliament—she refused. Her rejection was absolute. "I go to a ball given by rebels who intend to insult His Majesty!" she declared, her resolve unyielding. She wrote to the stewards, stating clearly that she could not attend. Yet, the irony of perception struck her the moment she stepped out in her chair. A hundred voices, white and black alike, erupted in cheers. They misinterpreted her refusal as an act of supreme patriotism. When she leaned forward, shouting "God save the King" with a voice that cut through the morning air, the crowd echoed her, the chant swelling into a tidal wave of loyalty that drowned out the dissent.

That night, the atmosphere shifted. My brother Harry, dressed in the scarlet coat of Wolfe's regiment, departed with Madam Fanny for the very ball my mother had rejected. We, as strangers to the local politics, were granted entry. I danced with the lady of Mount Vernon, a lively and amiable partner, though my mother's reception of her praise was grim. She viewed the Colonel's choice to dance with Mrs. Washington rather than the Dunmores as a symptom of provincial arrogance, a blindness to the Colonel's former status as a road-surveyor. Pride, she believed, blinded everyone, turning neighbors into rivals.

Three days later, the tension curdled into violence. On the appointed day of fasting and humiliation, my mother hosted a dinner, lighting her house against the city's enforced gloom. She invited Mr. Hardy to sing "God save the King," but when he faltered, she demanded "Britons, strike home!" The command rippled through the room, reaching the ears of the mob gathering outside. A stone flew, shattering the punch bowl before me. My mother did not flinch. Standing by the window, her frilled arms outstretched against the starlit sky, she sang louder, challenging the crowd to throw more stones. The silence that followed was heavy, broken only by the intervention of gentlemen from the tavern who diffused the situation with a mix of deference and threat. The concert ended prematurely, but the damage was done; windows were shattered, and fear settled deep in the bones of my household.

Militia drills commenced, powder barrels stacked in the cellar, and plans for resistance formulated. Yet, beneath the military preparations lay a strange contradiction. Everywhere, the cry was still "God save the King." The patriots claimed they yielded to no men in loyalty, glorying in the name of Britons, even as they plotted separation. I saw the logic in their hesitation: how could a handful of battalions and German mercenaries conquer thirteen million souls on their own soil? It seemed easier now, in retrospect, to trace the errors of the campaign—the paralysis of commanders, the misplaced strategies—but at the time, it felt like a tragedy ordained by Heaven. We watched the rise of a republic, a nation forged in the fires of winter and betrayal, led by a man who possessed a constancy rare among kings. Washington, calm amidst conspiracy, serene before the foe, forgiving despite ingratitude, emerged not just as a general but as a myth. Even he, however, recognized the danger of my work; reading my early drafts, he warned that my portrayal of the conflict would inevitably offend both sides.

This contrariety defined my family. Where Harry, transformed by marriage into a ceremonial figure demanding respect, advocated for the execution of the Governor and the Black Guards, I argued for the inclusion of enslaved men in the fight for liberty. "If you fight for freedom," I reasoned, "surely the negroes may fight, too." Harry's response was visceral, a roar of hatred promising death to any black man he encountered. My mother, however, remained steadfast in her traditionalism, viewing the idea of black insurrection as parricidal. She demanded obedience from all colors, a consistency that separated her from her son's radicalizing nature.

The fracture in our household crystallized around Mr. Gumbo. Once freed by me for his unwavering loyalty, he returned to Virginia only to find himself an outsider among the servants. Free while they were not, he adopted airs of superiority, recounting tales of Europe that bewildered the household. Jealousy festered, particularly between him and Sady, Hal's unfree son. Their conflicts escalated into brutal brawls, a clash of worlds that mirrored the larger political strife. The servants, including Molly, Gumbo's wife, resented his newfound status, accusing him of deceit and infidelity. When my mother, acting as a draconian judge, ordered Gumbo's whipping, the house erupted. Molly attacked her mistress, defending her husband's dignity against the indignity of punishment. I sided with the man; Hal and Fanny supported the lady. The resulting rift was temporary, healing quickly once we resolved to remove the "Standard of Insurrection"—Mr. Gumbo and his wife—from our home, sending them back to Europe where they belonged, leaving behind a house divided by the very lines of the war raging beyond its walls.

The colonies served as a dumping ground for the sins of the mother country. Convicts and indentured servants were shipped out, yes, but so too were the scapegraces, the younger sons, and the broken spirits for whom the old world had become unbearable. Like Cook distributing English beasts to his new islands, we exported our black sheep to browse and multiply in the wilderness. I had played a small part in this exodus, securing a colonial pulpit for Hagan, my kinsman, because England offered him no livelihood. He performed his duties with a brave honesty that credited my patronage, even as Madam Theo insisted my actions sprang from the highest moral principles. Yet, privately, I confess to a relief when she retreated to the wilderness to work out her penance. I would not have chosen her for our parish at home. Her kindness was real; she bore her own sorrows and soothed others with a devotion that shamed the rest of us. Still, we looked down on her. Oh, the shame of our pride.

She was not alone in this displacement. The Honourable William Esmond, stripped of the ability to cheat, borrow, or steal further at home, was settled in New York. His noble brother wished to forget him entirely. When the troubles erupted, William became a loud voice among the Loyalists in New York, rising to Captaincy and then Majorcy of volunteer corps. He declared publicly that Castlewood belonged only to his brother's pleasure, that his father had granted the estate to Madam Esmond for life, and that William sought to buy the reversion. We possessed the deed in our strongroom at Castlewood, registered at Williamsburg, rendering his claims legally null. But intentions mattered more than deeds. Hal and I resolved to confront him and expose the lie.

Madam Esmond's reaction was predictable. "My father, the Marquis of Esmond, a liar? And I, a cheat?" she cried, fearing he would seize her son's inheritance. She prepared letters to Lord Castlewood and the King at St. James's, stopped only by my assurance that William's duplicity was notorious and unlikely to yield truth in our specific case. Soon, news reached us of his activities: organizing loyalist addresses, promising to die for the mother country. Unable to bear the presence of such a man, Madam Esmond abandoned our rented house at Williamsburg. My family returned to Richmond, now deserted by the dissolved Assembly. Captain Hal and his wife returned to their plantation. I, annoyed by the drift of events, split my time between my own household and that of the Governor, whose eagerness for my counsel masked deep political fractures. Though the Assembly had adjourned to a tavern to propose a continental congress—a prelude to war—the opposition leaders remained friendly with the Governor, sharing his hospitality and pleasures. Once sessions ended, the gentry dispersed to hold county meetings. Everywhere, assemblies were dissolved, and a general congress was agreed upon. Philadelphia was chosen, and the conferences began, angry preludes to conflict.

We still sang "God Save the King," still petitioned the throne. But one morning, riding to visit my brother Harry at Fanny's Mount, separated from us by the Rappahannock, I learned from a groom that Master Harry had ridden toward Willis's Ordinary. Thinking no harm, I followed. Emerging from the trees onto a clearing, I found Captain Hal on horseback, surrounded by thirty or forty countrymen armed with pikes, scythes, fowling-pieces, and muskets. Officers stood nearby, drilling the men. As I approached, a strange expression crossed Hal's face.

"Present arms!" he commanded, and the ragged line attempted the salute.

"Captain Cade, this is my brother, Sir George Warrington," Hal announced.

"As a relation of yours, Colonel," the man addressed as Captain replied, extending a hand. "The gentleman is welcome."

"And—and a true friend to Virginia," Hal added, his face flushing.

"Yes, please God, gentlemen," I said. The regiment erupted in a hearty huzzah for the Colonel and his brother.

Once the drill concluded, the officers and men moved toward Willis's for refreshments, but Hal declined to drink that afternoon. We trotted homeward together.

"So, Hal, the cat's out of the bag!" I remarked.

He shot me a hard look. "I suspect there are wilder cats in it. It must come to this, George. Do not tell Madam."

"Good God!" I cried. "Do you mean that with fellows like those I saw, you intend to fight the greatest nation and its finest army?"

"I guess we'll get an awful whipping," Hal admitted grimly. "That's the fact. But then, George," he added, flashing his sweet, kind smile, "we are young. A whipping or two might do us good. Won't it do us good, Dolly, you old slut?" He playfully touched the whip to an old mongrel running beside him.

I had urged him before, in vain, to consider our British allegiance. He dismissed my arguments with a shrug: "You speak as an Englishman, casting your lot with your country; I have cast mine with mine." There was no answer to that logic. All that remained was for the stronger side to prevail. In the wars of the last century, which side held the right? The King or Parliament? The victors defined the right, and historically, the winners proved more humane than the Cavaliers would have been had they triumphed. Had the Tories won in America, the bloodshed would have been frightful—ropes, scaffolds, noble heads severed. A strange paradox: I stood on the Loyalist side yet wished for the Whigs to win.

Hal, however, distinguished himself with his regiment yet refused to insult the enemy. "The officers of the British army are gentlemen," he often said. "At least, they haven't changed much since my time. There may be scoundrels and ruffians among them, certainly among our own. Our business is to beat His Majesty's forces, not to call them names; any rascal can do that." From a name given to him by Mr. Lee, which many of his rough horsemen failed to grasp, Harry was frequently dubbed "Chevaleer Baird" in the Continental ranks. He was a knight, truly, without fear and without reproach.

When I asked how such a motley crew could stand against the British army, Hal answered with confidence. "They can beat them, Mr. George. That's what they can do."

"Great heavens!" I exclaimed. "Do you mean with your company of Wolfe's you'd hesitate to attack five hundred such?"

"With my company of the 67th, I would go anywhere. Agreed with you, I know more of soldiering than they at this moment—but place me on that open ground where you found us, armed as you please, with half a dozen friends and rifles hidden in the woods around me. Who gets the better? You know best, Mr. Braddock's aide-de-camp!"

There was no arguing with such certainty.

"You know my way of thinking, Hal," I said. "Having surprised you at your work, I must tell my lord what I have seen."

"Tell him, of course. You've seen our county militia exercising. You'll see the same in every colony from here to Saint Lawrence or Georgia. As an old soldier, they elected me colonel. What more natural?"

"Come, brother, let us trot on. Dinner will be ready, and Mrs. Fan does not like me to keep it waiting."

We headed for his house, open and welcoming to friend, neighbor, stranger, and traveler alike.

"So, Mrs. Fan," I said, "I have discovered what game my brother has been playing."

"I trust the Colonel will have plenty of sport ere long," she replied, tossing her head.

My wife believed Harry was hunting. I did not wish to undeceive her, though what I had witnessed and what he had told me filled me with natural anxiety.

The silence in the library was not empty; it was heavy with the weight of things unsaid. I sat at the mahogany desk, the inkwell a dark pool beside me, tasked with the mechanical arrangement of lies into a coherent narrative of war. Outside, the air hummed with a low-frequency vibration—the collective anxiety of a continent holding its breath. In Virginia, the gentry spoke of loyalty with the fervor of religious zealots, their voices rising in toast after toast to the King, promising to die rather than submit. Yet, beneath the surface of this performative patriotism, a fissure was widening, a crack running through the foundation of the colony itself.

I had written to my brother, Hal, urging him to reconsider his path, to see the folly of the resistance. My mother had joined the campaign, wielding scripture like a weapon, threatening divine retribution for his disobedience. Her letters were fiery, her logic rigid, but Hal's response was a masterpiece of evasion. Written with a hand that seemed borrowed from a stranger, it was a composition of such neat, temperate elegance that I suspected Mr. Belman, the Whig clergyman, had penned it.
