Hal had not answered my plea; he had simply walked away, disappearing into the countryside under the guise of inspecting horses, a ruse that masked his retreat toward the mountains and eventually the enemy lines.

With my efforts fruitless, I returned to the Governor's estate, where the atmosphere was thick with the scent of gunpowder and arrogance. The officials there were agitated, their faces flushed with the anticipation of conflict. They spoke of "Old England" with a savagery that bordered on madness, eager to ride down the "wicked Whigs" once the signal was given. They had appointed me Colonel of the Westmoreland Defenders, a corps raised in the vain hope that my name alone would fill its ranks. The officers were chosen quickly, but the recruits vanished as fast as they appeared, fleeing the shadow of the coming storm. Even my old tutor, Mr. Dempster, had ridden south to the Carolinas, seeking out the descendants of Jacobites to bolster the royalist cause, but the tide was turning faster than any sermon could arrest.

News from the north arrived in fragments, carried by riders who looked as though they had seen hell. Lexington. Shots fired. The war had begun. The almanacs recorded dates that would later be etched in stone, but here, in Williamsburg, the reality was a disorienting blur. We claimed loyalty, yet we watched as twenty thousand armed petitioners gathered around Boston, demanding freedom. Benedict Arnold, a name whispered with a mixture of awe and dread, had already begun his career of theft and treason, capturing forts with the audacity of a burglar. The British generals, marching from London with reinforcements, prepared to crush the rebellion, only to stumble at Breed's Hill. It was a blunder of epic proportions, a defeat that transformed a hill into a symbol of liberty, a beacon that burned brighter than any torch the Crown could wield.

And then, the final blow to our delusion. The Virginian regiment, selected to march under the new General-in-Chief, included Henry Esmond Warrington. My brother. By his side rode his wife, Fanny, a figure of small stature and immense courage. Their departure meant that the terrifying specter of civil war would not consume our immediate family, sparing us the sight of mothers fighting daughters, brothers against brothers. But the cost elsewhere was catastrophic. The people, inflamed by the news of Lexington, rallied behind the rebels. Farmers and ploughmen, doctors and lawyers, threw aside their tools for muskets, convinced that they could match the bravest sons of Britain.

Our Governor, a peer of Scotland and the Sovereign's representative, had been the loudest voice calling for unity. He had invited all loyal subjects to rally to the King's standard. But when the moment of truth arrived, he did not stand firm. Instead, he fled. Under the cover of night, he boarded a man-of-war, escaping to the sea, leaving his office and his people to face the consequences of his cowardice. The Assembly, stripped of its protector, was forced to rule in his absence. The mob, emboldened by his flight, descended upon the plantations. Runaway slaves, seeking refuge from the harsh discipline of the masters, joined the fugitive governor, landing ashore to burn and destroy. Where the shepherd had abandoned the flock, the wolves entered. The Governor's ship became a floating fortress of chaos, sending ambassadors to Indian tribes and inviting destruction upon the very soil he had sworn to defend. As the smoke rose over Norfolk and Hampton, the illusion of the "unconquerable power of England" shattered, revealing a leadership that had run before the first shot was truly fired.

The utility of a Colonel without a regiment is nil. A mathematical zero. The Governor and Council who had performed their ceremonial gratitude for endowing me with this title were absent, skulking aboard ships, engaging in occasional piracy and arson on the shore. My Lord Dunmore's Black allies had frightened away those of his own blood; furthermore, rumors circulated that he had dispatched envoys to the Southern Indians, promising them numbers and tomahawks to reduce our people to good behavior. "And these are to be our allies!" I said to my mother, exchanging ominous glances with her, recalling with ghastly clarity the savage whose face had glared over mine, whose knife had been at my throat when Florac struck him down on Braddock's Field. We placed Castlewood in a state of defense as good as we could devise; yet, in truth, it was the Red Men and the Blacks we feared, not the Rebels. I had never seen my mother lose courage except once, when she recounted the particulars of our father's death in a foray of Indians more than forty years prior. Seeing figures move before our house one night, nothing persuaded the good lady but that they were savages, and she sank to her knees, crying, "The Lord have mercy upon us! The Indians—the Indians!"

My Lord's Negro allies vanished onto his ships or wherever pay and plunder could be found; but the painted heroes from the South never appeared, though I confess to looking at my mother's grey head, my wife's brown hair, and our child's golden ringlets with a horrible pang of doubt lest these should fall victims to ruffian war. It was we who fought with such weapons, and enlisted these allies! Yet I dare not set myself up as an interpreter of Providence, pointing out the special finger of Heaven as many are wont to do. I would simply say that our employment of these Indians and the German mercenaries brought their own retribution in this war. In the field, where the mercenaries were attacked by the Provincials, they yielded, and it was triumphing over them that raised the spirit of the Continental army; and the murder of one woman, Miss McCrea, by a half-dozen drunken Indians, did more harm to the Royal cause than the loss of a battle or the destruction of regiments.

Once the Indian panic subsided, Madam Esmond's courage returned. She became seriously, and not unjustly, uneasy at the danger I ran myself, and the danger I brought upon others, by remaining in Virginia.

"What harm can they do me," she said, "a poor woman? If I have one son a colonel without a regiment, I have another with a couple of hundred Continentals behind him in Mr. Washington's camp. If the Royalists come, they will let me off for your sake; if the rebels appear, I shall have Harry's passport. I do not wish, sir, I do not like that your delicate wife and this dear little baby should be here, and only increase the risk of all of us! We must have them away to Boston or New York. Do not talk about defending me! Who will think of hurting a poor, harmless, old woman? If the rebels come, I shall shelter behind Mrs. Fanny's petticoats, and shall be much safer without you in the house than in it." She said this partly because it was reasonable, and more so because she wished to remove me and my family from danger; yet, regardless of the peril, she felt determined to remain in the land where her father was buried and where she was born. She was living backwards, so to speak. She had seen the new generation, blessed them, and bade them farewell. She belonged to the past, to old days and memories.

While we debated the Boston scheme, news arrived that the British had evacuated that luckless city altogether, never having ventured to attack Mr. Washington in his camp at Cambridge (though he lay there for many months without powder at our mercy). Instead, they waited until he procured ammunition, seized and fortified Dorchester Heights, which commanded the town, forcing the whole British army and colony to beat a retreat. That the King's troops won the battle at Bunker's Hill is no more doubtful than that they beat the French at Blenheim; yet throughout the war, their chiefs seemed constantly afraid of assaulting entrenched Continentals afterward. Else, why, from July to March, hesitate to strike an almost defenseless enemy? Why the hesitation at Long Island, when the Continental army was in our hand? Why that astonishing timidity—of Howe before Valley Forge, where the relics of a force starving, sickening, and in rags could scarcely man the lines they held before a great, victorious, and perfectly appointed army?

As the hopes and fears of the contending parties rose and fell, it was curious to mark the altered tone of the partisans. When news of the evacuation of Boston reached us in the countryside, every little Whig in the neighborhood made his bow to Madam and advised her to a speedy submission. She did not carry her loyalty quite so openly as heretofore, nor flaunt her flag in the faces of the public, but she never swerved. Every night and morning in private, poor Hagan prayed for the Royal Family in our own household; and on Sundays, any neighbors were welcome to attend the service, where my mother acted as a very emphatic clerk, delivering the prayer for the High Court of Parliament under our most religious and gracious King with stout conviction. The brave Hagan was a parson without a living, as I was a Militia Colonel without a regiment. Hagan had continued to pray stoutly for King George in Williamsburg long after His Excellency, our Governor, had run away. But on coming to church one Sunday to perform his duty, he found a corporal's guard at the church-door who told him that the Committee of Safety had put another divine in his place and requested he keep a quiet tongue in his head. He told the men to "lead him before their chiefs"—our honest friend always loved tall words and tragic attitudes—and accordingly was marched through the streets to the Capitol, with a chorus of white and colored blackguards at the skirts of his gown. He had an interview with Messrs. Henry and the new State officers, confronting the robbers, as he said, in their den. Of course, he intended to make an heroic speech before these gentlemen (he was one of many men who perhaps would have no objection to being made martyrs so that they might be roasted *coram populo*, or tortured in a full house), but Mr. Henry was determined to give him no such chance. After keeping Hagan three or four hours waiting in an anteroom in the company of negroes, when the worthy divine entered the new chief magistrate's room with an undaunted mien and began a prepared speech—"Sir, by what authority am I, a minister of the----" "Mr. Hagan," said the other, interrupting him, "I am too busy to listen to speeches. And as for King George, he has henceforth no more authority in this country than King Nebuchadnezzar. Mind you that, and hold your tongue, if you please! Stick to King John, sir, and King Macbeth; and if you will send round your benefit-tickets, all the Assembly shall come and hear you. Did you ever see Mr. Hagan on the boards, when you was in London, General?" So saying, Henry turned round upon Mr. Washington's second in command, General Lee, who was now come into Virginia upon State affairs, and our shamefaced good Hagan was bustled out of the room, reddening, and almost crying with shame. After this event, we thought that Hagan's ministrations were best confined to us in the country, and removed the worthy pastor from his restive lambs in the city.

The selection of Virginians to the very highest civil and military appointments of the new government bribed and flattered many of our leading people, who, otherwise, and but for the outrageous conduct of our government, might have remained faithful to the Crown and made good head against the rising rebellion. But, although we Loyalists were gagged and muzzled, though the Capitol was in the hands of the Whigs, and our vaunted levies of loyal recruits were so many Falstaff's regiments for the most part, the faithful still kept intelligences with one another in the colony and with our neighbors. Although we did not rise, and although we ran away, and although, in examination before committees, justices, and so forth, some of our frightened people gave themselves Republican airs and vowed perdition to kings and nobles, yet we knew each other pretty well. According as the chances were more or less favorable to us, the master more or less hard, we concealed our colors, showed our colors, half-showed our colors, or downright apostatized for the nonce, crying, "Down with King George!" Our negroes bore about, from house to house, all sorts of messages and tokens. Endless underhand plots and schemes were engaged in by those who could not afford the light. The battle over, the neutrals came and joined the winning side, shouting as loudly as the patriots. The runaways were not counted. Would any man tell me that the signers and ardent well-wishers of the Declaration of Independence were not in a minority of the nation, and that the minority did not win? We knew that a part of the defeated army of Massachusetts was about to make an important expedition southward, upon the success of which the very greatest hopes were founded. I, for one, being anxious to make a movement as soon as there was any chance of activity, had put myself in communication with the ex-Governor Martin of North Carolina, whom I proposed to join

The earth beneath the boots of the Continental army was hard-packed clay, scarred by the tread of armies that had come and gone. Breed's Hill stood as a monument not to victory, but to the stubborn refusal of the enemy to yield. The British chiefs, observing the slaughter inflicted from behind those lines, developed a peculiar respect for the American barricades. Whether constructed of stone or merely rows of blankets, they became immovable objects in the calculus of war. Even at White Plains, when Washington's forces were at the mercy of the victors, the conquering troops halted before a barrier of cornstalks and straw.

Winter followed, a season of dwindling spirits and empty bellies. The Congress troops lay starving and unarmed under the grasp of a superior force, while General Howe, in Philadelphia, allowed his great army to fiddle, gamble, and feast. Triumphal arches were erected in Britain, mocking the middle ages with tournaments and garlands offered by ladies to a clement chief. Yet, no statue was raised in America for this general. No one remembered the officer who fought their battles better, who retrieved their errors with adroitness, who ensured their defeats were little hurtful, and who, when the stronger force inevitably prevailed, showed an untiring tenderness in helping the disabled opponent rise again.

One imagines the fiery young warrior England sent to the continent, pacing endlessly around the defenses behind which the foe lay sheltered. Sleepless by day and night, consumed by a fierce wrath and a longing that ate away at him, he watched with intent eyes. He wound the track with an untiring scent, panting and hungry for blood, prowling through midnight forests or climbing silent precipices before dawn. He waited until his great heart was almost worn out, until the foe finally showed himself, springing upon him to grapple and die. Think of Wolfe at Quebec, and then hear Howe's fiddles as he sits smiling among the dancers in Philadelphia.

This contrast fueled a favorite scheme of the ministers at home and some generals in America: to establish a communication between Canada and New York, thereby cutting off New England. Burgoyne was entrusted with this plan, setting forth from Quebec with trumpets blowing before him, summoning the misguided rebels to lay down their arms. He brought a formidable force: German veterans, Indian warriors, and a brilliant train of artillery. It was expected that the people along his march would rally to the Royal cause. Initially, the Continental force opposing him was small, further weakened by the withdrawal of troops hurrying to meet the Canadian invasion. A British detachment from New York was to sweep up the Hudson, forcing a junction with Burgoyne at Albany. This was the moment when Washington's weakened army should have been struck; but a greater Power willed otherwise.

Looking over the game now, the blunders of the losing side appear with crystalline clarity. From beginning to end, they arrived too late. Supplies and reinforcements from home were too late. Troops were in difficulty, and succor reached them too late. The fleet appeared off York Town just too late, after Cornwallis had surrendered. A way of escape was opened to Burgoyne, but he resolved upon retreat too late. Officers in later days speculated infallibly on how a different wind would have saved America, how the French fleet might have been destroyed but for a tempest, how Washington might have fallen but for nightfall. Who has not speculated upon the "has been" and the "might have been"? I take my tattered old map-book from the shelf and see the board on which the great contest was played. I wonder at the curious chances that lost it. Putting aside any talk of national bravery, one cannot help but see that we held the best cards, and yet we lost the game.

The sport possessed a considerable fascination, stirring a languid blood. My brother Hal, settled on his plantation in Virginia, was perfectly satisfied with the sports and occupations he found there. The company of country neighbors sufficed him; he never tired of looking after his crops and people, fishing, shooting ducks, hunting in the woods, or enjoying his rubber and supper. Happy Hal, in his great barn of a house, under his roomy porches, with dogs lying round his feet; his friends, the Virginian Will Wimbles, at free quarters in his mansion; his negroes fat, lazy, and ragged; his shrewd little wife ruling over them and her husband, who always obeyed her implicitly when living, and who was pretty speedily consoled when she died! I say happy, though his lot would have been intolerable to me. Wife, friends, plantation, and town life at Richmond—how happy he whose foot fits the shoe which fortune gives him!

My income was five times as great; my house in England was larger, built of bricks and faced with freestone. My wife... would I have changed her for any other wife in the world? My children... well, I am contented with my Lady Warrington's opinion about them. But with all these plums and peaches and rich fruits poured into my lap, I fear I have been but an ingrate. Hodge, my gatekeeper, who shares his bread and scrap of bacon with a family as large as my own, seems to enjoy his meal as much as I do, though Mrs. Molly prepares her best dishes and Mr. Gumbo uncorks the choicest bottle from the cellar. Ah me! Sweetmeats have lost their savour for me, however they may rejoice my young ones from the nursery, and the perfume of claret palls upon old noses.

Our parson has poured out his sermons many and many a time. Perhaps I did not care for them much when he first broached them. Dost thou remember, honest friend? I say, Joe Blake, thou rememberest full well that October evening when we scrambled up an embrasure at Fort Clinton and a clubbed musket would have dashed these valuable brains out, had not Joe's sword whipped my rebellious countryman through the gizzard. Joe wore a red coat in those days—the uniform of the brave Sixty-third, whose leader, the bold Sill, fell pierced with many wounds beside him. He exchanged his red for black and my pulpit. His doctrines are sound, and his sermons short. We read the papers together over our wine. Not two months ago we read our old friend Howe's glorious deed of the first of June. We were told how the noble Rawdon, who fought with us at Fort Clinton, had joined the Duke of York; and to-day his Royal Highness is in full retreat before Pichegru, and he and my son Miles have taken Valenciennes for nothing! Ah, parson! Would you not like to put on your old Sixty-third coat? Though I doubt Mrs. Blake could never make the buttons and button-holes meet again over your big body. The boys were acting a play with my militia sword. Oh, that I were young again, Mr. Blake! That I had not the gout in my toe; and I would saddle Rosinante and ride back into the world, and feel the pulses beat again, and play a little of life's glorious game!

The last "hit" which I saw played was gallantly won by our side, though even in this party the Americans won the rubber. Our people gained only the ground they stood on, capturing and destroying the guns, stores, and ships, whilst our efforts at rescue were too late to prevent the catastrophe impending over Burgoyne's unfortunate army. After one of those delays which always retarded our plans and weakened the blows our chiefs intended to deliver, an expedition was got under weigh from New York at the close of September, 1777. Had it advanced a fortnight earlier, it might have saved the doomed force of Burgoyne. *Sed Dis aliter visum.* The delay here was not Sir Henry Clinton's fault, who could not leave his city unprotected, but the winds and weather which delayed the arrival of reinforcements we had long awaited from England. The fleet which brought them also brought long and fond letters from home, containing the very last news of the children under the care of their good Aunt Hetty and their grandfather. The mother's heart yearned towards the absent young ones. She made me no reproaches; but I could read her importunities in her anxious eyes, her terrors for me, and her longing for her children. "Why stay longer?" she seemed to say. "You who have no calling to this war, or to draw the sword against your countrymen—why continue to imperil your life and my happiness?" I understood her appeal. We were to enter upon no immediate service of danger. I told her Sir Henry was only going to accompany the expedition for part of the way. I would return with him, the reconnaissance over, and Christmas, please Heaven, should see our family once more united in England.

A force of three thousand men, including a couple of slender regiments of American Loyalists and New York Militia—with which latter my distinguished relative, Mr. Will Esmond, went as captain—was embarked at New York. Our armament sailed up the noble Hudson River, which presents finer aspects than the Rhine in Europe to my mind. Nor was any fire opened upon us from those beetling cliffs and precipitous "palisades," as they are called, by which we sailed; the enemy, strange to say, being for once unaware of the movement we contemplated. Our first landing was on the Eastern bank, at a place called Verplancks Point, whence the Congress troops withdrew after a slight resistance. Their leader, the tough old Putnam, so famous during the war, supposed that our march was to be directed towards the Eastern Highlands, by which we intended to penetrate to Burgoyne. Putnam

The air inside the house held the weight of a resolved silence. We had returned, the brood complete, and the mother's face bore the inscription of a joy that required no words, only the acknowledgment of her knees. Yet, the household expanded beyond its original bounds. Aunt Hetty refused the intimacy of our larger home, citing a pang too sharp to swallow: the surrender of her eldest children, who had belonged to her for three years, demanded a separation. She withdrew to Bury St. Edmunds, a nearby village, where she cultivated a private paradise for her oldest nephew and niece.

This retreat coincided with the death of Mr. B, the Jamaican planter, who left Hetty half his fortune. Only then did the narrative reveal the depth of his affection—a devotion forged in Jamaica, rejected by her, yet proven by his final gift. Meanwhile, the erosion of Hetty's independence continued, consumed by Monsieur Miles. The cost of his commission, his uniforms, his debts in the Minories, and his frivolities at the Cocoa-Tree drained her resources. Five hundred pounds a year could not sustain the architecture of his vanity. Hetty, resigned to the role of spinster, declared a vow: she would remain unmarried until she found a man equal to her father. She had found none. Sir George, she noted with a shrug, was not half as good.

Yet, when the General finally died—calm, aged, and ready to depart—the grief that settled the room belonged to my wife. She wept openly, confessing a terror that she had not loved him sufficiently. Hetty, conversely, maintained a stoic exterior, though her internal landscape shifted. She spoke of him constantly, recalling his mirth and tenderness, her face brightening into the guise of a girl again. In church, she gazed at the slab recording his virtues, telling no lies, sitting quietly amidst the memories.

I had suspected that Hal, the object of Hetty's youthful passion, still held a tether to her heart. Ten years prior, when he visited, I hoped he might propose to replace Mrs. Fanny, a loss that haunted my wife, who viewed her own lack of regret as a sin. Hal arrived submerged in sorrow, declaring no woman existed like her. The General, still living then, welcomed him as he had in his youth. The women played the same tunes from Oakhurst; hearts softened by nostalgia. Hal poured out his griefs to Hetty, recounting his wife's virtues and praising his beloved Aunt Lambert, whose eulogies were music to the faithful old husband, whose thoughts of his wife seemed never to pause for three waking minutes.

Later, General Hal traveled to Paris as an American officer in the fashionable blue and yellow, receiving attention at Versailles despite the Marquis de Lafayette's inability to secure the favor of the King and Queen. A Marquise took a liking to the Virginian General, offering marriage, but he resisted, fleeing back to England and Warrington. There, among listeners patient with his tales of his late wife, he found refuge.

We, the survivors, treated him with civility but lacked the enthusiasm of strangers, often stumbling when answering our children's questions about Uncle Hal's angel wife. The circle of us—Generals, Captain Miles, and Parson Blake, who had been knocked down at Monmouth to change coats and reclaim his living—fought the battles of the Revolution over bottles. The parson would cry, "By Jupiter, General," noting the political divide: Hal as the loyalist to the leaders, Sir George as the critic. When the parson prayed for the King, Hal followed loudly.

"I wear his coat," Hal insisted.
"If Wolfe had lived twenty years longer!" Lambert countered.
"You should hear the General talk about him!" Hal cried.
"Which General?" I asked, seeking to vex him.
"My General," Hal replied, raising a bumper. "His Excellency, General George Washington!"

The toast was met with agreement, though the parson's expression suggested distaste for the claret or the sentiment. Hal spoke endlessly of his General, eventually revealing a moment of disgrace. The friction centered on the Marquis de Lafayette, a figure who had risen rapidly through the ranks. Hal, an old soldier accustomed to merit, had expressed contempt for the congressional appointments, particularly the elevation of the Marquis, a boy of twenty who could not speak English. Washington, recognizing the strategic value of the Marquis and the diplomatic necessity of his favor, had protected him. Hal, however, had served as a colonel, spending his pay on his men rather than rank, viewing the hierarchy as irrelevant compared to the comfort of the troops.

During the winter of '77, at Valley Forge, amidst the scarcity of fire, blankets, and food, facing an enemy three times his size, Washington faced not only the British but the cowardice of Congress and the conspiracies of his own officers. The "Conway Cabal" emerged: an Irish Chevalier of St. Louis, promoted to brigadier, demanded a major-generalcy. Friends at Congress promised him the rank. Washington remonstrated, highlighting the injustice of elevating the youngest brigadier. Instead, he forwarded a letter from Conway to Gates, suggesting that a weak general and bad counselors would ruin America. Conway resigned, only to be appointed Inspector-General by Congress.

Hal recounted this era with passion, admitting his own jealousy had clouded his judgment. He realized he should have held his tongue regarding the "frogs" and the influx of Marquises. The General's demeanor grew cold, creating a schism. Mrs. Washington, perceptive and skilled in uncovering secrets, summoned Hal to the parade ground. She revealed the offense: Hal's casual remark about importing Marquises from Paris. The General, proud and sensitive, felt betrayed by a former comrade joining the opposition.

That night, Hal waited on the General alone. The atmosphere was charged with the gravity of altered times.
"It used not always to be Sir and Colonel Warrington, between me and your Excellency," Hal said.
"The times are altered," the General replied calmly.
"Et nos mutamur in illis," Hal responded. "Times and people are both changed."

The confrontation escalated. Hal accused the General of showing little trust, of allowing impertinent remarks at his table. The General asked which charge he preferred: the jealousy regarding the Marquis or the breach of friendship.
"Jealous, sir!" Hal exclaimed. "An aide-de-camp of Wolfe is not jealous of a Jack-a-dandy who was being whipped five years ago!"
"But you declined higher rank," the General noted, flushing slightly.
"But I never bargained to have a macaroni Marquis to command me!" Hal cried. "Since Congress and your Excellency choose to take your generals out of the nursery, I shall humbly ask leave to resign, and retire to my plantation."

"Do, Harry; that is true friendship!" the General said gently, urging him to leave while he was in difficulty.
"Sir!" Hal protested.
"Do as so many of the rest are doing... Et tu, Brute," the General muttered, referencing the play. "Well, well, Harry! I did not think it of you; but, at least, you are in the fashion."

Hal pressed the point, asking which charge the General would address first. The General admitted recommending the Marquis's promotion, acknowledging the disapproval of officers like Hal.
"I have spoken for myself, sir," Hal stated firmly.
"If you take me in that tone, Colonel Warrington, I have nothing to answer!" the General rose, fierce and defensive. "And presume that I can recommend officers for promotion without asking your previous sanction."
"Being on that tone, sir," Hal replied, rising to match him, "let me respectfully offer my resignation to your Excellency, founding my desire to resign upon the fact, that Congress, at your Excellency's recommendation, offers its highest commands to boys of twenty, who are scarcely even acquainted with our language."

He bowed, completing the formal severance, leaving the room filled with the unspoken weight of a friendship fractured by the machinery of war and the absurdity of rank.
